) first. Suppose, by contradiction, that there exists a sequence
\bigl(\varepsilon_{n},x_{n}\bigr)_{n}
such that

0\,\leq\,\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x_{n})\,\leq\,1/n
, and we will observe that the sequence
\bigl(x_{n}\bigr)_{n}
cannot be bounded. Indeed, relation
(
2.1.18
), computed on
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x_{n})
, would immediately imply that
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x_{n})
should
rather converge to
1
.
In any case, since
1/n<1
for
n\geq 2
and
x^{3}\in\;]0,1[\,
, we deduce that

-\,(\varepsilon_{n})^{m}\,\leq\,\widetilde{F}_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x_{n})\,=\,(\varepsilon_{n})^{2(m-1)}\,|\,(x_{n})^{h}\,|^{2}\,-\,(\varepsilon_{n})^{m}\,(x_{n})^{3}\,<\,0\,,

which in particular implies that
\widetilde{F}_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x_{n})
has to go to
0
for
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
. As a consequence, since

\Pi(1)=0
, by the mean value theorem (see e.g. Chapter 5 of
[
64
]
) and (
2.1.18
) we get

\widetilde{F}_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x_{n})\,=\,\Pi\bigl(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x_{n})\bigr)\,=\,\Pi^{\prime}(z_{n})\,\bigl(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x_{n})-1\bigr)\,=\,\frac{\partial_{\varrho}p\bigl(z_{n},\overline{\vartheta}\bigr)}{z_{n}}\,\bigl(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x_{n})-1\bigr)\,\longrightarrow\,0\,,

for some
z_{n}\in\,]\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x_{n}),1[\,\subset\,]0,1[\,
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. In turn, this relation,
combined with (
2.1.12
), implies that
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon_{n}}(x_{n})\longrightarrow 1
,
which is in contradiction with the fact that it has to be
\leq 1/n
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

The case
m=1
and
F=0
can be treated in a similar way. Let us now assume that
m=1
and
F\neq 0
: relation (
2.1.18
) in this case becomes

\Pi\bigl(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x)\bigr)\,=\,|\,x^{h}\,|^{2}\,-\,\varepsilon\,x^{3}\,.

(2.1.19)

We observe that the right-hand side of this identity is negative on the set

\left\{0\,\leq\,|\,x^{h}\,|^{2}\,\leq\,\varepsilon\,x^{3}\right\}
. By definition (
2.1.18
), this is equivalent to having
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x)\leq 1
.

In particular, the smallest value of
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x)

is attained for
x=x^{0}=(0,0,1)
, for which
\Pi\bigl(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x^{0})\bigr)=-\varepsilon
.
On the other hand, fixed a
x^{0}_{\varepsilon}
such that
|\,(x^{0}_{\varepsilon})^{h}\,|^{2}=\varepsilon
and
(x^{0}_{\varepsilon})^{3}=1
, we have

\Pi\bigl(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon}^{0})\bigr)=0
, and then
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x^{0}_{\varepsilon})=1
.
Therefore, by mean value theorem again we get

\displaystyle-\,\varepsilon\;=\;\Pi\bigl(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x^{0})\bigr)\,-\,\Pi\bigl(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x^{0}_{\varepsilon})\bigr)\,

\displaystyle=\,\frac{\partial_{\varrho}p\bigl(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(y_{\varepsilon}),\overline{\vartheta}\bigr)}{\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(y_{\varepsilon})}\,\bigl(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x^{0})\,-\,\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x^{0}_{\varepsilon})\bigr)\,=\,\frac{\partial_{\varrho}p\bigl(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(y_{\varepsilon}),\overline{\vartheta}\bigr)}{\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(y_{\varepsilon})}\,\bigl(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x^{0})\,-\,1\bigr),

for some suitable point
y_{\varepsilon}=\bigl((x_{\varepsilon})^{h},1\bigr)
lying on the line connecting
x^{0}=(0,0,1)
with
x^{0}_{\varepsilon}
.

From this equality and the structural hypothesis (
2.1.12
), since
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x^{0})\,-\,1<0
(due to the fact that
\Pi\bigl(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x^{0})\bigr)<0
),
we deduce that
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(y_{\varepsilon})>0
for all
\varepsilon>0
. On the other hand, (
2.1.19
) says that,
for
x^{3}
fixed, the function
\Pi\circ\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}
is radially increasing on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
: then, in particular

\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(y_{\varepsilon})\leq\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x^{0}_{\varepsilon})=1
.

Finally, thanks to these relations and the regularity properties (
2.1.10
) and (
2.1.11
), we see that

\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x^{0})\,=\,1\,-\,\varepsilon\,\frac{\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(y_{\varepsilon})}{\partial_{\varrho}p\bigl(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(y_{\varepsilon}),\overline{\vartheta}\bigr)}

remains strictly positive, at least for
\varepsilon
small enough.

For simplicity, and without loss of any generality, we assume from now on that
\varepsilon_{0}=1
in Lemma
2.1.5
.

Next, denoted as above
B_{l}(0)
the ball in the horizontal variables
x^{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
of center
0
and radius
l>0
, we define the cylinder
with smoothed corners

\mathbb{B}_{L}:=\left\{x\in\Omega\ :\ |x^{h}|<L\right\}=B_{L}(0)\times\,]0,1[\,.

We can now state the next boundedness property for the family
\bigl(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\bigr)_{\varepsilon}
.

Lemma 2.1.6

Let
m\geq 1
. Let
F
and
G
satisfy (
2.1.7
). Then, for any
l>0
, there exists a constant
C(l)>1
such that for all
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
one has

\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\,\leq\,C(l)\qquad\qquad\mbox{ on }\qquad\overline{\mathbb{B}}_{l}\,.

(2.1.20)

If
F=0
, then there exists
C>1
such that, for all
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
and all
x\in\Omega
, one has
\left|\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x)\right|\leq C
.

Proof:

Let us focus on the case
m>1
and
F\neq 0
for a while.
In order to see (
2.1.20
), we proceed in two steps. First of all, we fix
\varepsilon
and we show that
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}

is bounded in the previous set. Assume it is not: there exists a sequence
\bigl(x_{n}\bigr)_{n}\subset\overline{\mathbb{B}}_{l}

such that
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x_{n})\geq n
. But then, thanks to hypothesis (
2.1.13
), we can write

\Pi\bigl(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x_{n})\bigr)\,\geq\,\int^{n}_{1}\frac{\partial_{\varrho}p(z,\overline{\vartheta})}{z}{\rm\,d}z\,\geq\,C(\overline{\vartheta})\,\int^{n/\overline{\vartheta}^{3/2}}_{1/\overline{\vartheta}^{3/2}}\frac{P(Z)}{Z^{2}}{\rm\,d}Z\,,

and, by use of (
2.1.14
), it is easy to see that the last integral diverges to
+\infty
for
n\rightarrow+\infty
. On the other hand,
on the set
\overline{\mathbb{B}}_{l}
, the function
\widetilde{F}_{\varepsilon}
is uniformly bounded by the constant

l^{2}+1
, and, recalling formula (
2.1.18
), these two facts are in contradiction one with other.

So, we have proved that,
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\,\leq\,C(\varepsilon,l)
on the set
\overline{\mathbb{B}}_{l}
.
But, thanks to point (i) below (
2.1.18
), the pointwise convergence of
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}
to
1
becomes uniform in the
previous set, so that the constant
C(\varepsilon,l)
can be dominated by a new constant
C(l)
, just depending on the fixed
l
.

Let us now take
m=1
and
F\neq 0
. We start by observing that, again, the following property holds true:
for any
\varepsilon
and any
l>0
fixed, one has
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\,\leq\,C(\varepsilon,l)
in
\overline{\mathbb{B}}_{l}
.
Furthermore, by point (ii) below (
2.1.18
) we have that
\widetilde{\varrho}\in C^{2}(\Omega)
, and then
\widetilde{\varrho}
is locally bounded:
for any
l>0
fixed, we have
\widetilde{\varrho}\,\leq\,C(l)
on the set
\overline{\mathbb{B}}_{l}
. On the other hand, the pointwise convergence of

\bigl(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\bigr)_{\varepsilon}
towards
\widetilde{\varrho}
becomes uniform on the compact set
\overline{\mathbb{B}}_{l}
:
gluing these facts together, we infer that, in the previous bound for
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}
, we can replace
C(\varepsilon,l)
by a constant
C(l)
which is uniform in
\varepsilon
.

Consider now the case
F=0
, and any value
m\geq 1
. In this case, relation (
2.1.18
) becomes

\Pi\big(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\big)\,=\,\varepsilon^{m}\,G,\qquad\text{which implies}\qquad\left|\Pi\big(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\big)\right|\,\leq\,C\quad\mbox{ in }\;\Omega\,.

At this point, as a consequence of the structural assumptions (
2.1.10
), (
2.1.13
) and (
2.1.14
), we observe that

\Pi(z)\longrightarrow+\infty
for
z\rightarrow+\infty
. Then,
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}
must be uniformly bounded in
\Omega
.

This completes the proof of the lemma.

We conclude this paragraph by showing some additional bounds, which will be relevant in the sequel.

Proposition 2.1.7

Let
F\neq 0
. For any
l>0
, on the cylinder
\overline{\mathbb{B}}_{l}
one has, for any
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
:

(1)

\left|\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x)\,-\,1\right|\,\leq\,C(l)\,\varepsilon^{m}\,\text{ if }m\geq 2
;

(2)

\left|\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x)\,-\,1\right|\,\leq\,C(l)\,\varepsilon^{2(m-1)}\,\text{ if }1<m<2
;

(3)

\left|\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x)\,-\,\widetilde{\varrho}(x)\right|\,\leq\,C(l)\,\varepsilon\,\text{ if }m=1
.

When
F=0
and
m\geq 1
, instead, one has
\left|\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x)\,-\,1\right|\,\leq\,C\,\varepsilon^{m}
, for a constant
C>0
which is uniform in
x\in\Omega
and in
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
.

Proof:

Assume
F\neq 0
for a while. Let
m\geq 2
. Thanks to the Lemma
2.1.6
, the estimate on
\left|\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x)\,-\,1\right|
easily
follows applying the mean value theorem (see again e.g. Chapter 5 of
[
64
]
) to equation (
2.1.18
), and noticing that

\sup_{z\in[\rho_{*},C(l)]}\left|\frac{z}{\partial_{\varrho}p(z,\overline{\vartheta})}\right|\,<\,+\infty\,,

on
\overline{\mathbb{B}}_{l}
for any fixed
l>0
.
According to the hypothesis
m\geq 2
, we have
2(m-1)\geq m
. The claimed bound then follows.
The proof of the inequality for
1<m<2
is analogous, using this time that
2(m-1)\leq m
.

In order to prove the inequality for
m=1
, we consider the equations satisfied by
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}
and
\widetilde{\varrho}
: we have

\Pi\bigl(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x)\bigr)\,=\,|\,x^{h}\,|^{2}\,-\,\varepsilon\,x^{3}\qquad\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\qquad\Pi\bigl(\widetilde{\varrho}(x)\bigr)\,=\,|\,x^{h}\,|^{2}\,.

Now, we take the difference and we apply again the mean value theorem, finding

\Pi^{\prime}\big(z_{\varepsilon}(x)\big)\,\big(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x)\,-\,\widetilde{\varrho}(x)\big)\,=\,-\varepsilon\,x^{3}\,,

for some
z_{\varepsilon}(x)\in\,]\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x),\widetilde{\varrho}(x)[\,
(or with exchanged extreme points, depending on
x
). By Lemma
2.1.6
,
we have uniform (in
\varepsilon
) bounds on the set
\overline{\mathbb{B}}_{l}
, depending on
l
, for
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x)
and

\widetilde{\varrho}(x)
: then, from the previous identity, on this cylinder we find

\left|\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x)\,-\,\widetilde{\varrho}(x)\right|\,\leq\,C(l)\,\varepsilon\,.

The bounds in the case
F=0
can be shown in an analogous way. The proposition is now completely proved.

From now on, we will focus on the following cases:

\mbox{ either }\quad m\geq 2\,,\qquad\quad\mbox{ or }\quad\qquad m\geq 1\quad\mbox{ and }\quad F=0\,.

(2.1.21)

Notice that in all those cases, the target density profile
\widetilde{\varrho}
is constant, namely
\widetilde{\varrho}\equiv 1
.

2.1.1.4
Initial data and finite energy weak solutions

We address the singular perturbation problem described in Paragraph
2.1.1.1
for general
ill prepared initial data
, in the framework of
finite energy weak solutions
,
whose theory was developed in
[
39
]
.
Since we work with weak solutions based on dissipation estimates and control of entropy production rate, we need to assume that the initial data are close to the equilibrium states
(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})
that we have just identified.
Namely, we consider initial densities and temperatures of the following form:

\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}=\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{m}\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}^{(1)}\qquad\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\qquad\vartheta_{0,\varepsilon}=\overline{\vartheta}+\varepsilon^{m}\Theta_{0,\varepsilon}\,.

(2.1.22)

For later use, let us introduce also the following decomposition of the initial densities:

\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}\,=\,1\,+\,\varepsilon^{m}\,R_{0,\varepsilon}\qquad\qquad\mbox{ with }\qquad R_{0,\varepsilon}\,=\,\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}^{(1)}\,+\,\widetilde{r}_{\varepsilon}\,,\qquad\widetilde{r}_{\varepsilon}\,:=\,\frac{\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}-1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,.

(2.1.23)

Notice that
\widetilde{r}_{\varepsilon}
is in fact a datum of the system, since it only depends on
p
,
F
and
G
.

We suppose
\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}^{(1)}
and
\Theta_{0,\varepsilon}
to be bounded measurable functions satisfying the controls

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]}\left\|\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}^{(1)}\right\|_{(L^{2}\cap L^{\infty})(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\,\leq\,c\,,\qquad\sup_{\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]}\left(\left\|\Theta_{0,\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\,+\,\left\|\sqrt{\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}}\,\Theta_{0,\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\right)\,\leq\,c\,,

(2.1.24)

together with the mean-free conditions

\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}^{(1)}\,{\rm d}x=0\qquad\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\qquad\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\Theta_{0,\varepsilon}\,{\rm d}x=0\,.

As for the initial velocity fields, we will assume instead the following uniform bounds:

\sup_{\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]}\left(\left\|\sqrt{\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\,+\,\left\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\right)\,\leq\,c\,.

(2.1.25)

Remark 2.1.8

In view of Lemma
2.1.5
, the conditions in (
2.1.24
) and (
2.1.25
) imply in particular that

\sup_{\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]}\left(\left\|\Theta_{0,\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\,+\,\left\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\right)\,\leq\,c\,.

Thanks to the previous uniform estimates, up to extraction, we can assume that

\varrho^{(1)}_{0}\,:=\,\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\varrho^{(1)}_{0,\varepsilon}\;,\qquad R_{0}\,:=\,\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}R_{0,\varepsilon}\;,\qquad\Theta_{0}\,:=\,\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\Theta_{0,\varepsilon}\;,\qquad\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\,:=\,\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\,,

(2.1.26)

where we agree that the previous limits are taken in the weak-
*
topology of
L_{\rm loc}^{\infty}(\Omega)\,\cap\,L_{\rm loc}^{2}(\Omega)
.

Let us specify better what we mean for
finite energy weak solution
(see
[
39
]
for details). First of all, the equations have to be satisfied in a distributional sense:

-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\partial_{t}\varphi+\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi\right)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t=\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}\varphi(0,\cdot)\,{\rm d}x,

(2.1.27)

for any
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}([0,T[\,\times\overline{\Omega}_{\varepsilon})
;

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\left(-\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\psi}-\varrho_{\varepsilon}[\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}]:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\,\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\times(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2m}}p(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon}){\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}\right)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t

(2.1.28)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\left(-\mathbb{S}(\vartheta_{\varepsilon},\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}):\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}+\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\varrho_{\varepsilon}\nabla_{x}F+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\varrho_{\varepsilon}\nabla_{x}G\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\right)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t+\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}(0,\cdot)\,{\rm d}x,

for any test function
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in C^{\infty}_{c}([0,T[\,\times\overline{\Omega}_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{3})
such that
\big(\boldsymbol{\psi}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{\varepsilon}\big)_{|\partial{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}}=0
;

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\Bigl(-\varrho_{\varepsilon}s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})\partial_{t}\varphi-\varrho_{\varepsilon}s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi\Bigr)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t

(2.1.29)

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\frac{\boldsymbol{q}(\vartheta_{\varepsilon},\nabla_{x}\vartheta_{\varepsilon})}{\vartheta_{\varepsilon}}\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t-\langle\sigma_{\varepsilon};\varphi\rangle_{[{\cal{M}};C^{0}]([0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}_{\varepsilon})}=\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}s(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon},\vartheta_{0,\varepsilon})\varphi(0,\cdot)\,{\rm d}x,

for any
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}([0,T[\,\times\overline{\Omega}_{\varepsilon})
, with
\sigma_{\varepsilon}\in{\cal{M}}^{+}([0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}_{\varepsilon})
.
In addition, we require that the energy identity

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{2}\varrho_{\varepsilon}|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2m}}\varrho_{\varepsilon}e(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\varrho_{\varepsilon}F-\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\varrho_{\varepsilon}G\right)(t)\,{\rm d}x

(2.1.30)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}|\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2m}}\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}e(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon},\vartheta_{0,\varepsilon})-\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}F-\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}G\right)\,{\rm d}x

holds true for almost every
t\in\,]0,T[\,
. Notice that this is the integrated version of (
NSF

ε

4

).

Under the previous assumptions (collected in Paragraphs
2.1.1.1
and
2.1.1.2
and here above), at any
fixed
value of the parameter
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
,
the existence of a global in time finite energy weak solution
(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})
to system (
NSF

ε

1

) to (
NSF

ε

4

), related to the initial datum

(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon},\vartheta_{0,\varepsilon})
, has been proved in e.g.
[
39
]
(see Theorems 3.1 and 3.2 therein).
Moreover,
the following regularity of solutions
(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})
can be obtained, which justifies all the integrals appearing in (
2.1.27
) to (
2.1.30
): for any
T>0
fixed, one has

\varrho_{\varepsilon}\in C^{0}_{w}\big([0,T];L^{5/3}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\big),\quad\varrho_{\varepsilon}\in L^{q}\big((0,T)\times\Omega_{\varepsilon}\big)\ \mbox{ for some }q>\frac{5}{3}\,,\qquad\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}\big([0,T];W^{1,2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{3})\big)\,.

In addition, the mapping
t\mapsto(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})(t,\cdot)
is weakly continuous, and one has
(\varrho_{\varepsilon})_{|t=0}=\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}
together with
(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})_{|t=0}=\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}
. Finally,
the absolute temperature
\vartheta_{\varepsilon}
is a measurable function,
\vartheta_{\varepsilon}>0
a.e. in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega_{\varepsilon}
, and given any
T>0
, one has

\vartheta_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}\big([0,T];W^{1,2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\big)\cap L^{\infty}\big([0,T];L^{4}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\big),\quad\log\vartheta_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}\big([0,T];W^{1,2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\big)\,.

Notice that, in view of (
NSF

ε

1

), the total mass is conserved in time, in the following sense: for almost every
t\in[0,+\infty[\,
,
one has

\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\bigl(\varrho_{\varepsilon}(t)\,-\,\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\bigr)\,\,{\rm d}x\,=\,0\,.

(2.1.31)

Let us now remark that, since the entropy production rate is a non-negative measure, and in particular it may possess jumps, the total entropy

\varrho_{\varepsilon}s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})
may not be weakly continuous in time. To avoid
this problem, we introduce a time lifting
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
of the measure
\sigma_{\varepsilon}
(see Paragraph 5.4.7 in
[
39
]
for details) by the following formula:

\langle\Sigma_{\varepsilon},\varphi\rangle=\langle\sigma_{\varepsilon},I[\varphi]\rangle,\quad\mbox{ where }\quad I[\varphi](t,x)=\int_{0}^{t}\varphi(\tau,x){\rm\,d}\tau\quad\mbox{for any }\varphi\in L^{1}(0,T;C^{0}(\overline{\Omega}_{\varepsilon})).

(2.1.32)

The time lifting
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
can be identified with an abstract function

\Sigma_{\varepsilon}\in L^{\infty}_{w}(0,T;{\cal{M}}^{+}(\overline{\Omega}))
, where
L_{w}^{\infty}
stands for “weakly measurable”,
and
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
is defined by the relation

\langle\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(\tau),\varphi\rangle=\lim\limits_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\langle\sigma_{\varepsilon},\psi_{\delta}\varphi\rangle,\quad\mbox{ with }\quad\psi_{\delta}(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}0&{\mbox{for }}t\in[0,\tau),\\
\frac{1}{\delta}(t-\tau)&{\mbox{for }}t\in(\tau,\tau+\delta),\\
1&{\mbox{for }}t\geq\tau+\delta.\end{array}\right.

In particular, the measure
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
is well-defined for any
\tau\in[0,T]
, and the mapping

\tau\to\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(\tau)
is non-increasing in the sense of measures.

Then, the weak formulation of the entropy balance can be equivalently rewritten as

\begin{split}&\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\left[\varrho_{\varepsilon}s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})(\tau)\varphi(\tau)-\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}s(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon},\vartheta_{0,\varepsilon})\varphi(0)\right]\,{\rm d}x+\langle\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(\tau),\varphi(\tau)\rangle-\langle\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(0),\varphi(0)\rangle\\
&=\int_{0}^{\tau}\langle\Sigma_{\varepsilon},\partial_{t}\varphi\rangle\,{\rm d}t+\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon}s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})\partial_{t}\varphi+\varrho_{\varepsilon}s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi+\frac{\boldsymbol{q}(\vartheta_{\varepsilon},\nabla_{x}\vartheta_{\varepsilon})}{\vartheta_{\varepsilon}}\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi\right)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t,\end{split}

for any
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}([0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}_{\varepsilon})
, and the mapping

t\to\varrho_{\varepsilon}s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})(t,\cdot)+\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(t)\

is continuous with values in
{\cal{M}}^{+}(\overline{\Omega}_{\varepsilon})
, provided that

{\cal{M}}^{+}
is endowed with the weak-
*
topology.

Remark 2.1.9

We explicitly point out that the previous properties are
not
uniform in the small parameter
\varepsilon
. In order to deduce uniform properties on our family of weak solutions

\bigl(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon}\bigr)_{\varepsilon}
,
we “measure” the energy of the solutions with respect to the energy
at the equilibrium states
\left(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},0,\overline{\vartheta}\right)
.

To conclude this part, we introduce the ballistic free energy function

H_{\overline{\vartheta}}(\varrho,\vartheta)\,:=\,\varrho\bigl(e(\varrho,\vartheta)-\overline{\vartheta}s(\varrho,\vartheta)\bigr)\,,

and we define the
relative entropy functional
(for details, see in particular Chapters 1, 2 and 4 of
[
39
]
)

\mathcal{E}\left(\rho,\theta\;|\;\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta}\right)\,:=\,H_{\overline{\vartheta}}(\rho,\theta)-(\rho-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})\,\partial_{\varrho}H_{\overline{\vartheta}}(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})-H_{\overline{\vartheta}}(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})\,.

First of all, we notice that, by (
2.1.18
) and Gibbs’ relation (
2.1.3
), equation (
2.1.17
) can be rewritten as

\partial_{\varrho}H_{\overline{\vartheta}}(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})\,=\,\varepsilon^{2(m-1)}F+\varepsilon^{m}G

in
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
(up to some constant, that we have normalized to
0
).

Then, combining the total energy balance (
2.1.30
), the entropy equation (
2.1.29
) and the mass conservation (
2.1.31
), we obtain
the following total dissipation balance, for any
\varepsilon>0
fixed:

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91684pt\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\frac{1}{2}\varrho_{\varepsilon}|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}|^{2}(t)\,{\rm d}x\,+\,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2m}}\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{E}\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon}\;|\;\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta}\right)\,{\rm d}x+\frac{\overline{\vartheta}}{\varepsilon^{2m}}\sigma_{\varepsilon}\left[[0,t]\times\overline{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}\right]

(2.1.33)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\,\leq\,\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\frac{1}{2}\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}|\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}|^{2}\,{\rm d}x\,+\,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2m}}\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{E}\left(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon},\vartheta_{0,\varepsilon}\;|\;\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta}\right)\,{\rm d}x\,.

Inequality (
2.1.33
) will be the only tool to derive uniform estimates for the family of weak solutions we consider. As a matter of fact,
we will establish in Lemma
2.2.2
below that, under the previous assumptions on the initial data, the quantity on the right-hand side of (
2.1.33
)
is uniformly bounded for any
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
.

2.1.2
Main results

We can now state our main results. The first statement concerns the case when low Mach number effects are predominant with respect to the fast rotation, i.e.
m>1
.
For technical reasons which will appear clear in the course of the proof, when
F\neq 0
we need to take
m\geq 2
.

We also underline that the limit dynamics of
\boldsymbol{U}
is purely horizontal (see (
2.1.34
) below) on the plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\{0\}
accordingly to the celebrated Taylor-Proudman theorem.
Nonetheless the equations that involve
R
and
\Theta
(see (
2.1.35
) and (
2.1.36
) below) depend also on the vertical variable.

Theorem 2.1.10

For any
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
, let
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
be the domain defined by (
2.1.2
) and
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\,]0,1[\,
.
Let
p
,
e
,
s
satisfy Gibbs’ relation (
2.1.3
) and structural hypotheses from (
2.1.10
) to (
2.1.15
), and suppose that the diffusion coefficients
\mu
,
\eta
,
\kappa

enjoy growth conditions (
2.1.16
). Let
G\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)
be given as in (
2.1.7
).
Take either
m\geq 2
and
F\in W_{loc}^{1,\infty}(\Omega)
as in (
2.1.7
), or
m>1
and
F=0
.

For any fixed value of
\varepsilon\in\;]0,1]
, let initial data
\left(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon},\vartheta_{0,\varepsilon}\right)
verify the hypotheses fixed in Paragraph
2.1.1.4
, and let

\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon}\right)
be a corresponding weak solution to system (
NSF

ε

1

) to (
NSF

ε

4

), supplemented with structural hypotheses from (
2.1.4
) to (
2.1.6
) and with boundary conditions (
2.1.8
) and (
2.1.9
).
Assume that the total dissipation balance (
2.1.33
) is satisfied.
Let
\left(R_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\Theta_{0}\right)
be defined as in (
2.1.26
).

Then, for any
T>0
, one has the following convergence properties:

\displaystyle\varrho_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow 1\qquad\qquad\mbox{ in }\qquad

\displaystyle L^{\infty}\big([0,T];L_{\rm loc}^{5/3}(\Omega)\big)

\displaystyle R_{\varepsilon}:=\frac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\rightharpoonup}}R\qquad\qquad\mbox{ weakly-$*$ in }\qquad

\displaystyle L^{\infty}\bigl([0,T];L^{5/3}_{\rm loc}(\Omega)\bigr)

\displaystyle\Theta_{\varepsilon}:=\frac{\vartheta_{\varepsilon}-\bar{\vartheta}}{\varepsilon^{m}}\rightharpoonup\Theta\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{U}\qquad\qquad\mbox{ weakly in }\qquad

\displaystyle L^{2}\big([0,T];W_{\rm loc}^{1,2}(\Omega)\big)\,,

where
\boldsymbol{U}=(\boldsymbol{U}^{h},0)
, with
\boldsymbol{U}^{h}=\boldsymbol{U}^{h}(t,x^{h})
such that
{\rm div}_{h}\boldsymbol{U}^{h}=0
. In addition, the triplet
\Big(\boldsymbol{U}^{h},\,\,R,\,\,\Theta\Big)
is a weak solution
to the incompressible Oberbeck-Boussinesq system in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega
:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{U}^{h}+{\rm div}_{h}\left(\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\otimes\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\right)+\nabla_{h}\Gamma-\mu(\overline{\vartheta})\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{U}^{h}=\delta_{2}(m)\langle R\rangle\nabla_{h}F

(2.1.34)

\displaystyle c_{p}(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\Bigl(\partial_{t}\Theta\,+\,{\rm div}_{h}(\Theta\,\boldsymbol{U}^{h})\Bigr)\,-\,\kappa(\overline{\vartheta})\,\Delta\Theta\,=\,\overline{\vartheta}\,\alpha(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\cdot\nabla_{h}\big({\,G\,+\,\delta_{2}(m)F}\big)

(2.1.35)

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\Big(\partial_{\varrho}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,R\,+\,\partial_{\vartheta}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\Theta\Big)\,=\,\nabla_{x}G\,+\,\delta_{2}(m)\,\nabla_{x}F\,,

(2.1.36)

supplemented with the initial conditions

\boldsymbol{U}_{|t=0}=\mathbb{H}_{h}\left(\langle\boldsymbol{u}^{h}_{0}\rangle\right)\quad\text{ and }\quad\Theta_{|t=0}\,=\,\frac{\overline{\vartheta}}{c_{p}(1,\overline{\vartheta})}\,\Big(\partial_{\varrho}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,R_{0}\,+\,\partial_{\vartheta}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\Theta_{0}\,+\,\alpha(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,{\big(\,G\,+\,\delta_{2}(m)F\big)}\Big)

and the boundary condition
\nabla_{x}\Theta\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{|\partial\Omega}\,=\,0
,
where
\boldsymbol{n}
is the outer normal to
\partial\Omega\,=\,\{x_{3}=0\}\cup\{x_{3}=1\}
.

In (
2.1.34
),
\Gamma
is a distribution in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
and we have set
\delta_{2}(m)=1
if
m=2
,
\delta_{2}(m)=0
otherwise.
In (
2.1.35
), we have defined

c_{p}(\varrho,\vartheta)\,:=\,\partial_{\vartheta}e(\varrho,\vartheta)\,+\,\alpha(\varrho,\vartheta)\,\frac{\vartheta}{\varrho}\,\partial_{\vartheta}p(\varrho,\vartheta)\,,\qquad\alpha(\varrho,\vartheta)\,:=\,\frac{1}{\varrho}\,\frac{\partial_{\vartheta}p(\varrho,\vartheta)}{\partial_{\varrho}p(\varrho,\vartheta)}\,.

Remark 2.1.11

We notice that, after defining

\Upsilon:=\partial_{\varrho}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})R+\partial_{\vartheta}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\Theta\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\Upsilon_{0}\,:=\,\partial_{\varrho}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,R_{0}\,+\,\partial_{\vartheta}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\Theta_{0}\,,

from equation (
NSF

ε

3

) one would get, in the limit
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
, the equation

\partial_{t}\Upsilon+{\rm div}_{h}\left(\Upsilon\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\right)-\frac{\kappa(\overline{\vartheta})}{\overline{\vartheta}}\Delta\Theta=0\,,\qquad\qquad\Upsilon_{|t=0}\,=\,\Upsilon_{0}\,,

(2.1.37)

which is closer to the formulation of the target system given in
[
52
]
and
[
53
]
.
From (
2.1.37
) one easily recovers (
2.1.35
) by using (
2.1.36
). Formulation (
2.1.35
) is in the spirit of Chapters 4 and 5 of
[
39
]
.

The case
m=1
realizes the
quasi-geostrophic balance
in the limit. Namely the Mach and Rossby numbers have the same order of magnitude, and they keep in balance in the whole asymptotic process.
The next statement is devoted to this case. Due to technical reasons, in this instance we have to assume
F=0
.
Indeed, when
F\neq 0
, the coexistence of the centrifugal effects and the heat transfer deeply complicates the wave system and new technical troubles arise.

Theorem 2.1.12

For any
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
, let
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
be the domain defined by (
2.1.2
) and
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\,]0,1[\,
.
Let
p
,
e
,
s
satisfy (
2.1.3
) and the structural hypotheses from (
2.1.10
) to (
2.1.15
), and suppose that the diffusion coefficients
\mu
,
\eta
,
\kappa

enjoy (
2.1.16
). Let
F=0
and
G\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)
be as in (
2.1.7
). Take
m=1
.

For any fixed value of
\varepsilon
, let initial data
\left(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon},\vartheta_{0,\varepsilon}\right)
verify the hypotheses fixed in Paragraph
2.1.1.4
, and let

\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon}\right)
be a corresponding weak solution to system (
NSF

ε

1

) to (
NSF

ε

4

), supplemented with structural hypotheses from (
2.1.4
) to (
2.1.6
) and with boundary conditions (
2.1.8
) and (
2.1.9
).
Assume that the total dissipation balance (
2.1.33
) is satisfied.
Let
\left(R_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\Theta_{0}\right)
be defined as in (
2.1.26
).

Then, for any
T>0
, the convergence properties stated in the previous theorem still hold true: namely, one has

\displaystyle\varrho_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow 1\qquad\qquad\mbox{ in }\qquad

\displaystyle L^{\infty}\big([0,T];L_{\rm loc}^{5/3}(\Omega)\big)

\displaystyle R_{\varepsilon}:=\frac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1}{\varepsilon}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\rightharpoonup}}R\qquad\qquad\mbox{ weakly-$*$ in }\qquad

\displaystyle L^{\infty}\bigl([0,T];L^{5/3}_{\rm loc}(\Omega)\bigr)

\displaystyle\Theta_{\varepsilon}:=\frac{\vartheta_{\varepsilon}-\bar{\vartheta}}{\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup\Theta\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{U}\qquad\qquad\mbox{ weakly in }\qquad

\displaystyle L^{2}\big([0,T];W_{\rm loc}^{1,2}(\Omega)\big)\,,

where
\boldsymbol{U}=(\boldsymbol{U}^{h},0)
, with
\boldsymbol{U}^{h}=\boldsymbol{U}^{h}(t,x^{h})
such that
{\rm div}_{h}\boldsymbol{U}^{h}=0
. Moreover, let us introduce the real number
\mathcal{A}>0
by the formula

\mathcal{A}\,=\,\partial_{\varrho}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,+\,\frac{\left|\partial_{\vartheta}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\right|^{2}}{\partial_{\vartheta}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})}\,,

(2.1.38)

and define

\Upsilon\,:=\,\partial_{\varrho}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})R+\partial_{\vartheta}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\Theta\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad q\,:=\,\partial_{\varrho}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})R+\partial_{\vartheta}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\Theta-G-1/2\,.

Then we have

q\,=\,q(t,x^{h})\,=\,\partial_{\varrho}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\langle R\rangle\,+\,\partial_{\vartheta}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\langle\Theta\rangle\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\boldsymbol{U}^{h}=\nabla_{h}^{\perp}q\,.

Moreover, the couple
\Big(q,\Upsilon\Big)
satisfies (in the weak sense) the quasi-geostrophic type system

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\left(\frac{1}{\mathcal{A}}q-\Delta_{h}q\right)-\nabla_{h}^{\perp}q\cdot\nabla_{h}\left(\Delta_{h}q\right)+\mu(\overline{\vartheta})\Delta_{h}^{2}q\,=\,\frac{1}{\mathcal{A}}\,\langle X\rangle

(2.1.39)

\displaystyle c_{p}(1,\overline{\vartheta})\Big(\partial_{t}\Upsilon+\nabla_{h}^{\perp}q\cdot\nabla_{h}\Upsilon\Big)-\kappa(\overline{\vartheta})\Delta\Upsilon\,=\,\kappa(\overline{\vartheta})\,\alpha(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\Delta_{h}q\,,

(2.1.40)

supplemented with the initial conditions

\left(\frac{1}{\mathcal{A}}q-\Delta_{h}q\right)_{|t=0}=\left(\langle R_{0}\rangle+\frac{1}{2\mathcal{A}}\right)-{\rm curl}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{u}^{h}_{0}\rangle\,,\quad\Upsilon_{|t=0}=\partial_{\varrho}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})R_{0}+\partial_{\vartheta}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\Theta_{0}

and the boundary condition

\nabla_{x}\big(\Upsilon\,+\,\alpha(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,G\big)\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{|\partial\Omega}\,=\,0\,,

(2.1.41)

where
\boldsymbol{n}
is the outer normal to the boundary
\partial\Omega\,=\,\{x_{3}=0\}\cup\{x_{3}=1\}
. In (
2.1.39
), we have defined

X:=\mathcal{B}\frac{\kappa(\overline{\vartheta})}{c_{p}(1,\overline{\vartheta})}\left(\Delta\Upsilon\,+\,\alpha(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\Delta_{h}q-\frac{1}{\kappa(\overline{\vartheta})}\nabla^{\perp}_{h}q\cdot\nabla_{h}\Upsilon\right)\quad\quad\text{with}\quad\quad\mathcal{B}:=\frac{\partial_{\vartheta}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})}{\partial_{\vartheta}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})}\,.

(2.1.42)

Remark 2.1.13

Observe that
q
and
\Upsilon
can be equivalently chosen for describing the target problem. Indeed, straightforward computations show that

\displaystyle R\,

\displaystyle=\,-\,\frac{1}{\beta}\,\big(\partial_{\vartheta}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\Upsilon\,-\,\partial_{\vartheta}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,q\,-\,\partial_{\vartheta}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,G\big)

\displaystyle\Theta\,

\displaystyle=\,\frac{1}{\beta}\,\big(\partial_{\varrho}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\Upsilon\,-\,\partial_{\varrho}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,q\,-\,\partial_{\varrho}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,G\big)\,,

where we have set
\beta\,=\,\partial_{\varrho}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\partial_{\vartheta}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,-\,\partial_{\vartheta}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\partial_{\varrho}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})
. In particular, equation (
2.1.40
)
can be deduced from (
2.1.37
), which is valid also when
m=1
, using the expression of
\Theta
and the fact that

\beta\,=\,c_{p}(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\frac{\partial_{\varrho}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})}{\overline{\vartheta}}\,.

Here we have chosen to formulate the target entropy balance equation in terms of
\Upsilon
(as in
[
53
]
) rather than
\Theta
(as in Theorem
2.1.10
above),
because the equation for
\Upsilon
looks simpler (indeed, the equation for
\Theta
would make a term in
\partial_{t}q
appear). The price to pay is the non-homogeneous boundary
condition (
2.1.41
), which may look a bit unpleasant.

As pointed out for Theorem
2.1.10
, we notice that, despite the function
q
is defined in terms of
G
, the dynamics described by (
2.1.39
) is purely horizontal.
On the contrary, dependence on
x^{3}
and vertical derivatives appear in (
2.1.40
).

Remark 2.1.14

We have not investigated here the well-posedness of the target problems, formulated in Theorems
2.1.10
and
2.1.12
. Very likely, when
F=0
, by standard energy methods
(see e.g.
[
15
]
,
[
35
]
,
[
23
]
) it is possible to prove that those systems are well-posed in the energy space, globally in time.

Yet, it is not clear for us that the solutions identified in the previous theorems are (the unique) finite energy weak solutions to the target problems.

2.2
Analysis of the singular perturbation

The purpose of this section is twofold. First of all, in Subsection
2.2.1
we establish uniform bounds and further properties for our family
of weak solutions. Then, we study the singular operator underlying to the primitive equations (
NSF

ε

1

) to (
NSF

ε

4

), and determine constraints that the limit points of our family of weak solutions
have to satisfy (see Subsection
2.2.2
).

2.2.1
Uniform bounds

This section is devoted to establish uniform bounds on the sequence
\bigl(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon}\bigr)_{\varepsilon}
.
Since the Coriolis term does not contribute to the total energy balance of the system, most of the bounds can be proven as in the case without rotation; we refer to
[
39
]
for details.
First of all, let us introduce some preliminary material.

2.2.1.1
Preliminaries

Let us recall here some basic notations and results, which we need in proving our convergence results. We refer to Sections 4, 5 and 6 of
[
39
]
for more details.

Let us introduce the so-called “essential” and “residual” sets. Recall that the positive constant
\rho_{*}
has been defined in Lemma
2.1.5
.
Following the approach of
[
39
]
, we define

{\cal{O}}_{{\rm{ess}}}\,:=\,\left[2\rho_{*}/3\,,\,2\right]\,\times\,\left[\overline{\vartheta}/2\,,\,2\overline{\vartheta}\right]\,,\qquad{\cal{O}}_{{\rm{res}}}\,:=\,\,]0,+\infty[\,^{2}\setminus{\cal{O}}_{{\rm{ess}}}\,.

Then, we fix a smooth function

\mathfrak{b}\in C^{\infty}_{c}\bigl(\,]0,+\infty[\,\times\,]0,+\infty[\,\bigr)
such that
0\leq\mathfrak{b}\leq 1,\ \mathfrak{b}\equiv 1
on the set
{\cal{O}}_{{\rm{ess}}}
, and
we introduce the decomposition on essential and residual part of a measurable function
h
as follows:

h=[h]_{{\rm{ess}}}+[h]_{{\rm{res}}},\qquad\mbox{ with }\quad[h]_{{\rm{ess}}}:=\mathfrak{b}(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})h\,,\quad\ [h]_{{\rm{res}}}=\bigl(1-\mathfrak{b}(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})\bigr)h\,.

We also introduce the sets
\mathcal{M}^{\varepsilon}_{{\rm{ess}}}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\varepsilon}_{{\rm{res}}}
, defined as

\mathcal{M}^{\varepsilon}_{{\rm{ess}}}:=\left\{(t,x)\in\,]0,T[\,\times\,\Omega_{\varepsilon}\ :\ \bigl(\varrho_{\varepsilon}(t,x),\vartheta_{\varepsilon}(t,x)\bigr)\in{\cal{O}}_{{\rm{ess}}}\right\}\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\mathcal{M}^{\varepsilon}_{{\rm{res}}}:=\big(\,]0,T[\,\times\,\Omega_{\varepsilon}\,\big)\setminus\mathcal{M}^{\varepsilon}_{{\rm{ess}}}\,,

and their version at fixed time
t\geq 0
, i.e.

\mathcal{M}^{\varepsilon}_{{\rm{ess}}}[t]:=\{x\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}\ :(t,x)\in\mathcal{M}^{\varepsilon}_{{\rm{ess}}}\}\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\mathcal{M}^{\varepsilon}_{{\rm{res}}}[t]:=\Omega_{\varepsilon}\setminus\mathcal{M}^{\varepsilon}_{{\rm{ess}}}[t]\,.

The next result, which will be useful in the next subsection, is the analogous of Lemma 5.1 in
[
39
]
in our context. Here we need to pay attention to the fact that,
when
F\neq 0
, the estimates for the equilibrium states (recall Proposition
2.1.7
) are not uniform on the whole
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
.

Lemma 2.2.1

Fix
m\geq 1
and let
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}
and
\overline{\vartheta}
be the static states identified in Paragraph
2.1.1.3
. Under the previous assumptions, and with the notations introduced above,
we have the following properties.

Let
F\neq 0
. For all
l>0
, there exist
\varepsilon(l)
and positive constants
c_{j}\,=\,c_{j}(\rho_{*},\overline{\vartheta},l)
, with
j=1,2,3
, such that, for all
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon(l)
,
the next properties hold true, for all
x\in\overline{\mathbb{B}}_{l}
:

(a)

for all
(\rho,\theta)\,\in\,\mathcal{O}_{{\rm{ess}}}
, one has

c_{1}\,\left(\left|\rho-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x)\right|^{2}\,+\,\left|\theta-\overline{\vartheta}\right|^{2}\right)\,\leq\,\mathcal{E}\left(\rho,\theta\;|\;\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x),\overline{\vartheta}\right)\,\leq\,c_{2}\,\left(\left|\rho-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x)\right|^{2}\,+\,\left|\theta-\overline{\vartheta}\right|^{2}\right)\,;

(b)

for all
(\rho,\theta)\,\in\,\mathcal{O}_{{\rm{res}}}
, one has

\mathcal{E}\left(\rho,\theta\;|\;\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x),\overline{\vartheta}\right)\,\geq\,c_{3}\,.

When
F=0
, the previous constants
\big(c_{j}\big)_{j=1,2,3}
can be chosen to be independent of
l>0
.

Proof:

Let us start by considering the case
F\neq 0
. Fix
m\geq 1
. In view of Lemma
2.1.5
and Proposition
2.1.7
, for all
l>0
fixed, there exists
\varepsilon(l)
such that,
for all
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon(l)
, we have
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x)\,\in\,[\rho_{*},3/2]\,\subset\,\mathcal{O}_{{\rm{ess}}}
for all
x\in\overline{\mathbb{B}}_{l}
.
With this inclusion at hand, the first inequality is an immediate consequence of the decomposition

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\left(\rho,\theta\;|\;\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta}\right)\,

\displaystyle=\,\Bigl(H_{\overline{\vartheta}}(\rho,\theta)-H_{\overline{\vartheta}}(\rho,\overline{\vartheta})\Bigr)\,+\,\Bigl(H_{\overline{\vartheta}}(\rho,\overline{\vartheta})-H_{\overline{\vartheta}}(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})-(\rho-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})\,\partial_{\varrho}H_{\overline{\vartheta}}(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\,\partial_{\vartheta}H_{\overline{\vartheta}}(\rho,\eta)\,\bigl(\vartheta-\overline{\vartheta}\bigr)\,+\,\frac{1}{2}\partial^{2}_{\varrho\varrho}H_{\overline{\vartheta}}(z_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})\,\bigl(\rho-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\bigr)^{2}\,,

for some suitable
\eta
belonging to the interval connecting
\theta
and
\overline{\vartheta}
, and
z_{\varepsilon}
belonging to the interval connecting
\rho
and
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}
. Indeed,
it is enough to use formulas (2.49) and (2.50) of
[
39
]
, together with the fact that we are in the essential set.

Next, thanks again to the property
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x)\,\in\,[\rho_{*},3/2]\,\subset\,\mathcal{O}_{{\rm{ess}}}
, we can conclude, exactly as in relation (6.69) of
[
39
]
, that

\inf_{(\rho,\theta)\in\mathcal{O}_{\rm{res}}}\mathcal{E}\left(\rho,\theta\;|\;\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta}\right)\,\geq\,\inf_{(\rho,\theta)\in\partial\mathcal{O}_{\rm{ess}}}\mathcal{E}\left(\rho,\theta\;|\;\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta}\right)\,\geq\,c\,>\,0\,.

The case
F=0
follows by similar arguments, using that the various constants in Lemma
2.1.6
and Proposition
2.1.7
are uniform in
\Omega
.
This completes the proof of the lemma.

2.2.1.2
Uniform estimates for the family of weak solutions

With the total dissipation balance (
2.1.33
) and Lemma
2.2.1
at hand, we can derive uniform bounds for our family of weak solutions.
Since this derivation is somehow classical, we limit ourselves to recall the main inequalities and sketch the proofs; we refer the reader to Chapters 5, 6 and 8 of
[
39
]
for details.

To begin with, we remark that, owing to the assumptions fixed in Paragraph
2.1.1.4
on the initial data and to the structural hypotheses of Paragraphs
2.1.1.1
and
2.1.1.2
,
the right-hand side of (
2.1.33
) is
uniformly bounded
for all
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
.

Lemma 2.2.2

Under the assumptions fixed in Paragraphs
2.1.1.1
,
2.1.1.2
and
2.1.1.4
, there exists an absolute constant
C>0
such that, for all
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
,
one has

\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\frac{1}{2}\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}|\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}|^{2}\,\,{\rm d}x+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2m}}\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{E}\left(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon},\vartheta_{0,\varepsilon}\;|\;\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta}\right)\,\,{\rm d}x\,\leq\,C\,.

Proof:

The boundedness of the first term in the left-hand side is an obvious consequence of (
2.1.25
) and (
2.1.24
) for the density. So, let us show how to control the term containing

\mathcal{E}\left(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon},\vartheta_{0,\varepsilon}\;|\;\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta}\right)
. Owing to Taylor formula, one has

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\left(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon},\vartheta_{0,\varepsilon}\;|\;\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta}\right)\,

\displaystyle=\,\partial_{\vartheta}H_{\overline{\vartheta}}(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon},\eta_{0,\varepsilon})\,\bigl(\vartheta_{0,\varepsilon}-\overline{\vartheta}\bigr)\,+\,\frac{1}{2}\,\partial^{2}_{\varrho\varrho}H_{\overline{\vartheta}}(z_{0,\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})\,\bigl(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\bigr)^{2}\,,

where we can write
\eta_{0,\varepsilon}(x)\,=\,\overline{\vartheta}\,+\,\varepsilon^{m}\,\lambda_{\varepsilon}(x)\,\Theta_{0,\varepsilon}
and
z_{0,\varepsilon}\,=\,\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\,+\,\varepsilon^{m}\,\zeta_{\varepsilon}(x)\,\varrho^{(1)}_{0,\varepsilon}
,
with both the families
\bigl(\lambda_{\varepsilon}\bigr)_{\varepsilon}
and
\bigl(\zeta_{\varepsilon}\bigr)_{\varepsilon}
belonging to
L^{\infty}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
, uniformly in
\varepsilon
(in fact,

\lambda_{\varepsilon}(x)
and
\zeta_{\varepsilon}(x)
belong to the interval
\,]0,1[\,
for all
x\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}
).
Notice that
\big(\eta_{0,\varepsilon}\big)_{\varepsilon}\,\subset\,L^{\infty}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
and that
\eta_{0,\varepsilon}\geq c_{1}>0
and
z_{0,\varepsilon}\geq c_{2}>0
(at least for
\varepsilon
small enough).
By the structural hypotheses fixed in Paragraph
2.1.1.2
(and in particular Gibbs’ law), we get (see also formula (2.50) in
[
39
]
)

\displaystyle\partial_{\vartheta}H_{\overline{\vartheta}}(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon},\eta_{0,\varepsilon})\,

\displaystyle=\,4\,a\,\eta_{0,\varepsilon}^{2}\,\bigl(\eta_{0,\varepsilon}-\overline{\vartheta}\bigr)\,+\,\frac{\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}}{\eta_{0,\varepsilon}}\,\bigl(\eta_{0,\varepsilon}-\overline{\vartheta}\bigr)\,\partial_{\vartheta}e_{M}(\rho_{0,\varepsilon},\eta_{0,\varepsilon})\,.

(2.2.1)

In view of condition (
2.1.13
), we gather that
\left|\partial_{\vartheta}e_{M}\right|\,\leq\,c
; therefore, from hypotheses (
2.1.24
) and Remark
2.1.8

it is easy to deduce that

\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2m}}\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\partial_{\vartheta}H_{\overline{\vartheta}}(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon},\eta_{0,\varepsilon})\,\bigl(\vartheta_{0,\varepsilon}-\overline{\vartheta}\bigr)\,{\rm d}x\,\leq\,C\,.

Moreover, by (
2.1.10
) we get (keep in mind formula (2.49) of
[
39
]
)

\partial^{2}_{\varrho\varrho}H_{\overline{\vartheta}}(z_{0,\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})\,=\,\frac{1}{z_{0,\varepsilon}}\,\partial_{\varrho}p_{M}(z_{0,\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})\,=\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{\overline{\vartheta}}}\,\frac{1}{Z_{0,\varepsilon}}\,P^{\prime}(Z_{0,\varepsilon})\,,

where we have set
Z_{0,\varepsilon}\,=\,z_{0,\varepsilon}\,\overline{\vartheta}^{-3/2}
. Now, thanks to (
2.1.13
) again and to the fact that
z_{0,\varepsilon}
is strictly positive, we can estimate,
for some positive constants which depend also on
\overline{\vartheta}
,

\frac{1}{Z_{0,\varepsilon}}\,P^{\prime}(Z_{0,\varepsilon})\,\leq\,C\,\frac{P(Z_{0,\varepsilon})}{Z_{0,\varepsilon}^{2}}\,\leq\,C\left(\frac{P(Z_{0,\varepsilon})}{Z_{0,\varepsilon}^{2}}\,\mathds{1}_{\{0\leq Z_{0,\varepsilon}\leq 1\}}\,+\,\frac{P(Z_{0,\varepsilon})}{Z_{0,\varepsilon}^{5/3}}\,\mathds{1}_{\{Z_{0,\varepsilon}\geq 1\}}\right)\,\leq\,C\,,

where we have used also (
2.1.14
).

Hence, we can check that

\frac{1}{2\varepsilon^{2m}}\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\partial^{2}_{\varrho\varrho}H_{\overline{\vartheta}}(z_{0,\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})\,\bigl(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\bigr)^{2}\,{\rm d}x\,\leq\,C\,.

This inequality completes the proof of the lemma.

Owing to the previous lemma, from (
2.1.33
) we gather, for any
T>0
, the estimates

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\sqrt{\varrho_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{3})}\,

\displaystyle\leq\,c

(2.2.2)

\displaystyle\|\sigma_{\varepsilon}\|_{{\mathcal{M}}^{+}([0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}_{\varepsilon})}\,

\displaystyle\leq\,\varepsilon^{2m}\,c\,.

(2.2.3)

Fix now any
l>0
. Employing Lemma
2.2.1
(and keeping track of the dependence of constants only on
l
), we deduce

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|\left[\dfrac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right]_{\rm{ess}}(t)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{l})}\,+\,\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|\left[\dfrac{\vartheta_{\varepsilon}-\overline{\vartheta}}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right]_{\rm{ess}}(t)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{l})}\,

\displaystyle\leq\,c(l)\,.

(2.2.4)

In addition, we infer also that the measure of the “residual set” is small: more precisely, we have

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{l}}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{M}^{\varepsilon}_{\rm{res}}[t]}\,{\rm d}x\,\leq\,\varepsilon^{2m}\,c(l)\,.

(2.2.5)

Remark 2.2.3

When
F=0
, thanks to Lemma
2.1.6
and Proposition
2.1.7
,
one can see that estimates (
2.2.4
) and (
2.2.5
) hold on the whole
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
, without any need of taking the localisation on the cylinders
\mathbb{B}_{l}
.
From this observation, it is easy to see that, when
F=0
, we can replace
\mathbb{B}_{l}
with the whole
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
in all the following estimates.

Now, we fix
l\geq 0
. We estimate

\int_{\mathbb{B}_{l}}\left|\left[\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\log\varrho_{\varepsilon}\right]_{\rm{res}}\right|\,{\rm d}x\,=\,\int_{\mathbb{B}_{l}}\left|\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\log\varrho_{\varepsilon}\right|\,\mathds{1}_{\{0\leq\varrho_{\varepsilon}\leq 2\rho_{*}/3\}}\,{\rm d}x\,+\,\int_{\mathbb{B}_{l}}\left|\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\log\varrho_{\varepsilon}\right|\,\mathds{1}_{\{\varrho_{\varepsilon}\geq 2\}}\,{\rm d}x\,.

Thanks to (
2.2.5
), the former term in the right-hand side is easily controlled by
\varepsilon^{2m}
, up to a suitable multiplicative constant also depending on
l
.
As for the latter term, we have to argue in a different way. Owing to inequalities (
2.1.11
), (
2.1.12
), (
2.1.13
) and (
2.1.14
), we get that

\partial^{2}_{\varrho}H_{\overline{\vartheta}}(\varrho,\overline{\vartheta})\geq C/\varrho
; therefore, by direct integration we find

\displaystyle C\,\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\log\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,-\,C\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1\right)\,

\displaystyle\leq\,H_{\overline{\vartheta}}(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})\,-\,H_{\overline{\vartheta}}(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,-\,\partial_{\varrho}H_{\overline{\vartheta}}(1,\overline{\vartheta})(\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1)

\displaystyle\leq\,\mathcal{E}\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon}\;|\;\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta}\right)\,+\,\mathcal{E}\left(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta}\;|\;1,\overline{\vartheta}\right)\,+\,\Big(\partial_{\varrho}H(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\theta})-\partial_{\varrho}H(1,\overline{\theta})\Big)\big(\varrho_{\varepsilon}-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\big)\,,

since an expansion analogous to (
2.2.1
) allows to gather that
H_{\overline{\vartheta}}(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})\,-\,H_{\overline{\vartheta}}(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})\,\leq\,0
.
On the one hand, using (
2.1.33
), Proposition
2.1.7
and (
2.2.5
) one deduces

\left|\int_{\mathbb{B}_{l}\cap\mathcal{O}_{\rm{res}}}\left(\mathcal{E}\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon}\;|\;\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta}\right)\,+\,\mathcal{E}\left(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta}\;|\;1,\overline{\vartheta}\right)\,+\,\Big(\partial_{\varrho}H(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\theta})-\partial_{\varrho}H(1,\overline{\theta})\Big)\big(\varrho_{\varepsilon}-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\big)\,\right)\,{\rm d}x\right|\,\leq\,C\,\varepsilon^{2m}\,.

On the other hand,
\varrho_{\varepsilon}\log\varrho_{\varepsilon}-\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1\right)\,\geq\,\varrho_{\varepsilon}\left(\log\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1\right)\,\geq\,(1/2)\,\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\log\varrho_{\varepsilon}
whenever

\varrho_{\varepsilon}\geq e^{2}
. Hence, since we have

\int_{\mathbb{B}_{l}}\left|\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\log\varrho_{\varepsilon}\right|\,\mathds{1}_{\{2\leq\varrho_{\varepsilon}\leq e^{2}\}}\,{\rm d}x\,\leq\,C\,\varepsilon^{2m}

owing to (
2.2.5
) again,
we finally infer that, for any fix
l>0
,

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{l}}\left|\left[\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\log\varrho_{\varepsilon}\right]_{\rm{res}}(t)\right|\,{\rm d}x\,\leq\,c(l)\,\varepsilon^{2m}\,.

(2.2.6)

Owing to inequality (
2.2.6
), we deduce (exactly as in
[
39
]
, see estimates (6.72) and (6.73) therein) that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{l}}\bigl(\left|[\varrho_{\varepsilon}e(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})]_{\rm{res}}\right|+\left|[\varrho_{\varepsilon}s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})]_{\rm{res}}\right|\bigr)\,\,{\rm d}x\,

\displaystyle\leq\,\varepsilon^{2m}\,c(l)\,,

(2.2.7)

which in particular implies (again, we refer to Section 6.4.1 of
[
39
]
for details) the following bounds:

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{l}}[\varrho_{\varepsilon}]^{5/3}_{\rm{res}}(t)\,\,{\rm d}x\,+\,\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{l}}[\vartheta_{\varepsilon}]^{4}_{\rm{res}}(t)\,\,{\rm d}x\,

\displaystyle\leq\,\varepsilon^{2m}\,c(l)\,.

(2.2.8)

Let us move further. In view of (
2.1.4
), (
2.1.5
), (
2.1.6
) and (
2.1.16
), relation (
2.2.3
) implies

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}+\,^{t}\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}-\frac{2}{3}{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\,{\rm Id}\,\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}\,\,{\rm d}t\,

\displaystyle\leq\,c

(2.2.9)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\nabla_{x}\left(\frac{\vartheta_{\varepsilon}-\overline{\vartheta}}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right)\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{3})}\,\,{\rm d}t\,+\,\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\nabla_{x}\left(\frac{\log(\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-\log(\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right)\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{3})}\,\,{\rm d}t\,

\displaystyle\leq\,c\,.

(2.2.10)

Thanks to the previous inequalities and (
2.2.5
), we can argue as in Subsection 8.2 of
[
39
]
:
by generalizations of respectively Poincaré and Korn-Poincaré inequalities (see Propositions
B.7
and
B.8
), for all
l>0
we gather also

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\frac{\vartheta_{\varepsilon}-\overline{\vartheta}}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right\|^{2}_{W^{1,2}({\mathbb{B}_{l}};\mathbb{R}^{3})}\,\,{\rm d}t\,+\,\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\frac{\log(\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-\log(\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right\|^{2}_{W^{1,2}({\mathbb{B}_{l}};\mathbb{R}^{3})}\,\,{\rm d}t\,

\displaystyle\leq\,c(l)

(2.2.11)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\right\|^{2}_{W^{1,2}(\mathbb{B}_{l};\mathbb{R}^{3})}\,{\rm d}t\,

\displaystyle\leq\,c(l)\,.

(2.2.12)

Finally, we discover that

\displaystyle\int^{T}_{0}\left\|\left[\frac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right]_{{\rm{res}}}\right\|^{2}_{L^{30/23}(\mathbb{B}_{l})}\,{\rm d}t\,+\,\int^{T}_{0}\left\|\left[\frac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right]_{{\rm{res}}}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\right\|^{2}_{L^{30/29}(\mathbb{B}_{l})}\,{\rm d}t\,

\displaystyle\leq\,c(l)

(2.2.13)

\displaystyle\int^{T}_{0}\left\|\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,\left[\frac{\kappa(\vartheta_{\varepsilon})}{\vartheta_{\varepsilon}}\right]_{{\rm{res}}}\,\nabla_{x}\vartheta_{\varepsilon}(t)\right\|^{2}_{L^{1}(\mathbb{B}_{l})}\,{\rm d}t\,

\displaystyle\leq\,c(l)\,.

(2.2.14)

The argument for proving (
2.2.13
) and (
2.2.14
) is similar to one employed in the proof of Proposition 5.1 of
[
39
]
, but here it is important
to get bounds for the
L^{2}
norm in time (see also Remark
2.2.4
below). Indeed, we have that

\left[\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})\right]_{{\rm{res}}}\leq C\,\left[\varrho_{\varepsilon}+\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,|\log\varrho_{\varepsilon}|\,+\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,|\log\vartheta_{\varepsilon}-\log\overline{\vartheta}|+\vartheta_{\varepsilon}^{3}\,\right]_{{\rm{res}}}

(2.2.15)

and thanks to the previous uniform bounds (
2.2.8
) and (
2.2.11
), one has that

\big(\left[\varrho_{\varepsilon}\right]_{{\rm{res}}}\big)_{\varepsilon}\subset L_{T}^{\infty}(L_{\rm loc}^{5/3})
,

\big(\left[\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,|\log\varrho_{\varepsilon}|\,\right]_{{\rm{res}}}\big)_{\varepsilon}\subset L_{T}^{\infty}(L_{\rm loc}^{q})
for all
1\leq q<5/3
(see relation (5.60) in
[
39
]
),

\big(\left[\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,|\log\vartheta_{\varepsilon}-\log\overline{\vartheta}|\,\right]_{{\rm{res}}}\big)_{\varepsilon}\subset L_{T}^{2}(L_{\rm loc}^{30/23})
and finally

\big(\left[\vartheta_{\varepsilon}^{3}\,\right]_{{\rm{res}}}\big)_{\varepsilon}\subset L_{T}^{\infty}(L_{\rm loc}^{4/3})
.
Let us recall that the inclusion symbol means that the sequences are uniformly bounded in the respective spaces.
Then, it follows that the first term in (
2.2.13
) is in
L_{T}^{2}(L_{\rm loc}^{30/23})
.
Next, taking (
2.2.15
) we obtain

\left[\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\right]_{{\rm{res}}}\leq C\,\left[\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}+\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,|\log\varrho_{\varepsilon}|\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\,+\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,|\log\vartheta_{\varepsilon}-\log\overline{\vartheta}|\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}+\vartheta_{\varepsilon}^{3}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\,\right]_{{\rm{res}}}

and using the uniform bounds (
2.2.8
) and (
2.2.12
), we have that
\big(\left[\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\right]_{{\rm{res}}}\big)_{\varepsilon}\subset L_{T}^{2}(L_{\rm loc}^{30/23})
.
Now, we look at the second term. We know that
\big(\left[\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,|\log\varrho_{\varepsilon}|\,\right]_{{\rm{res}}}\big)_{\varepsilon}\subset L_{T}^{\infty}(L_{\rm loc}^{q})
for all
1\leq q<5/3
and

\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\in L_{T}^{2}(L_{\rm loc}^{6})
(thanks to Sobolev embeddings, see Theorem
B.1
). Then, we take
q
such that
1/p:=1/q+1/6<1
and so

\big(\left[\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,|\log\varrho_{\varepsilon}|\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\,\right]_{{\rm{res}}}\big)_{\varepsilon}\subset L_{T}^{2}(L_{\rm loc}^{p})\,.

Keeping (
2.2.8
), (
2.2.11
) and (
2.2.2
) in mind and using that

\left[\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,|\log\vartheta_{\varepsilon}-\log\overline{\vartheta}|\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\,\right]_{{\rm{res}}}=\left[\sqrt{\varrho_{\varepsilon}}\,|\log\vartheta_{\varepsilon}-\log\overline{\vartheta}|\,\sqrt{\varrho_{\varepsilon}}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\,\right]_{{\rm{res}}}\,,

we obtain that the third term is uniformly bounded in
L_{T}^{2}(L_{\rm loc}^{30/29})
.
Using again the uniform bounds, we see that the last term is in
L_{T}^{\infty}(L_{\rm loc}^{12/11})
.
Thus, we obtain (
2.2.13
).

To get (
2.2.14
), we use instead the following estimate (see Proposition 5.1 of
[
39
]
):

\left[\frac{k(\vartheta_{\varepsilon})}{\vartheta_{\varepsilon}}\right]_{{\rm{res}}}\left|\frac{\nabla_{x}\vartheta_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right|\leq C\left(\left|\frac{\nabla_{x}(\log\vartheta_{\varepsilon})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right|+\left[\vartheta_{\varepsilon}^{2}\right]_{{\rm{res}}}\left|\frac{\nabla_{x}\vartheta_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right|\right)\,.

Owing to the previous uniform bounds, the former term is uniformly bounded in
L_{T}^{2}(L_{\rm loc}^{2})
and the latter one is uniformly bounded in
L_{T}^{2}(L_{\rm loc}^{1})
.
So, we obtain the estimate (
2.2.14
).

Remark 2.2.4

Differently from
[
39
]
, here we have made the integrability indices in (
2.2.13
) and (
2.2.14
) explicit.
In particular, having the
L^{2}
norm in time will reveal to be fundamental for the compensated compactness argument, see Lemma
2.3.2
below.

2.2.2
Constraints on the limit dynamics

In this subsection, we establish some properties that the limit points of the family
\bigl(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon}\bigr)_{\varepsilon}
have to satisfy.
These are static relations, which do not characterise the limit dynamics yet.

2.2.2.1
Preliminary considerations

To begin with, we propose an extension of Proposition 5.2 of
[
39
]
, which will be heavily used in the sequel. Two are the novelties here: firstly,
for the sake of generality we will consider a non-constant density profile
\widetilde{\varrho}
in the limit (although this property is not used in our analysis); in addition, due to the
centrifugal force, when
F\neq 0
our result needs a localization procedure on compact sets.

Proposition 2.2.5

Let
m\geq 1
be fixed. Let
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}
and
\overline{\vartheta}
be the static solutions identified and studied in Paragraph
2.1.1.3
, and take
\widetilde{\varrho}

to be the pointwise limit of the family
\left(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\right)_{\varepsilon}
(in particular,
\widetilde{\varrho}\equiv 1
if
m>1
or
m=1
and
F=0
).
Let
(\varrho_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
and
(\vartheta_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
be sequences of non-negative measurable functions, and define

R_{\varepsilon}\,:=\,\frac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}-\widetilde{\varrho}}{\varepsilon^{m}}\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\Theta_{\varepsilon}\,:=\,\frac{\vartheta_{\varepsilon}-\overline{\vartheta}}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,.

Suppose that, in the limit
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
, one has the convergence properties

\left[R_{\varepsilon}\right]_{\rm ess}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\rightharpoonup}}\,R\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\left[\Theta_{\varepsilon}\right]_{\rm ess}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\rightharpoonup}}\,\Theta\qquad\quad\mbox{ in the weak-$*$ topology of}\ L^{\infty}\bigl([0,T];L^{2}(K)\bigr)\,,

(2.2.16)

for any compact
K\subset\Omega
, and that, for any
L>0
, one has

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{L}}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{M}^{\varepsilon}_{\rm res}[t]}\,dx\,\leq\,c(L)\,\varepsilon^{2m}\,.

(2.2.17)

Then, for any given function
G\in C^{1}(\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\rm ess})
, one has the convergence

\frac{[G(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})]_{{\rm{ess}}}-G(\widetilde{\varrho},\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\rightharpoonup}}\,\partial_{\varrho}G(\widetilde{\varrho},\overline{\vartheta})\,R\,+\,\partial_{\vartheta}G(\widetilde{\varrho},\overline{\vartheta})\,\Theta\qquad\mbox{ in the weak-$*$ topology of}\ L^{\infty}\bigl([0,T];L^{2}(K)\bigr)\,,

for any compact
K\subset\Omega
.

Proof:

The case
\widetilde{\varrho}\equiv 1
follows by a straightforward adaptation of the proof of Proposition 5.2 of
[
39
]
. So, let us immediately focus on the case
m=1
and
F\neq 0
,
so that the target profile
\widetilde{\varrho}
is non-constant.

We start by observing that, by virtue of (
2.2.17
) and Lemma
2.1.6
, the estimates

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\,\left\|\left[G(\widetilde{\varrho},\overline{\vartheta})\right]_{\rm res}\right\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{B}_{L})}\,\leq\,C(L)\,\varepsilon\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\,\left\|\left[G(\widetilde{\varrho},\overline{\vartheta})\right]_{\rm res}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{B}_{L})}\,\leq\,C(L)

hold true, for any
L>0
fixed. Combining those bounds with hypothesis (
2.2.16
), after taking
L>0
so large that
K\subset\mathbb{B}_{L}
, we see that it is enough to prove the convergence

\int_{K}\left[\frac{G(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-G(\widetilde{\varrho},\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon}\,-\,\partial_{\varrho}G(\widetilde{\varrho},\overline{\vartheta})\,R_{\varepsilon}\,-\,\partial_{\vartheta}G(\widetilde{\varrho},\overline{\vartheta})\,\Theta_{\varepsilon}\right]_{{\rm{ess}}}\,\psi\,\,{\rm d}x\,\longrightarrow\,0

(2.2.18)

for any compact
K
fixed and any
\psi\in L^{1}\bigl([0,T];L^{2}(K)\bigr)
.

Next, we remark that, whenever
G\in C^{2}(\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\rm ess})
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\left[\frac{G(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-G(\widetilde{\varrho},\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon}\,-\,\partial_{\varrho}G(\widetilde{\varrho},\overline{\vartheta})\,R_{\varepsilon}-\partial_{\vartheta}G(\widetilde{\varrho},\overline{\vartheta})\,\Theta_{\varepsilon}\right]_{{\rm{ess}}}\right|\,\leq

(2.2.19)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\leq\,C\,\varepsilon\,\left\|{\rm Hess}(G)\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\rm ess})}\left(\left[R_{\varepsilon}\right]_{\rm ess}^{2}+\left[\Theta_{\varepsilon}\right]_{\rm ess}^{2}\right),

where we have denoted by
{\rm Hess}(G)
the Hessian matrix of the function
G
with respect to its variables
(\varrho,\vartheta)
.
In particular, (
2.2.19
) implies the estimate

\displaystyle\left\|\left[\frac{G(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-G(\widetilde{\varrho},\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon}\,-\,\partial_{\varrho}G(\widetilde{\varrho},\overline{\vartheta})\,R_{\varepsilon}\,-\,\partial_{\vartheta}G(\widetilde{\varrho},\overline{\vartheta})\,\Theta_{\varepsilon}\right]_{{\rm{ess}}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{1}(K))}\,\leq\,C\,\varepsilon\,.

(2.2.20)

Property (
2.2.18
) then follows from (
2.2.20
), after noticing that both terms
\left[G(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-G(\widetilde{\varrho},\overline{\vartheta})\right]_{\rm{ess}}/\varepsilon

and
\left[\partial_{\varrho}G(\widetilde{\varrho},\overline{\vartheta})\,R_{\varepsilon}+\partial_{\vartheta}G(\widetilde{\varrho},\overline{\vartheta})\,\Theta_{\varepsilon}\right]_{{\rm{ess}}}

are uniformly bounded in
L_{T}^{\infty}\bigl(L^{2}(K)\bigr)
.

Finally, when
G
is just
C^{1}(\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\rm ess})
, we approximate it by a family of smooth functions
\bigl(G_{n}\bigr)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, uniformly in
C^{1}(\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\rm ess})
.
Obviously, for each
n
, convergence (
2.2.18
) holds true for
G_{n}
. Moreover, we have

\left|\left[\frac{G(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-G(\widetilde{\varrho},\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon}\right]_{\rm ess}-\left[\frac{G_{n}(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-G_{n}(\widetilde{\varrho},\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon}\right]_{\rm ess}\right|\,\leq\,C\,\left\|G\,-\,G_{n}\right\|_{C^{1}(\overline{\mathcal{O}}_{\rm ess})}\left(\left[R_{\varepsilon}\right]_{\rm ess}+\left[\Theta_{\varepsilon}\right]_{\rm ess}\right)\,,

and a similar bound holds for the terms presenting partial derivatives of
G
. In particular, these controls entail that the remainders, created replacing
G
by
G_{n}
in
(
2.2.18
), are uniformly small in
\varepsilon
, whenever
n
is sufficiently large.
This completes the proof of the proposition.

From now on, we will focus on the two cases (
2.1.21
): either
m\geq 2
and possibly
F\neq 0
, or
m\geq 1
and
F=0
. We explain this in the next remark.

Remark 2.2.6

If
1<m<2
and
F\neq 0
, the structure of the wave system (see Paragraph 4.1.1) is much more complicated, since the centrifugal force term becomes singular; in turn, this prevents us from proving
that the quantity
\gamma_{\varepsilon}
(see details below) is compact, a fact which is a key point in the convergence step. On the other hand, the idea of combining the centrifugal force term
with
\gamma_{\varepsilon}
, in order to gain compactness of a new quantity, does not seem to work either, because, owing to temperature variations (and differently from [15] where the temperature was constant), there is no direct relation
between the centrifugal force and the pressure term.

Recall that, in both cases presented in (
2.1.21
), the limit density profile is always constant, say
\widetilde{\varrho}\equiv 1
. Let us fix an arbitrary positive time

T>0
, which we keep fixed until the end of this paragraph.
Thanks to (
2.2.4
), (
2.2.8
) and Proposition
2.1.7
, we get

\|\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2}+L^{5/3}(K))}\,\leq\,\varepsilon^{m}\,c(K)\qquad\mbox{ for all }\;K\subset\Omega\quad\mbox{ compact.}

(2.2.21)

In particular, keeping in mind the notations introduced in (
2.1.22
) and (
2.1.23
), we can define

R_{\varepsilon}\,:=\frac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1}{\varepsilon^{m}}=\,\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\,+\,\widetilde{r}_{\varepsilon}\;,\qquad\quad\mbox{ where }\quad\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}(t,x)\,:=\,\frac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon^{m}}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\widetilde{r}_{\varepsilon}(x)\,:=\,\frac{\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}-1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,.

(2.2.22)

Thanks to (
2.2.4
), (
2.2.8
) and Proposition
2.1.7
, the previous quantities verify the following bounds:

\sup_{\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]}\left\|\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2}+L^{5/3}({\mathbb{B}_{l}}))}\,\leq\,c\qquad\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\qquad\sup_{\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]}\left\|\widetilde{r}_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}_{l})}\,\leq\,c\,.

(2.2.23)

As usual, here above the radius
l>0
is fixed (and the constants
c
depend on it). In addition, in the case
F=0
, there is no need of localising in
\mathbb{B}_{l}
, and one gets instead

\sup_{\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]}\left\|\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2}+L^{5/3}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}))}\,\leq\,c\qquad\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\qquad\sup_{\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]}\left\|\widetilde{r}_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\,\leq\,\sup_{\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]}\left\|\widetilde{r}_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\,\leq\,c\,.

In view of the previous properties, there exist
\varrho^{(1)}\in L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{5/3}_{\rm loc})
and

\widetilde{r}\in L^{\infty}_{\rm loc}
such that (up to the extraction of a suitable subsequence)

\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\rightharpoonup}}\,\varrho^{(1)}\qquad\quad\mbox{ and }\qquad\quad\widetilde{r}_{\varepsilon}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\rightharpoonup}}\,\widetilde{r}\,,

(2.2.24)

where we understand that limits are taken in the weak-
*
topology of the respective spaces.
Therefore,

R_{\varepsilon}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\rightharpoonup}}\,R\,:=\,\varrho^{(1)}\,+\,\widetilde{r}\qquad\qquad\qquad\mbox{ weakly-$*$ in }\quad L^{\infty}\bigl([0,T];L^{5/3}_{\rm loc}(\Omega)\bigr)\,.

(2.2.25)

Observe that
\widetilde{r}
can be interpreted as a datum of our problem.
Moreover, owing to Proposition
2.1.7
and (
2.2.4
), we also get

\left[R_{\varepsilon}\right]_{\rm ess}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\rightharpoonup}}R\qquad\qquad\mbox{ weakly-$*$ in }\quad L^{\infty}\bigl([0,T];L^{2}_{\rm loc}(\Omega)\bigr)\,.

In a pretty similar way, we also find that

\displaystyle\Theta_{\varepsilon}\,:=\,\frac{\vartheta_{\varepsilon}\,-\,\overline{\vartheta}}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup\,\Theta\qquad\qquad\mbox{ in }\qquad L^{2}\bigl([0,T];W^{1,2}_{\rm loc}(\Omega)\bigr)

(2.2.26)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\,

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup\,\boldsymbol{U}\qquad\qquad\mbox{ in }\qquad L^{2}\bigl([0,T];W_{\rm loc}^{1,2}(\Omega)\bigr)\,.

(2.2.27)

Let us infer now some properties that these weak limits have to satisfy, starting with the case of anisotropic scaling, namely, in view of (
2.1.21
), either
m\geq 2
, or
m>1
and
F=0
.

2.2.2.2
The case of anisotropic scaling

When
m\geq 2
, or
m>1
and
F=0
, the system presents multiple scales, which act and interact at the same time; however, the low Mach number limit has a predominant effect.
As established in the next proposition, this fact imposes some rigid constraints on the target profiles.

Proposition 2.2.7

Let
m\geq 2
, or
m>1
and
F=0
in (
NSF

ε

1

) to (
NSF

ε

4

).
Let
\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon}\right)_{\varepsilon}
be a family of weak solutions, related to initial data
\left(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon},\vartheta_{0,\varepsilon}\right)_{\varepsilon}

verifying the hypotheses of Paragraph
2.1.1.4
. Let
(R,\boldsymbol{U},\Theta)
be a limit point of the sequence

\left(R_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon},\Theta_{\varepsilon}\right)_{\varepsilon}
, as identified in Subsection
2.2.2.1
. Then,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}\,=\,\,\Big(\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\,,\,0\Big)\,,\qquad\qquad\mbox{ with }\qquad\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\,=\,\boldsymbol{U}^{h}(t,x^{h})\quad\mbox{ and }\quad{\rm div}\,_{\!h}\,\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\,=\,0

(2.2.28)

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\Big(\partial_{\varrho}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,R\,+\,\partial_{\vartheta}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\Theta\Big)\,=\,\nabla_{x}G\,+\,\delta_{2}(m)\nabla_{x}F\qquad\qquad\mbox{ a.e. in }\;\,\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega

(2.2.29)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\Upsilon+{\rm div}\,_{h}\left(\Upsilon\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\right)-\frac{\kappa(\overline{\vartheta})}{\overline{\vartheta}}\Delta\Theta=0\,,\qquad\qquad\mbox{ with }\qquad\Upsilon\,:=\,\partial_{\varrho}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})R+\partial_{\vartheta}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\Theta\,,

(2.2.30)

where the last equation is supplemented with the initial condition

\Upsilon_{|t=0}=\partial_{\varrho}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,R_{0}\,+\,\partial_{\vartheta}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\Theta_{0}
.

Proof:

Let us focus here on the case
m\geq 2
and
F\neq 0
. A similar analysis yields the result also in the case
m>1
, provided we take
F=0
.

First of all, let us consider the weak formulation of the mass equation (
NSF

ε

1

): for any test function
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}\bigl(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega\bigr)
, denoting
[0,T]\times K\,=\,{\rm Supp}\,\,\varphi
, with
\varphi(T,\cdot)\equiv 0
, we have

-\int^{T}_{0}\int_{K}\bigl(\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1\bigr)\,\partial_{t}\varphi\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\,-\,\int^{T}_{0}\int_{K}\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\,\cdot\,\nabla_{x}\varphi\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\,=\,\int_{K}\bigl(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}-1\bigr)\,\varphi(0,\,\cdot\,)\,{\rm d}x\,.

We can easily pass to the limit in this equation, thanks to the strong convergence
\varrho_{\varepsilon}\longrightarrow 1
provided by (
2.2.21
) and the weak convergence of

\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}
in
L_{T}^{2}\bigl(L^{6}_{\rm loc}\bigr)
(by (
2.2.27
) and Sobolev embeddings): we find

-\,\int^{T}_{0}\int_{K}\boldsymbol{U}\,\cdot\,\nabla_{x}\varphi\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\,=\,0\,,

for any test function
\varphi\,\in C_{c}^{\infty}\bigl([0,T[\,\times\Omega\bigr)
, which in particular implies

{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{U}=0\qquad\qquad\mbox{ a.e. in }\;\,\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega\,.

(2.2.31)

Let us now consider the momentum equation (
NSF

ε

2

), in its weak formulation (
2.1.28
).
First of all, we test the momentum equation on
\varepsilon^{m}\,\boldsymbol{\phi}
, for a smooth compactly supported
\boldsymbol{\phi}
.
By use of the uniform bounds we got in Subsection
2.2.1
, it is easy to see that the only terms which do not converge to
0
are the ones involving the pressure and
the gravitational force; in the endpoint case
m=2
, we also have the contribution of the centrifugal force. Hence, let us focus on them, and more precisely on the quantity

\displaystyle\Xi\,:

\displaystyle=\,\frac{\nabla_{x}p(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,-\,\varepsilon^{m-2}\,\varrho_{\varepsilon}\nabla_{x}F\,-\,\varrho_{\varepsilon}\nabla_{x}G

(2.2.32)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\nabla_{x}\left(p(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})\,-\,p(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})\right)\,-\,\varepsilon^{m-2}\,\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon}-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\right)\nabla_{x}F\,-\,\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon}-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\right)\nabla_{x}G\,,

where we have used relation (
2.1.17
).
By uniform bounds and (
2.2.25
), the second and third terms in the right-hand side of (
2.2.32
) converge to
0
,
when tested against any smooth compactly supported
\boldsymbol{\phi}
; notice that this is true actually for any
m>1
.
On the other hand, for the first item we can use the decomposition

\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,\nabla_{x}\left(p(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})\,-\,p(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})\right)\,=\,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,\nabla_{x}\left(p(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})\,-\,p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\right)\,-\,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,\nabla_{x}\left(p(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})\,-\,p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\right)\,.

Due to the smallness of the residual set (
2.2.5
) and to estimate (
2.2.8
), decomposing
p
into essential and residual part
and then applying Proposition
2.2.5
, we get the convergence

\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,\nabla_{x}\left(p(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})\,-\,p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\right)\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightharpoonup}}\;\nabla_{x}\left(\partial_{\varrho}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,R\,+\,\partial_{\vartheta}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\Theta\right)

in
L_{T}^{\infty}(H^{-1}_{\rm loc})
, for any
T>0
.
On the other hand, a Taylor expansion of
p(\,\cdot\,,\overline{\vartheta})
up to the second order around
1
gives, together with Proposition
2.1.7
, the bound

\left\|\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,\left(p(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})\,-\,p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\right)\,-\,\partial_{\varrho}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\widetilde{r}_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(K)}\,\leq\,C(K)\,\varepsilon^{m}\,,

for any compact set
K\subset\Omega
. From the previous estimate we deduce that

\left(p(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})\,-\,p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\right)/\varepsilon^{m}\,\longrightarrow\,\partial_{\varrho}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\widetilde{r}
in e.g.
