\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\bigl(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega\bigr)
.

Putting all these facts together and keeping in mind relation (
2.2.25
), thanks to (
2.2.32
) we finally find the celebrated
Boussinesq relation

\nabla_{x}\left(\partial_{\varrho}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\varrho^{(1)}\,+\,\partial_{\vartheta}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\Theta\right)\,=\,0\qquad\qquad\mbox{ a.e. in }\;\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega\,.

(2.2.33)

Remark 2.2.8

Notice that, dividing (
2.1.17
) by
\varepsilon^{m}
and passing to the limit in it, one gets the identity

\partial_{\varrho}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\nabla_{x}\widetilde{r}\,=\,\nabla_{x}G\,+\,\delta_{2}(m)\nabla_{x}F\,,

where we have set
\delta_{2}(m)=1
if
m=2
,
\delta_{2}(m)=0
otherwise.
Hence, relation (
2.2.33
) is equivalent to equality (
2.2.29
),
which might be more familiar to the reader (see formula (5.10) in Chapter 5 of
[
39
]
).

Up to now, the contribution of the fast rotation in the limit has not been seen: this is due to the fact that the incompressible
limit takes place faster than the high rotation limit, because
m>1
. Roughly speaking, the rotation term enters into the singular
perturbation operator as a “lower order” part; nonetheless, being singular, it does impose some conditions on the limit dynamics.

To make this rigorous, we test (
NSF

ε

2

) on
\varepsilon\,\boldsymbol{\phi}
, where this time we take
\boldsymbol{\phi}\,=\,{\rm curl}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}
, for some smooth compactly supported
\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\in C^{\infty}_{c}\bigl([0,T[\,\times\Omega\bigr)
.
Once again, by uniform bounds we infer that the
\partial_{t}
term, the convective term and the viscosity term all converge to
0
when
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
.
As for the pressure and the external forces, we repeat the same manipulations as before: making use of relation (
2.1.17
) again, we are reconducted to work on

\int^{T}_{0}\int_{K}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2m-1}}\nabla_{x}\left(p(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})\,-\,p(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})\right)\,-\,\frac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}\,\nabla_{x}F\,-\,\frac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon^{m-1}}\nabla_{x}G\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{\phi}\,\,{\rm d}x\,dt\,,

where the compact set
K\subset\Omega
is such that
{\rm Supp}\,\boldsymbol{\phi}\subset[0,T[\,\times K
, and
\varepsilon>0
is small enough.
According to (
2.2.21
), the two forcing terms converge to
0
, in the limit for
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
; on the other hand, the first term (which has no chance to be bounded uniformly in
\varepsilon
)
simply vanishes, due to the fact that
\boldsymbol{\phi}\,=\,{\rm curl}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}
.

Finally, using a priori bounds and properties (
2.2.25
) and
(
2.2.27
), it is easy to see that the rotation term converges to
\int^{T}_{0}\int_{K}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\times\boldsymbol{U}\cdot\boldsymbol{\phi}
.
In the end, passing to the limit for
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
we find

\mathbb{H}\left(\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\times\boldsymbol{U}\right)\,=\,0\qquad\qquad\text{and so}\qquad\qquad\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\times\boldsymbol{U}\,=\,\nabla_{x}\Phi\,

for some potential function
\Phi
. From this relation, which in components reads

\begin{pmatrix}-U^{2}\\
U^{1}\\
0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{1}\Phi\\
\partial_{2}\Phi\\
\partial_{3}\Phi\end{pmatrix}\,,

(2.2.34)

we deduce that
\Phi=\Phi(t,x^{h})
, i.e.
\Phi
does not depend
on
x^{3}
, and that the same property is inherited by
\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\,=\,\bigl(U^{1},U^{2}\bigr)
, i.e.
\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\,=\,\boldsymbol{U}^{h}(t,x^{h})
. Furthermore, from (
2.2.34
), it is also easy to see that
the
2
-D flow given by
\boldsymbol{U}^{h}
is incompressible, namely
{\rm div}\,_{\!h}\,\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\,=\,0
.
Combining this fact with (
2.2.31
), we infer that
\partial_{3}U^{3}\,=\,0
; on the other hand, thanks to the boundary condition
(
2.1.8
) we must have
\bigl(\boldsymbol{U}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\bigr)_{|\partial\Omega}\,=\,0
. Keeping in mind that

\partial\Omega\,=\,\bigl(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\{0\}\bigr)\cup\bigl(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\{1\}\bigr)
, we finally get
U^{3}\,\equiv\,0
,
whence (
2.2.28
) finally follows.

Next, we observe that we can by now pass to the limit in the weak formulation (
2.1.29
) of (
NSF

ε

3

).
The argument being analogous to the one used in
[
39
]
(see Paragraph 5.3.2), we only sketch it.
First of all, testing (
NSF

ε

3

) on
\varphi/\varepsilon^{m}
, for some
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}\bigl([0,T[\,\times\Omega\bigr)
, and using (
NSF

ε

1

), for
\varepsilon>0
small enough we get

\displaystyle-\int^{T}_{0}\!\!\int_{K}\varrho_{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-s(1,\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right)\partial_{t}\varphi-\int^{T}_{0}\!\!\int_{K}\varrho_{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-s(1,\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right)\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi

(2.2.35)

\displaystyle+\int^{T}_{0}\!\!\int_{K}\frac{\kappa(\vartheta_{\varepsilon})}{\vartheta_{\varepsilon}}\,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,\nabla_{x}\vartheta_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi-\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,\langle\sigma_{\varepsilon},\varphi\rangle_{[\mathcal{M}^{+},C^{0}]([0,T]\times K)}=\int_{K}\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}\left(\frac{s(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon},\vartheta_{0,\varepsilon})-s(1,\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right)\varphi(0)\,.

To begin with, let us decompose

\displaystyle\varrho_{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-s(1,\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right)=

(2.2.36)

\displaystyle=[\varrho_{\varepsilon}]_{{\rm{ess}}}\left(\frac{[s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})]_{{\rm{ess}}}-s(1,\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right)+\left[\frac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right]_{{\rm{res}}}\left([s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})]_{{\rm{ess}}}-s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\right)+\left[\frac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right]_{{\rm{res}}}\,.

Thanks to (
2.2.8
), we discover that the second term in the right-hand side strongly converges to
0
in

L_{T}^{\infty}(L^{5/3}_{\rm loc})
. Also the third term converges to
0
in the space
L_{T}^{2}(L^{30/23}_{\rm loc})
, as a consequence of (
2.2.5
) and (
2.2.13
).
Notice that these terms converge to
0
even when multiplied by
\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}
: to see this, it is enough to put (
2.2.5
),
(
2.2.13
), (
2.2.12
) and the previous properties together.

As for the first term in the right-hand side of (
2.2.36
), Propositions
2.2.5
and
2.1.7
and estimate (
2.2.21
) imply that it
weakly converges to
\partial_{\varrho}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,R\,+\,\partial_{\vartheta}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\Theta
, where
R
and
\Theta
are defined respectively in (
2.2.25
)
and (
2.2.26
). On the other hand, an application of the Div-Curl Lemma (see Theorem
B.14
) gives

[\varrho_{\varepsilon}]_{{\rm{ess}}}\left(\frac{[s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})]_{{\rm{ess}}}-s(1,\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right)\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\,\rightharpoonup\,\Bigl(\partial_{\varrho}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,R\,+\,\partial_{\vartheta}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\Theta\Bigr)\,\boldsymbol{U}

in the space
L_{T}^{2}(L^{3/2}_{\rm loc})
.
In addition, from (
2.2.3
) we deduce that

\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,\langle\sigma_{\varepsilon},\varphi\rangle_{[\mathcal{M}^{+},C^{0}]([0,T]\times K)}\,\longrightarrow\,0

when
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
.
Finally, a separation into essential and residual part of the coefficient
\kappa(\vartheta_{\varepsilon})/\vartheta_{\varepsilon}
, together with (
2.1.16
), (
2.2.4
),
(
2.2.8
), (
2.2.11
) and (
2.2.14
) gives

\frac{\kappa(\vartheta_{\varepsilon})}{\vartheta_{\varepsilon}}\,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,\nabla_{x}\vartheta_{\varepsilon}\,\rightharpoonup\,\frac{\kappa(\overline{\vartheta})}{\overline{\vartheta}}\,\nabla_{x}\Theta\qquad\qquad\mbox{ in }\qquad L^{2}\bigl([0,T];L^{1}_{\rm loc}(\Omega)\bigr)\,.

In the end, we have proved that equation (
2.2.35
) converges, for
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
, to equation

\displaystyle-\int^{T}_{0}\int_{\Omega}\Bigl(\partial_{\varrho}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})R+\partial_{\vartheta}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\Theta\Bigr)\left(\partial_{t}\varphi+\boldsymbol{U}\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi\right)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\,+

(2.2.37)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\int^{T}_{0}\int_{\Omega}\frac{\kappa(\overline{\vartheta})}{\overline{\vartheta}}\nabla_{x}\Theta\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t=\int_{\Omega}\Bigl(\partial_{\varrho}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,R_{0}\,+\,\partial_{\vartheta}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\Theta_{0}\Bigr)\,\varphi(0)\,{\rm d}x\,,

for all
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}([0,T[\,\times\Omega)
, with
T>0
any arbitrary time.
Relation (
2.2.37
) means that the quantity
\Upsilon
, defined in (
2.2.30
), is a weak solution of that equation,
related to the initial datum
\Upsilon_{0}:=\partial_{\varrho}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,R_{0}\,+\,\partial_{\vartheta}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\Theta_{0}
.
Equation (
2.2.30
) is in fact an equation for
\Theta
only, keep in mind Remark
2.1.11
.

2.2.2.3
The case of isotropic scaling

We focus now on the case of isotropic scaling, namely
m=1
. Recall that, in this instance, we also set
F=0
. In this case, the fast rotation and weak compressibility
effects are of the same order; in turn, this allows to reach the so-called
quasi-geostrophic balance
in the limit (see equation (
2.2.38
) below).

Proposition 2.2.9

Take
m=1
and
F=0
in system (
NSF

ε

1

) to (
NSF

ε

4

).
Let
\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon}\right)_{\varepsilon}
be a family of weak solutions to (
NSF

ε

1

) to (
NSF

ε

4

), associated with initial data

\left(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon},\vartheta_{0,\varepsilon}\right)
verifying the hypotheses fixed in Paragraph
2.1.1.4
.
Let
(R,\boldsymbol{U},\Theta)
be a limit point of the sequence
\left(R_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon},\Theta_{\varepsilon}\right)_{\varepsilon}
, as identified in Subsection

2.2.2.1
.
Then,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}\,=\,\,\Big(\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\,,\,0\Big)\,,\qquad\qquad\mbox{ with }\qquad\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\,=\,\boldsymbol{U}^{h}(t,x^{h})\quad\mbox{ and }\quad{\rm div}\,_{\!h}\,\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\,=\,0

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\,=\,\nabla^{\perp}_{h}q\;\mbox{ a.e. in }\;\,]0,T[\,\times\Omega\,,\quad\mbox{ with }

(2.2.38)

\displaystyle q\,=\,q(t,x^{h})\,:=\,\partial_{\varrho}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})R+\partial_{\vartheta}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\Theta-G-1/2

(2.2.39)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\Upsilon+{\rm div}_{h}\left(\Upsilon\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\right)-\frac{\kappa(\overline{\vartheta})}{\overline{\vartheta}}\Delta\Theta=0\,,\qquad\quad\mbox{ with }\qquad\Upsilon_{|t=0}\,=\,\Upsilon_{0}\,,

where
\Upsilon
and
\Upsilon_{0}
are the same quantities defined in Proposition
2.2.7
.

Proof:

Arguing as in the proof of Proposition
2.2.7
, it is easy to pass to the limit in the continuity equation and in the entropy balance. In particular, we obtain again
equations (
2.2.31
) and (
2.2.37
).

The only changes concern the analysis of the momentum equation, written in its weak formulation (
2.1.28
).
We start by testing it on
\varepsilon\,\boldsymbol{\phi}
, for a smooth compactly supported
\boldsymbol{\phi}
. Similarly to what done above, the uniform bounds of Subsection
2.2.1

allow us to say that the only quantity which does not vanish in the limit is the sum of the terms involving the Coriolis force, the pressure and the gravitational force:

\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\times\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\,+\frac{\nabla_{x}\left(p(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-p(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})\right)}{\varepsilon}\,-\,\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon}-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\right)\nabla_{x}G\,=\,\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)\,.

From this relation, following the same computations performed in the proof of Proposition
2.2.7
, in the limit
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
we obtain that

\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\times\boldsymbol{U}+\nabla_{x}\left(\partial_{\varrho}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\varrho^{(1)}\,+\,\partial_{\vartheta}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\Theta\right)\,=\,0\qquad\qquad\mbox{ a.e. in }\;\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega\,.

After defining
q
as in (
2.2.39
), i.e.

q\,:=\,\partial_{\varrho}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})R+\partial_{\vartheta}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\Theta-G-1/2

and keeping Remark
2.2.8
in mind, this equality can be equivalently written as

\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\times\boldsymbol{U}+\nabla_{x}q\,=\,0\qquad\qquad\mbox{ a.e. in }\;\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega\,.

As done in the proof to Proposition
2.2.7
, from this relation we immediately deduce that
q=q(t,x^{h})
and
\boldsymbol{U}^{h}=\boldsymbol{U}^{h}(t,x^{h})
.
In addition, we get
\boldsymbol{U}^{h}=\nabla^{\perp}_{h}q
, whence we gather that
q
can be viewed as a stream function for
\boldsymbol{U}^{h}
.
Using (
2.2.31
), we infer that
\partial_{3}U^{3}=0
, which in turn implies that
U^{3}\equiv 0
, thanks to (
2.1.8
).
The proposition is thus proved.

Remark 2.2.10

Notice that
q
is defined up to an additive constant. We fix it to be
-1/2
, in order to compensate the vertical mean of
G
and have a cleaner expression for
\langle q\rangle
(see Theorem
2.1.12
). As a matter of fact, it is
\langle q\rangle
the natural quantity to look at, see also Subsection
2.4.3
in this respect.

2.3
Convergence in presence of the centrifugal force

In this section we complete the proof of Theorem
2.1.10
, in the case when
m\geq 2
and
F\neq 0
. In the case
m>1
and
F=0
, some arguments of the proof slightly change,
due to the absence of the (unbounded) centrifugal force: we refer to Section
2.4
below for more details.

The uniform bounds of Subsection
2.2.1
allow us to pass to the limit in the mass and entropy equations, but they are
not enough for proving convergence in the weak formulation of the momentum equation: the main problem relies on identifying the weak limit of the convective term

\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}
.
For this, we need to control the strong oscillations in time of the solutions: this is the aim of Subsection
2.3.1
. In Subsection
2.3.2
, by using a compensated compactness argument together with Aubin-Lions Theorem (see Theorem
B.13
), we establish strong convergence of suitable quantities related to the velocity fields.
This property, which deeply relies on the structure of the wave system, allows us to pass to the limit in our equations (see Subsection
2.3.3
).

2.3.1
Analysis of the acoustic waves

The goal of the present subsection is to describe oscillations of solutions. First of all, we recast our equations into a wave system; there we also implement a localisation
procedure, due to the presence of the centrifugal force. Then, we establish uniform bounds for the quantities appearing in the wave system. Finally, we apply a regularisation in space for all the quantities, which is preparatory in view of the computations of Subsection
2.3.2
.

2.3.1.1
Formulation of the acoustic equation

Let us define

\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\,:=\,\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\,.

We start by writing the continuity equation in the form

\varepsilon^{m}\,\partial_{t}\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}\,+\,{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\,=\,0\,.

(2.3.1)

Of course, this relation, as well as the other ones which will follow, has to be read in the weak form.

Using continuity equation and resorting to the time lifting (
2.1.32
) of the measure
\sigma_{\varepsilon}
, straightforward computations
lead us to the following form of the entropy balance:

\varepsilon^{m}\partial_{t}\!\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\frac{s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-s(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon^{m}}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\Sigma_{\varepsilon}\right)\,=\,\varepsilon^{m}\,{\rm div}\,\!\!\left(\frac{\kappa(\vartheta_{\varepsilon})}{\vartheta_{\varepsilon}}\frac{\nabla_{x}\vartheta_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right)+s(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta}){\rm div}\,\!\!\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\right)-{\rm div}\,\!\!\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon}s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\right),

where, with a little abuse of notation, we use the identification

\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\Sigma_{\varepsilon}\,\varphi\,dx\,=\,\langle\Sigma_{\varepsilon},\varphi\rangle_{[\mathcal{M}^{+},C^{0}]}
. Next, since
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}

is smooth (recall relation (
2.1.18
) above), the previous equation can be finally written as

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{m}\,\partial_{t}\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\frac{s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-s(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,-\,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\Sigma_{\varepsilon}\right)\,=

(2.3.2)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad=\,\varepsilon^{m}\,\biggl({\rm div}\,\!\left(\frac{\kappa(\vartheta_{\varepsilon})}{\vartheta_{\varepsilon}}\,\frac{\nabla_{x}\vartheta_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right)\,-\,\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\,\cdot\,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,\nabla_{x}s(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})\,-\,{\rm div}\,\!\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\frac{s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-s(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\right)\biggr)\,.

Now, we turn our attention to the momentum equation. By (
2.1.17
) we find

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{m}\,\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\,+\,\nabla_{x}\left(\frac{p(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-p(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right)\,+\,\varepsilon^{m-1}\,\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\times\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\,=\,\varepsilon^{2(m-1)}\frac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon^{m}}\nabla_{x}F\,+

(2.3.3)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\,\varepsilon^{m}\left({\rm div}\,\mathbb{S}\!\left(\vartheta_{\varepsilon},\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\right)\,-\,{\rm div}\,\!\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\right)\,+\,\frac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon^{m}}\nabla_{x}G\right)\,.

At this point, let us introduce two real numbers
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
, such that the following relations are satisfied:

\mathcal{A}\,+\,\mathcal{B}\,\partial_{\varrho}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,=\,\partial_{\varrho}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\mathcal{B}\,\partial_{\vartheta}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,=\,\partial_{\vartheta}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,.

(2.3.4)

Due to Gibbs’ law (
2.1.3
) and the structural hypotheses of Paragraph
2.1.1.2
(see also Chapter 8 of
[
39
]
and
[
44
]
), we notice that

\mathcal{A}
is given by formula (
2.1.38
), and
\mathcal{A}>0
.

Taking a linear combination of (
2.3.1
) and (
2.3.2
), with coefficients respectively
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
,
and keeping in mind equation (
2.3.3
), we finally get the wave system

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\varepsilon^{m}\,\partial_{t}Z_{\varepsilon}\,+\,\mathcal{A}\,{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\,=\,\varepsilon^{m}\,\left({\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\,+\,X^{2}_{\varepsilon}\right)\\[4.30554pt]
\varepsilon^{m}\,\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\,+\,\nabla_{x}Z_{\varepsilon}\,+\,\varepsilon^{m-1}\,\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\times\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\,=\,\varepsilon^{m}\,\left({\rm div}\,\mathbb{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\,+\,\boldsymbol{Y}^{2}_{\varepsilon}\,+\,\nabla_{x}Y^{3}_{\varepsilon}\right)\,,\qquad\big(\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\big)_{|\partial\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\,=\,0\,,\end{array}\right.

(2.3.5)

where we have defined the quantities

\displaystyle Z_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}\,\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}\,+\,\mathcal{B}\,\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\frac{s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-s(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,-\,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\Sigma_{\varepsilon}\right)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}\left(\frac{\kappa(\vartheta_{\varepsilon})}{\vartheta_{\varepsilon}}\,\frac{\nabla_{x}\vartheta_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,-\,\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\frac{s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-s(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\right)

\displaystyle X^{2}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle-\,\mathcal{B}\,\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\,\cdot\,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,\nabla_{x}s(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})

\displaystyle\mathbb{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}\!\left(\vartheta_{\varepsilon},\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\right)\,-\,\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Y}^{2}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon^{m}}\nabla_{x}G\,+\,\varepsilon^{m-2}\,\frac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon^{m}}\nabla_{x}F

\displaystyle Y^{3}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\left(\mathcal{A}\,\frac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,+\mathcal{B}\,\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\frac{s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-s(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,-\,\mathcal{B}\,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\Sigma_{\varepsilon}\,-\,\frac{p(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-p(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right)\,.

We remark that system (
2.3.5
) has to be read in the weak sense: for any
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}\bigl([0,T[\,\times\overline{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}\bigr)
, one has

-\,\varepsilon^{m}\,\int^{T}_{0}\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}Z_{\varepsilon}\,\partial_{t}\varphi\,-\,\mathcal{A}\,\int^{T}_{0}\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi\,=\,\varepsilon^{m}\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}Z_{0,\varepsilon}\,\varphi(0)\,+\,\varepsilon^{m}\,\int^{T}_{0}\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\left(-\,\boldsymbol{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi\,+\,X^{2}_{\varepsilon}\,\varphi\right)\,,

and also, for any
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in C_{c}^{\infty}\bigl([0,T[\,\times\overline{\Omega}_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{3}\bigr)
such that
\big(\boldsymbol{\psi}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{\varepsilon}\big)_{|\partial{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}}=0
, one has

\displaystyle-\,\varepsilon^{m}\,\int^{T}_{0}\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,-\,\int^{T}_{0}\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}Z_{\varepsilon}\,{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}\,+\,\varepsilon^{m-1}\int^{T}_{0}\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\times\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=\,\varepsilon^{m}\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{V}_{0,\varepsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}(0)\,+\,\varepsilon^{m}\,\int^{T}_{0}\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\left(-\,\mathbb{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,+\,\boldsymbol{Y}^{2}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\,-\,Y^{3}_{\varepsilon}\,{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}\right)\,,

where we have set

Z_{0,\varepsilon}\,=\,\mathcal{A}\,\varrho^{(1)}_{0,\varepsilon}\,+\,\mathcal{B}\,\left(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}\,\frac{s(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon},\vartheta_{0,\varepsilon})-s(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right)\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\boldsymbol{V}_{0,\varepsilon}\,=\,\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\,.

(2.3.6)

At this point, analogously to
[
34
]
, for any fixed
l>0
, let us introduce a smooth cut-off

\displaystyle\chi_{l}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\quad\mbox{ radially decreasing}\,,\qquad\mbox{ with }\quad 0\leq\chi_{l}\leq 1\,,

(2.3.7)

\displaystyle\mbox{such that }\quad\chi_{l}\equiv 1\ \mbox{ on }\ \mathbb{B}_{l}\,,\quad\chi_{l}\equiv 0\ \mbox{ out of }\ \mathbb{B}_{2l}\,,\quad\left|\nabla_{h}\chi_{l}(x^{h})\right|\,\leq\,C(l)\;\;\text{for any}\;x^{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,.

Then we define

\Lambda_{\varepsilon,l}\,:=\,\chi_{l}\,Z_{\varepsilon}\,=\,\chi_{l}\,\mathcal{A}\,\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}\,+\,\chi_{l}\,\mathcal{B}\,\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\frac{s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-s(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,-\,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\Sigma_{\varepsilon}\right)\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,l}\,:=\,\chi_{l}\,\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\,.

(2.3.8)

For notational convenience, in what follows we keep using the notation

\Lambda_{\varepsilon}
and
\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon}
instead of
\Lambda_{\varepsilon,l}
and
\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,l}\,
, tacitly meaning the dependence on
l
.
So system (
2.3.5
) becomes

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\varepsilon^{m}\,\partial_{t}\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\,+\,\mathcal{A}\,{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon}\,=\,\varepsilon^{m}f_{\varepsilon}\\[4.30554pt]
\varepsilon^{m}\,\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon}\,+\,\nabla_{x}\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\,+\,\varepsilon^{m-1}\,\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\times\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon}\,=\,\varepsilon^{m}\boldsymbol{G}_{\varepsilon}\,,\qquad\big(\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\big)_{|\partial\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\,=\,0\,,\end{array}\right.

(2.3.9)

where we have defined
f_{\varepsilon}\,:=\,{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{F}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\,+\,F^{2}_{\varepsilon}\;
and
\;\boldsymbol{G}_{\varepsilon}\,:=\,{\rm div}\,\mathbb{G}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\,+\,\boldsymbol{G}^{2}_{\varepsilon}\,+\,\nabla_{x}G^{3}_{\varepsilon}
, with

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{F}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\,=\,\chi_{l}\,\boldsymbol{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\qquad\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\qquad F^{2}_{\varepsilon}\,=\,\chi_{l}X^{2}_{\varepsilon}\,-\,\boldsymbol{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla_{x}\chi_{l}\,+\,\mathcal{A}\,\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla_{x}\chi_{l}\,

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\,=\,\chi_{l}\,\mathbb{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\;,\qquad\boldsymbol{G}^{2}_{\varepsilon}\,=\,\chi_{l}\,\boldsymbol{Y}^{2}_{\varepsilon}\,+\,\left(\frac{Z_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon^{m}}-Y^{3}_{\varepsilon}\right)\,\nabla_{x}\chi_{l}\,-\,^{t}\mathbb{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla_{x}\chi_{l}\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad G^{3}_{\varepsilon}\,=\,\chi_{l}\,Y^{3}_{\varepsilon}\,.

2.3.1.2
Uniform bounds

Here we use estimates of Subsection
2.2.1
in order to show uniform bounds for the solutions and the data in the wave equation (
2.3.9
).
We start by dealing with the “unknowns”
\Lambda_{\varepsilon}
and
\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon}
.

Lemma 2.3.1

Let
\bigl(\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\bigr)_{\varepsilon}
and
\bigl(\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon}\bigr)_{\varepsilon}
be defined as above. Then, for any
T>0
and all
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
, one has

\|\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2}+L^{5/3}+L^{1}+\mathcal{M}^{+})}\leq c(l)\,,\quad\quad\|\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}_{T}(L^{2}+L^{30/23})}\leq c(l)\,.

Proof:

We start by writing
\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon}\,=\,\boldsymbol{W}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\,+\,\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon}^{2}
, where

\boldsymbol{W}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\,:=\,\chi_{l}\,[\varrho_{\varepsilon}]_{{\rm{ess}}}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\qquad\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\qquad\boldsymbol{W}^{2}_{\varepsilon}\,:=\,\chi_{l}\,[\varrho_{\varepsilon}]_{{\rm{res}}}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\,.

Since the density and temperature are uniformly bounded on the essential set, by (
2.2.12
) we infer that
\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon}^{1}
is uniformly bounded in

L_{T}^{2}(L^{2})
. On the other hand, by (
2.2.8
) and (
2.2.12
) again, we easily deduce that
\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon}^{2}
is uniformly bounded in

L_{T}^{2}(L^{p})
, where
3/5+1/6\,=\,1/p
. The claim about
\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon}
is hence proved.

Let us now consider
\Lambda_{\varepsilon}
, defined in (
2.3.8
), i.e.

\Lambda_{\varepsilon}=\Lambda_{\varepsilon,l}\,:=\,\chi_{l}\,Z_{\varepsilon}\,=\,\chi_{l}\,\mathcal{A}\,\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}\,+\,\chi_{l}\,\mathcal{B}\,\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\frac{s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-s(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,-\,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\Sigma_{\varepsilon}\right)\,.

First of all, owing to the bounds
\left\|\Sigma_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\mathcal{M}^{+})}\,\leq\,C\,\|\sigma_{\varepsilon}\|_{\mathcal{M}^{+}_{t,x}}
and (
2.2.3
), we have that

\left\|\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2m}}\,\chi_{l}\,\Sigma_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(\mathcal{M}^{+})}\leq c(l)\,,

uniformly in
\varepsilon>0
. Next, we can write the following decomposition:

\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\chi_{l}\,\frac{s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-s(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,=\,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,\chi_{l}\,\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})\,-\,\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\,s(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})\right)\,-\,\chi_{l}\,\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\,s(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})\,,

where the latter term in the right-hand side is bounded in
L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2}+L^{5/3})
in view of (
2.2.23
) and Proposition
2.1.7
. Concerning the former term,
we can write it as

\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\chi_{l}\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon}s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-\varrho_{\varepsilon}s(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})\right)=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\chi_{l}\bigl[\varrho_{\varepsilon}s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-\varrho_{\varepsilon}s(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})\bigr]_{{\rm{ess}}}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\chi_{l}\bigl[\varrho_{\varepsilon}s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})\bigr]_{{\rm{res}}}\,,

(2.3.10)

since the support of
\chi_{l}\varrho_{\varepsilon}s(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})
is contained in the essential set by Proposition
2.1.7
, for small enough
\varepsilon

(depending on the fixed
l>0
).
By (
2.2.7
), the last term on the right-hand side of (
2.3.10
) is uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{1})
; as for the first term, a Taylor expansion at the first order, together with
inequality (
2.2.4
) and the structural restrictions on
s
, immediately yields its uniform boundedness in
L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2})
.

The lemma is hence completely proved.

In the next lemma, we establish bounds for the source terms in the system of acoustic waves (
2.3.9
).

Lemma 2.3.2

For any
T>0
fixed, let us define the following spaces:

•

\mathcal{X}_{1}\,:=\,L^{2}\Bigl([0,T];\big(L^{2}+L^{1}+L^{3/2}+L^{30/23}+L^{30/29}\big)(\Omega)\Bigr)
;

•

\mathcal{X}_{2}\,:=\,L^{2}\Bigl([0,T];\big(L^{2}+L^{1}+L^{4/3}\big)(\Omega)\Bigr)
;

•

\mathcal{X}_{3}\,:=\,\mathcal{X}_{2}\,+\,L^{\infty}\Bigl([0,T];\big(L^{2}+L^{5/3}+L^{1}\big)(\Omega)\Bigr)
;

•

\mathcal{X}_{4}\,:=\,L^{\infty}\Bigl([0,T];\big(L^{2}+L^{5/3}+L^{1}+\mathcal{M}^{+}\big)(\Omega)\Bigr)
.

Then, for any
l>0
fixed, one has the following bounds, uniformly in
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
:

\left\|\boldsymbol{F}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{\mathcal{X}_{1}}\,+\,\left\|F^{2}_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{\mathcal{X}_{1}}\,+\,\left\|\mathbb{G}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{\mathcal{X}_{2}}\,+\,\left\|\boldsymbol{G}^{2}_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{\mathcal{X}_{3}}\,+\,\left\|G^{3}_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{\mathcal{X}_{4}}\,\leq\,C(l)\,.

In particular, the sequences
\bigl(f_{\varepsilon}\bigr)_{\varepsilon}
and

\bigl(\boldsymbol{G}_{\varepsilon}\bigr)_{\varepsilon}
, defined in system (
2.3.9
), are uniformly bounded in the space
L^{2}\big([0,T];W^{-1,1}(\Omega)\big)
, thus in
L^{2}\big([0,T];H^{-s}(\Omega)\big)
, for all
s>5/2
.

Proof:

We start by dealing with
\boldsymbol{F}^{1}_{\varepsilon}
. By relations (
2.2.10
) and (
2.2.14
), it is easy to see that

\left\|\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,\chi_{l}\,\frac{\kappa(\vartheta_{\varepsilon})}{\vartheta_{\varepsilon}}\,\nabla_{x}\vartheta_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{2}_{T}(L^{2}+L^{1})}\,\leq\,c(l)\,.

On the other hand, the analysis of the term

\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\chi_{l}\,\frac{s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-s(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}

is based on an analogous decomposition as used in the proof of Lemma
2.3.1
and on uniform bounds of Paragraph
2.2.1.2
: these facts allow us to bound
it in
L^{2}_{T}(L^{3/2}+L^{30/23}+L^{30/29})
.

The bounds for
F^{2}_{\varepsilon}
easily follow from the previous ones and Lemma
2.3.1
(indeed, the analysis for
\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon}

applies also to the terms of the form
\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}
which appear in the definition of
F^{2}_{\varepsilon}
), provided we show that

\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,\left|\chi_{l}\,\nabla_{x}\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\right|\,\leq\,C(l)\,.

The previous bound immediately follows from the equation

\nabla_{x}\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\,=\,\frac{\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}}{\partial_{\varrho}p(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})}\,\left(\varepsilon^{2(m-1)}\,\nabla_{x}F\,+\,\varepsilon^{m}\,\nabla_{x}G\right)\,,

which derives from (
2.1.17
).
Hence, by Proposition
2.1.7
and the definitions given in (
2.1.7
), we get

\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,\left|\chi_{l}\,\nabla_{x}\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\right|\,\leq\,C(l)\,\left(\varepsilon^{2(m-1)-m}+1\right)\,\leq\,C(l)\,.

The bound on
\mathbb{G}^{1}_{\varepsilon}
is an immediate consequence of (
2.2.9
) and (
2.2.2
).

Let us focus now on the term
\boldsymbol{G}^{2}_{\varepsilon}
. The control of the term
\,{}^{t}\mathbb{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla_{x}\chi_{l}
is the same as above. The control of
\chi_{l}\boldsymbol{Y}^{2}_{\varepsilon}
, instead,
gives rise to a bound in
L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2}+L^{5/3})
: this is easily seen once we write

\chi_{l}\,\boldsymbol{Y}^{2}_{\varepsilon}\,=\,\chi_{l}\,\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\,\nabla_{x}G\,+\,\varepsilon^{m-2}\,\chi_{l}\,\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\,\nabla_{x}F

and we use (
2.2.23
) and (
2.1.7
). Finally, we have the equality

\begin{split}\nabla_{x}\chi_{l}\,\left(\frac{Z_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon^{m}}-Y^{3}_{\varepsilon}\right)&=\nabla_{x}\chi_{l}\,\left(\frac{p(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-p(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right)\\
&=\nabla_{x}\chi_{l}\left[\frac{p(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-p(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right]_{\rm{ess}}+\nabla_{x}\chi_{l}\left[\frac{p(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right]_{\rm{res}}.\end{split}

The second term in the last line is uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{1})
, in view of (
2.2.8
).
For the first term, instead,
we can proceed as in (
2.3.10
).

At this point, we switch our attention to the term
G^{3}_{\varepsilon}
, whose analysis is more involved. By definition, we have

\begin{split}\chi_{l}\,Y^{3}_{\varepsilon}&:=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,\chi_{l}\,\left(\mathcal{A}\,\frac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,+\mathcal{B}\,\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\frac{s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-s(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,-\,\mathcal{B}\,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\Sigma_{\varepsilon}\,-\,\frac{p(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-p(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right)\\
&=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,\chi_{l}\,\left(\mathcal{A}\,\frac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,+\mathcal{B}\,\frac{s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-s(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,-\frac{p(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-p(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right)\\
&\qquad-\,\mathcal{B}\,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2m}}\,\chi_{l}\,\Sigma_{\varepsilon}\,+\,\mathcal{B}\,\chi_{l}\,\left(\frac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right)\,\frac{s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-s(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,,\end{split}

with
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
(see definition (
2.3.4
) above) such that

\mathcal{A}\,+\,\mathcal{B}\,\partial_{\varrho}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,=\,\partial_{\varrho}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\mathcal{B}\,\partial_{\vartheta}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,=\,\partial_{\vartheta}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,.

Next, we use a Taylor expansion to write

\begin{split}s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-s(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})&=s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-s(1,\overline{\vartheta})+s(1,\overline{\vartheta})-s(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})\\
&=\partial_{\varrho}\,s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,(\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1)+\partial_{\vartheta}\,s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,(\vartheta_{\varepsilon}-\overline{\vartheta})+\frac{1}{2}\,{\rm Hess}(s)[\xi_{1},\eta_{1}]\begin{pmatrix}\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1\\
\vartheta_{\varepsilon}-\overline{\vartheta}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1\\
\vartheta_{\varepsilon}-\overline{\vartheta}\end{pmatrix}\\
&+\partial_{\varrho}\,s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,(1-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})+\frac{1}{2}\,\partial_{\varrho\varrho}^{2}\,s(\xi_{2},\overline{\vartheta})\,(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}-1)^{2}\\
&=\partial_{\varrho}\,s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,(\varrho_{\varepsilon}-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})+\partial_{\vartheta}\,s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,(\vartheta_{\varepsilon}-\overline{\vartheta})\\
&+\frac{1}{2}\left({\rm Hess}(s)[\xi_{1},\eta_{1}]\begin{pmatrix}\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1\\
\vartheta_{\varepsilon}-\overline{\vartheta}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1\\
\vartheta_{\varepsilon}-\overline{\vartheta}\end{pmatrix}+\partial_{\varrho\varrho}^{2}\,s(\xi_{2},\overline{\vartheta})\,(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}-1)^{2}\right)\,,\end{split}

where
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\eta_{1}
are suitable points between
1
and
\varrho_{\varepsilon}
,
1
and
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}
,
\overline{\vartheta}
and
\vartheta_{\varepsilon}
respectively, and
we have denoted by
{\rm Hess}(s)[\xi,\eta]
the Hessian matrix of the function
s
with respect to its variables
\big(\varrho,\vartheta\big)
, computed at the point
(\xi,\eta)
.
Analogously, for the pressure term we have

\begin{split}p(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-p(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})&=\partial_{\varrho}\,p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,(\varrho_{\varepsilon}-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})+\partial_{\vartheta}\,p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,(\vartheta_{\varepsilon}-\overline{\vartheta})\\
&+\frac{1}{2}\left({\rm Hess}(p)[\xi_{3},\eta_{2}]\begin{pmatrix}\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1\\
\vartheta_{\varepsilon}-\overline{\vartheta}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1\\
\vartheta_{\varepsilon}-\overline{\vartheta}\end{pmatrix}+\partial_{\varrho\varrho}^{2}\,p(\xi_{4},\overline{\vartheta})\,(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}-1)^{2}\right)\,,\end{split}

where
\xi_{3},\xi_{4},\eta_{2}
are still between
1
and
\varrho_{\varepsilon}
,
1
and
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}
,
\overline{\vartheta}
and
\vartheta_{\varepsilon}
respectively.
Using now (
2.3.4
), we find that the first order terms cancel out, and we are left with

\begin{split}\chi_{l}\,Y^{3}_{\varepsilon}&=\frac{\mathcal{B}}{2\varepsilon^{2m}}\,\chi_{l}\,\left(\,{\rm Hess}(s)[\xi_{1},\eta_{1}]\begin{pmatrix}\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1\\
\vartheta_{\varepsilon}-\overline{\vartheta}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1\\
\vartheta_{\varepsilon}-\overline{\vartheta}\end{pmatrix}+\partial_{\varrho\varrho}^{2}\,s(\xi_{2},\overline{\vartheta})\,(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}-1)^{2}\right)\\
&-\,\frac{1}{2\varepsilon^{2m}}\,\chi_{l}\,\left({\rm Hess}(p)[\xi_{3},\eta_{2}]\begin{pmatrix}\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1\\
\vartheta_{\varepsilon}-\overline{\vartheta}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1\\
\vartheta_{\varepsilon}-\overline{\vartheta}\end{pmatrix}+\partial_{\varrho\varrho}^{2}\,p(\xi_{4},\overline{\vartheta})\,(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}-1)^{2}\right)\\
&-\,\frac{\mathcal{B}}{\varepsilon^{2m}}\,\chi_{l}\,\Sigma_{\varepsilon}\,+\,\mathcal{B}\,\chi_{l}\,\left(\frac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right)\,\frac{s(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\vartheta_{\varepsilon})-s(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\vartheta})}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,.\end{split}

Thanks to the uniform bounds established in Paragraph
2.2.1.2
and the decomposition into essential and residual parts, the claimed control in the space
\mathcal{X}_{4}
follows.

2.3.1.3
Regularization and description of the oscillations

Following
[
41
]
and
[
40
]
(see also
[
27
]
), it is convenient to reformulate our problem (
NSF

ε

1

) to (
NSF

ε

4

), supplemented with complete slip boundary
conditions (
2.1.8
) and (
2.1.9
), in a completely equivalent way, in the domain

\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}\,:=\,{B}_{L_{\varepsilon}}(0)\times\mathbb{T}^{1}\,,\qquad\qquad\mbox{ with }\qquad\mathbb{T}^{1}\,:=\,[-1,1]/\sim\,,

where
\sim
denotes the equivalence relation which identifies
-1
and
1
.
For this, it is enough to extend
\varrho_{\varepsilon}
,
\vartheta_{\varepsilon}
, and
\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{h}
as even functions with respect to
x^{3}
,
u_{\varepsilon}^{3}
and
G
as odd functions.

Correspondingly, we consider also the wave system (
2.3.9
) to be satisfied in the new domain
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}
. It goes without saying that the uniform bounds
established above hold true also when replacing
\Omega
with
\widetilde{\Omega}
, where we have set

\widetilde{\Omega}\,:=\,\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{1}\,.

Notice that the wave speed in (
2.3.9
) is proportional to
\varepsilon^{-m}
, while, in view of assumption (
2.1.2
), the domains
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}
are expanding at speed proportional
to
\varepsilon^{-m-\delta}
, for some
\delta>0
.
Therefore, no interactions of the acoustic-Poincaré waves with the boundary of
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}
take place (see also Remark
2.1.3
in this respect),
for any finite time
T>0
and sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
.
Thanks to this fact and the spatial localisation given by the cut-off function
\chi_{l}
, we can assume that (
2.3.9
) is satisfied
(still in a weak sense) on the whole
\widetilde{\Omega}
.

Now, for any
M\in\mathbb{N}
let us consider the low-frequency cut-off operator
{S}_{M}
of a Littlewood-Paley decomposition, as introduced in (
A.1.1
). We define

\Lambda_{\varepsilon,M}={S}_{M}\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\qquad\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\qquad\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}={S}_{M}\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon}\,.

The following result holds true.
Recall that we are omitting from the notation the dependence of all quantities on
l>0
, due to multiplication by the cut-off function
\chi_{l}
fixed above.

Proposition 2.3.3

For any
T>0
, we have the following convergence properties, in the limit
M\rightarrow+\infty
:

\begin{split}&\sup_{0<\varepsilon\leq 1}\,\left\|\Lambda_{\varepsilon}-\Lambda_{\varepsilon,M}\right\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T];H^{s})}\longrightarrow 0\quad\forall s<-3/2-\delta\\
&\sup_{0<\varepsilon\leq 1}\,\left\|\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon}-\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}\right\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T];H^{s})}\longrightarrow 0\quad\forall s<-4/5-\delta\,,\end{split}

(2.3.11)

for any
\delta>0
.
Moreover, for any
M>0
, the couple
(\Lambda_{\varepsilon,M},\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M})
satisfies the approximate wave equations

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\varepsilon^{m}\,\partial_{t}\Lambda_{\varepsilon,M}\,+\,\mathcal{A}\,{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}\,=\,\varepsilon^{m}\,f_{\varepsilon,M}\\[4.30554pt]
\varepsilon^{m}\,\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}\,+\varepsilon^{m-1}\,e_{3}\times\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}+\,\nabla_{x}\Lambda_{\varepsilon,M}\,=\,\varepsilon^{m}\,\boldsymbol{G}_{\varepsilon,M}\,,\end{array}\right.

(2.3.12)

where
(f_{\varepsilon,M})_{\varepsilon}
and
(\boldsymbol{G}_{\varepsilon,M})_{\varepsilon}
are families of smooth (in the space variables) functions satisfying, for any
s\geq 0
, the uniform bounds

\sup_{0<\varepsilon\leq 1}\,\left\|f_{\varepsilon,M}\right\|_{L^{2}([0,T];H^{s})}\,+\,\sup_{0<\varepsilon\leq 1}\,\left\|\boldsymbol{G}_{\varepsilon,M}\right\|_{L^{2}([0,T];H^{s})}\,\leq\,C(l,s,M)\,,

(2.3.13)

where the constant
C(l,s,M)
depends on the fixed values of
l>0
,
s\geq 0
and
M>0
, but not on
\varepsilon>0
.

Proof:

Thanks to Lemma
A.5
, properties (
2.3.11
) are straightforward consequences of the uniform bounds establish in Subsection
2.3.1.2
.

Next, applying the operator
{S}_{M}
to (
2.3.9
) immediately gives us system (
2.3.12
), where we have set

f_{\varepsilon,M}:={S}_{M}\left({\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{F}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\,+\,F^{2}_{\varepsilon}\right)\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\boldsymbol{G}_{\varepsilon,M}:={S}_{M}\left({\rm div}\,\mathbb{G}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\,+\,\boldsymbol{G}^{2}_{\varepsilon}\,+\,\nabla_{x}G^{3}_{\varepsilon}\right)\,.

Thanks to Lemma
2.3.2
and (
A.1.2
) (and also Lemma
A.5
), it is easy to verify inequality (
2.3.13
).

We also have an important decomposition for the approximated velocity fields and their
{\rm curl}\,
.

Proposition 2.3.4

For any
M>0
and any
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
, the following decompositions hold true:

\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}\,=\,\varepsilon^{m}\boldsymbol{t}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}+\boldsymbol{t}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad{\rm curl}\,\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}=\varepsilon^{m}\boldsymbol{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}+\boldsymbol{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}\,,

where, for any
T>0
and
s\geq 0
, one has

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{t}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}\right\|_{L^{2}([0,T];H^{s})}+\left\|\boldsymbol{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}\right\|_{L^{2}([0,T];H^{s})}\leq C(l,s,M)

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{t}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}\right\|_{L^{2}([0,T];H^{1})}+\left\|\boldsymbol{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}\right\|_{L^{2}\left([0,T];L^{2}\right)}\leq C(l)\,,

for suitable positive constants
C(l,s,M)
and
C(l)
, which are uniform with respect to
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
.

Proof:

We start by defining

\boldsymbol{t}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}\,:=\,{S}_{M}\left(\chi_{l}\left(\frac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right)\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\right)\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\boldsymbol{t}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}\,:=\,{S}_{M}\left(\chi_{l}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\right)\,.

(2.3.14)

Then, it is apparent that
\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}\,=\,\varepsilon^{m}\boldsymbol{t}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}\,+\,\boldsymbol{t}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}
.
The decomposition of
{\rm curl}\,\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}
is also easy to get, if we set
\boldsymbol{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{j}\,:=\,{\rm curl}\,\boldsymbol{t}_{\varepsilon,M}^{j}
, for
j=1,2
.
We have to prove uniform bounds for all those terms.
But this is an easy verification, thanks to the
L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{5/3}_{\rm loc})
bound on
R_{\varepsilon}
and the
L^{2}_{T}(H^{1}_{\rm loc})
bound on
\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}
, for any fixed time
T>0

(recall the estimates obtained in Subsection
2.2.1
above).

On the one hand, for the estimate

\left\|\boldsymbol{t}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}\right\|_{L_{T}^{2}(H^{s})}+\left\|\boldsymbol{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}\right\|_{L_{T}^{2}(H^{s})}\leq C(l,s,M)\,,

it is sufficient to employ relation (
A.1.2
) and Lemma
A.5
.

On the other hand, we have

\begin{split}\left\|S_{M}\left(\chi_{l}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}^{2}&\leq C\sum_{j=-1}^{M-1}2^{2j}\left\|\Delta_{j}\left(\chi_{l}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\\
&\leq C\|\chi_{l}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+C\sum_{j=0}^{M-1}\left\|\Delta_{j}\nabla_{x}\left(\chi_{l}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C(l)\,,\end{split}

and the estimate for
\boldsymbol{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}
follows from analogous computations. This completes the proof.

2.3.2
Convergence of the non-linear convective term

In this subsection we show convergence of the convective term, by using a compensated compactness argument.
Namely, we manipulate this term, by performing algebraic computations on the wave system formulated above. As a consequence, we derive two key pieces of information: on the one hand, we see that some non-linear terms are small remainders (in the sense specified by relations (
2.3.15
)
and (
2.3.17
) below); on the other hand, we derive a compactness property for a new quantity, called
\gamma_{\varepsilon,M}
.

The first step is to reduce the study to the case of smooth vector fields
\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}
.

Lemma 2.3.5

Let
T>0
. For any
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in C_{c}^{\infty}\bigl([0,T[\,\times\widetilde{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{3}\bigr)
, we have

\lim_{M\rightarrow+\infty}\limsup_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}\left|\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}}\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}}\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}\otimes\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\right|=0\,.

Proof:

Let
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in C_{c}^{\infty}\bigl(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\widetilde{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{3}\bigr)
, with
{\rm Supp}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}\subset[0,T]\times K
, for some compact set
K\subset\widetilde{\Omega}
.
Then, we take
l>0
in (
2.3.7
) so large that
K\subset\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}_{l}\,:=\,B_{l}(0)\times\mathbb{T}^{1}
.
Therefore, using (
2.2.22
), we get

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}}\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,=\,\int_{0}^{T}\int_{K}(\chi_{l}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,+\varepsilon^{m}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{K}R_{\varepsilon}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,.

As a consequence of the uniform bounds
\big(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\big)_{\varepsilon}\subset L^{2}_{T}(L^{6}_{\rm loc})
and
\big(R_{\varepsilon}\big)_{\varepsilon}\subset L^{\infty}_{T}(L_{\rm loc}^{5/3})

(recall (
2.2.23
) above), the second integral in the right-hand side is of order
\varepsilon^{m}
. As for the first one, using (
2.3.14
), we can write

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{K}(\chi_{l}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,=\,\int_{0}^{T}\int_{K}\boldsymbol{t}^{2}_{\varepsilon,M}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{K}\,({\rm Id}\,-{S}_{M})(\chi_{l}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,.

Observe that, in view of characterisation (
A.1.2
), one has the property (see also Lemma
A.5
)

\left\|({\rm Id}\,-{S}_{M})(\chi_{l}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})\right\|_{L_{T}^{2}(L^{2})}\,\leq\,C\,2^{-M}\,\left\|\nabla_{x}(\chi_{l}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})\right\|_{L_{T}^{2}(L^{2})}\,\leq\,C(l)\,2^{-M}\,.

Therefore, it is enough to consider the first term in the right-hand side of the last relation: we have

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{K}\boldsymbol{t}^{2}_{\varepsilon,M}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,=\,\int_{0}^{T}\int_{K}\boldsymbol{t}^{2}_{\varepsilon,M}\otimes\boldsymbol{t}^{2}_{\varepsilon,M}:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,+\,\int_{0}^{T}\int_{K}\,\boldsymbol{t}^{2}_{\varepsilon,M}\otimes({\rm Id}\,-{S}_{M})(\chi_{l}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}):\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,,

where, for the same reason as before, we gather that

\lim_{M\rightarrow+\infty}\limsup_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}\left|\int_{0}^{T}\int_{K}\boldsymbol{t}^{2}_{\varepsilon,M}\otimes({\rm Id}\,-{S}_{M})(\chi_{l}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}):\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}\right|=0\,.

It remains us to consider the integral

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{K}\boldsymbol{t}^{2}_{\varepsilon,M}\otimes\boldsymbol{t}^{2}_{\varepsilon,M}:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,=\,\int_{0}^{T}\int_{K}\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}\otimes\boldsymbol{t}^{2}_{\varepsilon,M}:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}-\varepsilon^{m}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{K}\boldsymbol{t}^{1}_{\varepsilon,M}\otimes\boldsymbol{t}^{2}_{\varepsilon,M}:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,,

where we notice that, owing to Proposition
2.3.4
, the latter term in the right-hand side is of order
\varepsilon^{m}
, so it vanishes at the limit.
As a last step, we write

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{K}\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}\otimes\boldsymbol{t}^{2}_{\varepsilon,M}:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,=\,\int_{0}^{T}\int_{K}\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}\otimes\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,-\,\varepsilon^{m}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{K}\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}\otimes\boldsymbol{t}^{1}_{\varepsilon,M}:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,.

Using Lemma
2.3.1
together with Bernstein’s inequalities of Lemma
A.2
, we see that the latter integral in the right-hand side is of order
\varepsilon^{m}
.
This concludes the proof of the lemma.

From now on, in order to avoid the appearance of (irrelevant) multiplicative constants everywhere, we suppose that the torus
\mathbb{T}^{1}
has been normalised so that its Lebesgue measure is equal to
1
.

In view of the previous lemma and of Proposition
2.2.7
, for any test-function

\boldsymbol{\psi}\in C_{c}^{\infty}\big([0,T[\,\times\widetilde{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{3}\big)\qquad\qquad\mbox{ such that }\qquad{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}=0\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\partial_{3}\boldsymbol{\psi}=0\,,

(2.3.15)

we have to pass to the limit in the term

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}}\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}\otimes\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,

\displaystyle=\,\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}}{\rm div}\,\left(\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}\otimes\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\,.

Notice that the integration by parts above is well-justified, since all the quantities inside the integrals are smooth. At this point, we observe that,
resorting to the notation in (
OSC
) presented in the introductory part, we can write

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}}{\rm div}\,\left(\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}\otimes\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\,=\,\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}+\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\,,

where we have defined the terms

\mathcal{T}^{1}_{\varepsilon,M}\,:=\,{\rm div}_{h}\left(\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\otimes\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\right)\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\mathcal{T}^{2}_{\varepsilon,M}\,:=\,{\rm div}_{h}\left(\langle\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\otimes\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\right)\,.

(2.3.16)

So, it is enough to focus on each of them separately.
For notational convenience, from now on we will generically denote by
\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}
any remainder term, that is any term satisfying the property

\lim_{M\rightarrow+\infty}\limsup_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}\left|\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}}\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\right|=0\,,

(2.3.17)

for all test functions
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in C_{c}^{\infty}\bigl([0,T[\,\times\widetilde{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{3}\bigr)
as in (
2.3.15
).

2.3.2.1
The analysis of the
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}
term

We start by dealing with
\mathcal{T}^{1}_{\varepsilon,M}
. Standard computations give

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}\,

\displaystyle=\,{\rm div}_{h}\left(\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\otimes\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\right)={\rm div}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle+\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\cdot\nabla_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle

(2.3.18)

\displaystyle={\rm div}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\,\nabla_{h}\left(\left|\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\right|^{2}\right)+{\rm curl}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle^{\perp}\,.

Notice that we can forget about the second term, because it is a perfect gradient and we are testing against divergence-free test functions.
For the first term, we take advantage of system (
2.3.12
): averaging the first equation with respect to
x^{3}
and multiplying it by
\langle\boldsymbol{W}^{h}_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle
, we arrive at

{\rm div}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,=\,-\frac{\varepsilon^{m}}{\mathcal{A}}\partial_{t}\langle\Lambda_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle+\frac{\varepsilon^{m}}{\mathcal{A}}\langle f_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,=\,\frac{\varepsilon^{m}}{\mathcal{A}}\langle\Lambda_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\partial_{t}\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle+\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}\,.

We remark that the term presenting the total derivative in time is in fact a remainder.
We use now the horizontal part of (
2.3.12
), where we take the vertical average and then multiply by
\langle\Lambda_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle
: we gather

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon^{m}}{\mathcal{A}}\langle\Lambda_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\partial_{t}\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\mathcal{A}}\langle\Lambda_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\nabla_{h}\langle\Lambda_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle+\frac{\varepsilon^{m}}{\mathcal{A}}\langle\Lambda_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{G}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle-\frac{\varepsilon^{m-1}}{\mathcal{A}}\langle\Lambda_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle^{\perp}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\varepsilon^{m-1}}{\mathcal{A}}\langle\Lambda_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle^{\perp}-\frac{1}{2\mathcal{A}}\nabla_{h}\left(\left|\langle\Lambda_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\right|^{2}\right)+\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\varepsilon^{m-1}}{\mathcal{A}}\langle\Lambda_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle^{\perp}+\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}\,,

where we repeatedly exploited the properties proved in Proposition
2.3.3
and we included in the remainder term also the perfect gradient.
Inserting this relation into (
2.3.18
), we find

\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}=\gamma_{\varepsilon,M}\,\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle^{\perp}+\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}\,,\qquad\qquad\mbox{ with }\qquad\gamma_{\varepsilon,M}:={\rm curl}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,-\,\frac{\varepsilon^{m-1}}{\mathcal{A}}\langle\Lambda_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\,.

We observe that, for passing to the limit in
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}
, there is no other way than finding some strong convergence property for
\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}
.
Such a property is in fact hidden in the structure of the wave system (
2.3.12
): in order to exploit it, some work on the term
\gamma_{\varepsilon,M}
is needed.
We start by rewriting the vertical average of the first equation in (
2.3.12
) as

\frac{\varepsilon^{2m-1}}{\mathcal{A}}\,\partial_{t}\langle\Lambda_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\,+\,\varepsilon^{m-1}{\rm div}\,_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,=\,\frac{\varepsilon^{2m-1}}{\mathcal{A}}\,\langle f_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,.

On the other hand, taking the vertical average of the horizontal components of (
2.3.12
) and then applying
{\rm curl}_{h}
, we obtain the relation

\varepsilon^{m}\,\partial_{t}{\rm curl}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,+\varepsilon^{m-1}\,{\rm div}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,=\,\varepsilon^{m}{\rm curl}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{G}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,.

Summing up the last two equations, we discover that

\partial_{t}\gamma_{\varepsilon,M}\,=\,{\rm curl}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{G}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,-\,\frac{\varepsilon^{m-1}}{\mathcal{A}}\,\langle f_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,.

(2.3.19)

Thanks to estimate (
2.3.13
) in Proposition
2.3.3
, we discover that (for any
M>0
fixed) the family

\left(\partial_{t}\,\gamma_{\varepsilon,M}\right)_{\varepsilon}

is uniformly bounded (with respect to
\varepsilon
) in e.g.
L_{T}^{2}(L^{2})
.
On the other hand, thanks to Lemma
2.3.1
and Sobolev embeddings, we have that (for any
M>0
fixed)
the sequence
(\gamma_{\varepsilon,M})_{\varepsilon}
is uniformly bounded (with respect to
\varepsilon
) in the space
L_{T}^{2}(H^{1})
.
Since the embedding
H_{\rm loc}^{1}\hookrightarrow L_{\rm loc}^{2}
is compact, the Aubin-Lions Theorem (see again the Appendix
B
) implies that, for any
M>0
fixed, the family
(\gamma_{\varepsilon,M})_{\varepsilon}
is compact
in
L_{T}^{2}(L_{\rm loc}^{2})
. Then, it converges strongly (up to extracting a subsequence) to a tempered distribution
\gamma_{M}
in the same space.
Of course, by definition of
\gamma_{\varepsilon,M}
(and whenever
m>1
), this tells us that also
\big({\rm curl}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\big)_{\varepsilon}
is compact in
L^{2}_{T}(L^{2}_{\rm loc})
.

Now, we have that
\gamma_{\varepsilon,M}
converges strongly to
\gamma_{M}
in
L_{T}^{2}(L_{\rm loc}^{2})
and
\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle
converges weakly to

\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{M}^{h}\rangle
in
L_{T}^{2}(L_{\rm loc}^{2})
(owing to Proposition
2.3.4
, for instance). Then, we deduce that

\gamma_{\varepsilon,M}\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle^{\perp}\longrightarrow\gamma_{M}\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{M}^{h}\rangle^{\perp}\qquad\text{ in }\qquad\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\big(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)\,.

Observe that, by definition of
\gamma_{\varepsilon,M}
, we must have
\gamma_{M}={\rm curl}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{M}^{h}\rangle
. On the other hand, by Proposition
2.3.4
and (
2.3.14
),
we know that
\langle\boldsymbol{W}_{M}^{h}\rangle=\langle{S}_{M}(\chi_{l}\boldsymbol{U}^{h})\rangle
.

In the end, we have proved that, for any
T>0
and any test-function
\boldsymbol{\psi}
as in (
2.3.15
),
one has the convergence (at any
M\in\mathbb{N}
fixed, when
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
)

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\,\,{\rm d}x^{h}\,{\rm d}t\,\longrightarrow\,\int^{T}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}{\rm curl}_{h}\langle{S}_{M}(\chi_{l}\boldsymbol{U}^{h})\rangle\;\langle{S}_{M}\big(\chi_{l}(\boldsymbol{U}^{h})^{\perp}\big)\rangle\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\,\,{\rm d}x^{h}\,{\rm d}t\,.

(2.3.20)

2.3.2.2
Dealing with the term
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}

Let us now consider the term
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}
, defined in (
2.3.16
). By the same computation as above, we infer that

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}\,

\displaystyle=\,\langle{\rm div}_{h}(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h})\;\;\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\,\langle\nabla_{h}|\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}|^{2}\rangle+\langle{\rm curl}_{h}\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\,\left(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}\rangle\,.

(2.3.21)

Let us now introduce now the quantities

\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\,:=\,(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h})^{\perp}-\partial_{3}^{-1}\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\accentset{\approx}{\omega}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}\,:=\,{\rm curl}_{h}\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\,.

Then we can write

\left({\rm curl}\,\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}\right)^{h}\,=\,\partial_{3}\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\left({\rm curl}\,\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}\right)^{3}\,=\,\accentset{\approx}{\omega}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}\,.

In addition, from the momentum equation in (
2.3.12
), where we take the mean-free part and then the
{\rm curl}\,
, we deduce the equations

\begin{cases}\varepsilon^{m}\partial_{t}\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}-\varepsilon^{m-1}\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}=\varepsilon^{m}\left(\partial_{3}^{-1}{\rm curl}\,\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{G}}_{\varepsilon,M}\right)^{h}\\[4.30554pt]
\varepsilon^{m}\partial_{t}\accentset{\approx}{\omega}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}+\varepsilon^{m-1}{\rm div}_{h}\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}=\varepsilon^{m}\,{\rm curl}_{h}\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{G}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\,.\end{cases}

(2.3.22)

Making use of the relations above and of Propositions
2.3.3
and
2.3.4
, we get

\begin{split}{\rm curl}_{h}\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\;\left(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}&=\accentset{\approx}{\omega}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}\left(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}\,=\,\varepsilon\partial_{t}\!\left(\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}\accentset{\approx}{\omega}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}-\varepsilon\accentset{\approx}{\omega}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}\left(\left(\partial_{3}^{-1}{\rm curl}\,\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{G}}_{\varepsilon,M}\right)^{h}\right)^{\perp}\\
&=-\varepsilon\left(\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}\partial_{t}\accentset{\approx}{\omega}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}+\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}=\left(\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}\,{\rm div}_{h}\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}+\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}\,.\end{split}

Hence, including also the gradient term into the remainders, from (
2.3.21
) we arrive at

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}\,

\displaystyle=\,\langle{\rm div}_{h}\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\,\left(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}+\left(\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}\right)\rangle+\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}

\displaystyle=\,\langle{\rm div}\,\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}\left(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}+\left(\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}\right)\rangle-\langle\partial_{3}\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}\left(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}+\left(\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}\right)\rangle+\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}\,.

The second term on the right-hand side of the last line is actually another remainder. Indeed, using the definition of the function
\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}
and the fact
that the test function
\boldsymbol{\psi}
does not depend on
x^{3}
, one has

\begin{split}\partial_{3}\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}\left(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}+\left(\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}\right)&=\partial_{3}\left(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}\left(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}+\left(\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}\right)\right)-\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}\,\partial_{3}\left(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}+\left(\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}\right)\\
&=\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{h}\left|\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}\right|^{2}=\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}\,.\end{split}

As for the first term, instead, we use the first equation in (
2.3.12
) to obtain

\begin{split}{\rm div}\,\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}\left(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}+\left(\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}\right)&=-\frac{\varepsilon^{m}}{\mathcal{A}}\partial_{t}\accentset{\approx}{\Lambda}_{\varepsilon,M}\left(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}+\left(\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}\right)+\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}\\
&=\frac{\varepsilon^{m}}{\mathcal{A}}\accentset{\approx}{\Lambda}_{\varepsilon,M}\,\partial_{t}\left(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}+\left(\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}\right)+\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}\,.\end{split}

Now, equations (
2.3.12
) and (
2.3.22
) immediately yield that

\frac{\varepsilon^{m}}{\mathcal{A}}\accentset{\approx}{\Lambda}_{\varepsilon,M}\,\partial_{t}\left(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}+\left(\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}\right)=\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}-\frac{1}{\mathcal{A}}\accentset{\approx}{\Lambda}_{\varepsilon,M}\,\nabla_{h}\left(\accentset{\approx}{\Lambda}_{\varepsilon,M}\right)=\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}-\frac{1}{2\mathcal{A}}\nabla_{h}\left|\accentset{\approx}{\Lambda}_{\varepsilon,M}\right|^{2}=\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}\,.

This relation finally implies that
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}\,=\,\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}
is a remainder, in the sense of relation (
2.3.17
):
for any
T>0
and any test-function
\boldsymbol{\psi}
as in (
2.3.15
),
one has the convergence
(at any
M\in\mathbb{N}
fixed, when
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
)

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\,\,{\rm d}x^{h}\,{\rm d}t\,\longrightarrow\,0\,.

(2.3.23)

Remark 2.3.6

Due to the presence of the term
\boldsymbol{Y}^{2}_{\varepsilon}
in (
2.3.5
), the choice
m\geq 2
is fundamental.
However, as soon as
F=0
, our analysis applies also in the case when
1<m<2
.

2.3.3
The limit dynamics

With the convergences established in (
2.2.25
) to (
2.2.27
) and in Subsection
2.3.2
, we can pass to the limit in equation (
2.1.28
).
Since all the integrals will be made on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
(in view of the choice of the test functions in (
2.3.15
) above), we can safely come back to the notation on
\Omega
instead of
\widetilde{\Omega}
.

To begin with, we take a test-function
\boldsymbol{\psi}
as in (
2.3.15
), specifically

\boldsymbol{\psi}=\big(\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\phi,0\big)\,,\qquad\qquad\mbox{ with }\qquad\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}\big([0,T[\,\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)\,,\quad\phi=\phi(t,x^{h})\,.

For such a
\boldsymbol{\psi}
, all the gradient terms vanish identically, as well as all the contributions
due to the vertical component of the equation. Hence, after using also (
2.1.17
), equation (
2.1.28
) becomes

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\Omega}

\displaystyle\left(-\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{h}\cdot\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}-\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{h}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{h}:\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\varrho_{\varepsilon}\big(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{h}\big)^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\right)\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t

(2.3.24)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\Omega}\left(-\mathbb{S}(\vartheta_{\varepsilon},\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}}):\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}(\varrho_{\varepsilon}-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})\,\nabla_{x}F\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\right)\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t+\int_{\Omega}\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}(0,\cdot)\,{\rm d}x\,.

Making use of the uniform bounds of Subsection
2.2.1
, we can pass to the limit in the
\partial_{t}
term, in the viscosity term and in the centrifugal force.
Moreover, our assumptions imply that
\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
in
L_{\rm loc}^{2}
.

Let us consider now the Coriolis term. We can write

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\varrho_{\varepsilon}\big(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{h}\big)^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\,

\displaystyle=\,\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\langle\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{h}\rangle\cdot\nabla_{h}\phi\,=\,-\varepsilon^{m-1}\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\langle R_{\varepsilon}\rangle\,\partial_{t}\phi\,-\,\varepsilon^{m-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\langle R_{0,\varepsilon}\rangle\,\phi(0,\cdot)\,,

which of course converges to
0
when
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
. Notice that the second equality derives from the mass equation (
2.1.27
), tested against
\phi
: namely,

-\varepsilon^{m}\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\langle\frac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\rangle\,\partial_{t}\phi\,-\,\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\langle\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{h}\rangle\cdot\nabla_{h}\phi\,=\,\varepsilon^{m}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\langle\frac{\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}-1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\rangle\,\phi(0,\cdot)\,.

It remains to deal with the convective term
\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{h}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{h}
. For this, we take advantage of
Lemma
2.3.5
and relations (
2.3.20
) and (
2.3.23
).
Next, we remark that, since
\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\in L^{2}_{T}(H_{\rm loc}^{1})
by (
2.2.27
), from (
A.1.3
) we gather the strong convergence

S_{M}(\chi_{l}\boldsymbol{U}^{h})\longrightarrow\chi_{l}\boldsymbol{U}^{h}
in
L_{T}^{2}(H^{s})
for any
s<1
and any
l>0
fixed, in the limit for
M\rightarrow+\infty
.
Therefore, in the term on the right-hand side of (
2.3.20
), we can perform equalities (
2.3.18
) backwards, and then pass to the limit also for
M\rightarrow+\infty
.
Using that
\chi_{l}\equiv 1
on
{\rm Supp}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}
by construction, we finally get the convergence (for
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
)

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{h}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{h}:\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\,\longrightarrow\,\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\otimes\boldsymbol{U}^{h}:\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\,.

In the end, letting
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
in (
2.3.24
), we may infer that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\cdot\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}+\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\otimes\boldsymbol{U}^{h}:\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\right)\,\,{\rm d}x^{h}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\mu(\overline{\vartheta})\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{U}^{h}:\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}-\delta_{2}(m)\langle\varrho^{(1)}\rangle\nabla_{h}F\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\right)\,\,{\rm d}x^{h}\,{\rm d}t-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{h}\rangle\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}(0,\cdot)\,{\rm d}x^{h}\,,

where
\delta_{2}(m)=1
if
m=2
,
\delta_{2}(m)=0
otherwise. At this point, Remark
2.2.8
applied to the case
m=2
yields the equality

\partial_{\varrho}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\nabla_{h}\langle\widetilde{r}\rangle\,=\,\nabla_{h}F
. Therefore, keeping in mind that
R=\varrho^{(1)}+\widetilde{r}
, we get

\langle\varrho^{(1)}\rangle\nabla_{h}F\,=\,\langle R\rangle\nabla_{h}F\,-\,\langle\widetilde{r}\rangle\nabla_{h}F\,=\,\langle R\rangle\nabla_{h}F\,-\,\frac{\partial_{\varrho}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})}{2}\,\nabla_{h}\left|\langle\widetilde{r}\rangle\right|^{2}\,.

Of course, the perfect gradient disappears from the weak formulation. Using this observation in the target momentum equation written above, we finally deduce (
2.1.34
).
This completes the proof of Theorem
2.1.10
, in the case when
m\geq 2
and
F\neq 0
.

When
m>1
and
F=0
, most of the arguments above still apply. We refer to the next section for more details.

2.4
Proof of the convergence in the case when
F=0

In the present section we prove the convergence result in the case
F=0
. For the sake of brevity, we focus on the case
m=1
, completing in this way the proof to Theorem
2.1.12
.
The case
m>1
follows by a similar analysis, using at the end the compensated compactness argument depicted in Subsection
2.3.2
(recall also Remark
2.3.6
above).

2.4.1
Analysis of the acoustic-Poincaré waves

We start by remarking that system (
NSF

ε

1

) to (
NSF

ε

4

) can be recasted in the form (
2.3.5
), with
m=1
: with the same notation introduced in Paragraph
2.3.1.1
,
and after setting
X_{\varepsilon}\,:=\,{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\,+\,X^{2}_{\varepsilon}
and
\boldsymbol{Y}_{\varepsilon}\,:=\,{\rm div}\,\mathbb{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\,+\,\boldsymbol{Y}^{2}_{\varepsilon}\,+\,\nabla_{x}Y^{3}_{\varepsilon}
,
we have

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\varepsilon\,\partial_{t}Z_{\varepsilon}\,+\,\mathcal{A}\,{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\,=\,\varepsilon\,X_{\varepsilon}\\[4.30554pt]
\varepsilon\,\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\,+\,\nabla_{x}Z_{\varepsilon}\,+\,\,\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\times\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\,=\,\varepsilon\,\boldsymbol{Y}_{\varepsilon}\,,\qquad\qquad\big(\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\big)_{|\partial\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\,=\,0\,,\end{array}\right.

(2.4.1)

where
\bigl(Z_{\varepsilon}\bigr)_{\varepsilon}
and
\bigl(\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\bigr)_{\varepsilon}
are defined as in Paragraph
2.3.1.1
.
This system is supplemented with the initial datum
\big(Z_{0,\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{V}_{0,\varepsilon}\big)
, where these two functions are defined as in relation (
2.3.6
) above.

2.4.1.1
Uniform bounds and regularisation

In the next lemma, we establish uniform bounds for
Z_{\varepsilon}
and
\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}
. Its proof is an easy adaptation of the one given in Lemma
2.3.1
, hence omitted. One has
to use the fact that, since
F=0
, all the bounds obtained in the previous sections hold now on the whole
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
, with constants which are uniform in
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
;
therefore, one can abstain from using the cut-off functions
\chi_{l}
.

Lemma 2.4.1

For any
T>0
and all
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
, we have

\sup_{\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]}\|Z_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}((L^{2}+L^{5/3}+L^{1}+\mathcal{M}^{+})(\Omega_{\varepsilon}))}\leq c\,,\quad\quad\sup_{\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]}\|\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}_{T}((L^{2}+L^{30/23})(\Omega_{\varepsilon}))}\leq c\,.

Now, we state the analogous of Lemma
2.3.2
for
m=1
and
F=0
.

Lemma 2.4.2

For
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
, let us introduce the following spaces:

(i)

\mathcal{X}^{\varepsilon}_{1}\,:=\,L^{2}_{\rm loc}\Bigl(\mathbb{R}_{+};\big(L^{2}+L^{1}+L^{3/2}+L^{30/23}+L^{30/29}\big)(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\Bigr)
;

(ii)

\mathcal{X}^{\varepsilon}_{2}\,:=\,L^{2}_{\rm loc}\Bigl(\mathbb{R}_{+};\big(L^{2}+L^{1}+L^{4/3}\big)(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\Bigr)
;

(iii)
