\mathcal{X}^{\varepsilon}_{3}\,:=\,L^{\infty}_{\rm loc}\Bigl(\mathbb{R}_{+};\big(L^{2}+L^{5/3}\big)(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\Bigr)
;

(iv)

\mathcal{X}^{\varepsilon}_{4}\,:=\,L^{\infty}_{\rm loc}\Bigl(\mathbb{R}_{+};\big(L^{2}+L^{5/3}+L^{1}+\mathcal{M}^{+}\big)(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\Bigr)
.

Then, one has the following uniform bound, for a constant
C>0
independent of
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
:

\left\|\boldsymbol{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{\mathcal{X}_{1}^{\varepsilon}}\,+\,\left\|X^{2}_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{\mathcal{X}_{1}^{\varepsilon}}\,+\,\left\|\mathbb{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{\mathcal{X}_{2}^{\varepsilon}}\,+\,\left\|\boldsymbol{Y}^{2}_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{\mathcal{X}_{3}^{\varepsilon}}\,+\,\left\|Y^{3}_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{\mathcal{X}_{4}^{\varepsilon}}\,\leq\,C\,.

In particular, one has that the sequences
(X_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
and
(\boldsymbol{Y}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
, defined in system (
2.4.1
), verify
1
1

1

For any
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote by
\lfloor s\rfloor
the entire part of
s
,
i.e. the greatest integer smaller than or equal to
s
.

\left\|X_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{2}_{T}(H^{-\lfloor s\rfloor-1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}))}\,+\,\left\|\boldsymbol{Y}_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{2}_{T}(H^{-\lfloor s\rfloor-1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}))}\,\leq\,C,

for all
s>5/2
and for a constant
C>0
independent of
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
.

Proof:

The proof follows the main lines of the proof of Lemma
2.3.2
. Here, we limit ourselves to point out that we have a slightly better control on

\boldsymbol{Y}^{2}_{\varepsilon}\,=\,\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\,\nabla_{x}G
, whose boundedness in
\mathcal{X}^{\varepsilon}_{3}
follows from (
2.1.7
) and the estimate analogous to (
2.2.23
) for the case
F=0
.

The next step consists in regularising all the terms appearing in (
2.4.1
). Here we have to pay attention: since the domains
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
are bounded,
we cannot use the Littlewood-Paley operators
S_{M}
directly.
Rather than multiplying by a cut-off function
\chi_{l}
as done in the previous section (a procedure which would create more complicated forcing terms in the wave system), we use here the arguments
of Chapter 8 of
[
39
]
(see also
[
44
]
,
[
71
]
), based on finite propagation speed properties for (
2.4.1
).

First of all, similarly to Paragraph
2.3.1.3
above, we extend our domains
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
and
\Omega
by periodicity in the third variable and denote

\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}\,:=\,{B}_{L_{\varepsilon}}(0)\times\mathbb{T}^{1}\qquad\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\qquad\widetilde{\Omega}\,:=\,\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{1}\,.

Thanks to the complete slip boundary conditions (
2.1.8
) and (
2.1.9
), system (
NSF

ε

1

) to (
NSF

ε

4

) can be equivalently reformulated in
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}
. Analogously, the wave system (
2.4.1
) can be recasted in
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}
in a completely equivalent way. From now on, we will focus on
the equations satisfied on the domain
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}
.

Next, we fix a smooth radially decreasing function
\omega\in{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, such that
0\leq\omega\leq 1
,
\omega(x)=0
for
|x|\geq 1
and

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\omega(x)\,{\rm d}x=1
. Next, we define the mollifying kernel
\big(\omega_{M}\big)_{M\in\mathbb{N}}
by the formula

\omega_{M}(x)\,:=\,2^{3M}\,\,\omega\!\left(2^{M}x\right)\qquad\qquad\text{for any}\;\,M\in\mathbb{N}\,\;\text{and any}\;\,x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\,.

Then, for any tempered distribution
\mathfrak{S}=\mathfrak{S}(t,x)
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\widetilde{\Omega}
and any
M\in\mathbb{N}
, we define

\mathfrak{S}_{M}\,:=\,\omega_{M}\,*\,\mathfrak{S}\,,

where the convolution is taken only with respect to the space variables.
Applying the mollifier
\omega_{M}
to (
2.4.1
), we deduce that
Z_{\varepsilon,M}\,:=\,\omega_{M}*Z_{\varepsilon}
and
\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}\,:=\,\omega_{M}*\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}
satisfy the regularised
wave system

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\varepsilon\,\partial_{t}Z_{\varepsilon,M}\,+\,\mathcal{A}\,{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}\,=\,\varepsilon\,X_{\varepsilon,M}\\[4.30554pt]
\varepsilon\,\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}\,+\,\nabla_{x}Z_{\varepsilon,M}\,+\,\,\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\times\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}\,=\,\varepsilon\,\boldsymbol{Y}_{\varepsilon,M}\end{array}\right.

(2.4.2)

in the domain
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varepsilon,M}
, where we have defined

\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varepsilon,M}\,:=\,\left\{x\in\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}\;:\quad{\rm dist}(x,\partial\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varepsilon})\geq 2^{-M}\right\}\,.

(2.4.3)

Since the mollification commutes with standard derivatives, we notice that

X_{\varepsilon,M}\,=\,{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon,M}\,+\,X^{2}_{\varepsilon,M}
and
\boldsymbol{Y}_{\varepsilon,M}\,=\,{\rm div}\,\mathbb{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon,M}\,+\,\boldsymbol{Y}^{2}_{\varepsilon,M}\,+\,\nabla_{x}Y^{3}_{\varepsilon,M}
. Moreover, system (
2.4.2
) is supplemented with the initial data

Z_{0,\varepsilon,M}\,:=\,\omega_{M}*Z_{0,\varepsilon}\qquad\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\qquad\boldsymbol{V}_{0,\varepsilon,M}\,:=\,\omega_{M}*\boldsymbol{V}_{0,\varepsilon}\,.

In accordance with Lemmas
2.4.1
and
2.4.2
, by standard properties of mollifying kernels (see Theorem
B.3
), we get the following properties: for all

k\in\mathbb{N}
, one has

\displaystyle\left\|Z_{\varepsilon,M}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(H^{k}(\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varepsilon,M}))}\,+\,\left\|\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}\right\|_{L^{2}_{T}(H^{k}(\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varepsilon,M}))}\,\leq\,C(k,M)

\displaystyle\left\|X_{\varepsilon,M}\right\|_{L^{2}_{T}(H^{k}(\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varepsilon,M}))}\,+\,\left\|\boldsymbol{Y}_{\varepsilon,M}\right\|_{L^{2}_{T}(H^{k}(\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varepsilon,M}))}\,\leq\,C(k,M)\,,

for some positive constants
C(k,M)
, only depending on the fixed
k
and
M
. Of course, the constants blow up when
M\rightarrow+\infty
, but they are uniform for
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
.

We have the following statement, analogous to Proposition
2.3.4
above. Its proof is also similar, hence omitted. In addition, we notice that the strong convergence follows
from standard properties of the mollifying kernel.

Proposition 2.4.3

For any
M>0
and any
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
, we have

\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}\,=\,\varepsilon\,\boldsymbol{v}_{\varepsilon,M}^{(1)}\,+\,\boldsymbol{v}_{\varepsilon,M}^{(2)}\,,

together with the following bounds: for any
T>0
, any compact set
K\subset\widetilde{\Omega}
and any
s\in\mathbb{N}
, one has
(for
\varepsilon>0
small enough, depending only on
K
)

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{v}_{\varepsilon,M}^{(1)}\right\|_{L^{2}([0,T];H^{s}(K))}\,\leq\,C(K,s,M)\qquad\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\qquad\left\|\boldsymbol{v}_{\varepsilon,M}^{(2)}\right\|_{L^{2}([0,T];H^{1}(K))}\,\leq\,C(K)\,,

for suitable positive constants
C(K,s,M)
and
C(K)
depending only on the quantities in the brackets, but uniform with respect to
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
.

In particular, we deduce the following fact: for any
T>0
and any compact
K\subset\widetilde{\Omega}
, there exist
\varepsilon_{K}>0
and
M_{K}\in\mathbb{N}
such that, for all
\varepsilon\in\,]0,\varepsilon_{K}]
and all
M\geq M_{K}
,
there are positive constants
C(K)
and
C(K,M)
for which

\left\|\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\,-\,\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}\right\|_{L^{2}_{T}(L^{2}(K))}\,\leq\,C(K,M)\,\varepsilon\,+\,C(K)\,2^{-M}\,.

(2.4.4)

2.4.1.2
Finite propagation speed and consequences

In this paragraph we show that, for the scopes of our study, we can safely assume that system (
2.4.2
) is set in the whole
\widetilde{\Omega}
and it is supplemented
with compactly supported initial data and external forces.

Take smooth initial data
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{V}_{0}}
and forces
\mathfrak{X}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Y}}
. Consider, in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\widetilde{\Omega}
, the wave system

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\varepsilon\,\partial_{t}\mathcal{Z}\,+\,\mathcal{A}\,{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{\mathcal{V}}\,=\,\varepsilon\,\mathfrak{X}\\[4.30554pt]
\varepsilon\,\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{V}}\,+\,\nabla_{x}\mathcal{Z}\,+\,\,\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\times\boldsymbol{\mathcal{V}}\,=\,\varepsilon\,\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Y}}\,,\end{array}\right.

(2.4.5)

supplemented with initial data
\mathcal{Z}_{|t=0}\,=\,\mathcal{Z}_{0}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{V}}_{|t=0}\,=\,\boldsymbol{\mathcal{V}_{0}}
.

System (
2.4.5
) is a symmetrizable (in the sense of Friedrichs) first-order
hyperbolic system with a skew-symmetric
0
-th order term. Therefore, classical arguments based on energy methods (see e.g. Chapter 3 of
[
60
]
and Chapter 7 of
[
1
]
) allow to establish the properties of finite propagation speed and domain of dependence for solutions to (
2.4.5
).

Namely, set
\lambda\,:=\,\sqrt{\mathcal{A}}/\varepsilon
to be the propagation speed of acoustic-Poincaré waves.
Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a cylinder included in
\widetilde{\Omega}
.
Then one has the following two properties.

(i)

Domain of dependence
: assume that

{\rm Supp}\,\mathcal{Z}_{0}\,,\;{\rm Supp}\,\boldsymbol{\mathcal{V}_{0}}\,\subset\,\mathfrak{B}\,,\qquad\qquad\qquad{\rm Supp}\,\mathfrak{X}(t)\,,\;{\rm Supp}\,\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Y}}(t)\,\subset\,\mathfrak{B}\quad\mbox{ for a.a. }t\in[0,T]\,;

then the corresponding solution
\big(\mathcal{Z},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{V}}\big)
to (
2.4.5
) is
identically zero
outside the cone

\Big\{(t,x)\in\,]0,T[\,\times\,\widetilde{\Omega}\;:\quad{\rm dist}\big(x,\mathfrak{B}\big)\,<\,\lambda\,t\Big\}\,.

(ii)

Finite propagation speed
: define the set

\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda T}\,:=\,\Big\{x\in\widetilde{\Omega}\;:\quad{\rm dist}\big(x,\mathfrak{B}\big)\,<\,\lambda\,T\Big\}

and assume that

{\rm Supp}\,\mathcal{Z}_{0}\,,\;{\rm Supp}\,\boldsymbol{\mathcal{V}_{0}}\,\subset\,\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda T}\,,\qquad\qquad\qquad{\rm Supp}\,\mathfrak{X}(t)\,,\;{\rm Supp}\,\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Y}}(t)\,\subset\,\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda T}\quad\mbox{ for a.a. }t\in[0,T]\,;

then the solution
\big(\mathcal{Z},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{V}}\big)
is
uniquely determined
by the data inside the cone

\mathcal{C}_{\lambda T}\,:=\,\Big\{(t,x)\in\,]0,T[\,\times\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda T}\;:\quad{\rm dist}\big(x,\partial\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda T}\big)\,>\,\lambda\,t\Big\}\,,

and in particular in the space-time cylinder
\,]0,T[\,\times\,\mathfrak{B}
.

Next, fix any test-function
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in C^{\infty}_{c}\big(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\widetilde{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{3}\big)
, and let
T>0
and the compact set
K\subset\widetilde{\Omega}
be such that
{\rm Supp}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}\subset[0,T[\,\times K
.
Take a cylindrical neighborhood
\mathfrak{B}
of
K
in
\widetilde{\Omega}
. It goes without saying that there exist an
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(\mathfrak{B})\in\,]0,1]
and a
M_{0}=M_{0}(\mathfrak{B})\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\overline{\mathfrak{B}}\,\subset\subset\,\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varepsilon,M}\qquad\qquad\mbox{ for all }\qquad 0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad M\geq M_{0}\,,

(2.4.6)

where the set
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varepsilon,M}
has been defined in (
2.4.3
) above. Take now a cut-off function
\mathfrak{h}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\widetilde{\Omega})
such that

\mathfrak{h}\equiv 1
on
\mathfrak{B}
(and hence on
K
), and solve problem (
2.4.5
) with compactly supported data

\mathcal{Z}_{0}\,=\,\mathfrak{h}\,Z_{0,\varepsilon,M}\,,\qquad\boldsymbol{\mathcal{V}_{0}}\,=\,\mathfrak{h}\,\boldsymbol{V}_{0,\varepsilon,M}\,,\qquad\mathfrak{X}\,=\,\mathfrak{h}\,X_{\varepsilon,M}\,,\qquad\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Y}}\,=\,\mathfrak{h}\,\boldsymbol{Y}_{\varepsilon,M}\,.

We point out that all the data are now localised around the compact set
K
.
Owing to assumption (
2.1.2
), the domains
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varepsilon,M}
are expanding at speed proportional to
\varepsilon^{-(1+\delta)}
, whereas, in view of finite propagation speed,
the support of the solution is expanding at speed proportional to
\varepsilon^{-1}
(keep in mind also Remark
2.1.3
). Thus, thanks to the inclusion (
2.4.6
),
the previous discussion implies that, up to take a smaller
\varepsilon_{0}
, for any
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
the corresponding solution

\big(\mathcal{Z},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{V}}\big)
of (
2.4.5
)
has support inside a cylinder
\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}_{L}\,:=\,B_{L}(0)\times\mathbb{T}^{1}\subset\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varepsilon}
, for some
L=L(T,K,\lambda)>0
, and it must coincide with the solution
\big(Z_{\varepsilon,M},\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}\big)

of (
2.4.2
) on the set
\,]0,T[\,\times\,\mathfrak{B}
, for all
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
and all
M\geq M_{0}
.
In particular, for all
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
and all
M\geq M_{0}
we have

\mathcal{Z}\,\equiv\,Z_{\varepsilon,M}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\boldsymbol{\mathcal{V}}\,\equiv\,\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}\qquad\qquad\mbox{ on }\qquad{\rm Supp}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}\,.

The previous argument shows that, without loss of generality, we can assume that the regularised wave system (
2.4.2
) is verified on the whole
\widetilde{\Omega}
,
with compactly supported initial data and forces, and with solutions supported on some cylinder
\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}_{L}
.
In particular, we can safely work with system (
2.4.2
) and its smooth solutions
\big(Z_{\varepsilon,M},\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}\big)
in the computations below.

2.4.2
Convergence of the convective term

Here we tackle the convergence of the convective term, employing again a compensated compactness argument. The first step is to reduce the study to the case of smooth vector fields
\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}
.
Arguing as in Lemma
2.3.5
, and using Proposition
2.4.3
and property (
2.4.4
), one can easily prove the following approximation result.
Again, the proof is omitted.

Lemma 2.4.4

Let
T>0
. For any
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in C_{c}^{\infty}\bigl([0,T[\,\times\widetilde{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{3}\bigr)
, we have

\lim_{M\rightarrow+\infty}\limsup_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}\left|\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}}\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}}\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}\otimes\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\ dxdt\right|=0\,.

Assume now
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in C_{c}^{\infty}\big([0,T[\,\times\widetilde{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{3}\big)
such that
{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}=0
and
\partial_{3}\boldsymbol{\psi}=0
.
Thanks to the previous lemma, it is enough to pass to the limit in the smooth term

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}}\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}\otimes\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,

\displaystyle=\,\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}}{\rm div}\,\left(\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}\otimes\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\,=\,\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}+\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\,,

where, for simplicity, we agree that the torus
\mathbb{T}^{1}
has been normalised so that its Lebesgue measure is equal to
1
and, analogously to (
2.3.16
), we have introduced the quantities

\mathcal{T}^{1}_{\varepsilon,M}\,:=\,{\rm div}_{h}\left(\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\otimes\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\right)\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\mathcal{T}^{2}_{\varepsilon,M}\,:=\,{\rm div}_{h}\left(\langle\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\otimes\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\right)\,.

We notice that the analysis of
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}
is similar to the one performed in Paragraph
2.3.2.2
, up to taking
m=1
and replacing
\boldsymbol{W}_{\varepsilon,M}
and

\Lambda_{\varepsilon,M}
by
\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}
and
Z_{\varepsilon,M}
respectively.
Indeed, it deeply relies on system (
2.3.22
), which remains unchanged when
m=1
.
Also in this case, we find (
2.3.23
).

Therefore, we can focus on the term
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}
only. Its study presents some differences with respect to Paragraph
2.3.2.1
, so let us give the full details.
To begin with, like in (
2.3.18
), we have

\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}\,=\,{\rm div}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\;\;\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\,\nabla_{h}\left(\left|\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\right|^{2}\right)+{\rm curl}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\;\;\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle^{\perp}\,.

Of course, we can forget about the second term, because it is a perfect gradient.
For the first term, we use system (
2.4.2
): averaging the first equation with respect to
x^{3}
and multiplying it by
\langle\boldsymbol{V}^{h}_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle
, we get

{\rm div}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\;\;\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,=\,-\frac{\varepsilon}{\mathcal{A}}\partial_{t}\langle Z_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle+\frac{\varepsilon}{\mathcal{A}}\langle X_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,=\,\frac{\varepsilon}{\mathcal{A}}\langle Z_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\partial_{t}\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle+\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}\,.

We now use the horizontal part of (
2.4.2
), multiplied by
\langle Z_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle
, and we gather

\begin{split}\frac{\varepsilon}{\mathcal{A}}\langle Z_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\partial_{t}\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle&=-\frac{1}{\mathcal{A}}\langle Z_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\nabla_{h}\langle Z_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle-\frac{1}{\mathcal{A}}\langle Z_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle^{\perp}+\frac{\varepsilon}{\mathcal{A}}\langle Z_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{Y}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\\
&=-\frac{1}{\mathcal{A}}\langle Z_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle^{\perp}+\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}\,.\end{split}

This latter relation yields that

\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}\,=\,\left({\rm curl}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle-\frac{1}{\mathcal{A}}\langle Z_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\right)\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle^{\perp}+\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}.

Now we use the horizontal part of (
2.4.2
): averaging it with respect to the vertical variable and
applying the operator
{\rm curl}_{h}
, we find

\varepsilon\,\partial_{t}{\rm curl}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,+\,{\rm div}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,=\,\varepsilon\,{\rm curl}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{Y}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,.

Taking the difference of this equation with the first one in (
2.4.2
), we discover that

\partial_{t}\gamma_{\varepsilon,M}\,=\,{\rm curl}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{Y}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,-\,\frac{1}{\mathcal{A}}\,\langle X_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\,,\qquad\qquad\mbox{ with }\qquad\gamma_{\varepsilon,M}:={\rm curl}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,-\,\frac{1}{\mathcal{A}}\langle Z_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\,.

An argument analogous to the one used after (
2.3.19
) above, based on Aubin-Lions Theorem, shows also in this case that

(\gamma_{\varepsilon,M})_{\varepsilon}
is compact in
L_{T}^{2}(L_{\rm loc}^{2})
. Then, this sequence converges strongly (up to extraction of a suitable subsequence)
to a tempered distribution
\gamma_{M}
in the same space.

Since
\gamma_{\varepsilon,M}\longrightarrow\gamma_{M}
strongly in
L_{T}^{2}(L_{\rm loc}^{2})
and

\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\rightharpoonup\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{M}^{h}\rangle
weakly in
L_{T}^{2}(L_{\rm loc}^{2})
, we deduce that

\gamma_{\varepsilon,M}\,\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle^{\perp}\,\longrightarrow\,\gamma_{M}\,\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{M}^{h}\rangle^{\perp}\qquad\text{ in }\qquad\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\big(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\big),

where
\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{M}^{h}\rangle=\langle{\omega}_{M}*\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\rangle
and
\gamma_{M}={\rm curl}_{h}\langle{\omega}_{M}*\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\rangle-(1/\mathcal{A})\langle Z_{M}\rangle
.
Notice that, in view of (
2.2.24
), (
2.2.26
), (
2.2.3
), Proposition
2.2.5
and the definitions given in (
2.3.4
), we have

Z_{M}\,=\,\partial_{\varrho}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\omega_{M}*\varrho^{(1)}\,+\,\partial_{\vartheta}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\omega_{M}*\Theta\,=\,\omega_{M}*q\,,

where
q
is the quantity defined in (
2.2.39
). Owing to the regularity of the target velocity
\boldsymbol{U}^{h}
, we can pass to the limit also for
M\rightarrow+\infty
, thus finding that

\int^{T}_{0}\!\!\!\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}}\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\,\longrightarrow\,\int^{T}_{0}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big(\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\otimes\boldsymbol{U}^{h}:\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\,+\,\frac{1}{\mathcal{A}}\,q\,(\boldsymbol{U}^{h})^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\big)\,{\rm d}x^{h}\,{\rm d}t\,,

(2.4.7)

for all test functions
\boldsymbol{\psi}
such that
{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}=0
and
\partial_{3}\boldsymbol{\psi}=0
. Recall the convention
|\mathbb{T}^{1}|=1
.
Notice that, since
\boldsymbol{U}^{h}=\nabla_{h}^{\perp}q
, the last term in the integral on the right-hand side is actually zero.

2.4.3
End of the proof

Thanks to the previous analysis, we are now ready to pass to the limit in equation (
2.1.28
).
As done above, we take a test-function
\boldsymbol{\psi}
such that

\boldsymbol{\psi}=\big(\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\phi,0\big)\,,\qquad\qquad\mbox{ with }\qquad\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}\big([0,T[\,\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)\,,\quad\phi=\phi(t,x^{h})\,.

Notice that
{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}=0
and
\partial_{3}\boldsymbol{\psi}=0
. Then, all the gradient terms and all the contributions coming from the vertical
component of the momentum equation vanish identically, when tested against such a
\boldsymbol{\psi}
. In particular, we have

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\varrho_{\varepsilon}\nabla_{x}G\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\equiv 0\,.

So, equation (
2.1.28
) reduces
to
2
2

2

Remark that, in view of our choice of the test-functions, we can safely come back to the notation on
\Omega
instead of
\widetilde{\Omega}
.

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\Omega}\left(-\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\psi}-\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}:\nabla\boldsymbol{\psi}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\varrho_{\varepsilon}\big(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{h}\big)^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}+\mathbb{S}(\vartheta_{\varepsilon},\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}}):\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}\right)=\int_{\Omega}\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}(0,\cdot)\,.

As done in Subsection
2.3.3
, we can limit ourselves to consider the rotation and convective terms only.
As for the former term, we start by using the mass equation in (
NSF

ε

1

) tested against
\phi
: we get (recalling also (
2.2.22
))

\begin{split}-\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\langle R_{\varepsilon}\rangle\,\partial_{t}\phi+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\,\langle\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{h}\rangle\cdot\nabla_{h}\phi\right)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\langle R_{0,\varepsilon}\rangle\,\phi(0,\cdot)\,,\end{split}

whence we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\varrho_{\varepsilon}\big(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{h}\big)^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\,

\displaystyle=\,\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\langle\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{h}\rangle\cdot\nabla_{h}\phi\,

\displaystyle=\,-\,\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\langle R_{\varepsilon}\rangle\,\partial_{t}\phi\,-\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\langle R_{0,\varepsilon}\rangle\,\phi(0,\cdot)\,.

Letting now
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
, thanks to the previous relation and (
2.4.7
), we finally gather

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\cdot\partial_{t}\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\phi+\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\otimes\boldsymbol{U}^{h}:\nabla_{h}(\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\phi)+\langle R\rangle\,\partial_{t}\phi\right)\,\,{\rm d}x^{h}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mu(\overline{\vartheta})\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{U}^{h}:\nabla_{h}(\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\phi)\,\,{\rm d}x^{h}\,\,{\rm d}t+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{h}\rangle\cdot\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\phi(0,\cdot)+\langle R_{0}\rangle\,\phi(0,\cdot)\right)\,\,{\rm d}x^{h}\,.

Now, keeping in mind the relation for
R
in Remark
2.1.13
, we have

\begin{split}R\,&=\,-\,\frac{1}{\beta}\,\big(\partial_{\vartheta}p(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,\Upsilon\,-\,\partial_{\vartheta}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,q\,-\,\partial_{\vartheta}s(1,\overline{\vartheta})\,G\big)\\
&=-\frac{1}{\mathcal{A}}\left(\mathcal{B}\Upsilon-q-G\right)\,,\end{split}

where we have also employed the definitions of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
in (
2.3.4
).

Thus, we can write

\begin{split}-\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\langle R\rangle\,\partial_{t}\phi\,\,{\rm d}x^{h}\,{\rm d}t&=\frac{1}{\mathcal{A}}\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\mathcal{B}\,\langle\Upsilon\rangle-q+\frac{1}{2}\right)\,\partial_{t}\phi\,\,{\rm d}x^{h}\,{\rm d}t\\
&=\frac{1}{\mathcal{A}}\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Big(\mathcal{B}\,\langle\Upsilon\rangle-q\Big)\,\partial_{t}\phi\,\,{\rm d}x^{h}\,{\rm d}t-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{2\mathcal{A}}\,\phi(0,\cdot)\,{\rm d}x^{h}\,.\end{split}

At the end, noticing that
\Upsilon
solves (in the sense of distributions) the transport-diffusion equation (
2.1.40
), we get

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\cdot\partial_{t}\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\phi+\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\otimes\boldsymbol{U}^{h}:\nabla_{h}(\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\phi)+\frac{1}{\mathcal{A}}\,q\,\partial_{t}\phi\right)\,\,{\rm d}x^{h}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mu(\overline{\vartheta})\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{U}^{h}:\nabla_{h}(\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\phi)\,\,{\rm d}x^{h}\,\,{\rm d}t+\frac{1}{\mathcal{A}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\langle X\rangle\,\phi\,{\rm d}x^{h}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{h}\rangle\cdot\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\phi(0,\cdot)+\left(\langle R_{0}\rangle+\frac{1}{2\mathcal{A}}\right)\phi(0,\cdot)\right)\,\,{\rm d}x^{h}\,,

where we have defined
X
as in (
2.1.42
).

Theorem
2.1.12
is finally proved.

Chapter 3
On the influence of gravity

In this chapter, we continue the investigation we began in Chapter
2
, regarding the multi-scale analysis of mathematical models for geophysical flows.
Our focus here is on the effect of gravity in regimes of
low stratification
, but which go beyond the choice of the scaling that, in light
of previous results, we call “critical”. For clarity of exposition, we consider the barotropic Navier-Stokes system with the Coriolis force, i.e.

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\varrho+{\rm div}\,(\varrho\boldsymbol{u})=0\ \\[8.61108pt]
\partial_{t}(\varrho\boldsymbol{u})+{\rm div}\,(\varrho\boldsymbol{u}\otimes\boldsymbol{u})+\dfrac{\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\times\varrho\boldsymbol{u}}{Ro}\,+\dfrac{1}{Ma^{2}}\nabla_{x}p(\varrho)={\rm div}\,\mathbb{S}(\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{u})+\dfrac{\varrho}{Fr^{2}}\nabla_{x}G\,,\end{cases}

(3.0.1)

where we will take
Ro=\varepsilon
,
Ma=\varepsilon^{m}
and
Fr=\varepsilon^{n}
with
m
and
n
in suitable ranges (see (
3.1.1
) below in this respect).

The results presented in this chapter are contained in
[
25
]
.

Before moving on, let us give a recapitulation of the contents of chapter.

In Section
3.1
we collect our assumptions and we state the main theorems.
In Section
3.2
we show the main consequences of the finite energy condition on the family of weak solutions we are going to consider.
Namely, we derive uniform bounds in suitable norms, which allow us to extract weak-limit points, and we explore the constraints those limit points have to satisfy. In Sections
3.3
and
3.4
, we complete the proof of our main results, showing convergence in the weak formulation of the equations
in the cases
m>1
and
m=1
, respectively.

3.1
Setting of the problem and main results

In this section, we introduce the primitive system and formulate our working framework (see Subsection
3.1.1
), then we state our main results
(in Subsection
3.1.2
).

3.1.1
The barotropic Navier-Stokes-Coriolis system

As already said in the introductory part, in this chapter we assumed that the motion of the fluid is described by system (
2.0.1
) with constant density and without the centrifugal force.

Thus, given a small parameter
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
, the barotropic Navier-Stokes system with Coriolis and gravitational forces (see system (
3.0.1
) in this respect) reads as follows:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varrho_{\varepsilon}+{\rm div}\,(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})=0

(NSC
{}_{\varepsilon}^{1}
)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})+{\rm div}\,(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\,\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\times\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2m}}\nabla_{x}p(\varrho_{\varepsilon})={\rm div}\,\mathbb{S}(\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})+\frac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon^{2n}}\nabla_{x}G\,,

(NSC
{}_{\varepsilon}^{2}
)

where we recall that
m
and
n
are taken

\mbox{ either }\qquad m\,>\,1\quad\mbox{ and }\quad m\,<\,2\,n\,\leq\,m+1\,,\qquad\qquad\mbox{ or }\qquad m\,=\,1\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\frac{1}{2}\,<\,n\,<\,1\,.

(3.1.1)

As in the previous Chapter
2
, here the unknowns in equations (
NSC

ε

1

)-(
NSC

ε

2

) are the density
\varrho_{\varepsilon}=\varrho_{\varepsilon}(t,x)\geq 0
of the fluid and its velocity field
\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}=\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, where
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
but now
x\in\Omega:=\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\;]0,1[\,
.
The viscous stress tensor in (
NSC

ε

2

) is given by Newton’s rheological law, that we recall here,

\mathbb{S}(\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})=\mu\left(\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}+\,^{t}\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}-\frac{2}{3}{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\,{\rm Id}\,\right)+\eta\,{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\,{\rm Id}\,\,,

(3.1.2)

where
\mu>0
and
\eta\geq 0
now don’t depend on the temperature variations. As for the gravitational force, we recall its formulation (see (
2.1.7
) in this regard):

G(x)=-x^{3}\,.

(3.1.3)

The precise expression of
G
will be useful in some computations below, although some generalisations are certainly possible.

As done in the previous Chapter
2
, the system is supplemented with
complete slip boundary conditions
, namely

\displaystyle\big(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\big)_{|\partial\Omega}=0\quad

\displaystyle\mbox{ and }\quad\bigl([\mathbb{S}(\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})\boldsymbol{n}]\times\boldsymbol{n}\bigr)_{|\partial\Omega}=0\,,

(3.1.4)

where
\boldsymbol{n}
denotes the outer normal to the boundary
\partial\Omega\,=\,\{x_{3}=0\}\cup\{x_{3}=1\}
.
Notice that this is a true simplification, because it avoids complications due to the presence of Ekman boundary layers, when passing to the limit

\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
.

Remark 3.1.1

As it is well-known (see e.g. Subsection
2.3.1.3
and
[
27
]
), the equations (
NSC

ε

1

)-(
NSC

ε

2

), supplemented by the complete slip boundary boundary conditions (
3.1.4
),
can be recasted as a periodic problem with respect to the vertical variable, in the new domain

\Omega\,=\,\mathbb{R}^{2}\,\times\,\mathbb{T}^{1}\,,\qquad\qquad\mbox{ with }\qquad\mathbb{T}^{1}\,:=\,[-1,1]/\sim\,,

where
\sim
denotes the equivalence relation which identifies
-1
and
1
. Indeed, the equations are invariant if we extend

\varrho
and
\boldsymbol{u}^{h}
as even functions with respect to
x^{3}
, and
u^{3}
as an odd function.

In what follows, we will always assume that such modifications have been performed on the initial data, and
that the respective solutions keep the same symmetry properties.

Now we need to impose structural restrictions on the pressure function
p
. We assume that

p\in C^{1}[0,\infty)\cap C^{2}(0,\infty),\qquad p(0)=0,\qquad p^{\prime}(\varrho)>0\quad\mbox{ for all }\,\varrho\geq 0\,.

(3.1.5)

Additionally to (
3.1.5
), we require that (remember also Remark
2.1.4
)

\text{there exists}\quad\;\gamma\,>\,\frac{3}{2}\quad\mbox{ such that }\qquad\lim\limits_{\varrho\to+\infty}\frac{p^{\prime}(\varrho)}{\varrho^{\gamma-1}}=p_{\infty}>0\,.

(3.1.6)

Without loss of generality, we can suppose that
p
has been renormalised so that
p^{\prime}(1)=1
.

Remark 3.1.2

For a more detailed discussion about the choice
\gamma>\overline{\gamma}:=3/2
, which is fundamental for the existence theory, we address the reader to
[
42
]
by Feireisl, Novotný and Petzeltová, and references therein. In particular, we remark that in two space dimensions
\overline{\gamma}
can be improved up to
1
.

3.1.1.1
Equilibrium states

Next, we focus our attention on the so-called
equilibrium states
. For each value of
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
fixed, the equilibria of system (
NSC

ε

1

)-(
NSC

ε

2

) consist of static densities
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}
satisfying

\nabla_{x}p(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})=\varepsilon^{2(m-n)}\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\nabla_{x}G\qquad\mbox{ in }\;\Omega\,.

(3.1.7)

Equation (
3.1.7
) identifies
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}
up to an additive constant: taking the target density to be
1
, we get

H^{\prime}(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})=\,\varepsilon^{2(m-n)}G+H^{\prime}(1)\,,\qquad\qquad\mbox{ where }\qquad H(\varrho)=\varrho\int_{1}^{\varrho}\frac{p(z)}{z^{2}}{\rm d}z\,.

(3.1.8)

Notice that relation (
3.1.8
) implies that

H^{\prime\prime}(\varrho)=\frac{p^{\prime}(\varrho)}{\varrho}\quad\text{ and }\quad H^{\prime\prime}(1)=1\,.

Therefore, we infer that, whenever
m\geq 1
and
m>n
as in the present chapter, for any
x\in\Omega
one has
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x)\longrightarrow 1
in the limit
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
.
More precisely, the next statement collects all the necessary properties of the static states. It corresponds to Lemma
2.1.5
and Proposition
2.1.7
of
Chapter
2
.

Proposition 3.1.3

Let the gravitational force
G
be given by (
3.1.3
).
Let
\bigl(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\bigr)_{0<\varepsilon\leq 1}
be a family of static solutions to equation (
3.1.7
)
in
\Omega
.

Then, there exist an
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and a
0<\rho_{*}<1
such that
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\geq\rho_{*}
for all
\varepsilon\in\,]0,\varepsilon_{0}]

and all
x\in\Omega
.
In addition, for any
\varepsilon\in\,]0,\varepsilon_{0}]
, one has:

\left|\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x)\,-\,1\right|\,\leq\,C\,\varepsilon^{2(m-n)}\,,

for a constant
C>0
which is uniform in
x\in\Omega
and in
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
.

Without loss of any generality, we can assume that
\varepsilon_{0}=1
in Proposition
3.1.3
.

In light of this analysis, it is natural to try to solve system (
NSC

ε

1

)-(
NSC

ε

2

) in
\Omega
, supplemented with the
far field conditions

\varrho_{\varepsilon}\longrightarrow\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\longrightarrow 0\qquad\qquad\text{ as }\quad|x|\rightarrow+\infty\,.

(3.1.9)

3.1.1.2
Initial data and finite energy weak solutions

In view of the boundary conditions (
3.1.9
) “at infinity”, we assume that the initial data are close (in a suitable sense) to the equilibrium states
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}
that we have just identified.
Namely, we consider initial densities of the following form:

\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}=\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{m}\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}^{(1)}.

(3.1.10)

For later use, let us introduce also the following decomposition of the initial densities:

\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}\,=\,1\,+\,\varepsilon^{2(m-n)}\,R_{0,\varepsilon}\qquad\qquad\mbox{ with }\qquad R_{0,\varepsilon}\,=\,\widetilde{r}_{\varepsilon}\,+\,\varepsilon^{2n-m}\,\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}^{(1)}\,,\qquad\widetilde{r}_{\varepsilon}\,:=\,\frac{\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}-1}{\varepsilon^{2(m-n)}}\,,

(3.1.11)

where again
\widetilde{r}_{\varepsilon}
is a datum of the system.

We suppose the density perturbations
\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}^{(1)}
to be measurable functions and satisfy the control

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]}\left\|\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}^{(1)}\right\|_{(L^{2}\cap L^{\infty})(\Omega)}\,\leq\,c\,,

(3.1.12)

together with the “mean-free condition”

\int_{\Omega}\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}^{(1)}\,{\rm d}x=0\,.

As for the initial velocity fields, we assume the following uniform bound

\sup_{\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]}\left\|\sqrt{\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\,\leq\,c\,\quad\text{which also implies}\quad\sup_{\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]}\,\left\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\,\leq\,c\,.

(3.1.13)

Thanks to the previous uniform estimates, up to extraction, we can identify the limit points

\displaystyle\varrho^{(1)}_{0}\,:=\,\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\varrho^{(1)}_{0,\varepsilon}\qquad\text{ weakly-$\ast$ in }

\displaystyle\qquad L^{\infty}\cap L^{2}

(3.1.14)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\,:=\,\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\qquad\text{ weakly in }

\displaystyle\qquad L^{2}\,.

(3.1.15)

At this point, let us specify better what we mean by
finite energy weak solution
(see
[
39
]
for details).

Definition 3.1.4

Let
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\,]0,1[
. Fix
T>0
and
\varepsilon>0
. Let
(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon})
be an initial datum satisfying (
3.1.10
) to (
3.1.13
). We say that the couple
(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})
is a
finite energy weak solution
of the system (
NSC

ε

1

)-(
NSC

ε

2

) in
\,]0,T[\,\times\Omega
,
supplemented with the boundary conditions (
3.1.4
) and far field conditions (
3.1.9
), related to the initial datum
(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon})
, if the following conditions hold:

(i)

the functions
\varrho_{\varepsilon}
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}
belong to the class

\varrho_{\varepsilon}\geq 0\,,\;\;\;\varrho_{\varepsilon}-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\,\in L^{\infty}\big(\,]0,T[\,;L^{2}+L^{\gamma}(\Omega)\big)\,,\;\;\;\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}\big(\,]0,T[\,;H^{1}(\Omega)\big),\;\;\;\big(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\big)_{|\partial\Omega}=0;

(ii)

the equations have to be satisfied in a distributional sense:

-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\partial_{t}\varphi+\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi\right)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t=\int_{\Omega}\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}\varphi(0,\cdot)\,{\rm d}x

(3.1.16)

for any
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}([0,T[\,\times\overline{\Omega})
and

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\Omega}\left(-\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\psi}-\varrho_{\varepsilon}[\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}]:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\,\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\times(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2m}}p(\varrho_{\varepsilon}){\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}\right)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t

(3.1.17)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\Omega}\left(-\mathbb{S}(\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}):\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2n}}\varrho_{\varepsilon}\nabla_{x}G\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\right)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t+\int_{\Omega}\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}(0,\cdot)\,{\rm d}x

for any test function
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in C^{\infty}_{c}([0,T[\,\times\overline{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{3})
such that
\big(\boldsymbol{\psi}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\big)_{|\partial{\Omega}}=0
;

(iii)

the energy inequality holds for almost every
t\in(0,T)
:

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91684pt\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{2}\varrho_{\varepsilon}|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}|^{2}(t)\,{\rm d}x\,+\,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2m}}\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{E}\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\right)(t)\,{\rm d}x+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{S}(\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}):\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}\tau

(3.1.18)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\,\leq\,\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{2}\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}|\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}|^{2}\,{\rm d}x\,+\,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2m}}\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{E}\left(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon},\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\right)\,{\rm d}x\,,

where
\mathcal{E}\left(\rho,\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\right)\,:=\,H(\rho)-(\rho-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})\,H^{\prime}(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})-H(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})
is the
relative internal energy
of the fluid.

The solution is
global
if the previous conditions hold for all
T>0
.

Under the assumptions fixed above, for any
fixed
value of the parameter
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
,
the existence of a global in time finite energy weak solution
(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})
to system (
NSC

ε

1

)-(
NSC

ε

2

), related to the initial datum

(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon})
, in the sense of the previous definition, can be proved as in the classical case, see e.g.
[
56
]
,
[
33
]
.
Notice that the mapping
t\mapsto(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})(t,\cdot)
is weakly continuous, and one has
(\varrho_{\varepsilon})_{|t=0}=\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}
together with
(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})_{|t=0}=\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}
.

We remark also that, in view of (
NSC

ε

1

), the total mass is conserved in time: for almost every
t\in[0,+\infty[\,
,
one has

\int_{\Omega}\bigl(\varrho_{\varepsilon}(t)\,-\,\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\bigr)\,\,{\rm d}x\,=\,0\,.

To conclude, as already highlighted in Chapter
2
, in our framework of finite energy weak solutions,
inequality (
3.1.18
) will be the only tool to derive uniform estimates for the family of weak solutions we are going to consider.

3.1.2
Main theorems

We can now state our main results. We point out that, due to the scaling (
3.1.1
), the relation
m>n
is always true,
so we will always be in a low stratification regime.

The first statement concerns the case when the effects linked to the pressure term are predominant in the dynamics (with respect to the fast rotation), i.e.
m>1
.

Theorem 3.1.5

Let
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\,]0,1[\,
and
G\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)
be as in (
3.1.3
). Take
m>1
and
m+1\geq 2n>m
.
For any fixed value of
\varepsilon\in\;]0,1]
, let initial data
\left(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\right)
verify the hypotheses fixed in Paragraph
3.1.1.2
, and let

\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\right)
be a corresponding weak solution to system (
NSC

ε

1

)-(
NSC

ε

2

), supplemented with the structural hypotheses (
3.1.2
) on
\mathbb{S}(\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})
and with boundary conditions (
3.1.4
) and far field conditions (
3.1.9
).
Let
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
be defined as in (
3.1.15
).

Then, for any
T>0
, one has that

\displaystyle\varrho_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow 1\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\mbox{ strongly in }\qquad L^{\infty}\big([0,T];L_{\rm loc}^{\min\{2,\gamma\}}(\Omega)\big)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{U}\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\mbox{ weakly in }\qquad L^{2}\big([0,T];H^{1}(\Omega)\big)\,,

where
\boldsymbol{U}=(\boldsymbol{U}^{h},0)
, with
\boldsymbol{U}^{h}=\boldsymbol{U}^{h}(t,x^{h})
such that
{\rm div}_{h}\boldsymbol{U}^{h}=0
. In addition, the vector field
\boldsymbol{U}^{h}
is a weak solution
to the following homogeneous incompressible Navier-Stokes system in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{U}^{h}+{\rm div}_{h}\left(\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\otimes\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\right)+\nabla_{h}\Gamma-\mu\Delta_{h}\boldsymbol{U}^{h}=0\,,

(3.1.19)

for a suitable pressure function
\Gamma\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
and related to the initial condition

\boldsymbol{U}_{|t=0}=\mathbb{H}_{h}\left(\langle\boldsymbol{u}^{h}_{0}\rangle\right)\,.

When
m=1
, the Mach and Rossby numbers have the same order of magnitude, and they keep in the so-called
quasi-geostrophic balance
at the limit. Namely, the next statement is devoted to this isotropic case.

Theorem 3.1.6

Let
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\,]0,1[\,
and
G\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)
be as in (
3.1.3
). Take
m=1
and
1/2<n<1
.
For any fixed value of
\varepsilon\in\;]0,1]
, let initial data
\left(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\right)
verify the hypotheses fixed in Paragraph
3.1.1.2
, and let

\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\right)
be a corresponding weak solution to system (
NSC

ε

1

)-(
NSC

ε

2

), supplemented with the structural hypotheses (
3.1.2
) on
\mathbb{S}(\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})
and with boundary conditions (
3.1.4
) and far field conditions (
3.1.9
).
Let
\left(\varrho^{(1)}_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\right)
be defined as in (
3.1.14
) and (
3.1.15
).

Then, for any
T>0
, one has the following convergence properties:

\displaystyle\varrho_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow 1\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\mbox{ strongly in }\qquad L^{\infty}\big([0,T];L_{\rm loc}^{\min\{2,\gamma\}}(\Omega)\big)

\displaystyle\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}:=\frac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}-\widetilde{\varrho_{\varepsilon}}}{\varepsilon}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\rightharpoonup}}\varrho^{(1)}\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\mbox{ weakly-$*$ in }\qquad L^{\infty}\big([0,T];L^{2}+L^{\gamma}(\Omega)\big)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{U}\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\mbox{ weakly in }\qquad L^{2}\big([0,T];H^{1}(\Omega)\big)\,,

where, as above,
\boldsymbol{U}=(\boldsymbol{U}^{h},0)
, with
\boldsymbol{U}^{h}=\boldsymbol{U}^{h}(t,x^{h})
such that
{\rm div}_{h}\boldsymbol{U}^{h}=0
.
Moreover, one has the balance
\boldsymbol{U}^{h}=\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\varrho^{(1)}
, and
\varrho^{(1)}
satisfies (in the weak sense) the quasi-geostrophic equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\left(\varrho^{(1)}-\Delta_{h}\varrho^{(1)}\right)-\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\varrho^{(1)}\cdot\nabla_{h}\left(\Delta_{h}\varrho^{(1)}\right)+\mu\Delta_{h}^{2}\varrho^{(1)}\,=\,0\,,

(3.1.20)

supplemented with the initial condition

\left(\varrho^{(1)}-\Delta_{h}\varrho^{(1)}\right)_{|t=0}=\langle\varrho_{0}^{(1)}\rangle-{\rm curl}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{u}^{h}_{0}\rangle\,.

3.2
Consequences of the energy inequality

In Definition
3.1.4
, we have postulated that the family of weak solutions
\big(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\big)_{\varepsilon}
considered in Theorems

3.1.5
and
3.1.6
satisfies the energy inequality (
3.1.18
).

In this section, we take advantage of the energy inequality to infer uniform bounds for
\big(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\big)_{\varepsilon}
: this will be done in Subsection
3.2.1
.
Thanks to those bounds, we can extract (in Subsection
3.2.2
) weak-limit points of the sequence of solutions and deduce some properties these limit points
have to satisfy.

3.2.1
Uniform bounds and weak limits

This subsection is devoted to establish uniform bounds on the sequence
\bigl(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\bigr)_{\varepsilon}
.
This can be done as in the classical case (see e.g.
[
39
]
for details), since again
the Coriolis term does not contribute to the total energy balance of the system.
However, for the reader’s convenience, let us present some details.

To begin with, let us recall the partition of the space domain
\Omega
into the so-called “essential” and “residual” sets.
For this, for
t>0
and for all
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
, we define the sets

\Omega_{\rm{ess}}^{\varepsilon}(t)\,:=\,\left\{x\in\Omega\;\big|\quad\varrho_{\varepsilon}(t,x)\in\left[1/2\,\rho_{*}\,,\,2\right]\right\}\,,\qquad\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{\rm{res}}(t)\,:=\,\Omega\setminus\Omega^{\varepsilon}_{\rm{ess}}(t)\,,

where the positive constant
\rho_{*}>0
has been defined in Proposition
3.1.3
.

Next, we observe that

\Big[\mathcal{E}\big(\rho(t,x),\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x)\big)\Big]_{\rm{ess}}\,\sim\,\left[\rho-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x)\right]_{\rm{ess}}^{2}\qquad\quad\mbox{ and }\qquad\quad\Big[\mathcal{E}\big(\rho(t,x),\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x)\big)\Big]_{\rm{res}}\,\geq\,C\left(1\,+\,\big[\rho(t,x)\big]_{\rm{res}}^{\gamma}\right)\,,

where
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}
is the static density state identified in Paragraph
3.1.1.1
. Here above, the multiplicative constants are all strictly positive and may depend on
\rho_{*}
, and we agree to write
A\sim B
whenever there exists a “universal” constant
c>0
such that
(1/c)B\leq A\leq c\,B
.

Thanks to the previous observations, we easily see that, under the assumptions fixed in Section
3.1
on the initial data,
the right-hand side of (
3.1.18
) is
uniformly bounded
for all
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
: specifically, we have

\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{2}\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}|\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}|^{2}\,\,{\rm d}x+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2m}}\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{E}\left(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon},\,\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\right)\,\,{\rm d}x\,\leq\,C\,.

Owing to the previous inequalities and the finite energy condition (
3.1.18
) on the family of weak solutions,
it is quite standard to derive, for any time
T>0
fixed and any
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
, the following estimates, that we recall here for the reader’s convenience:

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\sqrt{\varrho_{\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\,\mathbb{R}^{3})}\,

\displaystyle\leq\,c

(3.2.1)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|\left[\dfrac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon^{m}}\right]_{\rm{ess}}(t)\right\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\,

\displaystyle\leq\,c

(3.2.2)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{\Omega}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{M}^{\varepsilon}_{\rm{res}}[t]}\,dx\,

\displaystyle\leq\,c\,\varepsilon^{2m}

(3.2.3)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{\Omega}[\varrho_{\varepsilon}]^{\gamma}_{\rm{res}}(t)\,\,{\rm d}x\,\,

\displaystyle\leq\,c\,\varepsilon^{2m}

(3.2.4)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}+\,^{t}\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}-\frac{2}{3}{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\,{\rm Id}\,\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega;\,\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})}\,\,{\rm d}t\,

\displaystyle\leq\,c\,.

(3.2.5)

We refer to
[
39
]
(see also
[
35
]
,
[
34
]
,
[
30
]
) for the details of the computations.

Owing to (
3.2.5
) and a generalisation of the Korn-Poincaré inequality (see Proposition
B.8
in the Appendix), we gather that

\big(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\big)_{\varepsilon}\,\subset\,L^{2}_{T}(L^{2})
. On the other hand, by arguing as in
[
35
]
, we can use
(
3.2.1
), (
3.2.3
) and (
3.2.4
) to deduce that also

\big(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\big)_{\varepsilon}\,\subset\,L^{2}_{T}(L^{2})
. Putting those bounds together, we finally infer that

\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\right\|^{2}_{H^{1}(\Omega;\,\mathbb{R}^{3})}\,\,{\rm d}t\,\leq\,c\,.

(3.2.6)

In particular, there exist
\boldsymbol{U}\,\in\,L^{2}_{\rm loc}\big(\mathbb{R}_{+};H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})\big)
such that, up to a suitable extraction (not relabelled here),
we have

\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\,\rightharpoonup\,\boldsymbol{U}\qquad\qquad\mbox{ in }\quad L^{2}_{\rm loc}\big(\mathbb{R}_{+};H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})\big)\,.

(3.2.7)

Let us move further and consider the density functions. The previous estimates on the density tell us that we must find a finer decomposition for the densities. As a matter
of fact, for any time
T>0
fixed, we have

\|\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2}+L^{\gamma}+L^{\infty})}\,\leq\,c\,\varepsilon^{2(m-n)}\,.

(3.2.8)

In order to see (
3.2.8
), to begin with, we write

|\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1|\,\leq\,|\varrho_{\varepsilon}-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}|+|\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}-1|\,.

(3.2.9)

From (
3.2.2
), we infer that
\big[\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,-\,\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\big]_{\rm{ess}}
is of order
O(\varepsilon^{m})
in
L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2})
.
For the residual part of the same term, we can use (
3.2.4
) to discover that it is of order
O(\varepsilon^{2m/\gamma})
.
Observe that, if
1<\gamma<2
, the higher order is
O(\varepsilon^{m})
, whereas, in the case
\gamma\geq 2
, by use of (
3.2.4
) and (
3.2.3
) again, it is easy to get

\left\|\left[\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,-\,\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}\right]_{\rm{res}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2})}^{2}\,\leq\,C\,\varepsilon^{2m}\,.

(3.2.10)

Finally, we apply Proposition
3.1.3
to control the last term in the right-hand side of (
3.2.9
).
In the end, estimate (
3.2.8
) is proved.

This having been established, and keeping in mind the notation introduced in (
3.1.10
) and (
3.1.11
), we can introduce the
density oscillation functions

R_{\varepsilon}\,:=\,\frac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1}{\varepsilon^{2(m-n)}}\,=\,\widetilde{r}_{\varepsilon}\,+\,\varepsilon^{2n-m}\,\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\,,

where we have defined

\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}(t,x)\,:=\,\frac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon^{m}}\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\widetilde{r}_{\varepsilon}(x)\,:=\,\frac{\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}-1}{\varepsilon^{2(m-n)}}\,.

(3.2.11)

Thanks again to (
3.2.2
), (
3.2.4
) and Proposition
3.1.3
, we see that the previous quantities verify the following uniform bounds, for any time
T>0
fixed:

\sup_{\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]}\left\|\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2}+L^{\gamma}({\Omega}))}\,\leq\,c\qquad\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\qquad\sup_{\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]}\left\|\widetilde{r}_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\,\leq\,c\,.

(3.2.12)

In view of the previous properties, there exist
\varrho^{(1)}\in L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2}+L^{\gamma})
and

\widetilde{r}\in L^{\infty}
such that (up to the extraction of a new suitable subsequence)

\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\rightharpoonup}}\,\varrho^{(1)}\qquad\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\qquad\widetilde{r}_{\varepsilon}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\rightharpoonup}}\,\widetilde{r}

(3.2.13)

in the weak-
*
topology of the respective spaces.
In particular, we get

R_{\varepsilon}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\rightharpoonup}}\,\widetilde{r}\qquad\qquad\qquad\mbox{ weakly-$*$ in }\qquad L^{\infty}\bigl([0,T];L^{\min\{\gamma,2\}}_{\rm loc}(\Omega)\bigr)\,.

Remark 3.2.1

Observe that, owing to (
3.2.10
), when
\gamma\geq 2
we get

\sup_{\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]}\left\|\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2})}\,\leq\,c\,.

Therefore, in that case we actually have that
\varrho^{(1)}\,\in\,L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2})
and
\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle*}}{{\rightharpoonup}}\,\varrho^{(1)}
in that space.

Analogously, when
\gamma\geq 2
we also get

\|\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2}+L^{\infty})}\,\leq\,c\,\varepsilon^{2(m-n)}.

3.2.2
Constraints on the limit

In this subsection, we establish some properties that the limit points of the family
\bigl(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\bigr)_{\varepsilon}
, which have been
identified here above, have to satisfy.

We first need a preliminary result about the decomposition of the pressure function, which will be useful in the following computations.

Lemma 3.2.2

Let
(m,n)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
verify the condition
m+1\,\geq\,2n\,>\,m\geq 1
. Let
p
be the pressure term satisfying the structural hypotheses (
3.1.5
) and (
3.1.6
). Then, for any
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
, one has

\begin{split}\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2m}}\,\nabla_{x}\Big(p(\varrho_{\varepsilon})\,-\,p(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})\Big)\,=\,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\nabla_{x}\Big(p^{\prime}(1)\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\Big)\,+\,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2n-m}}\,\nabla_{x}\Pi_{\varepsilon}\,,\end{split}

(3.2.14)

where the functions
\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}
have been introduced in (
3.2.11
) and for all
T>0
the family
\big(\Pi_{\varepsilon}\big)_{\varepsilon}
verifies the uniform bound

\left\|\Pi_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{1}+L^{2}+L^{\gamma})}\,\leq\,C\,.

(3.2.15)

When
\gamma\geq 2
, one can dispense of the space
L^{\gamma}
in the previous control of
\big(\Pi_{\varepsilon}\big)_{\varepsilon}
.

Proof:

First of all, we write

\begin{split}\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2m}}\,\nabla_{x}\Big(p(\varrho_{\varepsilon})\,-\,p(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})\Big)\,&=\,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2m}}\,\nabla_{x}\Big(p(\varrho_{\varepsilon})\,-\,p(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})-p^{\prime}(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})(\varrho_{\varepsilon}-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})\Big)\\
&\qquad\qquad+\,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\,\nabla_{x}\Big(\big(p^{\prime}(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})\,-\,p^{\prime}(1)\big)\,\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\Big)\,+\,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{m}}\nabla_{x}\Big(p^{\prime}(1)\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\Big)\,.\end{split}

(3.2.16)

We start by analysing the first term on the right-hand side of (
3.2.16
). For the essential part, we can employ a Taylor expansion
to write

\left[p(\varrho_{\varepsilon})-p(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})-p^{\prime}(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})(\varrho_{\varepsilon}-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})\right]_{\rm{ess}}=\left[p^{\prime\prime}(z_{\varepsilon})(\varrho_{\varepsilon}-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})^{2}\right]_{\rm{ess}}\,,

where
z_{\varepsilon}
is a suitable point between
\varrho_{\varepsilon}
and
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}
. Thanks to the uniform bound (
3.2.2
), we have that this term is of order
O(\varepsilon^{2m})
in
L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{1})
, for any
T>0
fixed. For the residual part, we can use (
3.2.3
) and (
3.2.4
), together with the boundedness of the profiles
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}
(keep in mind Proposition
3.1.3
), to deduce that

\left\|\left[p(\varrho_{\varepsilon})-p(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})-p^{\prime}(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})(\varrho_{\varepsilon}-\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})\right]_{\rm{res}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{1})}\,\leq C\,\varepsilon^{2m}\,.

We refer to e.g. Lemma 4.1 of
[
30
]
for details.

In a similar way, a Taylor expansion for the second term on the right-hand side of (
3.2.16
) gives

\big(p^{\prime}(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})-p^{\prime}(1)\big)\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}\,=\,p^{\prime\prime}(\eta_{\varepsilon})(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}-1)\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}\,,

where
\eta_{\varepsilon}
is a suitable point between
\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}
and 1. Owing to Proposition
3.1.3
again and to bound (
3.2.12
),
we infer that this term is of order
O(\varepsilon^{2(m-n)})
in
L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2}+L^{\gamma})
, for any time
T>0
fixed.

Then, defining

\Pi_{\varepsilon}:=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2(m-n)}}\left[\frac{p(\varrho_{\varepsilon})-p(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})}{\varepsilon^{m}}-p^{\prime}(1)\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\right]

(3.2.17)

we have the control (
3.2.15
).

The final statement concerning the case
\gamma\geq 2
easily follows from Remark
3.2.1
.
This completes the proof of the lemma.

Notice that the last term appearing in (
3.2.14
) is singular in
\varepsilon
. This is in stark contrast with the situation considered in previous works, see e.g.
[
35
]
,
[
34
]
,
[
40
]
and
[
30
]
. However, its gradient structure will play a fundamental role in the computations below.

This having been pointed out, we can now analyse the constraints on the weak-limit points
\big(\varrho^{(1)},\boldsymbol{U}\big)
, identified in relations (
3.2.7
) and (
3.2.13
) above.

3.2.2.1
The case of large values of the Mach number:
m>1

We start by considering the case of anisotropic scaling, namely
m>1
and
m+1\geq 2n>m
. Notice that, in particular, one has
m>n
.

Proposition 3.2.3

Let
m>1
and
m+1\geq 2n>m
in (
NSC

ε

1

)-(
NSC

ε

2

).
Let
\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\right)_{\varepsilon}
be a family of weak solutions, related to initial data
\left(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\right)_{\varepsilon}

verifying the hypotheses of Paragraph
3.1.1.2
. Let
(\varrho^{(1)},\boldsymbol{U})
be a limit point of the sequence

\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\right)_{\varepsilon}
, as identified in Subsection
3.2.1
. Then,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}\,=\,\,\Big(\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\,,\,0\Big)\,,\qquad\qquad\mbox{ with }\qquad\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\,=\,\boldsymbol{U}^{h}(t,x^{h})\quad\mbox{ and }\quad{\rm div}\,_{\!h}\,\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\,=\,0\,,

(3.2.18)

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\varrho^{(1)}\,=\,0\qquad\qquad\mbox{ in }\;\,\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega)\,.

(3.2.19)

Proof:
First of all, let us consider the weak formulation of the mass equation (
NSC

ε

1

): for any test function
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}\bigl(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega\bigr)
, denoting
[0,T]\times K\,:=\,{\rm Supp}\,\,\varphi
, with
\varphi(T,\cdot)\equiv 0
, we have

-\int^{T}_{0}\int_{K}\bigl(\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1\bigr)\,\partial_{t}\varphi\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\,-\,\int^{T}_{0}\int_{K}\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\,\cdot\,\nabla_{x}\varphi\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\,=\,\int_{K}\bigl(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}-1\bigr)\,\varphi(0,\,\cdot\,)\,{\rm d}x\,.

We can easily pass to the limit in this equation, thanks to the strong convergence
\varrho_{\varepsilon}\longrightarrow 1
, provided by (
3.2.8
), and the weak convergence of

\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}
in
L_{T}^{2}\bigl(L^{6}_{\rm loc}\bigr)
, provided by (
3.2.7
) and Sobolev embeddings. Notice that one always has
1/\gamma\,+\,1/6\,\leq\,1
. In this way,
we find

-\,\int^{T}_{0}\int_{K}\boldsymbol{U}\,\cdot\,\nabla_{x}\varphi\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\,=\,0\,,

for any test function
\varphi\,\in C_{c}^{\infty}\bigl(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega\bigr)
taken as above. The previous relation in particular implies

{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{U}=0\qquad\qquad\mbox{ a.e. in }\;\,\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega\,.

(3.2.20)

Next, we test the momentum equation (
NSC

ε

2

) on
\varepsilon^{m}\,\boldsymbol{\phi}
, for a smooth compactly supported
\boldsymbol{\phi}
.
Using the uniform bounds established in Subsection
3.2.1
, it is easy to see that the term presenting the derivative in time, the viscosity term and the convective
term converge to
0
, in the limit
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
. Since
m>1
, also the Coriolis term vanishes when
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
. It remains us to consider
the pressure and gravity terms in the weak formulation (
3.1.17
) of the momentum equation:
using relation (
3.1.7
), we see that we can couple them to write

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2m}}\,\nabla_{x}p(\varrho_{\varepsilon})-\,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2n}}\,\varrho_{\varepsilon}\nabla_{x}G\,=\,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2m}}\nabla_{x}\Big(p(\varrho_{\varepsilon})\,-\,p(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})\Big)-\,\varepsilon^{m-2n}\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\nabla_{x}G\,.

(3.2.21)

By (
3.2.12
) and the fact that
m>n
, we readily see that the last term in the right-hand side of (
3.2.21
) converges to
0
,
when tested against any smooth compactly supported
\varepsilon^{m}\,\boldsymbol{\phi}
.
At this point, we use Lemma
3.2.2
to treat the first term on the right-hand side of (
3.2.21
).
So, taking
\boldsymbol{\phi}\in C^{\infty}_{c}([0,T[\,\times\Omega)
(for some
T>0
), we test the momentum equation against
\varepsilon^{m}\,\boldsymbol{\phi}
and using (
2.2.24
), in the limit
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
we find that

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}p^{\prime}(1)\varrho^{(1)}{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{\phi}\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t=0\,.

Recalling that
p^{\prime}(1)=1
, the previous relation implies (
2.2.29
) for
\varrho^{(1)}
.

In particular, that relation implies that
\varrho^{(1)}(t,x)\,=\,c(t)
for almost all
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega
, for a suitable function
c=c(t)
depending only on time.

Now, in order to see effects due to the fast rotation in the limit, we need to “filter out” the contribution coming from the low Mach number.
To this end, we test (
NSC

ε

2

) on
\varepsilon\,\boldsymbol{\phi}
, where this time we take
\boldsymbol{\phi}\,=\,{\rm curl}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}
, for some smooth compactly supported
\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\in C^{\infty}_{c}\bigl([0,T[\,\times\Omega\bigr)
, with
T>0
.
Once again, by uniform bounds we infer that the
\partial_{t}
term, the convective term and the viscosity term all converge to
0
when
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
.
As for the pressure and the gravitational force, we argue as in (
3.2.21
): since the structure of
\boldsymbol{\phi}
kills any gradient term, we are left with the convergence
of the integral

\int^{T}_{0}\int_{\Omega}\varepsilon^{m-2n+1}\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\nabla_{x}G\cdot\boldsymbol{\phi}\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\,\longrightarrow\,\delta_{0}(m-2n+1)\int^{T}_{0}\int_{\Omega}\varrho^{(1)}\nabla_{x}G\cdot\boldsymbol{\phi}\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\,,

where
\delta_{0}(\zeta)\,=\,1
if
\zeta=0
,
\delta_{0}(\zeta)\,=\,0
otherwise.
Finally, arguing as done for the mass equation, we see that the Coriolis term converges to the integral
\int^{T}_{0}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\times\boldsymbol{U}\cdot\boldsymbol{\phi}
.

Consider the case
m+1>2n
for a while.
Passing to the limit for
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
, we find that
\mathbb{H}\left(\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\times\boldsymbol{U}\right)\,=\,0
, which implies that

\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\times\boldsymbol{U}\,=\,\nabla_{x}\Phi
, for some potential function
\Phi
. From this relation, one easily deduces that
\Phi=\Phi(t,x^{h})
, i.e.
\Phi
does not depend
on
x^{3}
, and that the same property is inherited by
\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\,=\,\bigl(U^{1},U^{2}\bigr)
, i.e. one has
\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\,=\,\boldsymbol{U}^{h}(t,x^{h})
. Furthermore, since
\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\,=\,-\,\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\Phi
, we get that
{\rm div}\,_{\!h}\,\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\,=\,0
.
At this point, we
combine this fact with (
3.2.20
) to infer that
\partial_{3}U^{3}\,=\,0
; but, thanks to the boundary condition
(
3.1.4
), we must have
\bigl(\boldsymbol{U}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\bigr)_{|\partial\Omega}\,=\,0
, which implies that
U^{3}
has to vanish at the boundary of
\Omega
.
Thus, we finally deduce that
U^{3}\,\equiv\,0
, whence (
3.2.18
) follows (see also the proof of Proposition
2.2.7
in this regard).

Now, let us focus on the case when
m+1=2n
. The previous computations show that, when
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
, we get

\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\times\boldsymbol{U}+\varrho^{(1)}\nabla_{x}G\,=\,\nabla_{x}\Phi\qquad\qquad\mbox{ in }\;\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega)\,,

(3.2.22)

for a new suitable function
\Phi
. However, owing to (
3.2.19
), we can say that
\varrho^{(1)}\nabla_{x}G\,=\,\nabla_{x}\big(\varrho^{(1)}\,G\big)
; hence, the previous relations
can be recasted as
\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\times\boldsymbol{U}\,=\,\nabla_{x}\widetilde{\Phi}
, for a new scalar function
\widetilde{\Phi}
. Therefore, the same analysis as above applies,
allowing us to gather (
3.2.18
) also in the case
m+1=2n
.

3.2.2.2
The case
m=1

Now we focus on the case
m=1
. In this case, the fast rotation and weak compressibility
effects are of the same order: this allows to reach the so-called
quasi-geostrophic balance
in the limit.

Proposition 3.2.4

Take
m=1
and
1/2<n<1
in system (
NSC

ε

1

)-(
NSC

ε

2

).
Let
\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\right)_{\varepsilon}
be a family of weak solutions to (
NSC

ε

1

)-(
NSC

ε

2

), associated with initial data

\left(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\right)
verifying the hypotheses fixed in Paragraph
3.1.1.2
.
Let
(\varrho^{(1)},\boldsymbol{U})
be a limit point of the sequence
\left(\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\right)_{\varepsilon}
, as identified in Subsection
3.2.1
.
Then,

\displaystyle\varrho^{(1)}\,=\,\varrho^{(1)}(t,x^{h})\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\boldsymbol{U}\,=\,\,\Big(\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\,,\,0\Big)\,,\quad\mbox{ with }\quad\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\,=\,\nabla^{\perp}_{h}\varrho^{(1)}\;\mbox{ a.e. in }\;\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\,.

(3.2.23)

In particular, one has
\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\,=\,\boldsymbol{U}^{h}(t,x^{h})
and
{\rm div}\,_{\!h}\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\,=\,0
.

Proof:

Arguing as in the proof of Proposition
3.2.3
, it is easy to pass to the limit in the continuity equation. In particular, we obtain again relation (
3.2.20
) for
\boldsymbol{U}
.

Only the analysis of the momentum equation changes a bit with respect to the previous case
m>1
. Now, since the most singular terms are of order
\varepsilon^{-1}
(keep in mind Lemma
3.2.2
), we test
the weak formulation (
3.1.17
) of the momentum equation against
\varepsilon\,\boldsymbol{\phi}
, where
\boldsymbol{\phi}
is a smooth compactly supported function. Similarly to what done above, the uniform bounds of Subsection
3.2.1
allow us to infer that the only quantity which does not vanish in the limit is the sum of the terms involving the Coriolis force, the pressure and the gravitational force: more precisely, using also Lemma
3.2.2
, we have

\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\times\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\,+\frac{\nabla_{x}\Big(p(\varrho_{\varepsilon})-p(\widetilde{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})\Big)}{\varepsilon}\,-\,\varepsilon^{2(1-n)}\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\nabla_{x}G\,=\,\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)\,,

in the sense of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega)
.
Following the same computations performed in the proof of Proposition
3.2.3
, in the limit
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
it is easy to get that

\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\times\boldsymbol{U}+\nabla_{x}\left(p^{\prime}(1)\varrho^{(1)}\right)\,=\,0\qquad\qquad\mbox{ in }\;\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\big(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega\big)\,.

After recalling that
p^{\prime}(1)=1
, this equality can be equivalently written as

\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\times\boldsymbol{U}+\nabla_{x}\varrho^{(1)}\,=\,0\qquad\qquad\mbox{ a.e. in }\;\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega\,.

Notice that
\boldsymbol{U}
is in fact in
L^{2}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+};L^{2})
, therefore so is
\nabla_{x}\varrho^{(1)}
; hence the previous relation is in fact satisfied almost everywhere
in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega
.

At this point, we can repeat the same argument used in the proof of Proposition
3.2.3
to deduce (
3.2.23
).
The proposition is thus proved.

3.3
Convergence in the case
m>1

In this section, we complete the proof of Theorem
3.1.5
. Namely, we show convergence in the weak formulation of the primitive system, in the case when
m>1
and
m+1\geq 2n>m
.

In Proposition
3.2.3
, we have already seen how passing to the limit in the mass equation.
However, problems arise when tackling the convergence in the momentum equation. Indeed, the analysis carried out so far
is not enough to identify the weak limit of the convective term
\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}
, which is highly non-linear.
For proving that this term converges to the expected limit
\boldsymbol{U}\otimes\boldsymbol{U}
, the key point is to control the fast oscillations in time of the solutions,
generated by the singular terms in the momentum equation. For this, we will use a compensated compactness argument and we exploit the algebraic structure of the wave system
underlying the primitive equations (
NSC

ε

1

)-(
NSC

ε

2

).

In Subsection
3.3.1
, we start by giving a quite accurate description of those fast oscillations. Then, using that description, we are able, in Subsection
3.3.2
, to establish two fundamental properties: on the one hand, strong convergence of a suitable quantity related to the velocity fields; on the other hand, the other terms, which do not involve that quantity, tend to vanish when
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
.
In turn, this allows us to complete, in Subsection
3.3.3
, the proof of the convergence.

3.3.1
Analysis of the strong oscillations

The goal of the present subsection is to describe the fast oscillations in time of the solutions. First of all, we recast our equations into a wave system. Then, we establish uniform bounds for the quantities appearing in the wave system. Finally, we apply a regularisation
in space procedure for all the quantities, which is preparatory in view of the computations of Subsection
3.3.2
.

3.3.1.1
Formulation of the acoustic wave system

We introduce the quantity

\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\,:=\,\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\,.

Then, straightforward computations show that we can recast the continuity equation in the form

\varepsilon^{m}\,\partial_{t}\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}\,+\,{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\,=\,0\,,

(3.3.1)

where
\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}
is defined in (
3.2.11
).
Next, thanks to Lemma
3.2.2
and the static relation (
3.1.7
), we can derive the following form of the momentum equation:

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{m}\,\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\,+\,\varepsilon^{m-1}\,\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\times\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\,+p^{\prime}(1)\,\nabla_{x}\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\,

\displaystyle=\,\varepsilon^{2(m-n)}\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\nabla_{x}G\,-\,\nabla_{x}\Pi_{\varepsilon}\right)

(3.3.2)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\,\varepsilon^{m}\,\Big({\rm div}\,\mathbb{S}\!\left(\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\right)\,-\,{\rm div}\,\!\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\right)\Big)\,.

Then, if we define

\boldsymbol{f}_{\varepsilon}:={\rm div}\,\big(\mathbb{S}\!\left(\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\right)\,-\,\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\big)\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\boldsymbol{g}_{\varepsilon}:=\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\nabla_{x}G\,-\,\nabla_{x}\Pi_{\varepsilon}\,,

(3.3.3)

recalling that we have normalised the pressure function so that
p^{\prime}(1)=1
, we can rewrite the primitive system (
NSC

ε

1

)-(
NSC

ε

2

) in the following form:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\varepsilon^{m}\,\partial_{t}\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}\,+\,{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\,=\,0\\[4.30554pt]
\varepsilon^{m}\,\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\,+\,\nabla_{x}\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\,+\,\varepsilon^{m-1}\,\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\times\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\,=\,\varepsilon^{m}\,\boldsymbol{f}_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{2(m-n)}\boldsymbol{g}_{\varepsilon}\,.\end{array}\right.

(3.3.4)

We remark that system (
3.3.4
) has to be read in the weak sense: for any
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}\bigl([0,T[\,\times\overline{\Omega}\bigr)
, one has

-\,\varepsilon^{m}\,\int^{T}_{0}\int_{\Omega}\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}\,\partial_{t}\varphi\,-\,\int^{T}_{0}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi\,=\,\varepsilon^{m}\int_{\Omega}\varrho^{(1)}_{0,\varepsilon}\,\varphi(0)\,\,,

and also, for any
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in C_{c}^{\infty}\bigl([0,T[\,\times\overline{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{3}\bigr)
such that
(\boldsymbol{\psi}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})_{|\partial\Omega}=0
, one has

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt-\,\varepsilon^{m}\,\int^{T}_{0}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,-\,\int^{T}_{0}\int_{\Omega}\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}\,{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}\,+\,\varepsilon^{m-1}\int^{T}_{0}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\times\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=\,\varepsilon^{m}\int_{\Omega}\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}(0)\,+\,\varepsilon^{m}\,\int^{T}_{0}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{f}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}+\,\varepsilon^{2(m-n)}\,\int^{T}_{0}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{g}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\,.

Here we use estimates of Subsection
3.2.1
in order to show uniform bounds for the solutions and the data in the wave equation (
3.3.4
).
We start by dealing with the “unknown”
\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}
. Splitting the term into essential and residual parts, one can obtain for all
T>0
,

\|\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2}+L^{2\gamma/(\gamma+1)})}\leq c\,.

(3.3.5)

In the next lemma, we establish bounds for the source terms in the system of acoustic waves (
3.3.4
).

Lemma 3.3.1

Write
\boldsymbol{f}_{\varepsilon}\,=\,{\rm div}\,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{f}}_{\varepsilon}
and
\boldsymbol{g}_{\varepsilon}\,=\,\boldsymbol{g}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\,-\,\nabla_{x}\Pi_{\varepsilon}
, where we have defined the quantities
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{f}}_{\varepsilon}:=\mathbb{S}\!\left(\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\right)-\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}
,
\boldsymbol{g}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\,:=\,\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\,\nabla_{x}G
and the functions
\Pi_{\varepsilon}
have been introduced in (
3.2.17
) of Lemma
3.2.2
.

For any
T>0
fixed, one has the uniform embedding properties

\big(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{f}}_{\varepsilon}\big)_{\varepsilon}\,\subset\,L^{2}_{T}(L^{2}+L^{1})\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\big(\boldsymbol{g}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\big)_{\varepsilon}\,\subset\,L^{2}_{T}(L^{2}+L^{\gamma})\,.

In the case
\gamma\geq 2
, we may get rid of the space
L^{\gamma}
in the control of
\big(\boldsymbol{g}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\big)_{\varepsilon}
.

In particular, the sequences
\bigl(\boldsymbol{f}_{\varepsilon}\bigr)_{\varepsilon}
and

\bigl(\boldsymbol{g}_{\varepsilon}\bigr)_{\varepsilon}
, defined in system (
3.3.4
), are uniformly bounded in the space
L^{2}\big([0,T];H^{-s}(\Omega)\big)
, for all
s>5/2
.

Proof:

From (
3.2.1
), (
3.2.5
) and (
3.2.6
), we immediately infer the uniform bound for the family
\big(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{f}}_{\varepsilon}\big)_{\varepsilon}
in
L^{2}_{T}(L^{1}+L^{2})
,
from which we deduce also the uniform boundedness of
\big(\boldsymbol{f}_{\varepsilon}\big)_{\varepsilon}
in
L^{2}_{T}(H^{-s})
, for any
s>5/2
(see Theorem
B.2
in this respect).

Next, for bounding
\big(\boldsymbol{g}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\big)_{\varepsilon}
we simply use (
3.2.12
), together with Remark
3.2.1
when
\gamma\geq 2
.
Keeping in mind the bounds established in Lemma
3.2.2
, the uniform estimate for
\big(\boldsymbol{g}_{\varepsilon}\big)_{\varepsilon}
follows.

3.3.1.2
Regularization and description of the oscillations

As already mentioned in Remark
3.1.1
, in order to apply the Littlewood-Paley theory, it is convenient to reformulate problem (
NSC

ε

1

)-(
NSC

ε

2

) in the new domain (which we keep calling
\Omega
, with a little abuse of notation)

\Omega:=\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{1},\quad\quad\text{with}\quad\quad\mathbb{T}^{1}:=[-1,1]/\sim\,.

In addition, to avoid the appearing of the (irrelevant) multiplicative constants on the computations, we suppose that the torus
\mathbb{T}^{1}
has been renormalised so that its Lebesgue measure is equal to 1.

Now, for any
M\in\mathbb{N}
we consider the low-frequency cut-off operator
{S}_{M}
of a Littlewood-Paley decomposition, as introduced in (
A.1.1
) of Section
A.1
. Then, we define

\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon,M}={S}_{M}\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}\qquad\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\qquad\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}={S}_{M}\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\,.

(3.3.6)

The previous regularised quantities satisfy the following properties.
