Proposition 3.3.2

For any
T>0
, we have the following convergence properties, in the limit
M\rightarrow+\infty
:

\begin{split}&\sup_{0<\varepsilon\leq 1}\,\left\|\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}-\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon,M}\right\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T];H^{-s})}\longrightarrow 0\qquad\forall\,s>\max\left\{0,3\left(\frac{1}{\gamma}\,-\,\frac{1}{2}\right)\right\}\\
&\sup_{0<\varepsilon\leq 1}\,\left\|\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}-\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}\right\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T];H^{-s})}\longrightarrow 0\qquad\forall\,s>\frac{3}{2\,\gamma}\,.\end{split}

(3.3.7)

Moreover, for any
M>0
, the couple
(\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon,M},\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M})
satisfies the approximate wave equations

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\varepsilon^{m}\,\partial_{t}\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon,M}\,+\,\,{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}\,=\,0\\[4.30554pt]
\varepsilon^{m}\,\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}\,+\varepsilon^{m-1}\,e_{3}\times\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}+\,\nabla_{x}\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon,M}\,=\,\varepsilon^{m}\,\boldsymbol{f}_{\varepsilon,M}\,+\varepsilon^{2(m-n)}\boldsymbol{g}_{\varepsilon,M}\,,\end{array}\right.

(3.3.8)

where
(\boldsymbol{f}_{\varepsilon,M})_{\varepsilon}
and
(\boldsymbol{g}_{\varepsilon,M})_{\varepsilon}
are families of smooth (in the space variables) functions satisfying, for any
s\geq 0
, the uniform bounds

\sup_{0<\varepsilon\leq 1}\,\left\|\boldsymbol{f}_{\varepsilon,M}\right\|_{L^{2}([0,T];H^{s})}\,+\,\sup_{0<\varepsilon\leq 1}\,\left\|\boldsymbol{g}_{\varepsilon,M}\right\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T];H^{s})}\,\leq\,C(s,M)\,,

(3.3.9)

where the constant
C(s,M)
depends on the fixed values of
s\geq 0
and
M>0
, but not on
\varepsilon>0
.

Proof:

Thanks to characterization (
A.1.2
) of
H^{s}
, properties (
3.3.7
) are straightforward consequences of the uniform bounds establish in Subsection
3.2.1
. For instance, let us consider the functions
\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}
: when
\gamma\geq 2
, owing to Remark
3.2.1
one has

\big(\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}\big)_{\varepsilon}\,\subset\,L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2})
, and then we use estimate (
A.1.3
) from Section
A.1
.
When
1<\gamma<2
, instead, we first apply the dual Sobolev embedding (see Theorem
B.2
) to infer that
\big(\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}\big)_{\varepsilon}\,\subset\,L^{\infty}_{T}(H^{-\sigma})
,
with
\sigma\,=\,\sigma(\gamma)\,=\,3\big(1/\gamma-1/2\big)
, and then we use (
A.1.3
) again. The bounds for the momentum

\big(\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\big)_{\varepsilon}
can be deduced by a similar argument, after observing that
2\gamma/(\gamma+1)<2
always.

Next, applying the operator
{S}_{M}
to (
3.3.4
) immediately gives us system (
3.3.8
), where we have set

\boldsymbol{f}_{\varepsilon,M}:={S}_{M}\boldsymbol{f}_{\varepsilon}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\boldsymbol{g}_{\varepsilon,M}:={S}_{M}\boldsymbol{g}_{\varepsilon}\,.

Thanks to Lemma
3.3.1
and (
A.1.2
), it is easy to verify inequality (
3.3.9
).

At this point, we will need also the following important decomposition for the momentum vector fields
\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}
and their
{\rm curl}\,
.

Proposition 3.3.3

For any
M>0
and any
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
, the following decompositions hold true:

\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}\,=\,\varepsilon^{2(m-n)}\boldsymbol{t}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}+\boldsymbol{t}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad{\rm curl}\,\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}=\varepsilon^{2(m-n)}\boldsymbol{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}+\boldsymbol{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}\,,

where, for any
T>0
and
s\geq 0
, one has

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{t}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}\right\|_{L^{2}([0,T];H^{s})}+\left\|\boldsymbol{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}\right\|_{L^{2}([0,T];H^{s})}\leq C(s,M)

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{t}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}\right\|_{L^{2}([0,T];H^{1})}+\left\|\boldsymbol{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}\right\|_{L^{2}\left([0,T];L^{2}\right)}\leq C\,,

for suitable positive constants
C(s,M)
and
C
, which are uniform with respect to
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
.

Proof:

We decompose
\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}\,=\,\varepsilon^{2(m-n)}\boldsymbol{t}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}\,+\,\boldsymbol{t}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}
, where we define

\boldsymbol{t}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}\,:=\,{S}_{M}\left(\frac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1}{\varepsilon^{2(m-n)}}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\right)\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\boldsymbol{t}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}\,:=\,{S}_{M}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\,.

(3.3.10)

The decomposition of
{\rm curl}\,\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}
follows after setting
\boldsymbol{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{j}\,:=\,{\rm curl}\,\boldsymbol{t}_{\varepsilon,M}^{j}
, for
j=1,2
.

We have to prove uniform bounds for all those terms, by using the estimates established in Subsection
3.2.1
above.
First of all, we have that
\big(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\big)_{\varepsilon}\,\subset\,L^{2}_{T}(H^{1})
, for any
T>0
fixed. Then, we immediately gather the sought bounds for the vector fields
\boldsymbol{t}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}
and
\boldsymbol{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}
.

For the families of
\boldsymbol{t}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}
and
\boldsymbol{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}
, instead, we have to use the bounds provided by (
3.2.8
) and (when
\gamma\geq 2
)
Remark
3.2.1
. In turn, we see that for any
T>0
,

\left(\frac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1}{\varepsilon^{2(m-n)}}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\right)\,\subset\,L^{2}_{T}(L^{1}+L^{2}+L^{6\gamma/(\gamma+6)})\,\hookrightarrow\,L^{2}_{T}(H^{-\sigma})\,,

for some
\sigma>0
large enough. Therefore, the claimed bounds follow thanks to the regularising effect of the operators
S_{M}
. The proof of the proposition
is thus completed.

3.3.2
Analysis of the convection

In this subsection we show the convergence of the convective term.
The first step is to reduce its analysis to the case of smooth vector fields
\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}
.

Lemma 3.3.4

Let
T>0
. For any
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in C_{c}^{\infty}\bigl([0,T[\,\times\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}\bigr)
, we have

\lim_{M\rightarrow+\infty}\limsup_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}\left|\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}\otimes\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\right|=0\,.

Proof:

The proof is very similar to the one of Lemma
2.3.5
from Chapter
2
, for this reason we just outline it.

One starts by using the decomposition
\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,=\,1\,+\,\varepsilon^{2(m-n)}\,R_{\varepsilon}
to reduce (owing to the uniform bounds of Subsection
3.2.1
) the convective term to the “homogeneous counterpart”: for any test function
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in C^{\infty}_{c}\big(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}\big)
, one has

\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}\left|\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\right|\,=\,0\,.

Notice that, here, one has to use that
\gamma\geq 3/2
.

After that, we write

\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\,=\,S_{M}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\,+\,({\rm Id}\,-S_{M})\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\,=\,\boldsymbol{t}^{2}_{\varepsilon,M}\,+\,({\rm Id}\,-S_{M})\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}
.
Using Proposition
3.3.3
and the fact that
\left\|({\rm Id}\,-{S}_{M})\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L_{T}^{2}(L^{2})}\leq C\,2^{-M}\|\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}_{T}(L^{2})}\,\leq C\,2^{-M}
, which holds in view of estimate (
A.1.3
) from Section
A.1
and the uniform bound (
3.2.6
),
one can conclude.

From now on, for notational convenience, we generically denote by
\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}
any remainder term, that again is any term satisfying the property

\lim_{M\rightarrow+\infty}\limsup_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}\left|\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\right|=0\,,

(3.3.11)

for all test functions
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in C_{c}^{\infty}\bigl([0,T[\,\times\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}\bigr)
lying in the kernel of the singular perturbation operator,
namely (in view of Proposition
3.2.3
) such that

\boldsymbol{\psi}\in C_{c}^{\infty}\big([0,T[\,\times\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}\big)\qquad\qquad\mbox{ with }\qquad{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}=0\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\partial_{3}\boldsymbol{\psi}=0\,.

(3.3.12)

Notice that, in order to pass to the limit in the weak formulation of the momentum equation and derive the limit system, it is enough to use test functions
\boldsymbol{\psi}
as above.

Thus, for
\boldsymbol{\psi}
as in (
3.3.12
),
we have to pass to the limit in the term

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}\otimes\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,

\displaystyle=\,\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}{\rm div}\,\left(\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}\otimes\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\,.

Notice that the integration by parts above is well-justified, since all the quantities inside the integrals are now smooth with respect to the space variable. Owing to the structure of the test function, and
resorting to the notation (
OSC
) setted in the introductory part, we remark that we can write

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}{\rm div}\,\left(\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}\otimes\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\,=\,\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}+\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\,,

where we have defined the terms

\mathcal{T}^{1}_{\varepsilon,M}\,:=\,{\rm div}_{h}\left(\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\otimes\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\right)\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\mathcal{T}^{2}_{\varepsilon,M}\,:=\,{\rm div}_{h}\left(\langle\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\otimes\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\right)\,.

(3.3.13)

In the next two paragraphs, we will deal with each one of those terms separately. We borrow most of the arguments from Chapter
2

(see also
[
34
]
,
[
30
]
for a similar approach). However, the special structure of the gravity force will play a key role here,
in order (loosely speaking) to compensate the stronger singularity due to our scaling
2n>m
. Finally, we point out that, in what follows, all the equalities (which will involve the derivative in time) will hold in the sense of distributions.

3.3.2.1
Convergence of the vertical averages

We start by dealing with
\mathcal{T}^{1}_{\varepsilon,M}
. It is standard to write

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}\,

\displaystyle=\,{\rm div}_{h}\left(\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\otimes\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\right)={\rm div}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle+\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\cdot\nabla_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle

(3.3.14)

\displaystyle={\rm div}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\,\nabla_{h}\left(\left|\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\right|^{2}\right)+{\rm curl}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle^{\perp}\,.

Notice that the second term is a perfect gradient, so it vanishes when tested against divergence-free test functions. Hence, we can treat it as
a remainder, in the sense of (
3.3.11
).

For the first term in the second line of (
3.3.14
), instead, we take advantage of system (
3.3.8
): averaging the first equation with respect to
x^{3}
and multiplying it by
\langle\boldsymbol{V}^{h}_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle
, we arrive at

{\rm div}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,=\,-\varepsilon^{m}\partial_{t}\langle\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,=\,\varepsilon^{m}\langle\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\partial_{t}\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle+\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}\,.

We remark that the term presenting the total derivative in time is in fact a remainder, thanks to the factor
\varepsilon^{m}
in front of it.
Now, we use the horizontal part of (
3.3.8
), where we first take the vertical average and then multiply by
\langle\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle
:
since
m>1
, we gather

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{m}\langle\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\partial_{t}\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\quad=-\langle\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\nabla_{h}\langle\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle-\varepsilon^{m-1}\langle\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle^{\perp}+\varepsilon^{m}\langle\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{f}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle+\varepsilon^{2(m-n)}\langle\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{g}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\quad=-\varepsilon^{m-1}\langle\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle^{\perp}+\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}\,,

where we have repeatedly exploited the properties proved in Proposition
3.3.2
and we have included in the remainder term also the perfect gradient.
Inserting this relation into (
3.3.14
) yields

\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}=\left({\rm curl}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,-\,\varepsilon^{m-1}\langle\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\right)\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle^{\perp}+\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}\,.

Observe that the first addendum appearing in the right-hand side of the previous relation is bilinear. Thus, in order to pass to the limit in it, one needs some strong convergence properties. As a matter of fact, in the next computations we will work on the regularised wave system (
3.3.8
) to show that the quantity

\Gamma_{\varepsilon,M}:={\rm curl}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,-\,\varepsilon^{m-1}\langle\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle

is
compact
in some suitable space. In particular, as
m>1
, also
{\rm curl}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle
is compact.

In order to see this, we write the vertical average of the first equation in (
3.3.8
) as

\varepsilon^{2m-1}\,\partial_{t}\langle\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\,+\,\varepsilon^{m-1}{\rm div}\,_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,=0\,.

Next, we take the vertical average of the horizontal components of (
3.3.8
) and then we apply
{\rm curl}_{h}
: one obtains

\varepsilon^{m}\,\partial_{t}{\rm curl}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,+\varepsilon^{m-1}\,{\rm div}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,=\,\varepsilon^{m}{\rm curl}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{f}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle+\varepsilon^{2(m-n)}{\rm curl}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{g}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,.

At this point, we recall the definition (
3.3.3
) of
\boldsymbol{g}_{\varepsilon}
, and we see that
{\rm curl}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{g}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,\equiv\,0
.
This property is absolutely fundamental, since it allows to erase the last term in the previous relation, which otherwise would have represented an obstacle to get compactness for
\Gamma_{\varepsilon,M}
. Indeed, thanks to this observation, we can sum up the last two equations to get

\partial_{t}\Gamma_{\varepsilon,M}\,=\,{\rm curl}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{f}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,.

(3.3.15)

Using estimate (
3.3.9
) in Proposition
3.3.2
, we discover that, for any
M>0
fixed, the family

\left(\partial_{t}\,\Gamma_{\varepsilon,M}\right)_{\varepsilon}
is uniformly bounded (with respect to
\varepsilon
) in e.g. the space
L_{T}^{2}(L^{2})
.
On the other hand, we have that, again for any
M>0
fixed,
the sequence
(\Gamma_{\varepsilon,M})_{\varepsilon}
is uniformly bounded (with respect to
\varepsilon
) e.g. in the space
L_{T}^{2}(H^{1})
.
Since the embedding
H_{\rm loc}^{1}\hookrightarrow L_{\rm loc}^{2}
is compact, the Aubin-Lions Theorem implies that, for any
M>0
fixed, the family
(\Gamma_{\varepsilon,M})_{\varepsilon}
is compact
in
L_{T}^{2}(L_{\rm loc}^{2})
. Then, up to extraction of a suitable subsequence (not relabelled here), that family converges strongly to a tempered distribution
\Gamma_{M}
in the same space.

Now, we have that
\Gamma_{\varepsilon,M}
converges strongly to
\Gamma_{M}
in
L_{T}^{2}(L_{\rm loc}^{2})
and
\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle
converges weakly to

\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{M}^{h}\rangle
in
L_{T}^{2}(L_{\rm loc}^{2})
(owing to Proposition
3.3.3
, for instance). Then, we deduce

\Gamma_{\varepsilon,M}\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle^{\perp}\longrightarrow\Gamma_{M}\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{M}^{h}\rangle^{\perp}\qquad\text{ in }\qquad\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\big(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)\,.

Observe that, by definition of
\Gamma_{\varepsilon,M}
, we must have
\Gamma_{M}={\rm curl}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{M}^{h}\rangle
. On the other hand, owing to Proposition
3.3.3
and (
3.3.10
), we know that
\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{M}^{h}\rangle=\langle{S}_{M}\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\rangle
.
Therefore, in the end we have proved that, for
m>1
and
m+1\geq 2n>m
,
one has the convergence (at any
M\in\mathbb{N}
fixed, when
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
)

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\,{\rm d}x^{h}\,{\rm d}t\,\longrightarrow\,\int^{T}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}{\rm curl}_{h}\langle{S}_{M}\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\rangle\;\langle{S}_{M}(\boldsymbol{U}^{h})^{\perp}\rangle\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\,{\rm d}x^{h}\,{\rm d}t\,,

(3.3.16)

for any
T>0
and for any test-function
\boldsymbol{\psi}
as in (
3.3.12
).

3.3.2.2
Vanishing of the oscillations

We now focus on the term
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}
, defined in (
3.3.13
). Recall that
m>1
. In what follows, we consider separately the two cases
m+1>2n
and
m+1=2n
. As a matter of fact, in the case when
m+1=2n
, a bilinear term involving
\boldsymbol{g}_{\varepsilon,M}
has no power of
\varepsilon
in front of it, so it is not clear that it converges to
0
and, in fact, it might persist in the limit, giving rise to an additional term in the target system. To overcome this issue and show that this actually does not happen, we deeply exploit the structure of the wave system to recover a quantitative smallness for that term (namely, in terms of positive powers of
\varepsilon
).

The case
m+1>2n

Starting from the definition of
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}
, the same computations as above yield

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}\,

\displaystyle=\,\langle{\rm div}_{h}(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h})\;\;\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\,\langle\nabla_{h}|\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}|^{2}\rangle+\langle{\rm curl}_{h}\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\,\left(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}\rangle\,.

(3.3.17)

Let us now introduce the quantities

\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\,:=\,(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h})^{\perp}-\partial_{3}^{-1}\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\accentset{\approx}{\omega}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}\,:=\,{\rm curl}_{h}\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\,.

Then we can write

\left({\rm curl}\,\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}\right)^{h}\,=\,\partial_{3}\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\left({\rm curl}\,\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}\right)^{3}\,=\,\accentset{\approx}{\omega}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}\,.

In addition, from the momentum equation in (
3.3.8
), where we take the mean-free part and then the
{\rm curl}\,
, we deduce the equations

\begin{cases}\varepsilon^{m}\partial_{t}\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}-\varepsilon^{m-1}\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}=\varepsilon^{m}\left(\partial_{3}^{-1}{\rm curl}\,\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{f}}_{\varepsilon,M}\right)^{h}+\varepsilon^{2(m-n)}\left(\partial_{3}^{-1}{\rm curl}\,\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\varepsilon,M}\right)^{h}\\[4.30554pt]
\varepsilon^{m}\partial_{t}\accentset{\approx}{\omega}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}+\varepsilon^{m-1}{\rm div}_{h}\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}=\varepsilon^{m}\,{\rm curl}_{h}\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{f}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\,.\end{cases}

(3.3.18)

Making use of the relations above, recalling the definitions in (
3.3.3
), and thanks to Propositions
3.3.2
and
3.3.3
, we can write

\begin{split}{\rm curl}_{h}\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\;\left(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}&=\accentset{\approx}{\omega}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}\left(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}\,\\
&=\varepsilon\partial_{t}\!\left(\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}\accentset{\approx}{\omega}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}-\varepsilon\accentset{\approx}{\omega}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}\left(\left(\partial_{3}^{-1}{\rm curl}\,\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{f}}_{\varepsilon,M}\right)^{h}\right)^{\perp}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\varepsilon^{m+1-2n}\,\accentset{\approx}{\omega}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}\left(\left(\partial_{3}^{-1}{\rm curl}\,\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\varepsilon,M}\right)^{h}\right)^{\perp}\\
&=-\varepsilon\left(\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}\partial_{t}\accentset{\approx}{\omega}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}+\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}=\left(\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}\,{\rm div}_{h}\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}+\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}\,.\end{split}

(3.3.19)

We point out that, thanks to the scaling
m+1>2n
, we could include in the remainder also the last term appearing in the second equality, which was of order
O(\varepsilon^{m+1-2n})
.

Hence, putting the gradient term into
\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}
, from (
3.3.17
) we arrive at

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}\,

\displaystyle=\,\langle{\rm div}_{h}\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\,\left(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}+\left(\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}\right)\rangle+\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}

\displaystyle=\,\langle{\rm div}\,\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}\left(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}+\left(\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}\right)\rangle-\langle\partial_{3}\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}\left(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}+\left(\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}\right)\rangle+\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}\,.

At this point, the computations mainly follow the same lines of
[
34
]
(see also
[
30
]
).
First of all, we notice that, in the last line, the second term on the right-hand side is another remainder. Indeed, using the definition of the function
\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}
and the fact
that the test-function
\boldsymbol{\psi}
does not depend on
x^{3}
, one has

\begin{split}\partial_{3}\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}\left(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}+\left(\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}\right)&=\partial_{3}\left(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}+\left(\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}\right)\right)-\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}\,\partial_{3}\left(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}+\left(\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}\right)\\
&=\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{h}\left|\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}\right|^{2}=\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}\,.\end{split}

Next, in order to deal with the first term, we use the first equation in (
3.3.8
) to obtain

\begin{split}{\rm div}\,\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}\left(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}+\left(\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}\right)&=-\varepsilon^{m}\partial_{t}\accentset{\approx}{\varrho}^{(1)}_{\varepsilon,M}\left(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}+\left(\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}\right)+\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}\\
&=\varepsilon^{m}\accentset{\approx}{\varrho}_{\varepsilon,M}^{(1)}\,\partial_{t}\left(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}+\left(\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}\right)+\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}\,.\end{split}

Now, equations (
3.3.8
) and (
3.3.18
) immediately yield that

\varepsilon^{m}\accentset{\approx}{\varrho}^{(1)}_{\varepsilon,M}\,\partial_{t}\left(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}+\left(\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}\right)=\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}-\accentset{\approx}{\varrho}_{\varepsilon,M}^{(1)}\,\nabla_{h}\accentset{\approx}{\varrho}^{(1)}_{\varepsilon,M}=\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{h}\left|\accentset{\approx}{\varrho}_{\varepsilon,M}^{(1)}\right|^{2}=\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}\,.

This relation finally implies that
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}\,=\,\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}
is a remainder, in the sense of relation (
3.3.11
):
for any
T>0
and any test-function
\boldsymbol{\psi}
as in (
3.3.12
),
one has the convergence
(at any
M\in\mathbb{N}
fixed, when
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
)

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\,{\rm d}x^{h}\,{\rm d}t\,\longrightarrow\,0\,.

(3.3.20)

The case
m+1=2n

In the case
m+1=2n
, most of the previous computations may be reproduced exactly in the same way.
The only (fundamental) change concerns relation (
3.3.19
): since now
m+1-2n=0
, that equation reads

\begin{split}{\rm curl}_{h}\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\;\left(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}\,=\,\left(\accentset{\approx}{\Phi}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\right)^{\perp}\,{\rm div}_{h}\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\,-\accentset{\approx}{\omega}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}\left(\left(\partial_{3}^{-1}{\rm curl}\,\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\varepsilon,M}\right)^{h}\right)^{\perp}+\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}\,,\end{split}

(3.3.21)

and, repeating the same computations performed for
\mathcal{T}^{2}_{\varepsilon,M}
in the previous paragraph, we have

\mathcal{T}^{2}_{\varepsilon,M}=\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}-\langle\accentset{\approx}{\omega}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}\left(\left(\partial_{3}^{-1}{\rm curl}\,\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\varepsilon,M}\right)^{h}\right)^{\perp}\rangle\,.

Hence, the main difference with respect to the previous case is that we have to take care of the term
\widetilde{\omega}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}\left(\left(\partial_{3}^{-1}{\rm curl}\,\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\varepsilon,M}\right)^{h}\right)^{\perp}
, which is non-linear and of order
O(1)
, so it may potentially
give rise to oscillations which persist in the limit.

In order to show that this does not happen, we make use of definition (
3.3.3
) of
\boldsymbol{g}_{\varepsilon}
to compute

\displaystyle\left({\rm curl}\,\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\varepsilon,M}\right)^{h,\perp}\,

\displaystyle=\,\left({\rm curl}\,\left(\accentset{\approx}{\varrho}_{\varepsilon,M}^{(1)}\nabla_{x}G-\nabla_{x}\accentset{\approx}{\Pi}_{\varepsilon,M}\right)\right)^{h,\perp}

\displaystyle=\,\begin{pmatrix}-\partial_{2}\accentset{\approx}{\varrho}^{(1)}_{\varepsilon,M}\\
\partial_{1}\accentset{\approx}{\varrho}^{(1)}_{\varepsilon,M}\\
0\end{pmatrix}^{h,\perp}\,=-\,\nabla_{h}\accentset{\approx}{\varrho}_{\varepsilon,M}^{(1)}\,.

From this relation, in turn we get

\mathcal{T}^{2}_{\varepsilon,M}\,=\,\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}\,+\,\langle\accentset{\approx}{\omega}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}\,\partial_{3}^{-1}\nabla_{h}\accentset{\approx}{\varrho}_{\varepsilon,M}^{(1)}\rangle\,.

(3.3.22)

Now, we have to employ the potential part of the momentum equation in (
3.3.8
), which has not been used so far. Taking the oscillating
component of the solutions, we obtain

\nabla_{h}\accentset{\approx}{\varrho}_{\varepsilon,M}^{(1)}\,=-\,\varepsilon^{m}\,\partial_{t}\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}^{h}_{\varepsilon,M}\,-\varepsilon^{m-1}(\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}^{h}_{\varepsilon,M})^{\perp}+\varepsilon^{m}\,\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{f}}^{h}_{\varepsilon,M}\,+\varepsilon^{2(m-n)}\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{g}}^{h}_{\varepsilon,M}=-\,\varepsilon^{m}\,\partial_{t}\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}^{h}_{\varepsilon,M}\,+\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}\,.

Inserting this relation into (
3.3.22
) and using (
3.3.18
), we finally gather

\mathcal{T}^{2}_{\varepsilon,M}=-\varepsilon^{m}\langle\accentset{\approx}{\omega}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}\,\partial_{t}\partial_{3}^{-1}\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}^{h}_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle+\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}=\varepsilon^{m}\langle\partial_{t}\accentset{\approx}{\omega}_{\varepsilon,M}^{3}\,\partial_{3}^{-1}\accentset{\approx}{\boldsymbol{V}}^{h}_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle+\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}=\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}\,,

because we have taken
m>1
.

This relation finally implies that, also in the case when
m+1=2n
,
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}
is a remainder: for any
T>0
and any test-function
\boldsymbol{\psi}
as in (
3.3.12
), one has the convergence (
3.3.20
).

3.3.3
The limit system

Thanks to the computations of the previous subsections, we can now pass to the limit in equation (
3.1.17
). Recall that
m>1
and
m+1\geq 2n>m
here.

To begin with, we take a test-function
\boldsymbol{\psi}
as in (
3.3.12
), specifically

\boldsymbol{\psi}=\big(\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\phi,0\big)\,,\qquad\qquad\mbox{ with }\qquad\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}\big([0,T[\,\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)\,,\quad\phi=\phi(t,x^{h})\,.

(3.3.23)

We point out that since all the integrals will be made on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
(in view of the choice of the test functions in (
3.3.23
) above), we can work on the domain
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\,]0,1[\,
.

In addition, for such
\boldsymbol{\psi}
as in (
3.3.23
), all the gradient terms vanish identically, as well as all the contributions
due to the vertical component of the equation. In particular, we do not see any contribution of the pressure and gravity terms: equation (
3.1.17
) becomes

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\Omega}

\displaystyle\left(-\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{h}\cdot\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}-\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{h}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{h}:\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\varrho_{\varepsilon}\big(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{h}\big)^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\right)\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t

(3.3.24)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=-\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{S}(\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}}):\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t+\int_{\Omega}\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}(0,\cdot)\,{\rm d}x\,.

Making use of the uniform bounds of Subsection
3.2.1
, we can pass to the limit in the
\partial_{t}
term and in the viscosity term.
Moreover, our assumptions imply that
\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
in e.g.
L_{\rm loc}^{2}
.
Next, the Coriolis term can be arranged in a standard way: using the structure of
\boldsymbol{\psi}
and the mass equation (
3.1.16
), we can write

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\varrho_{\varepsilon}\big(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{h}\big)^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\,

\displaystyle=\,\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\langle\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{h}\rangle\cdot\nabla_{h}\phi\,=\,-\varepsilon^{m-1}\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\langle\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}\rangle\,\partial_{t}\phi\,-\,\varepsilon^{m-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\langle\varrho^{(1)}_{0,\varepsilon}\rangle\,\phi(0,\cdot)\,,

which of course converges to
0
when
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
.

It remains us to tackle the convective term
\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{h}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{h}
.
For it, we take advantage of Lemma
3.3.4
and relations (
3.3.16
) and (
3.3.20
), but
we still have to take care of the convergence for
M\rightarrow+\infty
in (
3.3.16
).
We start by performing equalities (
3.3.14
) backwards in the term on the right-hand side of (
3.3.16
): thus, we have to pass to the limit for
M\rightarrow+\infty

in

\int^{T}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\boldsymbol{U}_{M}^{h}\otimes\boldsymbol{U}_{M}^{h}:\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\,\,{\rm d}x^{h}\,\,{\rm d}t\,.

Now, we remark that, since
\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\in L^{2}_{T}(H^{1})
by (
3.2.7
), from (
A.1.3
) we gather the strong convergence

S_{M}\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\longrightarrow\boldsymbol{U}^{h}
in
L_{T}^{2}(H^{s})
for any
s<1
, in the limit for
M\rightarrow+\infty
.
Then, passing to the limit for
M\rightarrow+\infty
in the previous relation is an easy task: we finally get, for
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
,

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{h}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{h}:\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\,\longrightarrow\,\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\otimes\boldsymbol{U}^{h}:\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\,.

In the end, we have shown that, letting
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
in (
3.3.24
), one obtains

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\cdot\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}+\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\otimes\boldsymbol{U}^{h}:\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\right)\,\,{\rm d}x^{h}\,{\rm d}t=\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mu\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{U}^{h}:\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\,\,{\rm d}x^{h}\,{\rm d}t-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{h}\rangle\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}(0,\cdot)\,{\rm d}x^{h},

for any test-function
\boldsymbol{\psi}
as in (
3.3.12
).
This implies (
3.1.19
), concluding the proof of Theorem
3.1.5
.

3.4
Proof of the convergence for
m=1

In the present section, we complete the proof of the convergence in the case
m=1
and
1/2<n<1
.
We will use again the compensated compactness argument depicted in Subsection
3.3.2
,
and in fact most of the computations apply also in this case.

3.4.1
The acoustic-Poincaré waves system

When
m=1
, the wave system (
3.3.4
) takes the form

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\varepsilon\,\partial_{t}\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\,+\,{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\,=\,0\\[4.30554pt]
\varepsilon\,\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\,+\,\nabla_{x}\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}\,+\,\,\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\times\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\,=\,\varepsilon\,\boldsymbol{f}_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{2(1-n)}\boldsymbol{g}_{\varepsilon}\,,\end{array}\right.

(3.4.1)

where
\bigl(\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}\bigr)_{\varepsilon}
and
\bigl(\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\bigr)_{\varepsilon}
are defined as in Paragraph
3.3.1.1
.
This system is supplemented with the initial datum
\big(\varrho^{(1)}_{0,\varepsilon},\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\big)
.

Next, we regularise all the quantities, by applying the Littlewood-Paley cut-off operator
S_{M}
to (
3.4.1
): we deduce that
\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon,M}
and
\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}
, defined as in (
3.3.6
), satisfy the regularised wave system

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\varepsilon\,\partial_{t}\varrho_{\varepsilon,M}^{(1)}\,+\,{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}\,=\,0\\[4.30554pt]
\varepsilon\,\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}\,+\,\nabla_{x}\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon,M}\,+\,\,\boldsymbol{e}_{3}\times\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}\,=\,\varepsilon\,\boldsymbol{f}_{\varepsilon,M}+\varepsilon^{2(1-n)}\boldsymbol{g}_{\varepsilon,M}\,,\end{array}\right.

(3.4.2)

in the domain
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Omega
, where we recall that
\boldsymbol{f}_{\varepsilon,M}:=S_{M}\boldsymbol{f}_{\varepsilon}
and
\boldsymbol{g}_{\varepsilon,M}:=S_{M}\boldsymbol{g}_{\varepsilon}
.
It goes without saying that a result similar to Proposition
3.3.2
holds true also in this case.

As it is apparent from the wave system (
3.4.1
) and its regularised version, when
m=1
the pressure term and the Coriolis term are in balance, since they are of the same order. This represents the main change with respect to the case
m>1
, and it comes into play in the compensated compactness argument. Therefore, despite most of the computations may be repeated identical as in the previous section, let us present
the main points of the argument.

3.4.2
Handling the convective term when
m=1

Let us take care of the convergence of the convective term when
m=1
.

First of all, it is easy to see that Lemma
3.3.4
still holds true. Therefore,
given a test-function
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in C_{c}^{\infty}\big([0,T[\,\times\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3}\big)
such that
{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}=0
and
\partial_{3}\boldsymbol{\psi}=0
,
we have to pass to the limit in the term

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}\otimes\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,

\displaystyle=\,\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}{\rm div}\,\left(\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}\otimes\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\,=\,\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}+\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\,,

where we agree again that the torus
\mathbb{T}^{1}
has been normalised so that its Lebesgue measure is equal to
1
and we have adopted the same notation as in (
3.3.13
).

At this point, we notice that the analysis of
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{2}
can be performed as in Paragraph
3.3.2.2
, because we have

m+1>2n
, i.e.
n<1
.
Mutatis mutandis
, we find relation (
3.3.20
) also in the case
m=1
.

Let us now deal with the term
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}
. Arguing as in Paragraph
3.3.2.1
, we may write it as

\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon,M}^{1}\,=\,\left({\rm curl}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle-\langle\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\right)\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle^{\perp}+\mathcal{R}_{\varepsilon,M}.

Now we use the horizontal part of (
3.4.2
):
averaging it with respect to the vertical variable and applying the operator
{\rm curl}_{h}
, we find

\varepsilon\,\partial_{t}{\rm curl}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,+\,{\rm div}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,=\,\varepsilon\,{\rm curl}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{f}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,.

Taking the difference of this equation with the first one in (
3.4.2
), we discover that

\partial_{t}\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\varepsilon,M}\,=\,{\rm curl}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{f}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,,\qquad\qquad\mbox{ where }\qquad\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\varepsilon,M}:={\rm curl}_{h}\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle\,-\,\langle\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon,M}\rangle\,.

An argument analogous to the one used after (
3.3.15
) above, based on Aubin-Lions Theorem, shows that

\big(\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\varepsilon,M}\big)_{\varepsilon}
is compact in e.g.
L_{T}^{2}(L_{\rm loc}^{2})
. Then, this sequence converges strongly (up to extraction of a suitable subsequence, not relabelled here) to a tempered distribution
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{M}
in the same space.

Using the previous property, we may deduce that

\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\varepsilon,M}\,\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon,M}^{h}\rangle^{\perp}\,\longrightarrow\,\widetilde{\Gamma}_{M}\,\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{M}^{h}\rangle^{\perp}\qquad\text{ in }\qquad\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\big(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\big),

where we have
\langle\boldsymbol{V}_{M}^{h}\rangle=\langle S_{M}\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\rangle
and
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{M}={\rm curl}_{h}\langle S_{M}\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\rangle-\langle S_{M}\varrho^{(1)}\rangle
.

Owing to the regularity of the target velocity
\boldsymbol{U}^{h}
, we can pass to the limit also for
M\rightarrow+\infty
, as detailed in Subsection
3.3.3
above. Thus, we find

\int^{T}_{0}\!\!\!\int_{\Omega}\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\,\longrightarrow\,\int^{T}_{0}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\big(\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\otimes\boldsymbol{U}^{h}:\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\,-\,\varrho^{(1)}\,(\boldsymbol{U}^{h})^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\big)\,{\rm d}x^{h}\,{\rm d}t,

(3.4.3)

for all test functions
\boldsymbol{\psi}
such that
{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}=0
and
\partial_{3}\boldsymbol{\psi}=0
. Recall the convention
|\mathbb{T}^{1}|=1
.
Notice that, since
\boldsymbol{U}^{h}=\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\varrho^{(1)}
when
m=1
(keep in mind Proposition
3.2.4
), the last term in the integral on the right-hand side is actually zero.

3.4.3
End of the study

Thanks to the previous analysis, we are now ready to pass to the limit in equation (
3.1.17
) also in the case when
m=1
.
For this, we take a test-function
\boldsymbol{\psi}
as in (
3.3.23
);
notice in particular that
{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}=0
and
\partial_{3}\boldsymbol{\psi}=0
. Then, once again all the gradient terms and all the contributions coming from the vertical
component of the momentum equation vanish identically, when tested against such a
\boldsymbol{\psi}
. Recall that all the integrals will be performed on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
So, equation (
3.1.17
) reduces
to

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\Omega}\left(-\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\psi}-\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}:\nabla\boldsymbol{\psi}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\varrho_{\varepsilon}\big(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{h}\big)^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}+\mathbb{S}(\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}}):\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}\right)=\int_{\Omega}\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}(0,\cdot)\,.

For the rotation term, we can test the first equation in (
3.4.1
) against
\phi
to get

\begin{split}-\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\langle\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}\rangle\,\partial_{t}\phi+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\,\langle\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{h}\rangle\cdot\nabla_{h}\phi\right)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\langle\varrho^{(1)}_{0,\varepsilon}\rangle\,\phi(0,\cdot)\,,\end{split}

whence we deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\varrho_{\varepsilon}\big(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{h}\big)^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}^{h}\,

\displaystyle=\,\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\langle\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{h}\rangle\cdot\nabla_{h}\phi\,=\,-\,\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\langle\varrho^{(1)}_{\varepsilon}\rangle\,\partial_{t}\phi\,-\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\langle\varrho^{(1)}_{0,\varepsilon}\rangle\,\phi(0,\cdot)\,.

In addition, the convergence of the convective term has been performed in (
3.4.3
), and for the other terms, we can argue as in Subsection
3.3.3
.
Hence, letting
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
in the equation above, we get

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\cdot\partial_{t}\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\phi+\boldsymbol{U}^{h}\otimes\boldsymbol{U}^{h}:\nabla_{h}(\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\phi)+\varrho^{(1)}\,\partial_{t}\phi\right)\,{\rm d}x^{h}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=-\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mu\nabla_{h}\boldsymbol{U}^{h}:\nabla_{h}(\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\phi)\,{\rm d}x^{h}\,{\rm d}t+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{h}\rangle\cdot\nabla_{h}^{\perp}\phi(0,\cdot)+\langle\varrho^{(1)}_{0}\rangle\phi(0,\cdot)\right)\,{\rm d}x^{h}\,,

which is the weak formulation of equation (
3.1.20
). In the end, also Theorem
3.1.6
is proved.

Part II
The Euler equations

Chapter 4
The fast rotation limit for Euler

We conclude this thesis describing the behaviour of a fluid which, in contrast with Chapters
2
and
3
, evolves far from the physical boundaries. An example of such fluid flows is represented by currents in the middle of the oceans, i.e. far enough from the surface and the bottom. In this context, we can assume the following physical approximations:

•

the fluid is incompressible;

•

the density is a small perturbation of a constant profile (the
Boussinesq
approximation);

•

the domain is the
\mathbb{R}^{2}
plane: the motion of a
3
-D highly rotating fluid is, in a first approximation, planar (the
Taylor-Proudman
theorem).

Then, the system reads:

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\varrho+{\rm div}\,(\varrho\boldsymbol{u})=0\\
\partial_{t}(\varrho\boldsymbol{u})+{\rm div}\,(\varrho\boldsymbol{u}\otimes\boldsymbol{u})+\dfrac{1}{Ro}\varrho\boldsymbol{u}^{\perp}+\nabla p=0\\
{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{u}=0\end{cases}

(4.0.1)

in the domain
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Since in this chapter there will be no more competition between the horizontal and vertical scales, for notational convenience, we will drop everywhere (in the differential operators) the subscript
x
.

With respect to the previous systems (see (
2.0.1
) and (
3.0.1
) in this respect), the Euler equations (
4.0.1
) is an incompressible system without the viscosity effects and with a hyperbolic structure. For that reason, we will need different analysis techniques (see Appendix
A
) and in addition the functional framework will change (now we will be in regular spaces) in order to preserve the initial regularity.

The topics presented here are part of the pre-print
[
65
]
.

Let us now give a summary of the chapter.

In Section
4.1
, we collect our assumptions and we state our main results.
In Section
4.2
, we investigate the well-posedness issues in the Sobolev spaces
H^{s}
for any
s>2
.
In Section
4.3
, we study the singular perturbation problem, establishing constraints that the limit points have to satisfy and proving the convergence to the quasi-homogeneous Euler system thanks to a
compensated compactness
technique.
In Section
4.4
we review, for the quasi-homogeneous limiting system, the results presented in
[
18
]
and
[
16
]
, and
we explicitly derive the lifespan of solutions to the limit equations (see relation (
4.4.34
) in this regard).

In the last section, we deal with the lifespan analysis for system (
4.0.1
) and we point out some consequences of the continuation criterion we have established (see in particular Subsection
4.5.1
).

4.1
The density-dependent Euler problem

In this section, we formulate our working setting (Subsection
4.1.1
) and we state the main results
(Subsection
4.1.2
).

4.1.1
Formulation

In this subsection, we present the rescaled density-dependent Euler equations with the Coriolis force, which we are going to consider in our study, and we formulate the main working hypotheses.

To begin with, let us introduce the “primitive system”, that is the rescaled incompressible Euler system (
4.0.1
), supplemented with the scaling
Ro=\varepsilon
, where
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
is a small parameter. Thus, the system consists of continuity equation (conservation of mass), the momentum equation and the divergence-free condition: respectively

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\varrho_{\varepsilon}+{\rm div}\,(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})=0\\
\partial_{t}(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})+{\rm div}\,(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})+\dfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\perp}+\dfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}=0\\
{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}=0\,.\end{cases}

(4.1.1)

We point out that, here above in (
4.1.1
), the domain
\Omega
is the plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and the unknowns are
\varrho_{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

In (
4.1.1
), the pressure term has to scale like
1/\varepsilon
, since it is the only force that allows to compensate the effect of fast rotation, at the geophysical scale.

From now on, in order to make the Lipschitz condition (
A.3.1
) holds, we fix

s>2\,.

We assume that the initial density is a small perturbation of a constant profile.
Namely, we consider initial densities of the following form:

\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}=1+\varepsilon\,R_{0,\varepsilon}\,,

(4.1.2)

where we suppose
R_{0,\varepsilon}
to be a bounded measurable function satisfying the controls

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]}\left\|R_{0,\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,\leq\,C

(4.1.3)

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]}\left\|\nabla R_{0,\varepsilon}\right\|_{H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,\leq\,C

(4.1.4)

and the initial mass density is bounded and bounded away from zero, i.e. for all
\varepsilon\in\;]0,1]
:

0<\underline{\varrho}\leq\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}(x)\,\leq\,\overline{\varrho}\,,\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}

(4.1.5)

where
\underline{\varrho},\overline{\varrho}>0
are positive constants.

As for the initial velocity fields, due to framework needed for the well-posedness issues, we require the following uniform bound

\sup_{\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]}\left\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\right\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,\leq\,C\,.

(4.1.6)

Thanks to the previous uniform estimates, we can assume (up to passing to subsequences) that there exist
R_{0}\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, with
\nabla R_{0}\in H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that

\begin{split}R_{0}:=\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}R_{0,\varepsilon}\quad&\text{in}\quad L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\\
\nabla R_{0}:=\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\nabla R_{0,\varepsilon}\quad&\text{in}\quad H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\\
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}:=\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\quad&\text{in}\quad H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\,,\end{split}

(4.1.7)

where we agree that the previous limits are taken in the corresponding weak-
\ast
topology.

4.1.2
Main statements

We can now state our main results. We recall the notation
\big(f_{\varepsilon}\big)_{\varepsilon}\subset X
to denote that the family
\big(f_{\varepsilon}\big)_{\varepsilon}
is uniformly (in
\varepsilon
) bounded in
X
.

The following theorem establishes the local well-posedness of system (
4.1.1
) in the Sobolev spaces
B^{s}_{2,2}\equiv H^{s}
(see Section
4.2
) and gives a lower bound for the lifespan of solutions (see Section
4.5
).

Theorem 4.1.1

For any
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
, let initial densities
\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}
be as in (
4.1.2
) and satisfy the controls (
4.1.3
) to (
4.1.5
). Let
\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}
be divergence-free vector fields such that
\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for
s>2
.

Then, for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a time
T_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}>0
such that the system (
4.1.1
) has a unique solution
(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon},\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon})
where

•

\varrho_{\varepsilon}
belongs to the space
C^{0}([0,T_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}]\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
\nabla\varrho_{\varepsilon}\in C^{0}([0,T_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}];H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
;

•

\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}
belongs to the space
C^{0}([0,T_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}];H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
;

•

\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}
belongs to the space
C^{0}([0,T_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}];H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
.

Moreover, the lifespan
T_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}
of the solution to the two-dimensional density-dependent incompressible Euler equations with the Coriolis force is bounded from below by

\frac{C}{\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\|_{H^{s}}}\log\left(\log\left(\frac{C\,\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\|_{H^{s}}}{\max\{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}(0),\,\varepsilon\,\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}(0)\,\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\|_{H^{s}}\}}+1\right)+1\right)\,,

(4.1.8)

where
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}(0):=\|\nabla R_{0,\varepsilon}\|_{H^{s-1}}+\varepsilon\,\|\nabla R_{0,\varepsilon}\|_{H^{s-1}}^{\lambda+1}
, for some suitable
\lambda\geq 1
.

In particular, one has

\inf_{\varepsilon>0}T_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}>0\,.

Looking at (
4.1.8
), we stress the fact that the fast rotational effects are not enough to state a global well-posedness result for system (
4.1.1
), in the sense that
T^{\ast}_{\varepsilon}
does not tend to
+\infty
when
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
.

Now, once we have stated the local in time well-posedness for system (
4.1.1
) in the Sobolev spaces
H^{s}
, in Section
4.3
we address the singular perturbation problem describing, in a rigorous way, the limit dynamics depicted by the quasi-homogeneous incompressible Euler system (
4.1.9
) below.

Theorem 4.1.2

Let
s>2
. For any fixed value of
\varepsilon\in\;]0,1]
, let initial data
\left(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\right)
verify the hypotheses fixed in Paragraph
4.1.1
, and let

\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\right)
be a corresponding solution to system (
4.1.1
).
Let
\left(R_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\right)
be defined as in (
4.1.7
).

Then, one has the following convergence properties:

\displaystyle\varrho_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow 1\qquad\qquad\mbox{ in }\qquad

\displaystyle L^{\infty}\big([0,T^{\ast}];L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big)\,,

\displaystyle R_{\varepsilon}:=\frac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1}{\varepsilon}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\rightharpoonup}}R\qquad\qquad\mbox{ in }\qquad

\displaystyle L^{\infty}\bigl([0,T^{\ast}];L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\bigr)\,,

\displaystyle\nabla R_{\varepsilon}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\rightharpoonup}}\nabla R\qquad\qquad\mbox{ in }\qquad

\displaystyle L^{\infty}\bigl([0,T^{\ast}];H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\bigr)\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\rightharpoonup}}\boldsymbol{u}\qquad\qquad\mbox{ in }\qquad

\displaystyle L^{\infty}\big([0,T^{\ast}];H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big)\,.

In addition,
\Big(R\,,\,\boldsymbol{u}\Big)
is a solution
to the quasi-homogeneous incompressible Euler system in
[0,T^{\ast}]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
:

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}R+{\rm div}\,(R\boldsymbol{u})=0\\
\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}+{\rm div}\,\left(\boldsymbol{u}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}\right)+R\boldsymbol{u}^{\perp}+\nabla\Pi=0\\
{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{u}\,=\,0\,,\end{cases}

(4.1.9)

where
\nabla\Pi
is a suitable pressure term belonging to
L^{\infty}\big([0,T^{\ast}];H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big)
.

Remark 4.1.3

Due to the fact that the system (
4.1.9
) is well-posed in the previous functional setting (see Theorem
4.1.4
below), we get the convergence of the whole sequence of weak solutions to the solutions of the target equations on the large time interval where the weak solutions to the primitive equations exist.

Then, at the limit, we have found that the dynamics is prescribed by the quasi-homogeneous incompressible Euler system (
4.1.9
), for which we state the local well-posedness in
H^{s}
(see Section
4.4
). It is worth to remark that the global well-posedness issue for this system is still an open problem.

Theorem 4.1.4

Take
s>2
. Let
\big(R_{0},u_{0}\big)
be initial data such that
R_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and

\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\,\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, with
\nabla R_{0}\in H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\,=\,0
.

Then, there exists a time
T^{\ast}>0
such that, on
[0,T^{\ast}]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, problem (
4.1.9
) has a unique solution
(R,\boldsymbol{u},\nabla\Pi)
with the following properties:

•

R\in C^{0}\big([0,T^{\ast}]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)
and
\nabla R\in C^{0}\big([0,T^{\ast}];H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big)
;

•

\boldsymbol{u}
belongs to
C^{0}\big([0,T^{\ast}];H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big)
;

•

the pressure term
\nabla\Pi
is in
C^{0}\big([0,T^{\ast}];H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big)
.

In addition, the lifespan
T^{\ast}>0
of the solution
(R,\boldsymbol{u},\nabla\Pi)
to the
2
-D quasi-homogeneous Euler system (
4.1.9
) enjoys the following lower bound:

T^{\ast}\geq\frac{C}{\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\|_{H^{s}}}\log\left(\log\left(C\frac{\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\|_{H^{s}}}{\|R_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\nabla R_{0}\|_{H^{s-1}}}+1\right)+1\right)\,,

(4.1.10)

where
C>0
is a “universal” constant, independent of the initial datum.

The proof of the previous “asymptotically global” well-posedness result is presented in Subsection
4.4.3
.

4.2
Well-posedness for the original problem

This section is devoted to the well-posedness issue in the
H^{s}
spaces stated in Theorem
4.1.1
. We recall that, due to the Littlewood-Paley theory, we have the equivalence between
H^{s}
and
B^{s}_{2,2}
spaces.

We also underline that in this section we keep
\varepsilon\in\;]0,1]
fixed. However, we will take care of it, explicitly pointing out the dependence to the Rossby number in all the computations in order to get controls that are uniform with respect to the
\varepsilon
-parameter. The choice in keeping explicit the dependence on the rotational parameter is motivated by the fact that we will perform the fast rotation limit (see Section
4.3
below).

First of all, since
\varrho_{\varepsilon}
is a small perturbation of a constant profile, we set

\alpha_{\varepsilon}:=\frac{1}{\varrho_{\varepsilon}}-1=\varepsilon a_{\varepsilon}\quad\text{with}\quad a_{\varepsilon}:=-R_{\varepsilon}/\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,.

(4.2.1)

The choice of looking at
\alpha_{\varepsilon}
is dictated by the fact that we will extensively exploit the elliptic equation

-{\rm div}\,\left(\alpha_{\varepsilon}\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}\right)=\,\varepsilon\,{\rm div}\,\left(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\perp}\right)\,.

(4.2.2)

Now, using the divergence-free condition, we can rewrite the system (
4.1.1
) in the following way (see also Lemma 3 in
[
22
]
):

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}a_{\varepsilon}+\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla a_{\varepsilon}=0\\
\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}+\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}+\dfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\perp}+(1+\varepsilon a_{\varepsilon})\dfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}=0\\
{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}=0\,,\end{cases}

(4.2.3)

with the initial condition
(a_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})_{|t=0}=(a_{0,\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon})
.

We start by presenting the proof of existence of solutions at the claimed regularity.
For that scope, we follow a standard procedure: first, we construct a sequence of smooth approximate solutions. Next, we deduce uniform bounds (with respect to the approximation parameter and also to
\varepsilon
) for those regular solutions. Finally, by use of those uniform bounds and an energy method, together with an interpolation argument, we are able to take the limit in the approximation parameter and gather the existence of a solution to the original problem.

We end this Section
4.2
, proving uniqueness of solutions in the claimed functional setting, by using a relative entropy method.

4.2.1
Construction of smooth approximate solutions

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let us define

(a_{0,\varepsilon}^{n},\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}^{n}):=(S_{n}a_{0,\varepsilon},S_{n}\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon})\,,

where
S_{n}
is the low frequency cut-off operator introduced in (
A.1.1
) in Section
A.1
. We stress also the fact that
a_{0,\varepsilon}\in C^{0}_{\rm loc}
, since
a_{0,\varepsilon}
and
\nabla a_{0,\varepsilon}
are in
L^{\infty}
.

Then, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have the density functions
a_{0,\varepsilon}^{n}\in L^{\infty}
. Moreover, one has that
\nabla a_{0,\varepsilon}^{n}
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}^{n}
belong to
H^{\infty}:=\bigcap_{\sigma\in\mathbb{R}}H^{\sigma}
which is embedded (for a suitable topology on
H^{\infty}
) in the space
C_{b}^{\infty}
of
C^{\infty}
functions which are globally bounded together with all their derivatives.

In addition, by standard properties of mollifiers (see Section
B.2
of the Appendix
B
), one has the following strong convergences

\begin{split}a^{n}_{0,\varepsilon}\rightarrow a_{0,\varepsilon}\quad&\text{in}\quad C_{\rm loc}^{0}\\
\nabla a^{n}_{0,\varepsilon}\rightarrow\nabla a_{0,\varepsilon}\quad&\text{in}\quad H^{s-1}\\
\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}^{n}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\quad&\text{in}\quad H^{s}\,.\end{split}

(4.2.4)

This having been established, we are going to define a sequence of approximate solutions to system (
4.2.3
) by induction. First of all, we set
(a_{\varepsilon}^{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{0},\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{0})=(a^{0}_{0,\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}^{0}_{0,\varepsilon},0)
. Then, for all
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
, we have that
\nabla a_{\varepsilon}^{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{0}\in H^{\sigma}
and
a^{0}_{\varepsilon}\in L^{\infty}
with
{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{0}=0
. Next, assume that the couple
(a_{\varepsilon}^{n},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n})
is given such that, for all
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
,

a^{n}_{\varepsilon}\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+};L^{\infty})\quad\nabla a_{\varepsilon}^{n},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+};H^{\sigma})\quad\quad\text{and}\quad{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}=0\,.

First of all, we define
a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}
as the unique solution to the linear transport equation

\partial_{t}a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}+\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\cdot\nabla a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}=0\quad\text{with}\quad{(a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1})}_{|t=0}=a_{0,\varepsilon}^{n+1}\,.

(4.2.5)

Since, by inductive hypothesis and embeddings,
\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}
is divergence-free, smooth and uniformly bounded with all its derivatives, we can deduce that
a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+};L^{\infty})
. Moreover, from

\partial_{t}\,\partial_{i}a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}+\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\cdot\nabla\,\partial_{i}a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}=-\partial_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\cdot\nabla a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\quad\text{with}\quad{(\partial_{i}a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1})}_{|t=0}=\partial_{i}a_{0,\varepsilon}^{n+1}\quad\text{for}\;i=1,2

and thanks to the Theorem
A.15
, we can propagate all the
H^{\sigma}
norms of the initial datum. We deduce that
a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+};L^{\infty})
and
\nabla a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+};H^{\sigma})
for any
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
.
Next, we consider the approximate linear iteration

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}+\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}+\dfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\perp,n+1}+(1+\varepsilon a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1})\dfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}=0\\
{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}=0\\
(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1})_{|t=0}=\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}^{n+1}\,.\end{cases}

(4.2.6)

At this point, one can solve the previous linear problem finding a unique solution
\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+};H^{\sigma})
for any
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
and the pressure term
\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}
can be uniquely determined (we refer to
[
20
]
for details in this respect).

4.2.2
Uniform estimates for the approximate solutions

We now have to show (by induction) uniform bounds for the sequence
(a_{\varepsilon}^{n},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n},\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
we have constructed above.

We start by finding uniform estimates for
a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}
. Thanks to equation (
4.2.5
) and the divergence-free condition on
\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}
, we can propagate the
L^{\infty}
norm for any
t\geq 0
:

\|a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\|a_{0,\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\|a_{0,\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\,.

(4.2.7)

At this point we want to estimate
\nabla a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}
in
H^{s-1}
. We have for
i=1,2
:

\partial_{t}\,\partial_{i}a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}+\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\cdot\nabla\,\partial_{i}a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}=-\partial_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\cdot\nabla a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\,.

Taking the non-homogeneous dyadic blocks
\Delta_{j}
, we obtain

\partial_{t}\Delta_{j}\,\partial_{i}a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}+\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\cdot\nabla\Delta_{j}\,\partial_{i}a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}=[\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\cdot\nabla,\Delta_{j}]\,\partial_{i}a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}-\Delta_{j}\left(\partial_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\cdot\nabla a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\right)\,.

Multiplying the previous relation by
\Delta_{j}\,\partial_{i}a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}
, we have

\|\Delta_{j}\,\partial_{i}a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}(t)\|_{L^{2}}\leq\|\Delta_{j}\,\partial_{i}a_{0,\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{2}}+C\int_{0}^{t}\left(\left\|[\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\cdot\nabla,\Delta_{j}]\,\partial_{i}a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\|\Delta_{j}\left(\partial_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\cdot\nabla a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\right)\|_{L^{2}}\right)\,\,{\rm d}\tau\,.

We apply now the second commutator estimate stated in Lemma
A.13
to the former term in the integral on the right-hand side, getting

2^{j(s-1)}\left\|[\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\cdot\nabla,\Delta_{j}]\,\partial_{i}a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\,c_{j}(t)\left(\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\partial_{i}a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{H^{s-1}}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\|_{H^{s-1}}\|\partial_{i}a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\,,

where
(c_{j}(t))_{j\geq-1}
is a sequence in the unit ball of
\ell^{2}
.

Instead, the latter term can be bounded in the following way:

2^{j(s-1)}\|\Delta_{j}\left(\partial_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\cdot\nabla a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\right)\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\,c_{j}(t)\,\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\|_{H^{s-1}}\|\nabla a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{H^{s-1}}\,.

Then, due to the embedding
H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for
\sigma>1
, one has

2^{j(s-1)}\|\Delta_{j}\,\nabla a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}(t)\|_{L^{2}}\leq 2^{j(s-1)}\|\Delta_{j}\nabla a_{0,\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{2}}+\int_{0}^{t}C\,c_{j}(\tau)\left(\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\|_{H^{s}}\|\nabla a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{H^{s-1}}\right)\,\,{\rm d}\tau\,.

At this point, after summing on indices
j\geq-1
, thanks to the Minkowski inequality (for which we refer to Proposition
B.6
) combined with a Grönwall type argument (see Section
B.6
), we finally obtain

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|\nabla a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}(t)\|_{H^{s-1}}\leq\|\nabla a_{0,\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{H^{s-1}}\,\exp\left(\int_{0}^{T}C\,\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}(t)\|_{H^{s}}\,\,{\rm d}t\right)\,.

(4.2.8)

Now, we have to estimate the velocity field
\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}
and for that purpose we start with the
L^{2}
estimate. We take the momentum equation in the original form:

\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\left(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}+\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\right)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\perp,n+1}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}=0\,,

(4.2.9)

where we construct
\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}:=1/(1+\varepsilon a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1})
starting from
a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}
. Notice that
\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}
satisfies the transport equation

\partial_{t}\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}+\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\cdot\nabla\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}=0\,.

At this point, we test equation (
4.2.9
) against
\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}
. We integrate by parts on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, deriving the following estimate:

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\partial_{t}|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}|^{2}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\cdot\nabla|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}|^{2}=0\,,

which implies (making use of the transport equation for
\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}
)

\left\|\sqrt{\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}(t)}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}(t)\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq\left\|\sqrt{\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}^{n+1}}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}^{n+1}\right\|_{L^{2}}\,.

From the previous bound, due to the assumption (
4.1.5
), we can deduce the preservation of the
L^{2}
norm for the velocity field
\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}
:

\left\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}(t)\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\left\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}^{n+1}\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\left\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{2}}\,.

Taking now the operator
\Delta_{j}
in the momentum equation in (
4.2.6
), we obtain

\partial_{t}\Delta_{j}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}+\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\cdot\nabla\Delta_{j}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}=[\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\cdot\nabla,\Delta_{j}]\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Delta_{j}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\perp,n+1}-\Delta_{j}\left[\left(1+\varepsilon a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\right)\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\right]

and multiplying again by
\Delta_{j}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}
, we have cancellations so that

\left\|\Delta_{j}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}(t)\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq\left\|\Delta_{j}\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}^{n+1}\right\|_{L^{2}}+C\int_{0}^{t}\left(\left\|[\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\cdot\nabla,\Delta_{j}]\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\Delta_{j}\left(a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}}\right)\,\,{\rm d}\tau\,.

(4.2.10)

As done before, we employ here the commutator estimates of Lemma
A.13
in order to have

\begin{split}2^{js}\left\|[\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\cdot\nabla,\Delta_{j}]\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\right\|_{L^{2}}&\leq C\,c_{j}\,\left(\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{H^{s}}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\|_{H^{s}}\right)\\
&\leq C\,c_{j}\,\left(\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\|_{H^{s}}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{H^{s}}\right)\,.\end{split}

For the latter term on the right-hand side of (
4.2.10
), we take advantage of the Bony decomposition (see Paragraph
A.2
) and we apply Proposition
A.10
. We may infer that

\begin{split}\left\|a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\right\|_{H^{s}}\leq C\left(\|a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\nabla a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{H^{s-1}}\right)\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{H^{s}}\,.\end{split}

To finish with, we have to find a uniform bound for the pressure term. For that, we apply the
{\rm div}\,
operator in (
4.2.6
). Thus, we aim at solving the elliptic problem

-{\rm div}\,\left(\left(1+\varepsilon a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\right)\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\right)=\,\varepsilon\,{\rm div}\,(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1})-{\rm curl}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\,.

(4.2.11)

Thanks to the assumption (
4.1.5
) and Lemma
B.10
, we can obtain

\begin{split}\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{2}}&\leq C\left(\varepsilon\,\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\perp,n+1}\|_{L^{2}}\right)\\
&\leq C\left(\varepsilon\,\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\|_{L^{2}}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{H^{s}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{2}}\right)\,.\end{split}

(4.2.12)

Now, we apply the spectral cut-off operator
\Delta_{j}
to (
4.2.11
). We get

-\,{\rm div}\,\left(A_{\varepsilon}\Delta_{j}\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\right)={\rm div}\,\left(\left[\Delta_{j},A_{\varepsilon}\right]\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\right)+\,{\rm div}\,\Delta_{j}F_{\varepsilon}\,,

for all
j\geq 0
and where we have defined
A_{\varepsilon}:=\left(1+\varepsilon a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\right)
and
F_{\varepsilon}:=\varepsilon\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}+\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\perp,n+1}
.
Hence multiplying both sides by
\Delta_{j}\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}
and integrating over
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we have

-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Delta_{j}\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}{\rm div}\,\left(A_{\varepsilon}\Delta_{j}\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\right)\,\,{\rm d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Delta_{j}\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}{\rm div}\,\left(\left[\Delta_{j},A_{\varepsilon}\right]\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\right)\,\,{\rm d}x+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Delta_{j}\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}{\rm div}\,\Delta_{j}F_{\varepsilon}\,\,{\rm d}x.

Since for
j\geq 0
we have
\|\Delta_{j}\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{2}}\sim 2^{j}\|\Delta_{j}\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{2}}
(according to Lemma
A.2
) and using Hölder’s inequality (see Proposition
B.5
) for the right-hand side, we obtain for all
j\geq 0
:

2^{j}\|\Delta_{j}\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq C\|\Delta_{j}\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{2}}\left(\|{\rm div}\,\left[\Delta_{j},A_{\varepsilon}\right]\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{2}}+\|{\rm div}\,\Delta_{j}F_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}\right)\,.

To deal with the former term on the right-hand side, we take advantage of the following commutator estimate (see Lemma
A.12
):

\|{\rm div}\,\left[\Delta_{j},A_{\varepsilon}\right]\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\,c_{j}\,2^{-j(s-1)}\|\nabla A_{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{s-1}}\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{H^{s-1}}\,,

for a suitable sequence
(c_{j})_{j}
belonging to the unit sphere of
\ell^{2}
.

After multiplying by
2^{j(s-1)}
, we get

2^{js}\|\Delta_{j}\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\left(c_{j}\,\|\nabla A_{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{s-1}}\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{H^{s-1}}+2^{j(s-1)}\|{\rm div}\,\Delta_{j}F_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}\right)\,.

Taking the
\ell^{2}
norm of both sides and summing up the low frequency blocks related to
\Delta_{-1}\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}
, we may have

\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{H^{s}}\leq C\left(\|\nabla A_{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{s-1}}\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{H^{s-1}}+\|{\rm div}\,F_{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{s-1}}+\|\Delta_{-1}\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{2}}\right)\,.

We observe that
\|\Delta_{-1}\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{2}}
and

\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{H^{s-1}}\leq C\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{2}}^{1/s}\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{H^{s}}^{1-1/s}\,.

Therefore,

\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{H^{s}}\leq C\left(\|\nabla A_{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{s-1}}\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{2}}^{1/s}\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{H^{s}}^{1-1/s}+\|{\rm div}\,F_{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{s-1}}+\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{2}}\right)\,.

Then applying Young’s inequality (see Proposition
B.4
) we finally infer that

\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{H^{s}}\leq C\left(\left(1+\|\nabla A_{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{s-1}}\right)^{s}\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{2}}+\|{\rm div}\,F_{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{s-1}}\right)\,.

(4.2.13)

It remains to analyse the term
{\rm div}\,F_{\varepsilon}
where
F_{\varepsilon}:=\varepsilon\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}+\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\perp,n+1}
. Due to the divergence-free conditions, we can write

{\rm div}\,(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1})=\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}:\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}

and as
H^{s-1}
is an algebra, the term
{\rm div}\,(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1})
is in
H^{s-1}
, with

\|{\rm div}\,(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1})\|_{H^{s-1}}\leq C\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\|_{H^{s}}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{H^{s}}\,.

(4.2.14)

Putting (
4.2.12
) and (
4.2.14
) into (
4.2.13
), we find that

\begin{split}\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{H^{s}}&\leq C\left(1+\varepsilon\|\nabla a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{H^{s-1}}\right)^{s}\left(\varepsilon\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\|_{L^{2}}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{H^{s}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\perp,n+1}\|_{L^{2}}\right)\\
&+C\,\left(\varepsilon\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\|_{H^{s}}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{H^{s}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\perp,n+1}\|_{H^{s}}\right)\\
&\leq C\left(1+\varepsilon\|\nabla a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{H^{s-1}}\right)^{s}\left(\varepsilon\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\|_{H^{s}}+1\right)\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{H^{s}}\,,\end{split}

(4.2.15)

which implies the
L^{\infty}_{T}(H^{s})
estimate for the pressure term:

\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s}}\leq C\left(1+\varepsilon\|\nabla a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s-1}}\right)^{s}\left(\varepsilon\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s}}+1\right)\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s}}\,.

(4.2.16)

Combining all the previous estimates together with a Grönwall type inequality (again we refer to Section
B.6
), we finally obtain an estimate for the velocity field:

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}(t)\|_{H^{s}}\leq\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{H^{s}}\exp\left(\int_{0}^{T}A_{n}(t)\,\,{\rm d}t\right)\,,

(4.2.17)

where

\begin{split}A_{n}(t)=C\left(\|a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\nabla a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}(t)\|_{H^{s-1}}\right)\left(1+\varepsilon\|\nabla a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}(t)\|_{H^{s-1}}\right)^{s}\left(\varepsilon\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}(t)\|_{H^{s}}+1\right)+C\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}(t)\|_{H^{s}}\,.\end{split}

We point out that the above constants
C
do not depend on
n
nor on
\varepsilon
.

The scope in what follows is to obtain uniform estimates by induction. Thanks to the assumptions stated in Paragraph
4.1.1
, we can suppose that the initial data satisfy

\|a_{0,\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\frac{C_{0}}{2}\,,\quad\quad\|\nabla a_{0,\varepsilon}\|_{H^{s-1}}\leq\frac{C_{1}}{2}\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\|_{H^{s}}\leq\frac{C_{2}}{2}\,,

for some
C_{0},C_{1},C_{2}>0
. Due to the relation (
4.2.7
) we immediately infer that, for all
n\geq 0
,

\|a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{\infty}}\leq C\|a_{0,\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\,C_{0}\quad\text{for all }t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}.

At this point, the aim is to show (by induction) that the following uniform bounds hold for all
n\geq 0
:

\begin{split}&\|\nabla a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T^{\ast}}H^{s-1}}\leq C_{1}\\
&\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T^{\ast}}H^{s}}\leq C_{2}\\
&\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T^{\ast}}H^{s}}\leq C_{3}\,,\end{split}

(4.2.18)

provided that
T^{\ast}
is sufficiently small.

The previous estimates in (
4.2.18
) obviously hold for
n=0
. At this point, we will prove them for
n+1
, supposing that the controls in (
4.2.18
) are true for
n
.
From (
4.2.8
), (
4.2.17
) and (
4.2.16
) we obtain
