\displaystyle\|\nabla a_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s-1}}\leq\frac{C_{1}}{2}\exp\Big(CTC_{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s}}\leq\frac{C_{2}}{2}\exp\Big(CT(C_{0}+C_{1})\left(1+\varepsilon C_{1}\right)^{s}(\varepsilon C_{2}+1)C_{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s}}\leq C(\varepsilon C_{2}+1)\left(1+\varepsilon C_{1}\right)^{s}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+1}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s}}\,.

So we can choose
T^{\ast}
such that
\exp\Big(\max\{C_{0}+C_{1},\,1\}\,CT\left(1+C_{1}\right)^{s}(1+C_{2})\,C_{2}\Big)\leq 2
. Notice that
T^{\ast}
does not depend on
\varepsilon
.

Thus, by induction, (
4.2.18
) holds for the step
n+1
, and therefore it is true for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

4.2.3
Convergence

To prove the convergence, we will estimate the difference between two iterations at different steps. First of all, let us define

\widetilde{a}_{\varepsilon}^{n}:=a_{\varepsilon}^{n}-a^{n}_{0,\varepsilon}\,,

that satisfies the transport equation

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\widetilde{a}^{n}_{\varepsilon}+\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n-1}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{a}_{\varepsilon}^{n}=-\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n-1}\cdot\nabla a_{0,\varepsilon}^{n}\\
\widetilde{a}{{}_{\varepsilon}^{n}}_{|t=0}=0\,.\end{cases}

Hence, since the right-hand side is definitely uniformly bounded (with respect to
n
) in
L^{1}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+};L^{2})
, from classical results for transport equations we get that
(\widetilde{a}^{n}_{\varepsilon})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is uniformly bounded in
C^{0}([0,T];L^{2})
. Now, we want to prove that the sequence
(\widetilde{a}^{n}_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n},\nabla\Pi^{n}_{\varepsilon})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence in
C^{0}([0,T];L^{2})
.
So, let us define, for
(n,l)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
, the following quantities,

\displaystyle\delta{a}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}:=a_{\varepsilon}^{n+l}-a_{\varepsilon}^{n}

\displaystyle\delta\widetilde{a}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}:=\widetilde{a}_{\varepsilon}^{n+l}-\widetilde{a}_{\varepsilon}^{n}=\delta a_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}-\delta a_{0,\varepsilon}^{n,l}\,,\quad\text{ where }\quad\delta{a}_{0,\varepsilon}^{n,l}:=a_{0,\varepsilon}^{n+l}-a_{0,\varepsilon}^{n}

\displaystyle\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}:=\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+l}-\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}

(4.2.19)

\displaystyle\delta\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}:=\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n+l}-\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n}\,.

Of course, we have that
{\rm div}\,\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}=0
for any
(n,l)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
.

The previous quantities defined in (
4.2.19
) solve the system

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\delta\widetilde{a}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}+\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+l-1}\cdot\nabla\delta\widetilde{a}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}=-\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n-1,l}\cdot\nabla a_{\varepsilon}^{n}-\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+l-1}\cdot\nabla\delta a^{n,l}_{0,\varepsilon}\\[4.30554pt]
\partial_{t}\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}+\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+l-1}\cdot\nabla\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}=-\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n-1,l}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\\[2.15277pt]
\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\dfrac{1}{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l})^{\perp}-(1+\varepsilon a_{\varepsilon}^{n+l})\dfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\nabla\delta\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}-\delta a_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n}\\[8.61108pt]
{\rm div}\,\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}=0\\[4.30554pt]
(\delta\widetilde{a}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l},\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l})_{|t=0}=(0,\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}^{n,l})\,.\end{cases}

(4.2.20)

We perform an energy estimate for the first equation in (
4.2.20
), getting

\|\delta\widetilde{a}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}(t)\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\int_{0}^{t}\left(\|\nabla a_{\varepsilon}^{n}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n-1,l}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+l-1}\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla\delta a_{0,\varepsilon}^{n,l}\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\,\,{\rm d}\tau\,.

Moreover, from the momentum equation multiplied by
\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}
, integrating by parts over
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we obtain

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}\,|\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}|^{2}=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n-1,l}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n})\cdot\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(a_{\varepsilon}^{n+l}\,\nabla\delta\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n,l})\cdot\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\delta a_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}\,\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n})\cdot\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}\,,\end{split}

which implies

\begin{split}\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}(t)\|_{L^{2}}&\leq C\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}^{n,l}\|_{L^{2}}+C\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}(\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n-1,l}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}}+\|a_{\varepsilon}^{n+l}(\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\delta\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}}\,\,{\rm d}\tau\\
&+C\int_{0}^{t}\left(\|\delta\widetilde{a}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}}+\|\delta a_{0,\varepsilon}^{n,l}\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}\cap L^{\infty}}\,\,{\rm d}\tau\,,\end{split}

where we have also employed the fact that
\delta a_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}=\delta\widetilde{a}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}+\delta a_{0,\varepsilon}^{n,l}
.

Finally, for the pressure term we take the
{\rm div}\,
operator in the momentum equation of system (
4.2.20
), obtaining

-{\rm div}\,\left((1+\varepsilon a_{\varepsilon}^{n+l})\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\nabla\delta\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}\right)={\rm div}\,\left(-\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+l-1}+\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n-1,l}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l})^{\perp}+\delta a_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n}\right),

so that we have

\begin{split}\|\nabla\delta\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}\|_{L^{2}}&\leq C\varepsilon\left(\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n-1,l}\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\delta a_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}\,\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n}\|_{L^{2}}\right)\\
&+C\varepsilon\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+l-1}\|_{L^{\infty}}+C\|(\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l})^{\perp}\|_{L^{2}}\\
&\leq C\varepsilon\left(\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n-1,l}\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\delta\widetilde{a}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\delta a_{0,\varepsilon}^{n,l}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n}\|_{L^{2}}\right)\\
&+C\varepsilon\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n+l-1}\|_{L^{\infty}}+C\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}\|_{L^{2}}\,.\end{split}

(4.2.21)

At this point, applying Grönwall lemma (see Lemma
B.15
) and using the bounds established in Paragraph
4.2.2
, we thus argue that for
t\in[0,T^{\ast}]
:

\begin{split}\|\delta\widetilde{a}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}(t)\|_{L^{2}}+\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}(t)\|_{L^{2}}&\leq C_{T^{\ast}}\left(\|\nabla\delta a_{0,\varepsilon}^{n,l}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\delta a_{0,\varepsilon}^{n,l}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}^{n,l}\|_{L^{2}}\right)\\
&+C_{T^{\ast}}\int_{0}^{t}\left(\|\delta\widetilde{a}_{\varepsilon}^{n-1,l}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}}+\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n-1,l}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}}\right)\,{\rm d}\tau\,,\end{split}

where the constant
C_{T^{\ast}}
depends on
T^{\ast}
and on the initial data.

After setting

F_{0}^{n}:=\sup_{l\geq 0}\left(\|\nabla\delta a_{0,\varepsilon}^{n,l}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\delta a_{0,\varepsilon}^{n,l}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}^{n,l}\|_{L^{2}}\right)

and

G^{n}(t):=\sup_{l\geq 0}\sup_{[0,t]}\left(\|\delta\widetilde{a}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}}+\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}}\right),

by induction we may conclude that, for all
t\in[0,T^{\ast}]
,

\begin{split}G^{n}(t)\leq C_{T^{\ast}}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{(C_{T^{\ast}}T^{\ast})^{k}}{k!}F_{0}^{n-k}+\frac{\left(C_{T^{\ast}}T^{\ast}\right)^{n}}{n!}G^{0}(t)\,.\end{split}

Now, bearing (
4.2.4
) in mind, we have

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}F_{0}^{n}=0\,.

Hence, we may infer that

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\sup_{l\geq 0}\sup_{t\in[0,T^{\ast}]}\left(\|\delta\widetilde{a}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}(t)\|_{L^{2}}+\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n,l}(t)\|_{L^{2}}\right)=0\,.

(4.2.22)

Property (
4.2.22
) implies that both
(\widetilde{a}_{\varepsilon}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are Cauchy sequences in
C^{0}([0,T^{\ast}];L^{2})
: therefore, such sequences converge to some functions
\widetilde{a}_{\varepsilon}
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}
in the same space. Taking advantage of previous computations in (
4.2.21
), we have also that
(\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converge to a function
\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}
in
C^{0}([0,T^{\ast}];L^{2})
.

Now, we define
a_{\varepsilon}:=a_{0,\varepsilon}+\widetilde{a}_{\varepsilon}
. Hence,
a_{\varepsilon}-a_{0,\varepsilon}
is in
C^{0}([0,T^{\ast}];L^{2})
.
Moreover, as
(\nabla a_{\varepsilon}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}([0,T^{\ast}];H^{s-1})
and Sobolev spaces have the
Fatou property
(we refer to Proposition
A.4
in this respect), we deduce that
\nabla a_{\varepsilon}
belongs to the same space. Moreover, since
(a^{n}_{\varepsilon})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}([0,T^{\ast}]\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we also have that
a_{\varepsilon}\in L^{\infty}([0,T^{\ast}]\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Analogously, as
(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}([0,T^{\ast}];H^{s})
, we deduce that
\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}
and
\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}
belong to
L^{\infty}([0,T^{\ast}];H^{s})
.

Due to an interpolation argument, we see that the above sequences converge strongly in every intermediate
C^{0}([0,T^{\ast}];H^{\sigma})
for all
\sigma<s
. This is enough to pass to the limit in the equations satisfied by
(a_{\varepsilon}^{n},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{n},\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. Hence,
(a_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon},\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon})
satisfies the original problem (
4.2.3
).

This having been established, we look at the time continuity of
a_{\varepsilon}
. We exploit the transport equation:

\partial_{t}a_{\varepsilon}=-\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla a_{\varepsilon}\,,

noticing that the term on the right-hand side belongs to
L^{\infty}_{T^{\ast}}(L^{\infty})
. Thus, we can deduce that
\partial_{t}a_{\varepsilon}\in L^{\infty}_{T^{\ast}}(L^{\infty})
. Moreover, by embeddings, we already know that
\nabla a_{\varepsilon}\in L^{\infty}_{T^{\ast}}(L^{\infty})
. The previous two relations imply that
a_{\varepsilon}\in W^{1,\infty}_{T^{\ast}}(L^{\infty})\cap L^{\infty}_{T^{\ast}}(W^{1,\infty})
. That give us the desired regularity property
a_{\varepsilon}\in C^{0}([0,T^{\ast}]\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
. In addition, looking at the momentum equation in (
4.2.3
) and employing Theorem
A.15
, one obtains the claimed time regularity property for
\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}
. At this point, the time regularity for the pressure term
\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}
is recovered from the elliptic problem (
4.2.2
).

4.2.4
Uniqueness

We conclude this section showing the uniqueness of solutions in our framework.

We start by stating a uniqueness result, that is a consequence of a standard stability result based on energy methods. Since the proof is similar to the convergence argument of the previous paragraph, we will omit it (see e.g.
[
21
]
for details). We recall that, in what follows, the parameter
\varepsilon>0
is fixed.

Theorem 4.2.1

Let
\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{(1)},\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\right)
and
\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{(2)},\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}\right)
be two solutions
to the Euler system (
4.1.1
) associated with the initial data
\left(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}^{(1)}\right)
and
\left(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}^{(2)}\right)
. Assume that, for some
T>0
, one has the following properties:

(i)

the densities
\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}
and
\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}
are bounded and bounded away from zero;

(ii)

the quantities
\delta\varrho_{\varepsilon}:=\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}-\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}
and
\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}:=\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}-\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}
belong to the space
C^{1}\big([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big)
;

(iii)

\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}
,
\nabla\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}
and
\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}
belong to
L^{1}\big([0,T];L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big)
.

Then, for all
t\in[0,T]
, we have the stability inequality:

\|\delta\varrho_{\varepsilon}(t)\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\sqrt{\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}(t)}\,\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}(t)\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq\left(\|\delta\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\sqrt{\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}^{(2)}}\,\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{2}}\right)\,e^{CA(t)}\,,

(4.2.23)

for a universal constant
C>0
, where we have defined

A(t):=\int_{0}^{t}\left(\left\|\frac{\nabla\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}}{\sqrt{\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}+\left\|\frac{\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}}{\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\sqrt{\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\,\,{\rm d}\tau\,.

It is worth to notice that, adapting the
relative entropy
arguments presented
in Subsection 4.3 of
[
18
]
, we can replace (in the statement above) the
C^{1}_{T}(L^{2})
requirement for
\delta\varrho_{\varepsilon}
and
\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}
with the
C^{0}_{T}(L^{2})
regularity. However, one needs to pay an additional
L^{2}
assumption on the densities. In this way, we will have a weak-strong uniqueness type result and we will prove it in the next theorem.

Concerning weak-strong results for density-dependent fluids, we refer to
[
48
]
, where Germain exhibited a weak-strong uniqueness property within a class of (weak) solutions to the compressible Navier-Stokes system satisfying a relative entropy inequality with respect to a (hypothetical) strong solution of the same problem (see also the work
[
43
]
by Feireisl, Novotný and Sun).
Moreover, in
[
36
]
, the authors established the weak-strong uniqueness property in the class of finite energy weak solutions, extending thus the classical results of Prodi
[
63
]
and Serrin
[
67
]
to the class of compressible flows.

Before presenting the proof of the weak-strong uniqueness result, we state the definition of a
finite energy weak solution
to system (2.1), such that
\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}-1\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. We also recall that our densities have the form
\varrho_{\varepsilon}=1+\varepsilon R_{\varepsilon}
.

Definition 4.2.2

Let
T>0
and
\varepsilon\in\;]0,1]
be fixed. Let
(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon})
be an initial datum fulfilling the assumptions in Paragraph
4.1.1
.
We say that
(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})
is a finite energy weak solution to system (
4.1.1
) in
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, related to the previous initial datum, if:

•

\varrho_{\varepsilon}\in L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1\in C^{0}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
;

•

\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\cap C_{w}^{0}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
;

•

the mass equation is satisfied in the weak sense:

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Big(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,\partial_{t}\varphi+\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\varphi\Big)\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}\varphi(0,\cdot)\,{\rm d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varrho_{\varepsilon}(T)\varphi(T,\cdot)\,{\rm d}x\,,

for all
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R})
;

•

the divergence-free condition
{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}=0
is satisfied in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(]0,T[\,\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
;

•

the momentum equation is satisfied in the weak sense:

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Big(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\psi}+[\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}]:\nabla\boldsymbol{\psi}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\Big)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}&\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}(0)\,{\rm d}x\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varrho_{\varepsilon}(T)\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}(T)\boldsymbol{\psi}(T)\,{\rm d}x,\end{split}

for any
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in C_{c}^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that
{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}=0
;

•

for almost every
t\in[0,T]
, the two following energy balances hold true:

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varrho_{\varepsilon}(t)|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}(t)|^{2}\,{\rm d}x\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}|\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}|^{2}\,{\rm d}x\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\varrho_{\varepsilon}(t)-1)^{2}\,{\rm d}x\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}-1)^{2}\,{\rm d}x\,.

Theorem 4.2.3

Let
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
be fixed. Let
\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\right)
and
\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}\right)
be two finite energy weak solutions
to the Euler system (
4.1.1
) as in Definition
4.2.2
with initial data
\left(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}^{(1)}\right)
and
\left(\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}^{(2)}\right)
. Assume that, for some
T>0
, one has the following properties:

(i)

\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}
and
\nabla\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}
belong to
L^{1}\big([0,T];L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big)
;

(ii)

\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}
is in
L^{1}\big([0,T];L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big)
.

Then, for all
t\in[0,T]
, we have the stability inequality (
4.2.23
).

Proof:
We start by defining, for
i=1,2
:

R_{\varepsilon}^{(i)}:=\frac{\varrho_{\varepsilon}^{(i)}-1}{\varepsilon}\quad\text{and}\quad R_{0,\varepsilon}^{(i)}:=\frac{\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}^{(i)}-1}{\varepsilon}\,,

and we notice that, owing to the continuity equation in (
4.1.1
) and the divergence-free conditions
{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{(i)}=0
, one has

\partial_{t}R_{\varepsilon}^{(i)}+{\rm div}\,(R_{\varepsilon}^{(i)}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{(i)})=0\quad\text{with}\quad R_{\varepsilon}^{(i)}(0)=R_{0,\varepsilon}^{(i)}.

(4.2.24)

For simplicity of notation, we fix
\varepsilon=1
throughout this proof and let us assume for a while the couple
(R^{(1)},\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)})
be a pair of smooth functions such that
R^{(1)},\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}=0
, with the support of
R^{(1)}
and
\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}
included in
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
First of all, we use
\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}
as a test function in the weak formulation of the momentum equation, finding that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varrho^{(2)}(T)\boldsymbol{u}^{(2)}(T)\cdot\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}(T)\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varrho_{0}^{(2)}\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{(2)}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{(1)}\,{\rm d}x+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varrho^{(2)}\boldsymbol{u}^{(2)}\cdot\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t

(4.2.25)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\varrho^{(2)}\boldsymbol{u}^{(2)}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}^{(2)}):\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varrho^{(2)}\boldsymbol{u}^{(2)}\cdot(\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)})^{\perp}\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\,,

where we have also noted that
(\boldsymbol{u}^{(2)})^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}=-\boldsymbol{u}^{(2)}\cdot(\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)})^{\perp}
.

Next, testing the mass equation against
|\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}|^{2}/2
, we obtain

\begin{split}\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varrho^{(2)}(T)|\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}(T)|^{2}\,{\rm d}x&=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varrho_{0}^{(2)}|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{(1)}|^{2}\,{\rm d}x+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varrho^{(2)}\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}\cdot\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\\
&+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varrho^{(2)}\boldsymbol{u}^{(2)}\cdot\nabla|\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}|^{2}\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varrho_{0}^{(2)}|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{(1)}|^{2}\,{\rm d}x+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varrho^{(2)}\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}\cdot\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\\
&+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\varrho^{(2)}\boldsymbol{u}^{(2)}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}):\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t.\end{split}

(4.2.26)

Recall also that the energy inequality reads

\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varrho^{(2)}(T)|\boldsymbol{u}^{(2)}(T)|^{2}\,{\rm d}x\leq\dfrac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varrho_{0}^{(2)}|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{(2)}|^{2}\,{\rm d}x\,.

Now, we take care of the density oscillations
R^{(i)}
. We test the transport equation (
4.2.24
) for
R^{(2)}
against
R^{(1)}
, getting

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}R^{(2)}(T)R^{(1)}(T)\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}R_{0}^{(2)}R_{0}^{(1)}\,{\rm d}x

(4.2.27)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}R^{(2)}\partial_{t}R^{(1)}\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}R^{(2)}\boldsymbol{u}^{(2)}\cdot\nabla R^{(1)}\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t.

Recalling Definition
4.2.2
, we have the following energy balance:

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|R^{(2)}(T)|^{2}\,{\rm d}x\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|R^{(2)}_{0}|^{2}\,{\rm d}x\,.

At this point, testing
\partial_{t}1+{\rm div}\,(1\,\boldsymbol{u}^{(2)})=0
against
|R^{(1)}|^{2}/2
, we may infer that

\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|R^{(1)}(T)|^{2}\,{\rm d}x=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|R_{0}^{(1)}|^{2}\,{\rm d}x+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}R^{(1)}\partial_{t}R^{(1)}\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}R^{(1)}\boldsymbol{u}^{(2)}\cdot\nabla R^{(1)}\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t.

(4.2.28)

Now, for notational convenience, let us define

\delta R:=R^{(2)}-R^{(1)}\quad\text{and}\quad\delta\boldsymbol{u}:=\boldsymbol{u}^{(2)}-\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}\,.

Putting all the previous relations together, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Big(\varrho^{(2)}(T)|\delta\boldsymbol{u}(T)|^{2}+|\delta R(T)|^{2}\Big)\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Big(\varrho_{0}^{(2)}|\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{0}|^{2}+|\delta R_{0}|^{2}\Big)\,{\rm d}x-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varrho^{(2)}\boldsymbol{u}^{(2)}\cdot(\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)})^{\perp}\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Big(\varrho^{(2)}\delta\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}+\partial_{t}R^{(1)}\,\delta R\Big)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t

(4.2.29)

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Big((\varrho^{(2)}\boldsymbol{u}^{(2)}\otimes\delta\boldsymbol{u}):\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}+\delta R\,\boldsymbol{u}^{(2)}\cdot\nabla R^{(1)}\Big)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\,.

Next, we remark that we can write

(\varrho^{(2)}\boldsymbol{u}^{(2)}\otimes\delta\boldsymbol{u}):\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}=(\varrho^{(2)}\boldsymbol{u}^{(2)}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)})\cdot\delta\boldsymbol{u}

and that we have
\boldsymbol{u}^{(2)}\cdot(\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)})^{\perp}=\delta\boldsymbol{u}\cdot(\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)})^{\perp}
by orthogonality.

Therefore, relation (
4.2.29
) can be recasted as

\begin{split}\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Big(\varrho^{(2)}(T)|\delta\boldsymbol{u}(T)|^{2}+|\delta R(T)|^{2}\Big)\,{\rm d}x&\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Big(\varrho_{0}^{(2)}|\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{0}|^{2}+|\delta R_{0}|^{2}\Big)\,{\rm d}x\\
&-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varrho^{(2)}\Big(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}+\boldsymbol{u}^{(2)}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}+(\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)})^{\perp}\Big)\cdot\delta\boldsymbol{u}\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\\
&-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Big(\partial_{t}R^{(1)}+\boldsymbol{u}^{(2)}\cdot\nabla R^{(1)}\Big)\,\delta R\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\,.\end{split}

At this point, we add and subtract the quantities
\pm(\varrho^{(2)}\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)})\cdot\,\delta\boldsymbol{u}\pm\varrho^{(2)}\frac{1}{\varrho^{(1)}}\nabla\Pi^{(1)}\cdot\,\delta\boldsymbol{u}
and
\pm(\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}\cdot\nabla R^{(1)})\,\delta R
, yielding

\begin{split}\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Big(\varrho^{(2)}(T)|\delta\boldsymbol{u}(T)|^{2}+|\delta R(T)|^{2}\Big)\,{\rm d}x&\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Big(\varrho_{0}^{(2)}|\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{0}|^{2}+|\delta R_{0}|^{2}\Big)\,{\rm d}x\\
&-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Big(\varrho^{(2)}\delta\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}+\delta R\frac{1}{\varrho^{(1)}}\nabla\Pi^{(1)}\Big)\cdot\delta\boldsymbol{u}\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\\
&-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla R^{(1)})\,\delta R\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\,,\end{split}

(4.2.30)

where we have used the fact that
(R^{(1)},\,\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)})
is solution to the Euler system and
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla\Pi^{(1)}\cdot\delta\boldsymbol{u}\,\,{\rm d}x=0
.

Therefore, setting
\mathcal{E}(T):=\|\sqrt{\varrho^{(2)}(T)}\delta\boldsymbol{u}(T)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|\delta R(T)\|^{2}_{L^{2}}
, from relation (
4.2.30
) we can deduce that

\mathcal{E}(T)\leq\mathcal{E}(0)+C\int_{0}^{T}\left(\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varrho^{(2)}}\varrho^{(1)}}\nabla\Pi^{(1)}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}+\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varrho^{(2)}}}\nabla R^{(1)}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\mathcal{E}(t)\,{\rm d}t\,.

An application of Grönwall lemma (we refer to Section
B.6
) yields the desired stability inequality (
4.2.23
).

In order to get the result, having the regularity stated in the theorem, we argue by density.

Thanks to the regularity (stated in Definition
4.2.2
) of weak solutions to the Euler equations (
4.1.1
) and assumption
(i)
of the theorem, all the terms appearing in relations (
4.2.25
) and (
4.2.26
) are well-defined, if in addition we have
\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}\in L^{1}_{T}(L^{2})
. However, this condition on the time derivative of the velocity field
\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}
comes for free from the momentum equation

\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}=-\left(\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}+(\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)})^{\perp}+\frac{1}{\varrho^{(1)}}\nabla\Pi^{(1)}\right)\,.

(4.2.31)

Since
\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}\in L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2})
with
\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}\in L^{1}_{T}(L^{\infty})
and
\varrho^{(1)}\in L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{\infty})
, condition
(ii)
implies that the right-hand side of (
4.2.31
) is in
L^{1}_{T}(L^{2})
. Recalling the regularity in Definition
4.2.2
of
\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}
, one gets
\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}\in W^{1,1}_{T}(L^{2})
and hence
\boldsymbol{u}^{(1)}\in C^{0}_{T}(L^{2})
.

Analogously in order to justify computations in (
4.2.27
) and (
4.2.28
), besides the previous regularity conditions, one needs the additional assumption
\partial_{t}R^{(1)}\in L^{1}_{T}(L^{2})
. Once again, one can take advantage of the continuity equation (
4.2.24
) to obtain the required regularity for
\partial_{t}R^{(1)}
. Finally, condition
(ii)
is necessary to make sense of relation (
4.2.30
).

This concludes the proof of the theorem.

4.3
Asymptotic analysis

The main goal of this section is to show the convergence when
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
: we achieve it employing a
compensated compactness
technique. We point out that, in the sequel, the time
T>0
is fixed by the existence theory developed in Section
4.2
.

We will show that (
4.0.1
) converges towards a limit system, represented by the quasi-homogeneous incompressible Euler equations:

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}R+\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla R=0\\
\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}+\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}+R\boldsymbol{u}^{\perp}+\nabla\Pi=0\\
{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{u}=0\,.\end{cases}

(4.3.1)

The previous system consists of a transport equation for the quantity
R
(that can be interpreted as the deviation with respect to the constant density profile) and an Euler type equation for the limit velocity field
\boldsymbol{u}
.

In Section
4.2
, we have proved that the sequence
(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon},\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
is uniformly bounded (with respect to
\varepsilon
) in suitable spaces.
Next, thanks to the uniform bounds, we extract weak limit points, for which one has to find some constraints: the singular terms have to vanish at the limit (see Subsection
4.3.1
).

Finally, after performing the
compensated compactness
arguments, we describe the limit dynamics (see Paragraph
4.3.2
below).

The choice of using this technique derives from the fact that the oscillations in time of the solutions are out of control (see Subsection
4.3.2
). To overcome this issue, rather than employing the standard
H^{s}
estimates, we take advantage of the weak formulation of the problem. We test the equations against divergence-free test functions: this will lead to useful cancellations. In particular, we avoid to study the pressure term.
At the end, we close the argument by noticing that the weak limit solutions are actually regular solutions.

4.3.1
Preliminaries and constraint at the limit

We start this subsection by recalling the uniform bounds developed in Section
4.2
. The fluctuations
R_{\varepsilon}
satisfy the controls

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]}\left\|R_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{\infty})}\,\leq\,C

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]}\left\|\nabla R_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(H^{s-1})}\,\leq\,C\,,

where
R_{\varepsilon}:=(\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1)/\varepsilon
as above.

As for the velocity fields, we have obtained the following uniform bound:

\sup_{\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]}\left\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(H^{s})}\,\leq\,C\,.

Thanks to the previous uniform estimates, we can assume (up to passing to subsequences) that there exist
R\in L^{\infty}_{T}(W^{1,\infty})
, with
\nabla R\in L^{\infty}_{T}(H^{s-1})
, and
\boldsymbol{u}\in L^{\infty}_{T}(H^{s})
such that

\begin{split}R:=\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}R_{\varepsilon}\quad&\text{in}\quad L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{\infty})\\
\nabla R:=\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}\nabla R_{\varepsilon}\quad&\text{in}\quad L^{\infty}_{T}(H^{s-1})\\
\boldsymbol{u}:=\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\quad&\text{in}\quad L^{\infty}_{T}(H^{s})\,,\end{split}

(4.3.2)

where we agree that the previous limits are taken in the corresponding weak-
\ast
topology.

Remark 4.3.1

It is evident that
\varrho_{\varepsilon}-1=O(\varepsilon)
in
L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{\infty})
and therefore that
\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}
weakly-
\ast
converge to
\boldsymbol{u}
e.g. in the space
L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2})
.

Next, we notice that the solutions stated in Theorem
4.1.1
are
strong
solutions. In particular, they satisfy in a weak sense the mass equation and the momentum equation, respectively:

-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\partial_{t}\varphi+\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi\right)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}\varphi(0,\cdot)\,{\rm d}x\,,

(4.3.3)

for any
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}([0,T[\,\times\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R})
;

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(-\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\psi}-\varrho_{\varepsilon}[\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}]:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\,\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\right)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}(0,\cdot)\,{\rm d}x\,,

(4.3.4)

for any test function
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in C^{\infty}_{c}([0,T[\,\times\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that
{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}=0
;

Moreover, the divergence-free condition on
\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}
is satisfied in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(]0,T[\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

Before going on, in the following lemma, we
characterize the limit for the quantity
R_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}
. We recall that
R_{\varepsilon}
satisfies

\partial_{t}R_{\varepsilon}=-{\rm div}\,(R_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})\,,\quad\quad(R_{\varepsilon})_{|t=0}=R_{0,\varepsilon}.

(4.3.5)

Lemma 4.3.2

Let
(R_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
be uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
with
(\nabla R_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}\subset L^{\infty}_{T}(H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
, and let the velocity fields
(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
be uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
. Moreover, for any
\varepsilon\in\;]0,1]
, assume that the couple
(R_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})
solves the transport equation (
4.3.5
). Let
(R,\boldsymbol{u})
be the limit point identified in (
4.3.2
). Then, up to an extraction:

(i)

R_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow R
in
C^{0}_{T}(C^{0}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
;

(ii)

the product
R_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}
converges to
R\boldsymbol{u}
in the distributional sense.

Proof:

We look at the transport equation (
4.3.5
) for
R_{\varepsilon}
.
We employ Proposition
A.10
on the term in the right-hand side, obtaining

\|R_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{s}}\leq C\left(\|R_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{s}}+\|\nabla R_{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{s-1}}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\,.

By embeddings, this implies that the sequence
(\partial_{t}R_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
is uniformly bounded e.g. in
L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{\infty})
and so
(R_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
is bounded in
W^{1,\infty}_{T}(L^{\infty})
uniformly in
\varepsilon
. On the other hand, we know that
(\nabla R_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
is bounded in
L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{\infty})
. Then, by Ascoli-Arzelà theorem (see Theorem
B.12
), we gather that the family
(R_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
is compact in e.g.
C^{0}_{T}(C_{\rm loc}^{0})
and hence we deduce the strong convergence property, up to passing to a suitable subsequence (not relabelled here),

R_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow R\quad\text{in}\quad C^{0}([0,T]\,;C_{\rm loc}^{0})\,.

Finally, since
(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
is weakly-
\ast
convergent e.g. in
L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2})
to
\boldsymbol{u}
, we get
R_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\rightharpoonup}}R\boldsymbol{u}
in the space
L^{\infty}_{T}(L_{\rm loc}^{2})
.

Now, as anticipated in the introduction of this section, we have to highlight the constraint that the limit points have to satisfy. We have to point out that this condition does not fully characterize the limit dynamics (see Subsection
4.3.2
below).

The only singular term (of order
\varepsilon^{-1}
) appearing in the equations is the Coriolis force. Then, we test the momentum equation in (
4.3.4
)
against
\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\psi}
with
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in C_{c}^{\infty}([0,T[\,\times\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that
{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}=0
.
Keeping in mind the assumptions on the initial data and due to the fact that
(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
is uniformly bounded in e.g.
L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2})
and so is
(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
in
L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{1})
, it follows that all the terms in equation (
4.3.4
), apart from the Coriolis operator, go to
0
in the limit for
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
.

Therefore,
we infer that, for any
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in C_{c}^{\infty}([0,T[\,\times\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that
{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}=0
,

\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\,{\rm d}x\,{\rm d}t=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\,{\rm d}x\,{\rm d}t=0\,.

This property tells us that
\boldsymbol{u}^{\perp}=\nabla\pi
, for some suitable function
\pi
.

However, this relation does
not
add more information on the limit dynamics, since we already know that the divergence-free condition
{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}=0
is satisfied for all
\varepsilon>0
.

4.3.2
Wave system and convergence

The goal of the present subsection is to describe oscillations of solutions in order to show convergence to the limit system. The Coriolis term is responsible for strong oscillations in time of solutions, which may prevent the convergence. To overcome this issue we implement a strategy based on
compensated compactness
arguments. Namely, we perform algebraic manipulations on the wave system (see (
4.3.10
) below), in order to derive
compactness properties for the quantity
\widetilde{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}:={\rm curl}\,(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})
. This will be enough to pass to the limit in the momentum equation (and, in particular, in the convective term).

Let us define

\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}:=\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\,,

that is uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}_{T}(H^{s})
, due to Proposition
A.10
.

Now, using the fact that
\varrho_{\varepsilon}=1+\varepsilon R_{\varepsilon}
, we recast the continuity equation in the following way:

\varepsilon\partial_{t}R_{\varepsilon}+{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}=0\,.

(4.3.6)

In light of the uniform bounds and convergence properties stated in Lemma
4.3.2
, we can easily pass to the limit in the previous formulation (or rather in (
4.3.5
)) finding

\partial_{t}R+{\rm div}\,(R\boldsymbol{u})=0\,.

(4.3.7)

We decompose

\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\perp}=\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\perp}+\varepsilon\,R_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\perp}

and from the momentum equation one can deduce

\varepsilon\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}+\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}+\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\perp}=\varepsilon f_{\varepsilon}\,,

(4.3.8)

where we have defined

f_{\varepsilon}:=-{\rm div}\,(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})-R_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\perp}\,.

(4.3.9)

In this way, we can rewrite system (
4.1.1
) in the wave form

\begin{cases}\varepsilon\partial_{t}R_{\varepsilon}+{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}=0\\
\varepsilon\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}+\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}+\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\perp}=\varepsilon f_{\varepsilon}\,.\end{cases}

(4.3.10)

Applying again Proposition
A.10
, one can show that the terms
\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}
and
R_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\perp}
are uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}_{T}(H^{s})
. Thus, it follows that
(f_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}\subset L^{\infty}_{T}(H^{s-1})
.

However, the uniform bounds in Section
4.2
are not enough for proving convergence in the weak formulation of the momentum equation. Indeed, on the one hand, those controls allow to pass to the limit in the
\partial_{t}
term and in the initial datum; on the other hand, the non-linear term and the Coriolis force are out of control. We postpone the convergence analysis of the Coriolis force in the next Paragraph
4.3.3
and now we focus on the
the convective term
{\rm div}\,(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})
in (
4.3.4
). We proceed as follows: first of all, we reduce our study to the constant density case (see Lemma
4.3.3
below); next, we apply the
compensated compactness
argument.

Lemma 4.3.3

Let
T>0
. For any test function
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in C_{c}^{\infty}([0,T[\times\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we get

\limsup_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}\left|\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}:\nabla\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}:\nabla\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\right|=0\,.

(4.3.11)

Proof:

Let
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}([0,T[\times\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
{\rm Supp}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}\subset[0,T]\times K
for some compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Therefore, we can write

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{K}\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}:\nabla\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{K}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}:\nabla\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{T}\int_{K}R_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}:\nabla\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\,.

As a consequence of the uniform bounds e.g.
(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}\subset L^{\infty}_{T}(H^{s})
and
(R_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}\subset L_{T}^{\infty}(L^{\infty})
, the second integral in the right-hand side is of order
\varepsilon
.

Thanks to Lemma
4.3.3
, we are reduced to study the convergence (with respect to
\varepsilon
) of the integral

-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}:\nabla\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}{\rm div}\,(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\,.

Owing to the divergence-free condition we can write:

{\rm div}\,(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})=\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{2}\nabla|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}|^{2}+\omega_{\varepsilon}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\perp}\,,

(4.3.12)

where we have denoted
\omega_{\varepsilon}:={\rm curl}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}=-\partial_{2}u^{1}+\partial_{1}u^{2}
.

Notice that the former term, since it is a perfect gradient, vanishes identically when tested against
\boldsymbol{\psi}
such that
{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}=0
.
As for the latter term we take advantage of equation (
4.3.8
). Taking the
{\rm curl}\,
, we get

\partial_{t}\widetilde{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}={\rm curl}\,f_{\varepsilon}\,,

(4.3.13)

where we have set
\widetilde{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}:={\rm curl}\,\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}
with
\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}:=\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}
. We recall also that
f_{\varepsilon}
defined in (
4.3.9
) is uniformly bounded in the space
L^{\infty}_{T}(H^{s-1})
.
Then, relation (
4.3.13
) implies that the family
(\partial_{t}\widetilde{\gamma}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}_{T}(H^{s-2})
. As a result, we get
(\widetilde{\gamma}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}\subset W^{1,\infty}_{T}(H^{s-2})
. On the other hand, the sequence
(\nabla\widetilde{\gamma}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
is also uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}_{T}(H^{s-2})
.
At this point, the Ascoli-Arzelà theorem (we refer to Theorem
B.12
in this regard) gives compactness of
(\widetilde{\gamma}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
in e.g.
C^{0}_{T}(H^{s-2}_{\rm loc})
. Then, it converges (up to extracting a subsequence) to a tempered distribution
\widetilde{\gamma}
in the same space. Thus, it follows that

\widetilde{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}\longrightarrow\widetilde{\gamma}\quad\text{in}\quad C^{0}_{T}(H_{\rm loc}^{s-2})\,.

But since we already know the convergence
\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}:=\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\rightharpoonup}}\boldsymbol{u}
in e.g.
L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2})
, it follows that
\widetilde{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}:={\rm curl}\,\boldsymbol{V}_{\varepsilon}\longrightarrow\omega:={\rm curl}\,\boldsymbol{u}
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
, hence
\widetilde{\gamma}={\rm curl}\,\boldsymbol{u}=\omega
.

Finally, writing
\varrho_{\varepsilon}=1+\varepsilon R_{\varepsilon}
, we obtain

\widetilde{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}:={\rm curl}\,(\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})=\omega_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon{\rm curl}\,(R_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})\,,

where the family
({\rm curl}\,(R_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}))_{\varepsilon}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}_{T}(H^{s-1})
. From this relation and the previous analysis, we deduce the strong convergence (up to an extraction) for
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
:

\omega_{\varepsilon}\longrightarrow\omega\qquad\text{ in }\qquad L^{\infty}_{T}(H^{s-2}_{\rm loc})\,.

In the end, we have proved the following convergence result for the convective term
{\rm div}\,(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})
.

Lemma 4.3.4

Let
T>0
. Up to passing to a suitable subsequence, one has the following convergence for
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
:

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}:\nabla\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\longrightarrow\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega\,\boldsymbol{u}^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\,,

for any test function
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in C_{c}^{\infty}([0,T[\times\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that
{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}=0
.

As a consequence of the previous lemma, performing equalities (
4.3.12
) backwards, for the convective term
\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}
we find that

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}:\nabla\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\longrightarrow\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\boldsymbol{u}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}:\nabla\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t

(4.3.14)

for
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
and for all smooth divergence-free test functions
\boldsymbol{\psi}
.

4.3.3
Description of the limit system

With the convergence established in Paragraph
4.3.2
, we can pass to the limit in the momentum equation.

To begin with, we take a test-function
\boldsymbol{\psi}
such that

\boldsymbol{\psi}=\nabla^{\perp}\varphi\quad\quad\text{with}\quad\quad\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}([0,T[\times\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R})\,.

(4.3.15)

For such a
\boldsymbol{\psi}
, all the gradient terms vanish identically. First of all, we recall the momentum equation in its weak formulation:

\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(-\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\psi}-\varrho_{\varepsilon}[\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}]:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\,\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\right)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}(0,\cdot)\,{\rm d}x\,.

(4.3.16)

Making use of the uniform bounds, we can pass to the limit in the
\partial_{t}
term and thanks to our assumptions and embeddings we have
\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
in e.g.
L_{\rm loc}^{2}
.

Let us consider now the Coriolis term. We can write:

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\varrho_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}R_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\,.

Since
\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}
is divergence-free, the latter term vanishes when tested against such
\boldsymbol{\psi}
defined as in (
4.3.15
). On the other hand, again thanks to Lemma
4.3.2
, one can get

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}R_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\longrightarrow\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}R\boldsymbol{u}^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\,\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\,.

In the end, letting
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
in (
4.3.16
), we gather (remembering also (
4.3.14
))

\int_{0}^{T}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(-\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\psi}-\boldsymbol{u}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}:\nabla_{x}\boldsymbol{\psi}+\,R\boldsymbol{u}^{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}\right)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}(0,\cdot)\,{\rm d}x\,,

for any test function
\boldsymbol{\psi}
defined as in (
4.3.15
).

From this relation, we immediately obtain that

\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}+{\rm div}\,(\boldsymbol{u}\otimes\boldsymbol{u})+R\boldsymbol{u}^{\perp}+\nabla\Pi=0\,,

for a suitable pressure term
\nabla\Pi
. This term appears as a result of the weak formulation of the problem. It can be viewed as a Lagrangian multiplier associated to the to the divergence-free constraint on
\boldsymbol{u}
. Finally, the quantity
R
satisfies the transport equation found in (
4.3.7
).

We conclude this paragraph, devoting our attention to the analysis of the regularity of
\nabla\Pi
. We apply the
{\rm div}\,
operator to the momentum equation in (
4.3.1
), deducing that
\Pi
satisfies

-\Delta\Pi={\rm div}\,G\qquad\text{where}\qquad G:=\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}+R\boldsymbol{u}^{\perp}\,.

(4.3.17)

On the one hand, Lemma
B.10
gives

\|\nabla\Pi\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\|G\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\left(\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|R\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{2}}\right)\,.

This implies that
\nabla\Pi\in L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2})
.

On the other hand, owing to the divergence-free condition on
\boldsymbol{u}
, we have

\|\Delta\Pi\|_{H^{s-1}}\leq C\left(\|\boldsymbol{u}\|^{2}_{H^{s}}+\|R\|_{L^{\infty}}\|u\|_{H^{s}}+\|\nabla R\|_{H^{s-1}}\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\,,

where we have also used Proposition
A.10
.

In the end, we deduce that
\Delta\Pi\in L^{\infty}_{T}(H^{s-1})
. Thus, we conclude that
\nabla\Pi\in L^{\infty}_{T}(H^{s})
.

At this point, employing classical results on solutions to transport equations in Sobolev spaces, we may infer the claimed
C^{0}
time regularity of
\boldsymbol{u}
and
R
. Moreover, thanks to the fact that
R
and
\boldsymbol{u}
are both continuous in time, from the elliptic equation (
4.3.17
), we get that also
\nabla\Pi\in C^{0}_{T}(H^{s})
.

4.4
Well-posedness for the quasi-homogeneous system

In this section, for the reader’s convenience, we review the well-posedness theory of the quasi-homogeneous Euler system (
4.1.9
), in particular, the “asymptotically global” well-posedness result presented in
[
16
]
. In the first Subsection
4.4.1
, we sketch the local well-posedness theorem for system (
4.1.9
) in the
H^{s}
framework. Actually, equations (
4.1.9
) are locally well-posedness in all
B^{s}_{p,r}
Besov spaces, under the condition (
A.3.1
). We refer to
[
18
]
where the authors apply the standard Littlewood-Paley machinery to the quasi-homogeneous ideal MHD system to recover local in time well-posedness in spaces
B^{s}_{p,r}
for any
1<p<+\infty
. The case
p=+\infty
was reached in
[
16
]
with a different approach based on the vorticity formulation of the momentum equation (see also Subsection
4.4.2
for more details concerning the “critical” case
p=+\infty
).

In Subsection
4.4.3
, we explicitly derive the lower bound for the lifespan of solutions to system (
4.1.9
). The reason in detailing the derivation of (
4.4.34
) for
T^{\ast}
is due to the fact that it is much simpler than the one presented in
[
16
]
, where (due to the presence of the magnetic field) the lifespan behaves like the fifth iterated logarithm of the norms of the initial oscillation
R_{0}
and the initial magnetic field. In addition, that lower bound (see (
4.4.34
) below) improves the standard lower bound coming from the hyperbolic theory, where the lifespan is bounded from below by the inverse of the norm of the initial data.

4.4.1
Local well-posedness in
H^{s}
spaces

In this subsection, we state the well-posedness result for system (
4.1.9
) in the
H^{s}
functional framework with
s>2
, in which we have analysed the well-posedness issue for system (
4.1.1
). We limit ourselves to present the proof, by energy methods, of uniqueness of solutions (see Subsection
4.4.1.1
) and the implications of the continuation criterion (see Subsection
4.4.1.3
): in order to show that, we need some preparatory material, stated in Subsection
4.4.1.2
.

Theorem 4.4.1

Take
s>2
. Let
\big(R_{0},u_{0}\big)
be initial data such that
R_{0}\in L^{\infty}
, with
\nabla R_{0}\in H^{s-1}
, and
the divergence-free vector field
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\,\in H^{s}
.

Then, there exists a time
T^{\ast}>0
such that, on
[0,T^{\ast}]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, problem (
4.1.9
) has a unique solution
(R,\boldsymbol{u},\nabla\Pi)
with:

•

R\in C^{0}\big([0,T^{\ast}]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)
and
\nabla R\in C^{0}_{T^{\ast}}(H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
;

•

\boldsymbol{u}
and
\nabla\Pi
belong to
C^{0}_{T^{\ast}}(H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
.

Moreover, if
(R,\boldsymbol{u},\nabla\Pi)
is a solution to (
4.1.9
) on
[0,T^{\ast}[\,\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
(
T^{\ast}<+\infty
) with the properties described above, and

\int_{0}^{T^{\ast}}\big\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}(t)\big\|_{L^{\infty}}\,{\rm d}t<+\infty\,,

then the triplet
(R,\boldsymbol{u},\nabla\Pi)
can be continued beyond
T^{\ast}
into a solution of system (
4.1.9
) with the same regularity.

4.4.1.1
Uniqueness by an energy argument

Uniqueness in our functional framework is a consequence of the following stability result, whose proof is based on an energy method for the difference of two solutions to the quasi-homogeneous Euler system (
4.1.9
).
We present here the classical proof with the
C^{1}_{T}
regularity assumption on the time variable (see condition
(i)
in the theorem below). In order to relax this requirement, one has to argue as done in Theorem
4.2.3
, with the additional
L^{2}
integrability condition on densities.

Theorem 4.4.2

Let
(R_{1},\boldsymbol{u}_{1})
and
(R_{2},\boldsymbol{u}_{2})
be two solutions to the quasi-homogeneous Euler system (
4.1.9
). Assume that, for some
T>0
, one has the following properties:

(i)

the two quantities
\delta R\,:=\,R_{1}-R_{2}
and
\delta\boldsymbol{u}\,:=\,\boldsymbol{u}_{1}-\boldsymbol{u}_{2}
belong to the space
C^{1}\big([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big)
;

(ii)

\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\in L^{1}\big([0,T];W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big)
and
\nabla R_{1}\in L^{1}\big([0,T];L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big)
.

Then, for all
t\in[0,T]
, we have the stability inequality:

\|\delta R(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C\left(\|\delta R_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)\,e^{CB(t)}\,,

(4.4.1)

for a universal constant
C>0
, where we have defined

B(t):=\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left(\|\nabla R_{1}(\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(\tau)\|_{W^{1,\infty}}\right)\,\,{\rm d}\tau\,.

(4.4.2)

Proof:

First of all, we take the difference of the two systems (
4.1.9
) solved by the triplets
(R_{1},\boldsymbol{u}_{1},\nabla\Pi_{1})
and
(R_{2},\boldsymbol{u}_{2},\nabla\Pi_{2})
, obtaining

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\delta R+\boldsymbol{u}_{2}\cdot\nabla\delta R=-\delta\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla R_{1}\\
\partial_{t}\delta\boldsymbol{u}+\boldsymbol{u}_{2}\cdot\nabla\delta\boldsymbol{u}+R_{2}\,\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\perp}+\nabla\delta\Pi=-\delta\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{1}-\delta R\,\boldsymbol{u}_{1}^{\perp}\\
{\rm div}\,\delta\boldsymbol{u}=0\,,\end{cases}

(4.4.3)

where
\delta\Pi:=\Pi_{1}-\Pi_{2}
.

We start by testing the first equation of (
4.4.3
) against
\delta R
and we get

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|\delta R\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla R_{1})\,\delta R\leq\frac{1}{2}\|\nabla R_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}}\left(\|\delta R\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)\,.

Next, testing the second equation on
\delta\boldsymbol{u}
, due to the divergence-free conditions
{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{1}={\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{2}=0
, we gather

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{1})\cdot\,\delta\boldsymbol{u}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\delta R\;\boldsymbol{u}_{1}^{\perp})\cdot\delta\boldsymbol{u}\leq\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}}\left(\|\delta R\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right).

Putting the previous inequalities together, we finally infer

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\left(\|\delta R\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)\leq C\left(\|\nabla R_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\|_{W^{1,\infty}}\right)\left(\|\delta R\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)\,.

An application of Grönwall’s lemma gives us the stability estimate (
4.4.1
), i.e.

\|\delta R(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C\left(\|\delta R_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)\,e^{CB(t)}\,,

for a universal constant
C>0
and
B(t)
defined as in (
4.4.2
).

At this point, the uniqueness in the claimed framework (see Theorem
4.4.1
) follows from the previous statement. Let us sketch the proof.

We take an initial datum
(R_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0})
satisfying the assumptions in Theorem
4.4.1
. We consider two solutions
(R_{1},\boldsymbol{u}_{1})
and
(R_{2},\boldsymbol{u}_{2})
of system (
4.1.9
), related to the initial datum
(R_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0})
. Moreover, those solutions have to fulfill the regularity properties stated in the quoted theorem.

Now, due to embeddings, we have only to detail how the previous solutions match the condition
(i)
in Theorem
4.4.2
. We focus on the regularity of
\delta R
, since similar arguments apply to
\delta\boldsymbol{u}
.

We look at the first equation in (
4.4.3
):
\delta R
is transported by the divergence-free vector field
\boldsymbol{u}_{2}
, with in addition the presence of an “external force”
g:=-\delta\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla R_{1}
. Thanks to the regularity properties presented in Theorem
4.4.1
and embeddings, we know that
\delta\boldsymbol{u}\in C^{0}_{T}(L^{2})
and
R_{1}\in C^{0}_{T}(W^{1,\infty})
. Thus, one can deduce that
g\in C^{0}_{T}(L^{2})
. Therefore, from classical results for transport equations, we get that
\delta R\in C^{1}_{T}(L^{2})
, as claimed.

In the end, recalling that at the initial time
(\delta R,\delta\boldsymbol{u})_{|t=0}=0
, we can apply Theorem
4.4.2
to infer that
\|(\delta R,\delta\boldsymbol{u})\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2})}=0
. This implies the desired uniqueness.

4.4.1.2
A priori estimates

We start by bounding the
L^{p}
norms of the solutions. First, since
R
is transported by
\boldsymbol{u}
we have, for any
t\geq 0
,

\|R(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}=\|R_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}\,.

In addition, an energy estimate for the momentum equation in (
4.1.9
) yields

\|\boldsymbol{u}(t)\|_{L^{2}}\leq\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}\,.

(4.4.4)

Making use of the dyadic blocks
\Delta_{j}
, for
i=1,2
we find

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\Delta_{j}\,\partial_{i}R+\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\Delta_{j}\,\partial_{i}R=[\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla,\Delta_{j}]\,\partial_{i}R-\Delta_{j}(\partial_{i}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla R)\\
\partial_{t}\Delta_{j}\boldsymbol{u}+\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\Delta_{j}\boldsymbol{u}+\Delta_{j}\nabla\Pi=[\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla,\Delta_{j}]\boldsymbol{u}-\Delta_{j}(R\boldsymbol{u}^{\perp})\,.\end{cases}

(4.4.5)

Following the same lines performed in Subsection
4.2.2
, we can write

\begin{split}2^{j(s-1)}\|\Delta_{j}\nabla R(t)\|_{L^{2}}+2^{js}\|\Delta_{j}\boldsymbol{u}(t)\|_{L^{2}}&\leq C\left(2^{j(s-1)}\|\Delta_{j}\nabla R_{0}\|_{L^{2}}+2^{js}\|\Delta_{j}\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}\right)\\
&+C\int_{0}^{t}c_{j}\,(\tau)\|\boldsymbol{u}(\tau)\|_{H^{s}}\|R(\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}}\,\,{\rm d}\tau\\
&+C\int_{0}^{t}c_{j}(\tau)\left(\|\boldsymbol{u}(\tau)\|_{H^{s}}\|\nabla R(\tau)\|_{H^{s-1}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}(\tau)\|_{H^{s}}^{2}\right)\,\,{\rm d}\tau\,,\end{split}

for suitable sequences
(c_{j}(t))_{j\geq-1}
belonging to the unit sphere of
\ell^{2}
.

Now, we define for all
t\geq 0
:

\widetilde{E}(t):=\|R(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\nabla R(t)\|_{H^{s-1}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}(t)\|_{H^{s}}\,.

(4.4.6)

Thanks to the previous bounds, employing Minkowski’s inequality (see Section
B.3
), we gather

\widetilde{E}(t)\leq C\,\widetilde{E}(0)+C\int_{0}^{t}\widetilde{E}(\tau)^{2}\,\,{\rm d}\tau\,.

At this point, the goal is to close the estimate, bounding the integral on the right-hand side in a small time.

To this purpose, we define the time
T^{\ast}>0
such that

T^{\ast}:=\sup\left\{t>0:\int_{0}^{t}\widetilde{E}(\tau)^{2}\,\,{\rm d}\tau\leq\widetilde{E}(0)\right\}\,.

(4.4.7)

Then, we deduce
\widetilde{E}(t)\leq C\,\widetilde{E}(0)
for all times
t\in[0,T^{\ast}]
and for some positive constant
C=C(s)
.

4.4.1.3
The continuation criterion

This subsection is devoted to the implications of the continuation result (Proposition
4.4.3
below) for solutions to system (
4.1.9
). The proof is omitted, since it is an easy adaptation of the more complex case we will present in Subsection
4.4.2.5
.

Proposition 4.4.3

Let
T>0
and let
(R,\boldsymbol{u})
be a solution to system (
4.1.9
) on
[0,T[\,\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, enjoying the properties described in the previous Theorem
4.1.4
for all
t<T
. Assume that

\int_{0}^{T}\big\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}(t)\big\|_{L^{\infty}}\,{\rm d}t<+\infty\,.

(4.4.8)

Then,

\sup_{0\leq t<T}\left(\|R\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\nabla R\|_{H^{s-1}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{H^{s}}\right)<+\infty\,.

As an immediate corollary we have that if
T<+\infty
, then the couple
(R,\boldsymbol{u})
can be continued beyond
T
into a solution of system (
4.1.9
) with the same regularity.

As a matter of fact, Proposition
4.4.3
ensures that
\|R\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{\infty})}
,
\|\nabla R\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(H^{s-1})}
and
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}({H^{s}})}
are finite. From the previous Subsection
4.4.1.2
, we know that there exists a time
\overline{\tau}
depending on
s
,
\|R\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{\infty})}
,
\|\nabla R\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(H^{s-1})}
,
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}({H^{s}})}
and on the norm of the data such that for all
\widetilde{T}<T
, the quasi-homogeneous system with data
\big(R(\widetilde{T}),\boldsymbol{u}(\widetilde{T})\big)
has a unique solution until time
\overline{\tau}
. Now, taking
\widetilde{T}=T-\overline{\tau}/2
, we get a continuation of
(R,\boldsymbol{u})
up to time
T+\overline{\tau}/2
.

4.4.2
Well-posedness in Besov spaces

The main goal of this subsection is to review the lifespan estimate presented in
[
16
]
(for the MHD system) in order to get (
4.1.10
) (we refer also to Subsection
4.4.3
for the details of the proof). To show that, one has to work in critical Besov spaces where one can take advantage of the improved estimates for linear transport equations
à la
Hmidi-Keraani-Vishik. In order to ensure that the condition (
A.3.1
) is satisfied, the lowest regularity space we can reach is
B^{1}_{\infty,1}
. In addition, since
\boldsymbol{u}\in B^{1}_{\infty,1}
, we have that the
B^{0}_{\infty,1}
norm of the
{\rm curl}\,\boldsymbol{u}
can be bounded
linearly
with respect to
\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{1}_{t}(L^{\infty})}
, instead of exponentially as in classical
B^{s}_{p,r}
estimates (see Theorem
A.16
).

Finally, we construct a “bridge” between
H^{s}
and
B^{1}_{\infty,1}
Besov spaces establishing a continuation criterion, in the spirit of the one by Beale-Kato-Majda in
[
6
]
(see Subsection
4.4.2.5
).

We start by proving the local well-posedness result for system (
4.1.9
) in
B^{s}_{\infty,r}
and, in particular, in the end-point space
B^{1}_{\infty,1}
. In this regard, Subsection
4.4.2.1
is devoted to the
a priori
estimates, presenting also the standard lower bound (coming from the hyperbolic theory) for the lifespan of solutions. Next, we construct the smooth approximate solutions (in Subsection
4.4.2.2
) showing the uniform bounds for those regular solutions in Subsection
4.4.2.3
, and sketching the convergence (in the regularisation parameter
n
) argument in Subsection
4.4.2.4
.

Theorem 4.4.4

Let
(s,r)\in\mathbb{R}\times[1,+\infty]
such that
s>1
or
s=r=1
. Let
(R_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0})
be an initial datum such that
R_{0}\in B^{s}_{\infty,r}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and the divergence-free vector field
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap B^{s}_{\infty,r}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Then, there exists a time
T^{\ast}>0
such that system (
4.1.9
) has a unique solution
(R,\boldsymbol{u})
with the following regularity properties, if
r<+\infty
:

•

R\in C^{0}([0,T^{\ast}];B^{s}_{\infty,r}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\cap C^{1}([0,T^{\ast}];B^{s-1}_{\infty,r}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
;

•

\boldsymbol{u}
and
\nabla\Pi
belong to
C^{0}([0,T^{\ast}];B^{s}_{\infty,r}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\cap C^{1}([0,T^{\ast}];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap B^{s-1}_{\infty,r}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
.

In the case when
r=+\infty
, we need to replace
C^{0}([0,T^{\ast}];B^{s}_{\infty,r}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
by the space
C_{w}^{0}([0,T^{\ast}];B^{s}_{\infty,r}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
.

We highlight that the physically relevant
L^{2}
condition on
\boldsymbol{u}
, in the previous theorem, is necessary to control the low frequency part of the solution, so as to reconstruct the velocity from its
{\rm curl}\,
(see Lemma
4.4.5
below).

4.4.2.1
A priori estimate in
B^{s}_{\infty,r}

To begin with, we prove a general relation between a function and its
{\rm curl}\,
that will be useful in the sequel.

Lemma 4.4.5

Assume
f\in(L^{2}\cap B^{s}_{\infty,r})(\mathbb{R}^{2})
to be divergence-free. Denote by
{\rm curl}\,f:=-\partial_{2}f^{1}+\partial_{1}f^{2}
its
{\rm curl}\,
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Then, we have

\|f\|_{L^{2}\cap B^{s}_{\infty,r}}\sim\|f\|_{L^{2}}+\|{\rm curl}\,f\|_{B^{s-1}_{\infty,r}}\,.

(4.4.9)

Proof:

Using the divergence-free condition
{\rm div}\,f=0
, we can write the
Biot-Savart law
:

f^{1}=(-\Delta)^{-1}\partial_{2}\,{\rm curl}\,f\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad f^{2}=-(-\Delta)^{-1}\partial_{1}\,{\rm curl}\,f\,.

From that, we deduce

\|f\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}\sim\left\|\Delta_{-1}(-\Delta)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{2}(-1)^{i}\partial_{i}\,{\rm curl}\,f\right\|_{L^{\infty}}+\left\|\mathbbm{1}_{\{\nu\geq 0\}}\,2^{\nu s}\|\Delta_{\nu}(-\Delta)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{2}(-1)^{i}\partial_{i}\,{\rm curl}\,f\|_{L^{\infty}}\right\|_{\ell^{r}}\,.

On the one hand, if
\nu\geq 0
we know that
\Delta_{\nu}{\rm curl}\,f
is spectrally supported in an annulus, on which the symbol of
(-\Delta)^{-1}\partial_{i}
is smooth. Hence by employing Bernstein inequalities of Lemma
A.2
, we gather

2^{\nu s}\|\Delta_{\nu}(-\Delta)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\partial_{i}\,{\rm curl}\,f\|_{L^{\infty}}\sim\,2^{(s-1)\nu}\|\Delta_{\nu}{\rm curl}\,f\|_{L^{\infty}}\,.

On the other hand, using the fact that the symbol of
(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla{\rm curl}\,
is homogeneous of degree zero and bounded on the unit sphere, Bernstein inequalities yield

\|\Delta_{-1}(-\Delta)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\partial_{i}\,{\rm curl}\,f\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\|\Delta_{-1}(-\Delta)^{-1}\nabla{\rm curl}\,f\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{2}}\,.

Therefore,

\|f\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}\leq C\left(\|f\|_{L^{2}}+\|{\rm curl}\,f\|_{B_{\infty,r}^{s-1}}\right)\,.

This completes the proof of the lemma.

In the sequel of this subsection, we will show
a priori
estimates for smooth solutions in the relevant norms.

We start by recalling that the
L^{2}
norm of the velocity field is preserved. In other words, we have:

\|\boldsymbol{u}(t)\|_{L^{2}}=\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}\,.

(4.4.10)

Thanks to Lemma
4.4.5
, in order to bound
\boldsymbol{u}
in
B^{s}_{\infty,r}
, it will be enough to focus on estimates for
{\rm curl}\,\boldsymbol{u}
in
B^{s-1}_{\infty,r}
. Hence, we apply the
{\rm curl}\,
operator to the second equation in system (
4.1.9
) to get

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}R+\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla R=0\\
\partial_{t}\omega+\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\omega=-{\rm div}\,(R\boldsymbol{u})\,,\end{cases}

(4.4.11)

where we recall
\omega:={\rm curl}\,\boldsymbol{u}=-\partial_{2}u^{1}+\partial_{1}u^{2}
.

Now, since
R
is transported by
\boldsymbol{u}
we have, for any
t\geq 0
,

\|R(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}=\|R_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\|R_{0}\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}\,.

At this point we apply the dyadic blocks
\Delta_{j}
to the system (
4.4.11
) and we find

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\Delta_{j}R+\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\Delta_{j}R=[\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla,\Delta_{j}]R\\
\partial_{t}\Delta_{j}\omega+\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\Delta_{j}\omega=[\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla,\Delta_{j}]\omega-\Delta_{j}{\rm div}\,(R\boldsymbol{u})\,.\end{cases}

(4.4.12)

For the term
{\rm div}\,(R\boldsymbol{u})
, we have

\|{\rm div}\,(R\boldsymbol{u})\|_{B^{s-1}_{\infty,r}}\leq C\,\|R\boldsymbol{u}\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}\leq C\left(\|R\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|R\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}\right)\,.

(4.4.13)

Next, employing the commutator estimates (see Lemma
A.13
), we get

\begin{split}2^{js}\|[\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla,\Delta_{j}]R\|_{L^{\infty}}&\leq C\,c_{j}(t)\,\left(\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|R\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{B^{s-1}_{\infty,r}}\|\nabla R\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\\
&\leq C\,c_{j}(t)\,\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}\|R\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}\end{split}

(4.4.14)

and

\begin{split}2^{j(s-1)}\|[\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla,\Delta_{j}]\omega\|_{L^{\infty}}&\leq C\,c_{j}(t)\,\left(\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\omega\|_{B^{s-1}_{\infty,r}}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{B^{s-1}_{\infty,r}}\|\omega\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\\
&\leq C\,c_{j}(t)\,\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}^{2}\,,\end{split}

(4.4.15)

for suitable sequences
(c_{j}(t))_{j\geq-1}
belonging to the unit sphere of
\ell^{r}
.

Remark 4.4.6

We point out that we need the second estimate in Lemma
A.13
to deal with (
4.4.15
) in the cases
s<2
, and
s=2
and
r\neq 1
. In those cases, the Besov space
B^{s-1}_{\infty,r}
is not contained in the Lipschitz space
W^{1,\infty}
.

Summing up estimates (
4.4.13
), (
4.4.14
) and (
4.4.15
), one may derive

\begin{split}2^{js}\|\Delta_{j}R(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}+2^{j(s-1)}\|\Delta_{j}\omega(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}&\leq C\left(2^{js}\|\Delta_{j}R_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}+2^{j(s-1)}\|\Delta_{j}\omega_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\\
&+C\int_{0}^{t}c_{j}(\tau)\left(\|\boldsymbol{u}(\tau)\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}\|R(\tau)\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}(\tau)\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}^{2}\right)\,\,{\rm d}\tau.\end{split}

(4.4.16)

At this point, we define for all
t\geq 0
:

E(t):=\|R(t)\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}(t)\|_{L^{2}}+\|\omega(t)\|_{B^{s-1}_{\infty,r}}\,.

Thanks to the
L^{2}
estimate (
4.4.10
) and the bound (
4.4.16
), employing the Minkowski inequality, one may infer that

E(t)\leq C\,E(0)+\int_{0}^{t}E(\tau)^{2}\,\,{\rm d}\tau\,.

We define now
T^{\ast}>0
such that

T^{\ast}=\sup\left\{t>0:\int_{0}^{t}E(\tau)^{2}\,\,{\rm d}\tau\leq E(0)\right\}\,.

Then, we deduce
E(t)\leq C\,E(0)
for all times
t\in[0,T^{\ast}]
and for some positive constant
C=C(s,r,d)
. Therefore, for all
t\in[0,T^{\ast}]
, we gather

\int_{0}^{t}E(\tau)^{2}\,\,{\rm d}\tau\leq CtE(0)^{2}\,.

By using the definition of
T^{\ast}
and Lemma
4.4.5
, we finally argue that

T^{\ast}\geq\frac{C}{\|R_{0}\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\|_{L^{2}\cap B^{s}_{\infty,r}}}\,.

(4.4.17)

In other words, we have shown that one can close the estimates for a small time
T^{\ast}
, which is bounded from below by (
4.4.17
).

4.4.2.2
Construction of approximate solutions

Since the material in this subsection is standard and already presented in Subsection
4.2.1
for system (
4.1.1
), we will only sketch it.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let

(R^{n}_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{n}):=(S_{n}R_{0},\,S_{n}\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\,,

where
S_{n}
is the low frequency cut-off operator as in (
A.1.1
). By the assumption
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in L^{2}
, we have
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{n}\in H^{\infty}
and similarly
R_{0}^{n}\in C^{\infty}_{b}
. Moreover, one has

\begin{split}R_{0}^{n}\rightarrow R_{0}\quad&\text{in}\quad B^{s}_{\infty,r}\\
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{n}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\quad&\text{in}\quad L^{2}\cap B^{s}_{\infty,r}\,.\end{split}

(4.4.18)

Now, we will define the sequence of approximate solutions. First of all, we take
(R^{0},\boldsymbol{u}^{0})=(R^{0}_{0},\boldsymbol{u}^{0}_{0})
. Then, for all
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
we get
R^{0}\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+};B^{\sigma}_{\infty,r})
and
\boldsymbol{u}^{0}\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+};H^{\sigma})
with
{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{u}^{0}=0
. Next, we assume that
(R^{n},\boldsymbol{u}^{n})
is given such that, for all
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
,

R^{n}\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+};B^{\sigma}_{\infty,r}),\quad\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+};H^{\sigma})\quad\text{and}\quad{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{u}^{n}=0\,.

We start by defining
R^{n+1}
as the unique solution to the linear transport equation

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}R^{n+1}+\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\cdot\nabla R^{n+1}=0\\
R^{n+1}_{|t=0}=R_{0}^{n+1}\,,\end{cases}

(4.4.19)

and we deduce that
R^{n+1}\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+};B^{\sigma}_{\infty,r})
for all
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
.

Next, we solve the linear transport equation with the divergence-free condition:

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}+\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}+\nabla\Pi^{n+1}=-R^{n+1}\boldsymbol{u}^{\perp,n}\\
{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}=0\\
\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{|t=0}=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{n+1}\,.\end{cases}

(4.4.20)

We point out that the right-hand side term belongs to
L^{1}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+};H^{\sigma})
for any
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
. At this point, one can solve the previous linear problem by energy methods (see Propositions 3.2 and 3.4 in
[
20
]
) to find an unique solution
\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}_{+};H^{\sigma})
.

4.4.2.3
Uniform bounds

We show now uniform bounds for the sequence
(R^{n},\boldsymbol{u}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
constructed in the previous Paragraph
4.4.2.2
.

We argue by induction and prove that there exists a time
T^{\ast}>0
such that, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\in[0,T^{\ast}]
, one has

\displaystyle\|R^{n}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\|R_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}

(4.4.21)

\displaystyle\|R^{n}(t)\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}(t)\|_{L^{2}\cap B^{s}_{\infty,r}}\leq CK_{0}\,e^{CK_{0}t}\,,

(4.4.22)

where the constant
C>0
does not depend on the data neither on the solutions, and where we have defined

K_{0}:=\|R^{n}_{0}\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{0}\|_{L^{2}\cap B^{s}_{\infty,r}}\,.

It is clear that the couple
(R^{0},\boldsymbol{u}^{0})
satisfies the previous bounds. Assume now that
(R^{n},\boldsymbol{u}^{n})
verifies (
4.4.21
) and (
4.4.22
) on some interval
[0,T^{\ast}]
. Then, we have to prove the same properties for the step
n+1
.

We start by bounding
R^{n+1}
. We deduce that, for any
t\geq 0
,

\|R^{n+1}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}=\|R^{n+1}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\|R_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\|R_{0}\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}\,.

Next, employing an energy estimate for the velocity field, one can get

\begin{split}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}(t)\|_{L^{2}}&\leq\|\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}+C\int_{0}^{t}\|R^{n+1}(\tau)\boldsymbol{u}^{\perp,n}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}}\,\,{\rm d}\tau\\
&\leq C\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}+C\|R_{0}\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}\int_{0}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}}\,\,{\rm d}\tau\,.\end{split}

At this point, to get uniform bounds for the Besov norms, we resort the vorticity formulation:

\partial_{t}\omega^{n+1}+\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\cdot\nabla\omega^{n+1}=\mathcal{L}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1})+{\rm div}\,(R^{n+1}\boldsymbol{u}^{n})\,,

where

\mathcal{L}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1})=\sum_{k=1}^{2}\partial_{2}u_{k}^{n}\,\partial_{k}u_{1}^{n+1}-\partial_{1}u_{k}^{n}\,\partial_{k}u_{2}^{n+1}\,.

(4.4.23)

Since the bound for
{\rm div}\,(R^{n+1}\boldsymbol{u}^{n})
is analogous to the one performed in (
4.4.13
), it remains to bound
\mathcal{L}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1})
in
B^{s-1}_{\infty,r}
.

Lemma 4.4.7

Let
(\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w})
be a couple of divergence-free vector fields in
B^{s}_{\infty,r}
. Then, one has

\|\mathcal{L}(\nabla\boldsymbol{v},\nabla\boldsymbol{w})\|_{B^{s-1}_{\infty,r}}\leq C\left(\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\boldsymbol{w}\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{w}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}\right)\,.

Proof:

The estimate easily follows from Corollary
A.8
if
s>1
. Then, we have to show the bound when
\nabla\boldsymbol{v}
and
\nabla\boldsymbol{w}
are in
B^{0}_{\infty,1}
which is not an algebra.

Due to the fact that
\boldsymbol{v}
and
\boldsymbol{w}
are divergence-free, we can write

\mathcal{L}(\nabla\boldsymbol{v},\nabla\boldsymbol{w})=\sum_{k=1}^{2}\partial_{k}(w^{1}\,\partial_{2}v^{k})-\partial_{k}(w^{2}\,\partial_{1}v^{k})\,.

(4.4.24)

Now, making use of Bony decomposition (we refer to Section
A.2
for more details), we have

\mathcal{L}(\nabla\boldsymbol{v},\nabla\boldsymbol{w})=\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{T}}(\nabla\boldsymbol{v},\nabla\boldsymbol{w})+\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{R}}(\nabla\boldsymbol{v},\nabla\boldsymbol{w})\,,

where

\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{T}}(\nabla\boldsymbol{v},\nabla\boldsymbol{w}):=\sum_{k=1}^{2}\mathcal{T}_{\partial_{k}w^{1}}(\partial_{2}v^{k})+\mathcal{T}_{\partial_{2}v^{k}}(\partial_{k}w^{1})-\mathcal{T}_{\partial_{k}w^{2}}(\partial_{1}v^{k})-\mathcal{T}_{\partial_{1}v^{k}}(\partial_{k}w^{2})

and

\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{R}}(\nabla\boldsymbol{v},\nabla\boldsymbol{w})=\sum_{k=1}^{2}\mathcal{R}(\partial_{k}w^{1},\,\partial_{2}v^{k})-\mathcal{R}(\partial_{k}w^{2},\,\partial_{1}v^{k})\,.

On the one hand, thanks to Proposition
A.7
, we can estimate the paraproducts in the following way:

\|\mathcal{T}_{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}(\nabla\boldsymbol{w})\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}+\|\mathcal{T}_{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}(\nabla\boldsymbol{v})\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\leq C\left(\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{w}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{w}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\right)\,.

On the other hand, due to relation (
4.4.24
) we may write

\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{R}}(\nabla\boldsymbol{v},\nabla\boldsymbol{w})=\sum_{k=1}^{2}\partial_{k}\mathcal{R}(w^{1},\,\partial_{2}v^{k})-\partial_{k}\mathcal{R}(w^{2},\,\partial_{1}v^{k})\,.

Now, again thanks to Proposition
A.7
we have

\|\partial_{k}\mathcal{R}(w^{2},\,\partial_{1}v^{k})\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\leq C\,\|\mathcal{R}(w^{2},\,\partial_{1}v^{k})\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}\leq C\|\nabla\boldsymbol{w}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,\infty}}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}\leq C\|\nabla\boldsymbol{w}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}

since
L^{\infty}\hookrightarrow B^{0}_{\infty,\infty}
. Similar argumentations apply to
\|\partial_{k}\mathcal{R}(w^{1},\,\partial_{2}v^{k})\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}
.

Then, one has
