\|\mathcal{L}(\nabla\boldsymbol{v},\nabla\boldsymbol{w})\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,r}}\leq C\left(\|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\boldsymbol{w}\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,r}}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{w}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,r}}\right)\,.

This concludes the proof in the case
s=1
.

Therefore, applying Lemma
4.4.7
with
\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{u}^{n}
and
\boldsymbol{w}=\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}
, one can get

\|\mathcal{L}(\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{n},\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1})\|_{B^{s-1}_{\infty,r}}\leq C\left(\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}\right)\,.

Reached this point, one can exactly proceed as in the proof for the
a priori
estimates, finding that

\begin{split}2^{js}\|\Delta_{j}R^{n+1}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}+2^{j(s-1)}\|\Delta_{j}\omega^{n+1}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}&\leq C\left(2^{js}\|\Delta_{j}R^{n+1}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}+2^{j(s-1)}\|\Delta_{j}\omega^{n+1}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\\
&+C\int_{0}^{t}c_{j}(\tau)\left(\|\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}+\|R^{n+1}\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}\right)\|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}\,\,{\rm d}\tau,\end{split}

where the sequence
(c_{j}(t))_{j\geq-1}
belongs to the unit sphere of
\ell^{r}
.

Now, we define for all
t\geq 0
:

\overline{E}^{n+1}(t):=\|R^{n+1}(t)\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{n+1}(t)\|_{L^{2}\cap B^{s}_{\infty,r}}\,.

Thus, recalling Lemma

4.4.5
, from the previous inequalities we obtain

\overline{E}^{n+1}(t)\leq C\,\overline{E}^{n+1}(0)+C\int_{0}^{t}\overline{E}^{n+1}(\tau)\|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}\cap B^{s}_{\infty,r}}\,\,{\rm d}\tau.

An application of Grönwall arguments and the fact that
\overline{E}^{n+1}(0)\leq CK_{0}
, give

\overline{E}^{n+1}(t)\leq CK_{0}\exp\left(C\int_{0}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}\cap B^{s}_{\infty,r}}\,{\rm d}\tau\right),

where
K_{0}:=\|R^{n}_{0}\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}_{0}\|_{L^{2}\cap B^{s}_{\infty,r}}
.

Next, from the inductive assumption (
4.4.22
), we get

\int_{0}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{n}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}\cap B^{s}_{\infty,r}}\,{\rm d}\tau\leq e^{CK_{0}t}-1

and we notice that for
0\leq x\leq 1
one has
e^{x}-1\leq x+x^{2}\leq 2x
. So, if we choose
T^{\ast}>0
such that
CK_{0}T^{\ast}\leq 1
, we have

\overline{E}^{n+1}(t)\leq CK_{0}\exp(e^{CK_{0}t}-1)\leq CK_{0}\exp(CK_{0}t)\quad\quad\text{for}\;\;t\in[0,T^{\ast}]\,.

In this way we have completed the proof of the uniform bounds.

4.4.2.4
Convergence

We show now convergence of the sequence
(R^{n},\boldsymbol{u}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
towards a solution
(R,\boldsymbol{u})
of the original problem.
The proof follows the arguments already performed in Subsection
4.2.3
: we limit ourselves to highlight only the main steps.

We define

\widetilde{R}^{n}:=R^{n}-R^{n}_{0}

which satisfies

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\widetilde{R}^{n+1}=-\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\cdot\nabla R^{n+1}\\
\widetilde{R}^{n+1}_{|t=0}=0\,.\end{cases}

Thus, one can check that
(\widetilde{R}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is uniformly bounded in
C^{0}([0,T];L^{2})
.

Now, we will prove that
(\widetilde{R}^{n},\boldsymbol{u}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence in
C^{0}([0,T];L^{2})
. For any couple
(n,l)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
, we introduce

\begin{split}&\delta\widetilde{R}^{n,l}:=\widetilde{R}^{n+l}-\widetilde{R}^{n}\\
&\delta R^{n,l}:=R^{n+l}-R^{n}\\
&\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{n,l}:=\boldsymbol{u}^{n+l}-\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\\
&\delta\Pi^{n,l}:=\Pi^{n+l}-\Pi^{n}\,,\end{split}

and we have that
{\rm div}\,\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{n,l}=0
for any
(n,l)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
.

Taking the difference between the
(n+l)
-iterate and the
n
-iterate, we may find

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\delta\widetilde{R}^{n,l}+\boldsymbol{u}^{n+l-1}\cdot\nabla\delta\widetilde{R}^{n,l}=-\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{n-1,l}\cdot\nabla R^{n}+\boldsymbol{u}^{n+l-1}\cdot\nabla\delta R_{0}^{n,l}\\
\partial_{t}\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{n,l}+\boldsymbol{u}^{n+l-1}\cdot\nabla\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{n,l}+\nabla\delta\Pi^{n,l}=-\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{n-1,l}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{n}-R^{n+l}(\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\perp})^{n-1,l}-\delta R^{n,l}\boldsymbol{u}^{\perp,n-1}\,,\end{cases}

(4.4.25)

supplemented with the initial data
(\delta\widetilde{R}^{n,l},\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{n,l})_{|t=0}=(0,\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{n,l}_{0})
.

An energy estimate for the first equation of (
4.4.25
) yields

\|\delta\widetilde{R}^{n,l}(t)\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\int_{0}^{t}\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{n-1,l}\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla R^{n}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{n+l-1}\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla\delta R_{0}^{n,l}\|_{L^{\infty}}\,\,{\rm d}\tau\,,

and similarly from the second equation we obtain

\begin{split}\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{n,l}(t)\|_{L^{2}}&\leq C\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{n,l}\|_{L^{2}}+C\int_{0}^{t}\left(\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{n-1,l}\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{n}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{n-1,l}\|_{L^{2}}\|R^{n+l}\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\,\,{\rm d}\tau\\
&+C\int_{0}^{t}\left(\|\delta\widetilde{R}^{n,l}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\delta R^{n,l}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\|\boldsymbol{u}^{n-1}\|_{L^{2}\cap L^{\infty}}\,\,{\rm d}\tau\,.\end{split}

Employing the uniform bounds established in Paragraph
4.4.2.3
and the embeddings, we note that

\sup_{t\in[0,T^{\ast}]}\left(\|\nabla R^{n}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{n}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|R^{n+l}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)+\int_{0}^{T^{\ast}}\|\boldsymbol{u}^{n+l-1}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}^{n-1}\|_{L^{2}\cap L^{\infty}}\,{\rm d}t\leq C_{T^{\ast}},

for a constant
C_{T\ast}
which depends only on
T^{\ast}
and on the initial data.

Therefore, thanks to the Grönwall lemma, we get

\|\delta\widetilde{R}^{n,l}(t)\|_{L^{2}}+\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{n,l}(t)\|_{L^{2}}\leq C_{T^{\ast}}\left(\|\delta R^{n,l}_{0}\|_{W^{1,\infty}}+\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{n,l}\|_{L^{2}}+\int_{0}^{t}\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{n-1,l}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}}\,{\rm d}\tau\right),

for all
t\in[0,T^{\ast}]
.

As already done in Subsection
4.2.3
, after setting

F_{0}^{n}:=\sup_{l\geq 0}\left(\|\delta R^{n,l}_{0}\|_{W^{1,\infty}}+\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}_{0}^{n,l}\|_{L^{2}}\right)\quad\quad\text{ and }\quad\quad G^{n}(t):=\sup_{l\geq 0}\sup_{[0,t]}\left(\|\delta\widetilde{R}^{n,l}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{n,l}\|_{L^{2}}\right)\,,

we may infer that, for all
t\in[0,T^{\ast}]
,

G^{n}(t)\leq C_{T^{\ast}}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{(C_{T^{\ast}}T^{\ast})^{k}}{k!}F_{0}^{n-k}+\frac{(C_{T^{\ast}}T^{\ast})^{n}}{n!}G^{0}(t)\,,

and, bearing in mind (
4.4.18
),
we have

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}F^{n}_{0}=0\,.

Hence,

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\sup_{l\geq 0}\sup_{t\in[0,T^{\ast}]}\left(\|\delta\widetilde{R}^{n,l}(t)\|_{L^{2}}+\|\delta\boldsymbol{u}^{n,l}(t)\|_{L^{2}}\right)=0\,.

This property implies that
(\widetilde{R}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\boldsymbol{u}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are Cauchy sequences in
C^{0}_{T^{\ast}}(L^{2})
. Hence, converge to some function
\widetilde{R}
and
\boldsymbol{u}
in the same space.

Define
R:=\widetilde{R}+R_{0}
. We notice that, owing to the embedding
L^{2}\hookrightarrow B^{-1}_{\infty,2}
, and thanks to the uniform bounds and to an interpolation argument, the sequence
(\boldsymbol{u}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
strongly converges in any intermediate space
L^{\infty}_{T^{\ast}}(B^{\sigma}_{\infty,r})
with
\sigma<s
and in particular in
L^{\infty}([0,T^{\ast}]\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Moreover, we have that
R^{n}=\widetilde{R}^{n}+R^{n}_{0}
strongly converges to
R
in
L^{\infty}_{T^{\ast}}(L^{2}_{\rm loc})
. This is enough to pass to the limit in the weak formulation of (
4.4.19
) and (
4.4.20
) finding that
(R,\boldsymbol{u})
is a weak solution to the original problem for a suitable pressure term
\nabla\Pi
. The regularity for
(R,\boldsymbol{u})
in
B^{s}_{\infty,r}
follows by the uniform bounds and Fatou’s property in Besov spaces.

Moreover, an argument similar to the one performed in Subsection
4.2.3
apply here to show the desired regularity for the pressure term, after noticing that

\|\nabla\Pi\|_{L^{2}\cap B^{s}_{\infty,r}}\sim\|\nabla\Pi\|_{L^{2}}+\|\Delta\Pi\|_{B^{s-1}_{\infty,r}}\,.

Finally, employing classical results for transport equations in Besov spaces (remember Theorem
A.15
), we can get the claimed time continuity of
R
with values in
B^{s}_{\infty,r}
, of
\boldsymbol{u}
with values in
L^{2}\cap B^{s}_{\infty,r}
and of
\nabla\Pi
with values in
L^{2}\cap B^{s}_{\infty,r}
. In addition, the sought regularity properties for the time derivatives
\partial_{t}R
and
\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}
follow from an analysis of system (
4.1.9
).

4.4.2.5
Continuation criterion in Besov spaces

We conclude this section showing the following continuation criterion for solutions of system (
4.1.9
) in
B^{s}_{\infty,r}
, where the couple
(s,r)
satisfies the Lipschitz condition (
A.3.1
).

Proposition 4.4.8

Let
(R_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\in B^{s}_{\infty,r}\times(L^{2}\cap B^{s}_{\infty,r})
with
{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{0}=0
. Given a time
T>0
, let
(R,\boldsymbol{u})
be a solution of (
4.1.9
) on
[0,T[
that belongs to
L^{\infty}_{t}(B^{s}_{\infty,r})\times L^{\infty}_{t}(L^{2}\cap B^{s}_{\infty,r})
for any
t\in[0,T[
. If we assume that

\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\,\,{\rm d}t<+\infty\,,

(4.4.26)

then
(R,\boldsymbol{u})
can be continued beyond
T
into a solution of (
4.1.9
) with the same regularity.

Moreover, the lifespan of a solution
(R,\boldsymbol{u})
to system (
4.1.9
) does not depend on
(s,r)
and, in particular, the lifespan of solutions in Theorem
4.1.4
is the same as the lifespan in

B^{1}_{\infty,1}\times\left(L^{2}\cap B^{1}_{\infty,1}\right)
.

Proof:

It is enough to show that, under condition (
4.4.26
), the solution
(R,\boldsymbol{u})
remains bounded in the space
L^{\infty}_{T}(B^{s}_{\infty,r})\times L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2}\cap B^{s}_{\infty,r})
. Recalling the
a priori
estimates for the non-linear terms in system (
4.4.12
), we have

\begin{split}2^{js}\|[\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla,\Delta_{j}]R\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\,c_{j}(t)\,\left(\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\nabla R\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\left(\|R\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}\right)\end{split}

and

\begin{split}2^{j(s-1)}\|[\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla,\Delta_{j}]\omega\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq Cc_{j}(t)\,\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}\,,\end{split}

where we have used the fact that
\|\omega\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}
and
\|\omega\|_{B^{s-1}_{\infty,r}}\leq C\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}
.
Moreover, from relation (
4.4.13
), we obtain

\begin{split}\|{\rm div}\,(R\boldsymbol{u})\|_{B^{s-1}_{\infty,r}}\leq C\left(\|R\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\left(\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}+\|R\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}\right)\,.\end{split}

Summing the previous bounds, for all
0\leq t\leq T
, we get

\begin{split}\|R(t)\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}+\|\omega(t)\|_{B^{s-1}_{\infty,r}}&\leq C\left(\|R_{0}\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}+\|\omega_{0}\|_{B^{s-1}_{\infty,r}}\right)\\
&+C\int_{0}^{t}\left(\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|R\|_{W^{1,\infty}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\left(\|R\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}\right)\,\,{\rm d}\tau\,.\end{split}

At this point, we have to find estimates for
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}
and
\|R\|_{W^{1,\infty}}
. To deal with
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}
, we separate low and hight frequencies deducing

\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\|\Delta_{-1}\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\sum_{j\geq 0}\|\Delta_{j}\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}+\sum_{j\geq 0}\|\Delta_{j}\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\,,

where we have also employed the Bernstein inequalities (see Lemma
A.2
).

For the high frequency terms, we can write

\sum_{j\geq 0}\|\Delta_{j}\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\sum_{j\geq 0}2^{-j}\|\Delta_{j}\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\,.

Therefore,

\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\left(\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\,.

(4.4.27)

Now, we focus on the bound for
\|R\|_{W^{1,\infty}}
. On the one hand,
\|R(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}=\|R_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}
, for all
t\geq 0
.
On the other hand, differentiating the continuity equation, we obtain

\|\nabla R(t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{\infty})}\leq\|\nabla R_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}\exp\left(C\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\,\,{\rm d}t\right)\,.

(4.4.28)

Thus, using the previous relations and recalling equation (
4.4.9
), we finally have

\begin{split}\|R(t)\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}(t)\|_{L^{2}\cap B^{s}_{\infty,r}}&\leq C\left(\|R_{0}\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\|_{L^{2}\cap B^{s}_{\infty,r}}\right)\\
&+C\int_{0}^{t}\left(\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|R_{0}\|_{W^{1,\infty}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}\right)\left(\|R\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{2}\cap B^{s}_{\infty,r}}\right)\,\,{\rm d}\tau\,.\end{split}

In the end, employing Grönwall’s type inequalities, we may conclude that, under the assumption (
4.4.26
),

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(\|R(t)\|_{B^{s}_{\infty,r}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}(t)\|_{L^{2}\cap B^{s}_{\infty,r}}\right)<+\infty\,.

4.4.3
The asymptotically global well-posedness result

In this paragraph we focus on finding an asymptotic behaviour (in the regime of small oscillations for the densities) for the lifespan of solutions to system (
4.1.9
). Namely, for small fluctuations
R_{0}
of size
\delta>0
, the lifespan of solutions to this system tends to infinity when
\delta\rightarrow 0^{+}
. To show that, we have to take advantage of the
linear
estimate in Theorem
A.16
for the transport equations in Besov spaces with zero regularity index. For that reason, it is important to work with the vorticity formulation of (
4.1.9
), since
\omega\in B^{0}_{\infty,1}
. Thanks to the continuation criterion presented in Proposition
4.4.8
, it is enough to find the bound of the lifespan in the lowest regularity space
B^{1}_{\infty,1}
.

To begin with, we recall relation (
4.4.9
), i.e.

\|f\|_{L^{2}\cap B^{s}_{\infty,r}}\sim\|f\|_{L^{2}}+\|{\rm curl}\,f\|_{B^{s-1}_{\infty,r}}\,.

Therefore, due to the previous relation, we can define (for
t\geq 0
)

\mathcal{E}(t):=\|\boldsymbol{u}(t)\|_{L^{2}}+\|\omega(t)\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\sim\|\boldsymbol{u}(t)\|_{L^{2}\cap B^{1}_{\infty,1}}\,.

(4.4.29)

Since the
L^{2}
norm of the velocity field is preserved, to control
\boldsymbol{u}
in
B^{1}_{\infty,1}
, it will be enough to find estimates for
{\rm curl}\,\boldsymbol{u}
in
B^{0}_{\infty,1}
. Hence, we apply again the
{\rm curl}\,
operator to the second equation in system (
4.1.9
) to get the system (
4.4.11
), i.e.

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}R+\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla R=0\\
\partial_{t}\omega+\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\omega=-{\rm div}\,(R\boldsymbol{u})\,.\end{cases}

Making use of Theorem
A.16
, we obtain

\|\omega(t)\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\leq C\left(\|\omega_{0}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}+\int_{0}^{t}\|{\rm div}\,(R\boldsymbol{u})\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\,{\rm d}\tau\right)\left(1+\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\,{\rm d}\tau\right)\,.

Now, we look at the bound for
{\rm div}\,(R\boldsymbol{u})
, finding that

\|{\rm div}\,(R\boldsymbol{u})\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\leq C\left(\|R\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|R\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}\right)\leq C\|R\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}\mathcal{E}(\tau)\,.

Then, we deduce

\mathcal{E}(t)\leq C\left(\mathcal{E}(0)+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{E}(\tau)\|R(\tau)\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}\,\,{\rm d}\tau\right)\left(1+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{E}(\tau)\,\,{\rm d}\tau\right)\,.

(4.4.30)

At this point, Theorem
A.15
implies that

\|R(t)\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}\leq\|R_{0}\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}\exp\left(C\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{E}(\tau)\,\,{\rm d}\tau\right)\,.

Plugging this bound into (
4.4.30
) gives

\mathcal{E}(t)\leq C\left(1+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{E}(\tau)\,\,{\rm d}\tau\right)\left(\mathcal{E}(0)+\|R_{0}\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{E}(\tau)\exp\left(\int_{0}^{\tau}\mathcal{E}(s)\,\,{\rm d}s\right)\,\,{\rm d}\tau\right)\,.

We now define

T^{\ast}:=\sup\left\{t>0:\|R_{0}\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{E}(\tau)\exp\left(\int_{0}^{\tau}\mathcal{E}(s)\,\,{\rm d}s\right)\,\,{\rm d}\tau\leq\mathcal{E}(0)\right\}\,.

(4.4.31)

Then, for all
t\in[0,T^{\ast}]
, we deduce

\mathcal{E}(t)\leq C\left(1+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{E}(\tau)\,\,{\rm d}\tau\right)\mathcal{E}(0)

and thanks to the Grönwall’s lemma we infer

\mathcal{E}(t)\leq C\mathcal{E}(0)\,e^{C\mathcal{E}(0)t}\,,

(4.4.32)

for a suitable constant
C>0
.

It remains to find a control on the integral of
\mathcal{E}(t)
. We have

\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{E}(\tau)\,\,{\rm d}\tau\leq e^{C\mathcal{E}(0)t}-1

and, due to the previous bound (
4.4.32
), we get

\begin{split}\|R_{0}\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{E}(\tau)\exp\left(\int_{0}^{\tau}\mathcal{E}(s)\,\,{\rm d}s\right)\,\,{\rm d}\tau&\leq C\|R_{0}\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{E}(0)\,e^{C\mathcal{E}(0)\tau}\exp\left(e^{C\mathcal{E}(0)\tau}-1\right)\,\,{\rm d}\tau\\
&\leq C\|R_{0}\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}\left(\exp\left(e^{C\mathcal{E}(0)t}-1\right)-1\right)\,.\end{split}

(4.4.33)

Finally, by definition (
4.4.31
) of
T^{\ast}
, we can argue that

\mathcal{E}(0)\leq C\|R_{0}\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}\left(\exp\left(e^{C\mathcal{E}(0)T^{\ast}}-1\right)-1\right)\,,

which gives the following lower bound for the lifespan of solutions:

T^{\ast}\geq\frac{C}{\mathcal{E}(0)}\log\left(\log\left(C\frac{\mathcal{E}(0)}{\|R_{0}\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}}+1\right)+1\right)\,.

From there, recalling the definition (
4.4.29
) for
\mathcal{E}(0)
, we have

T^{\ast}\geq\frac{C}{\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\|_{L^{2}\cap B^{1}_{\infty,1}}}\log\left(\log\left(C\frac{\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\|_{L^{2}\cap B^{1}_{\infty,1}}}{\|R_{0}\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}}+1\right)+1\right)\,,

(4.4.34)

for a suitable constant
C>0
. This is the claimed lower bound stated in Theorem
4.1.4
.

4.5
The lifespan of solutions to the primitive problem

The main goal of this section is to present an “asymptotically global” well-posedness result for system (
4.2.3
), when the size of fluctuations of the densities goes to zero, in the spirit of Subsection
4.4.3
. We start by showing a continuation type criterion for system (
4.2.3
) and discussing the related consequences (see Subsection
4.5.1
below for details).
We conclude this section presenting the asymptotic behaviour of the lifespan of solutions to system (
4.2.3
): the lifespan may be very large, if the size of non-homogeneities
a_{0,\varepsilon}
defined in (
4.2.1
) is small (see relation (
4.5.14
) below). We point out that it is
not
clear at all that the global existence holds in a fast rotation regime without any assumption of smallness on the size of the densities.

4.5.1
The continuation criterion and consequences

In this paragraph, we start by presenting a continuation type result in Sobolev spaces for system (
4.2.3
), in the spirit of the Beale-Kato-Majda continuation criterion
[
6
]
.
The proof is an adaptation of the arguments in
[
4
]
by Bae, Lee and Shin.

Proposition 4.5.1

Take
\varepsilon\in\;]0,1]
fixed. Let
(a_{0,\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon})\in L^{\infty}\times H^{s}
with
\nabla a_{0,\varepsilon}\in H^{s-1}
and
{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{0,\varepsilon}=0
. Given a time
T>0
, let
(a_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon},\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon})
be a solution of (
4.2.3
) on
[0,T[
that belongs to
L^{\infty}_{t}(L^{\infty})\times L^{\infty}_{t}(H^{s})\times L^{\infty}_{t}(H^{s})
and
\nabla a_{\varepsilon}\in L^{\infty}_{t}(H^{s-1})
for any
t\in[0,T[
. If we assume that

\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\,\,{\rm d}t<+\infty\,,

(4.5.1)

then
(a_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon},\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon})
can be continued beyond
T
into a solution of (
4.2.3
) with the same regularity.

Proof:

As already pointed out in the proof of Proposition
4.4.8
, it would be enough to show that

\sup_{0\leq t<T}\left(\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{s}}+\|\nabla a_{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{s-1}}\right)<+\infty\,.

Since
\varepsilon\in\,]0,1]
is fixed and does not play any role in the following proof, for notational convenience, we set it equal to 1.

We start by recalling that, from the continuity equation of (
4.2.3
), one gets

\partial_{t}\partial_{i}a+\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\partial_{i}a=-\partial_{i}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla a\quad\quad\text{for}\;i=1,2\,.

(4.5.2)

So, applying the operator
\Delta_{j}
to the above relation and using the divergence-free condition
{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{u}=0
, one has

\partial_{t}\Delta_{j}\partial_{i}a+\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\Delta_{j}\partial_{i}a=-\Delta_{j}\left(\partial_{i}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla a\right)+[\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla,\Delta_{j}]\partial_{i}a\,.

Therefore, thanks to the commutator estimates (see Lemma
A.13
), one may argue that

2^{j(s-1)}\|[\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla,\Delta_{j}]\partial_{i}a\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\,c_{j}(t)\left(\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\left(\|\nabla a\|_{H^{s-1}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{H^{s}}\right)\,,

where
(c_{j}(t))_{j\geq-1}
is a sequence in the unit ball of
\ell^{2}
, and due to Corollary
A.8
one has

\|\partial_{i}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla a\|_{H^{s-1}}\leq C\left(\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla a\|_{H^{s-1}}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{H^{s-1}}\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\,.

At this point, we recall the bounds for the momentum equation in system (
4.2.3
). First of all, we apply the non-homogeneous dyadic blocks
\Delta_{j}
, getting

\partial_{t}\Delta_{j}\boldsymbol{u}+\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\Delta_{j}\boldsymbol{u}+\Delta_{j}\boldsymbol{u}^{\perp}+\nabla\Delta_{j}\Pi+\Delta_{j}\left(a\nabla\Pi\right)=[\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla,\Delta_{j}]\boldsymbol{u}\,.

Then, we obtain

2^{js}\|[\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla,\Delta_{j}]\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\,c_{j}(t)\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{H^{s}}

with
(c_{j}(t))_{j\geq-1}
a sequence in the unit ball of
\ell^{2}
, and in addition we have

\|a\nabla\Pi\|_{H^{s}}\leq C\left(\|a\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\Pi\|_{H^{s}}+\|\nabla\Pi\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla a\|_{H^{s-1}}\right)\,.

Summing up the previous inequalities, for all
t\in[0,T[
we may infer that

\begin{split}\|\nabla a(t)\|_{H^{s-1}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}(t)\|_{H^{s}}&\leq\left(\|\nabla a_{0}\|_{H^{s-1}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\|_{H^{s}}\right)+C\int_{0}^{t}\|a\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\Pi\|_{H^{s}}\,{\rm d}\tau\\
&+C\int_{0}^{t}\left(\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\nabla\Pi\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\left(\|\nabla a\|_{H^{s-1}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{H^{s}}\right)\,{\rm d}\tau\,.\end{split}

To close the proof, under the hypothesis of the theorem, we have to find the bounds
\|\nabla a\|_{L^{1}_{T}(L^{\infty})}
,
\|\nabla\Pi\|_{L^{1}_{T}(L^{\infty})}
and
\|\nabla\Pi\|_{L^{1}_{T}(H^{s})}
.

From the continuity equation (
4.5.2
), we obtain

\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{\infty})}\leq C\|\nabla a_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}\exp\left(\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\,{\rm d}\tau\right)\,.

Now, we focus on the estimate
\|\nabla\Pi\|_{H^{s}}
.
We recall again the elliptic equation

-{\rm div}\,(A\nabla\Pi)={\rm div}\,F\,\quad\text{where}\quad F:=\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}+\boldsymbol{u}^{\perp}\quad\text{and}\quad A:=1/\varrho\,.

(4.5.3)

From the previous relation, it is a standard matter to deduce that (see also Proposition
B.11
):

\|\nabla\Pi\|_{H^{s}}\leq C\left(\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{H^{s}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{H^{s}}+\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\Pi\|_{H^{s-1}}+\|\nabla\Pi\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla a\|_{H^{s-1}}\right)\,.

Using an interpolation argument, one has

\|\nabla\Pi\|_{H^{s-1}}\leq C\|\nabla\Pi\|_{L^{2}}^{1/s}\|\nabla\Pi\|_{H^{s}}^{1-1/s}

and due to the Young’s inequality we end up with

\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\Pi\|_{H^{s-1}}\leq C\left(\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}^{s}\|\nabla\Pi\|_{L^{2}}+\left(1-\frac{1}{s}\right)\|\nabla\Pi\|_{H^{s}}\right)\,.

In addition, we already know that

\|\nabla\Pi\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\left(\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{2}}\right)\leq C\left(\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}\right)\leq C\left(\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}+1\right)\,.

As it is apparent the constant term on the right-hand side will be irrelevant in the next computations: hence, it will be omitted supposing e.g. that
\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}>1
.

At this point, we have only to take care of the
L^{\infty}
bound for the pressure term. Thanks to an application of Gagliardo-Nirenberg (see Theorem
B.9
) and Young inequalities, we get

\|\nabla\Pi\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\|\Delta\Pi\|_{L^{4}}^{2/3}\|\nabla\Pi\|_{L^{2}}^{1/3}\leq C\left(\|\Delta\Pi\|_{L^{4}}+\|\nabla\Pi\|_{L^{2}}\right)\leq C\left(\|\Delta\Pi\|_{L^{4}}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\,.

Again from the elliptic equation (
4.5.3
), one can find

\Delta\Pi=-\varrho\nabla a\cdot\nabla\Pi-\varrho\,{\rm div}\,\left(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\right)-\varrho\,{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{u}^{\perp}

and then

\begin{split}\|\Delta\Pi\|_{L^{4}}&\leq C\left(\|\varrho\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\Pi\|_{L^{4}}+\|\varrho\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{4}}+\|\varrho\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{4}}\right)\\
&\leq C\left(\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\Pi\|_{L^{4}}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{4}}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{4}}\right)\,,\end{split}

where we have employed the fact that the densities are bounded from above.

Once again, due to the Gagliardo-Nirenberg’s inequality, we obtain

\|\nabla\Pi\|_{L^{4}}\leq C\|\Delta\Pi\|_{L^{4}}^{1/3}\|\nabla\Pi\|_{L^{2}}^{2/3}\,.

So,

\|\Delta\Pi\|_{L^{4}}\leq C\left(\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\Delta\Pi\|_{L^{4}}^{1/3}\|\nabla\Pi\|_{L^{2}}^{2/3}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{4}}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{4}}\right)\,.

Therefore, Young inequality implies that

\begin{split}\|\Delta\Pi\|_{L^{4}}&\leq C\left(\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}^{3/2}\|\nabla\Pi\|_{L^{2}}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{4}}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{4}}\right)\\
&\leq C\left(\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}^{3/2}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{4}}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{4}}\right)\,.\end{split}

At the end, we ensure that

\|\nabla\Pi\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\left(\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}^{3/2}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{4}}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{4}}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\,.

Now, we have to estimate
\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{4}}
: to do so, we take advantage of the vorticity formulation

\partial_{t}\omega+\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\omega=-\nabla a\wedge\nabla\Pi\,,

where
\nabla a\wedge\nabla\Pi:=\partial_{1}a\,\partial_{2}\Pi-\partial_{2}a\,\partial_{1}\Pi
.

From that formulation, we get the following bound for all
t\in[0,T[
:

\|\omega(t)\|_{L^{4}}\leq\|\omega_{0}\|_{L^{4}}+C\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\Pi\|_{L^{4}}\,{\rm d}\tau\,.

As done in the previous computations, we can deduce that

\begin{split}\|\omega(t)\|_{L^{4}}&\leq\|\omega_{0}\|_{L^{4}}+C\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\Delta\Pi\|_{L^{4}}^{1/3}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2/3}\\
&\leq\|\omega_{0}\|_{L^{4}}+C\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}\left(\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}^{3/2}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{4}}+\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{4}}\right)^{1/3}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2/3}\\
&\leq\|\omega_{0}\|_{L^{4}}+C\int_{0}^{t}\left(\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}^{3/2}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\omega\|_{L^{4}}^{1/3}+\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2/3}\|\omega\|_{L^{4}}^{1/3}\right).\end{split}

At this point, we apply the Young’s inequality to infer:

(i)

\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}^{3/2}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\left(\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)
;

(ii)

\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\omega\|_{L^{4}}^{1/3}\leq C\left(\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\omega\|_{L^{4}}+\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)
;

(iii)

\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2/3}\|\omega\|_{L^{4}}^{1/3}\leq C\left(\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\omega\|_{L^{4}}\right)
.

In the end, for all
t\in[0,T[\,
,

\begin{split}\|\omega(t)\|_{L^{4}}&\leq\|\omega_{0}\|_{L^{4}}+C\int_{0}^{t}\left(\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\omega\|_{L^{4}}+\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\omega\|_{L^{4}}\right)\,{\rm d}\tau\\
&+C\int_{0}^{t}\left(\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\,{\rm d}\tau\,.\end{split}

Hence, thanks to the Grönwall’s lemma, we get

\|\omega(t)\|_{L^{4}}\leq\exp\left[C\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}\left(\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}+1\right)\right]\left[\|\omega_{0}\|_{L^{4}}+C\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\|_{L^{\infty}}\left(\|\nabla a\|_{L^{\infty}}+1\right)\right].

Recalling condition (
4.5.1
), the theorem is thus proved.

At this point, we discuss some consequences of the previous result.

In particular, it would be enough to control
\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}
in
L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2}\cap B^{1}_{\infty,1})
in order to have the existence of the solution until the time
T
.
Indeed, if we are able to control the norm
\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{2}\cap B^{1}_{\infty,1})}
, then we are able to bound
\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{\infty})}
. This will imply (
4.5.1
) and, therefore, the solution will exist until time
T
.

Let us give some details. First of all, we have

\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{\infty})}\leq C\,\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(B^{1}_{\infty,1})}\,.

As already pointed out in Lemma
4.4.5
, to control the
B^{1}_{\infty,1}
norm of
\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}
it is enough to have a
L^{2}
estimate for
\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}
and a
B^{0}_{\infty,1}
estimate for its
{\rm curl}\,
. Those estimates are the topic of the next Subsection
4.5.2
, provided that the time
T>0
is defined as in (
4.5.13
) below.

Therefore,
\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(L^{\infty})}<+\infty
and so

\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}<+\infty\,,

for such
T>0
.

Finally, we note that we have already shown the existence and uniqueness of solutions to system (
4.2.3
) in the Sobolev spaces
H^{s}
with
s>2
(see Section
4.2
) and, thanks to Proposition
A.6
, those spaces are continuously embedded in the space
B^{1}_{\infty,1}
. Thus, the solution will exist until
T
.

4.5.2
The asymptotic lifespan

In this paragraph we focus our attention on the lifespan
T^{\ast}_{\varepsilon}
of solutions
(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon},\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon})
to the primitive system (
4.2.3
).
We point out that if we consider the initial densities as in (
4.1.2
), i.e.
\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}=1+\varepsilon R_{0,\varepsilon}
, it is not clear to us how to show that
T_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}\longrightarrow+\infty
when
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
. Nevertheless, on the one hand, as soon as the densities are of the form
\varrho_{0,\varepsilon}=1+\varepsilon^{1+\alpha}R_{0,\varepsilon}
(with
\alpha>0
), we obtain that
T^{\ast}_{\varepsilon}\sim\log\log\left(1/\varepsilon\right)
.
On the other hand, independently of the rotational effects, we can state an “asymptotically global” well-posedness result in the regime of small oscillations: namely, we get
T^{\ast}_{\varepsilon}\geq T^{\ast}(\delta)
, with
T^{\ast}(\delta)\longrightarrow+\infty
, when the size
\delta>0
of
R_{0,\varepsilon}
goes to
0^{+}
(this is coherent with the result in
[
22
]
for a density-depend fluid in the absence of Coriolis force).

Therefore, the main goal of this subsection is to prove estimate (
4.1.8
) of Theorem
4.1.1
.

First of all, we have to take advantage of the vorticity formulation of system (
4.2.3
). To do so, we apply the
{\rm curl}\,
operator to the momentum equation, obtaining

\partial_{t}\omega_{\varepsilon}+\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\omega_{\varepsilon}+\nabla a_{\varepsilon}\,\wedge\,\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}=0\,,

(4.5.4)

where we recall
\omega_{\varepsilon}:={\rm curl}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}
and
\nabla a_{\varepsilon}\,\wedge\,\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}:=\partial_{1}a_{\varepsilon}\,\partial_{2}\Pi_{\varepsilon}-\partial_{2}a_{\varepsilon}\partial_{1}\Pi_{\varepsilon}
.

We notice that the vorticity formulation is the key point to bypass the issues coming from the Coriolis force, whose singular effects disappear in (
4.5.4
).

Next, we make use of Theorem
A.16
and we deduce that

\|\omega_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\leq C\left(\|\omega_{0,\varepsilon}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}+\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla a_{\varepsilon}\,\wedge\,\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\,{\rm d}\tau\right)\left(1+\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\,{\rm d}\tau\right)\,.

(4.5.5)

We start by bounding the
B^{0}_{\infty,1}
norm of
\nabla a_{\varepsilon}\,\wedge\,\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}
. We observe that

\begin{split}\partial_{1}a_{\varepsilon}\,\partial_{2}\Pi_{\varepsilon}-\partial_{2}a_{\varepsilon}\,\partial_{1}\Pi_{\varepsilon}&=\mathcal{T}_{\partial_{1}a_{\varepsilon}}\partial_{2}\Pi_{\varepsilon}-\mathcal{T}_{\partial_{2}a_{\varepsilon}}\partial_{1}\Pi_{\varepsilon}+\mathcal{T}_{\partial_{2}\Pi_{\varepsilon}}\partial_{1}a_{\varepsilon}-\mathcal{T}_{\partial_{1}\Pi_{\varepsilon}}\partial_{2}a_{\varepsilon}\\
&+\partial_{1}\mathcal{R}(a_{\varepsilon}-\Delta_{-1}a_{\varepsilon},\,\partial_{2}\Pi_{\varepsilon})-\partial_{2}\mathcal{R}(a_{\varepsilon}-\Delta_{-1}a_{\varepsilon},\,\partial_{1}\Pi_{\varepsilon})\\
&+\mathcal{R}(\partial_{1}\Delta_{-1}a_{\varepsilon},\,\partial_{2}\Pi_{\varepsilon})+\mathcal{R}(\partial_{2}\Delta_{-1}a_{\varepsilon},\,\partial_{1}\Pi_{\varepsilon})\,.\end{split}

(4.5.6)

Applying Proposition
A.7
directly to the terms involving the paraproduct
\mathcal{T}
, we have

\|\mathcal{T}_{\nabla a_{\varepsilon}}\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}+\|\mathcal{T}_{\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}}\nabla a_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\leq C\left(\|\nabla a_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}+\|\nabla a_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\,.

Next, we have to deal with the remainders
\mathcal{R}
. We start by bounding the
B^{0}_{\infty,1}
norm of
\partial_{1}\mathcal{R}(a_{\varepsilon}-\Delta_{-1}a_{\varepsilon},\,\partial_{2}\Pi_{\varepsilon})
.
One has:

\begin{split}\|\partial_{1}\mathcal{R}(a_{\varepsilon}-\Delta_{-1}a_{\varepsilon},\,\partial_{2}\Pi_{\varepsilon})\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}&\leq C\|\mathcal{R}(a_{\varepsilon}-\Delta_{-1}a_{\varepsilon},\,\partial_{2}\Pi_{\varepsilon})\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}\\
&\leq C\left(\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,\infty}}\|(\text{Id}-\Delta_{-1})\,a_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}\right)\\
&\leq C\left(\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla a_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\right)\,,\end{split}

where we have employed the localization properties of the Littlewood-Paley decomposition. In a similar way, one can argue for

\partial_{2}\mathcal{R}(a_{\varepsilon}-\Delta_{-1}a_{\varepsilon},\,\partial_{1}\Pi_{\varepsilon})
.

It remains to bound
\mathcal{R}(\partial_{1}\Delta_{-1}a_{\varepsilon},\,\partial_{2}\Pi_{\varepsilon})
, and analogously one can treat the term
\mathcal{R}(\partial_{2}\Delta_{-1}a_{\varepsilon},\,\partial_{1}\Pi_{\varepsilon})
in (
4.5.6
).
We obtain that

\|\mathcal{R}(\partial_{1}\Delta_{-1}a_{\varepsilon},\,\partial_{2}\Pi_{\varepsilon})\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\leq C\|\mathcal{R}(\partial_{1}\Delta_{-1}a_{\varepsilon},\,\partial_{2}\Pi_{\varepsilon})\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}\leq C\left(\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\partial_{1}\Delta_{-1}a_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}\right)\,.

Employing the spectral properties of operator
\Delta_{-1}
, one has that

\|\partial_{1}\Delta_{-1}a_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}\leq C\|\Delta_{-1}\nabla a_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\,.

Then,

\|\mathcal{R}(\partial_{1}\Delta_{-1}a_{\varepsilon},\,\partial_{2}\Pi_{\varepsilon})\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\leq C\left(\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla a_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\right)\,.

Finally, we get

\|\nabla a_{\varepsilon}\,\wedge\,\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\leq C\left(\|\nabla a_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}+\|\nabla a_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\,.

So plugging the previous estimate in (
4.5.5
), one gets

\|\omega_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\leq C\left(\|\omega_{0,\varepsilon}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}+\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla a_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\,{\rm d}\tau\right)\left(1+\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\,{\rm d}\tau\right)\,.

At this point, we define

E_{\varepsilon}(t):=\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}(t)\|_{L^{2}\cap B^{1}_{\infty,1}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}(t):=\|\nabla a_{\varepsilon}(t)\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\,.

(4.5.7)

In this way, we have

E_{\varepsilon}(t)\leq C\left(E_{\varepsilon}(0)+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}(\tau)\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}(\tau)\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\,{\rm d}\tau\right)\left(1+\int_{0}^{t}E_{\varepsilon}(\tau)\,{\rm d}\tau\right)\,.

(4.5.8)

Next, we recall that, for
i=1,2
:

\partial_{t}\,\partial_{i}a_{\varepsilon}+\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\,\partial_{i}a_{\varepsilon}=-\partial_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla a_{\varepsilon}

and, due to the divergence-free condition on
\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}
, we can write

\partial_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla a_{\varepsilon}=\sum_{j}\partial_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{j}\,\partial_{j}a_{\varepsilon}=\sum_{j}\Big(\partial_{i}(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{j}\,\partial_{j}a_{\varepsilon})-\partial_{j}(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{j}\,\partial_{i}a_{\varepsilon})\Big)\,.

So, using Proposition
A.7
and the fact that

\|\mathcal{\partial}_{i}\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{j},\,\partial_{j}a_{\varepsilon})\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\leq C\,\|\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{j},\,\partial_{j}a_{\varepsilon})\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}\leq C\,\|\nabla a_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}\,,

we may finally get

\|\partial_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla a_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\leq C\|\nabla a_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}\,.

Thus,

\|\nabla a_{\varepsilon}(t)\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\leq\|\nabla a_{0,\varepsilon}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\exp\left(C\int_{0}^{t}\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}\,{\rm d}\tau\right)\,.

Therefore, recalling (
4.5.7
), one has

\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}(t)\leq\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}(0)\exp\left(C\int_{0}^{t}E(\tau)\,{\rm d}\tau\right)\,.

(4.5.9)

The next goal is to bound the pressure term in
B^{0}_{\infty,1}
. Actually, we shall bound its
B^{1}_{\infty,1}
norm. Similarly to the analysis performed in Subsection
4.2.2
for the
H^{s}
norm (see e.g. inequality (
4.2.13
) in this respect), there exists some exponent
\lambda\geq 1
such that

\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}\leq C\left(\left(1+\varepsilon\|\nabla a_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}^{\lambda}\right)\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}+\varepsilon\,\|\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,{\rm div}\,(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}+\|\varrho_{\varepsilon}\,{\rm div}\,\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\perp}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\right)\,.

The
L^{2}
estimate for the pressure term follows in a similar way to one performed in (
4.2.12
), i.e.

\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\,\varepsilon\,\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}\,.

(4.5.10)

Next, as showed above in the bound for
\|\partial_{i}\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla a_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}
, combining Bony’s decomposition with the fact that
{\rm div}\,(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})=\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}:\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}
, we may infer:

\|{\rm div}\,(\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon})\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\leq C\,\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}\,.

Now, our scope is to estimate the
B^{1}_{\infty,1}
norm of the density. To do so, we make use of the following proposition, whose proof can be found in
[
21
]
.

Proposition 4.5.2

Let
I
be an open interval of
\mathbb{R}
and
\overline{F}:I\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
a smooth function. Then, for any compact subset
J\subset I
,
s>0
and
(p,r)\in[1,+\infty]^{2}
, there exists a constant
C
such that for any function
g
valued in
J
and with gradient in
B^{s-1}_{p,r}
, we have
\nabla\big(\overline{F}(g)\big)\in B^{s-1}_{p,r}
and

\left\|\nabla\big(\overline{F}(g)\big)\right\|_{B^{s-1}_{p,r}}\leq C\left\|\nabla g\right\|_{B^{s-1}_{p,r}}\,.

Then, from the definition of
B^{1}_{\infty,1}
and the previous proposition, the
B^{1}_{\infty,1}
estimate for
\varrho_{\varepsilon}
reads:

\|\varrho_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}\leq C\,\left(\overline{\varrho}+\varepsilon\,\|\nabla a_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\right)\,.

Finally, plugging the
L^{2}
estimate (
4.5.10
) and all the above inequalities in (
4.5.2
), one may conclude that

\begin{split}\|\nabla\Pi_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}&\leq C\left(1+\varepsilon\,\|\nabla a_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}^{\lambda}\right)\left(\varepsilon\,\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}\right)\\
&+C\left(1+\varepsilon\,\|\nabla a_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,1}}\right)\left(\varepsilon\,\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{u}_{\varepsilon}\|_{B^{1}_{\infty,1}}\right)\\
&\leq C(1+\varepsilon\,\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}^{\lambda})(\varepsilon\,E_{\varepsilon}^{2}+E_{\varepsilon})+C(1+\varepsilon\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon})(\varepsilon\,E_{\varepsilon}^{2}+E_{\varepsilon})\\
&\leq C(\varepsilon\,E_{\varepsilon}^{2}+E_{\varepsilon})(1+\varepsilon\,\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon\,\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}^{\lambda})\\
&\leq C(\varepsilon\,E_{\varepsilon}^{2}+E_{\varepsilon})(1+\varepsilon\,\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}^{\lambda})\,.\end{split}

(4.5.11)

We insert now in (
4.5.8
) the estimates found in (
4.5.11
) and in (
4.5.9
), deducing that

\begin{split}&E_{\varepsilon}(t)\leq C\left(E_{\varepsilon}(0)+\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(0)\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(C\int_{0}^{\tau}E_{\varepsilon}(s)\,\,{\rm d}s\right)\left(\varepsilon\,E_{\varepsilon}^{2}(\tau)+E_{\varepsilon}(\tau)\right)\,\,{\rm d}\tau\right)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\left(1+\int_{0}^{t}E_{\varepsilon}(\tau)\,{\rm d}\tau\right),\end{split}

(4.5.12)

where we have set
\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(0):=\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}(0)+\varepsilon\,\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}(0)^{\lambda+1}
.

At this point, we define
T_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}>0
such that

T_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}:=\sup\left\{t>0:\,\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(0)\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(C\int_{0}^{\tau}E_{\varepsilon}(s)\,\,{\rm d}s\right)\left(\varepsilon\,E_{\varepsilon}^{2}(\tau)+E_{\varepsilon}(\tau)\right)\,\,{\rm d}\tau\leq E_{\varepsilon}(0)\right\}\,.

(4.5.13)

So, from (
4.5.12
) and using Grönwall’s inequality, we obtain that

E_{\varepsilon}(t)\leq C\,E_{\varepsilon}(0)e^{CtE_{\varepsilon}(0)}\,,

for all
t\in[0,T_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}]
.

The previous estimate implies that, for all
t\in[0,T_{\varepsilon}^{\ast}]
, one has

\int_{0}^{t}E_{\varepsilon}(\tau)\,{\rm d}\tau\leq e^{CtE_{\varepsilon}(0)}-1\,.

Analogously to inequality (
4.4.33
) in Subsection
4.4.3
, we can argue that

\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(0)\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(C\int_{0}^{\tau}E_{\varepsilon}(s)\,\,{\rm d}s\right)E_{\varepsilon}(\tau)\,\,{\rm d}\tau\leq C\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(0)\left(\exp\left(e^{CtE_{\varepsilon}(0)}-1\right)-1\right)\,.

Then, it remains to control

\varepsilon\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(0)\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(C\int_{0}^{\tau}E_{\varepsilon}(s)\,\,{\rm d}s\right)E_{\varepsilon}^{2}(\tau)\,\,{\rm d}\tau\,.

For this term, we may infer that

\begin{split}\varepsilon\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(0)\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(C\int_{0}^{\tau}E_{\varepsilon}(s)\,\,{\rm d}s\right)E_{\varepsilon}^{2}(\tau)\,\,{\rm d}\tau&\leq C\,\varepsilon\,\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(0)\int_{0}^{t}E_{\varepsilon}^{2}(0)e^{C\tau E_{\varepsilon}(0)}\,\exp\left(e^{C\tau E_{\varepsilon}(0)}-1\right)\,\,{\rm d}\tau\\
&\leq C\,\varepsilon\,\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(0)E_{\varepsilon}(0)\left(\exp\left(e^{CtE_{\varepsilon}(0)}-1\right)-1\right)\,.\end{split}

In the end, by definition (
4.5.13
) of
T^{\ast}_{\varepsilon}
, we deduce

T^{\ast}_{\varepsilon}\geq\frac{C}{E_{\varepsilon}(0)}\log\left(\log\left(\frac{CE_{\varepsilon}(0)}{\max\{\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(0),\,\varepsilon\,\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}(0)E_{\varepsilon}(0)\}}+1\right)+1\right),

(4.5.14)

for a suitable constant
C>0
. This concludes the proof of Theorem
4.1.1
.

Future perspectives

We conclude this manuscript pointing out some possible upcoming goals.

First of all, we would like to continue the studies started in Chapter
2
and Chapter
3
in two different directions:

•

on the one hand, we will investigate the regimes not covered yet, i.e. either
m=1
with the centrifugal effects or
(m+1)/2<n<m
;

•

on the other hand, we would focus on the well-posedness analysis for the Oberbeck-Boussinesq limiting system.

In a second instance, we would like to dedicate ourselves to the analysis of a different system that describes the evolution of temperature on the ocean surface: the so-called
surface quasi-geostrophic system
. Specifically, we are interested in the asymptotic analysis in regimes of fast rotational effects and we would like to inspect the well-posedness of such system on manifolds, like the sphere.

To conclude, we highlight that we would like to spend more time regarding the lifespan of solutions to Euler equations in order to study more deeply the stabilization effects due to the rotation.

Perspectives d’avenir

Nous concluons ce manuscrit en soulignant quelque but possible à venir.

Tout d’abord, nous continuerons les études commencées dans le chapitre
2
et dans le chapitre
3
vers deux directions différentes :

•

d’une part, nous étudierons les régimes pas encore couverts dans nos études précédentes, soit
m=1
avec les effets centrifuges soit
(m+1)/2<n<m
;

•

d’autre part, nous nous dédierons à l’analyse du caractère bien posé pour le système limite du type Oberbeck-Boussinesq.

Dans un second temps, nous nous consacrerons à l’analyse d’un autre système décrivant l’évolution de la température à la surface de l’océan, qu’on appelle le
système quasi-géostrophique à la surface
. Plus précisément, nous nous intéressons à l’analyse asymptotique des régimes en rotation rapide et nous aimerions examiner le caractère bien posé d’un tel système sur des variétés, comme la sphère.

En conclusion, nous soulignons qu’on aimerait consacrer plus de temps à l’étude de la durée de vie des solutions des équations d’Euler afin de comprendre plus en profondeur les effets de stabilisation dûs à la rotation.

Appendix A
Tools

The goal of this appendix is to present the tools, which have been useful in our analysis. Unless otherwise specified, we refer to Chapter 2 of
[
5
]
for details.

A.1
Littlewood-Paley theory: introduction

We start by exhibiting some tools from Littlewood-Paley theory.

For simplicity of exposition, we deal with the
\mathbb{R}^{d}
case, with
d\geq 1
; however, the whole construction can be adapted also to the
d
-dimensional torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}
, and to the “hybrid” case

\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\times\mathbb{T}^{d_{2}}
.

First of all, we introduce the
Littlewood-Paley decomposition
. For this
we fix a smooth radial function
\chi
such that it satisfies the following properties:

(i)

{\rm Supp}\,\chi\subset B(0,2)
;

(ii)

\chi\equiv 1
in a neighborhood of the ball
B(0,1)
;

(iii)

the map
r\mapsto\chi(r\,e)
is non-increasing over
\mathbb{R}_{+}
for all unitary vectors
e\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Set now

\varphi\left(\xi\right):=\chi\left(\xi\right)-\chi\left(2\xi\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\varphi_{j}(\xi):=\varphi(2^{-j}\xi)\quad\text{for all}\quad j\geq 0.

The dyadic blocks
(\Delta_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
are defined by
1
1

1

We agree that
f(D)
stands for
the pseudo-differential operator
u\mapsto\mathcal{F}^{-1}[f(\xi)\,\widehat{u}(\xi)]
.

\Delta_{j}\,:=\,0\quad\mbox{ if }\;j\leq-2,\qquad\Delta_{-1}\,:=\,\chi(D)\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\Delta_{j}\,:=\,\varphi(2^{-j}D)\quad\mbox{ if }\;j\geq 0\,.

For any
j\geq 0
fixed, we also introduce the
low frequency cut-off operator

S_{j}\,:=\,\chi(2^{-j}D)\,=\,\sum_{k\leq j-1}\Delta_{k}\,.

(A.1.1)

Note that
S_{j}
is a convolution operator. More precisely, after defining

K_{0}\,:=\,\mathcal{F}^{-1}\chi\qquad\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\qquad K_{j}(x)\,:=\,\mathcal{F}^{-1}[\chi(2^{-j}\cdot)](x)=2^{jd}K_{0}(2^{j}x)\,,

for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
and all tempered distributions
u\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
we have that
S_{j}u\,=\,K_{j}\,*\,u
.
Thus the
L^{1}
norm of
K_{j}
is independent of
j\geq 0
, hence
S_{j}
maps continuously
L^{p}
into itself, for any
1\leq p\leq+\infty
.

Moreover, the following property states the usefulness of such a decomposition.

Lemma A.1

For any
u\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, then one has the equality
u=\sum_{j}\Delta_{j}u
in the sense of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.

Let us also recall the so-called
Bernstein inequalities
, which describe the way derivatives take effect on the spectrally localized functions.

Lemma A.2

Let
0<r<R
. A constant
C
exists so that, for any non-negative integer
k
, any couple
(p,q)

in
[1,+\infty]^{2}
, with
p\leq q
, and any function
u\in L^{p}
, we have, for all
\lambda>0
,

\begin{split}&(i)\quad\text{if}\quad{\rm Supp}\,\,\widehat{u}\subset B(0,\lambda R),\quad\text{then}\quad\|\nabla^{k}u\|_{L^{q}}\,\leq\,C^{k+1}\,\lambda^{k+d\left(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}\right)}\,\|u\|_{L^{p}};\\
&(ii)\quad\text{if}\quad{{\rm Supp}\,}\,\widehat{u}\subset\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\,\lambda r\leq|\xi|\leq\lambda R\},\quad\text{then}\quad C^{-k-1}\,\lambda^{k}\|u\|_{L^{p}}\,\leq\,\|\nabla^{k}u\|_{L^{p}}\,\leq\,C^{k+1}\,\lambda^{k}\|u\|_{L^{p}}\,.\end{split}

By use of Littlewood-Paley decomposition, we can define the class of Besov spaces.

Definition A.3

Let
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
1\leq p,r\leq+\infty
. The
non-homogeneous Besov space

B^{s}_{p,r}
is defined as the subset of tempered distributions
u
for which

\|u\|_{B^{s}_{p,r}}\,:=\,\left\|\left(2^{js}\,\|\Delta_{j}u\|_{L^{p}}\right)_{j\geq-1}\right\|_{\ell^{r}}\,<\,+\infty\,.

The spaces just defined have the following important topological properties (which have been broadly employed in Chapter
4
).

Proposition A.4

Let the triplet
(s,p,r)
be in
\mathbb{R}\times[1,+\infty]^{2}
. The set
B^{s}_{p,r}
is a Banach space and satisfies the Fatou property, namely, if
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a bounded sequence of
B^{s}_{p,r}
, then an element
f
of
B^{s}_{p,r}
and a subsequence
f_{\psi(n)}
exist such that

\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}f_{\psi(n)}=f\,\text{ in }\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad\|f\|_{B^{s}_{p,r}}\leq C\liminf_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\|f_{\psi(n)}\|_{B^{s}_{p,r}}\,.

In addition, Besov spaces are interpolation spaces between Sobolev spaces. In fact, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
p\in[1,+\infty]

we have the chain of continuous embeddings

B^{k}_{p,1}\hookrightarrow W^{k,p}\hookrightarrow B^{k}_{p,\infty}.

In the case when
1<p<+\infty
, the previous chain of embeddings can be refined to

B^{k}_{p,\min(p,2)}\hookrightarrow W^{k,p}\hookrightarrow B^{k}_{p,\max(p,2)}\,.

In particular, for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
we deduce that
B^{s}_{2,2}\equiv H^{s}
, with equivalence of norms:

\|f\|_{H^{s}}\,\sim\,\left(\sum_{j\geq-1}2^{2js}\,\|\Delta_{j}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right)^{\!\!1/2}\,.

(A.1.2)

Observe that, from that equivalence, we easily get the following property:

Lemma A.5

For any
f\in H^{s}
and any
j\in\mathbb{N}
, one has

\left\|\big({\rm Id}\,-S_{j}\big)f\right\|_{H^{\sigma}}\,\leq\,C\,\|\nabla f\|_{H^{s-1}}\,2^{-j(s-\sigma)}\quad\quad\text{for all}\quad\quad\,\sigma\leq s\,,

(A.1.3)

where
C>0
is a “universal” constant, independent of
f
,
j
,
s
and
\sigma
.

Proof:

We make use of the characterization (
A.1.2
) of
H^{\sigma}
and we write

\begin{split}\left\|({\rm Id}\,-S_{j})f\right\|_{H^{\sigma}}^{2}&\leq C\sum_{k\geq j}2^{2k\sigma}\left\|\Delta_{k}f\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,2^{2ks}\;2^{-2ks}\leq C\sum_{k\geq j}2^{-2k(s-\sigma)}\;2^{2ks}\left\|\Delta_{k}f\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\\
&\leq C\sum_{k\geq j}2^{-2k(s-\sigma)}\;2^{2k(s-1)}\left\|\Delta_{k}\nabla f\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C\sum_{k\geq j}2^{-2k(s-\sigma)}\;\left\|\nabla f\right\|_{H^{s-1}}^{2}\\
&\leq C\;2^{-2j(s-\sigma)}\|\nabla f\|^{2}_{H^{s-1}}\,,\end{split}

for all
\sigma\leq s
.
Then, we obtain (
A.1.3
), concluding the proof of the lemma.

As an immediate consequence of the first Bernstein inequality (see Lemma
A.2
), one gets the following embedding result, which generalises the Sobolev embeddings.

Proposition A.6

The space
B^{s_{1}}_{p_{1},r_{1}}
is continuously embedded in the space
B^{s_{2}}_{p_{2},r_{2}}
for all indices satisfying
p_{1}\,\leq\,p_{2}
and either

s_{2}\,<\,s_{1}-d\big(1/p_{1}-1/p_{2}\big)
, or
s_{2}\,=\,s_{1}-d\big(1/p_{1}-1/p_{2}\big)
and
r_{1}\leq r_{2}
.

In particular, we get the following chain of continuous embeddings:

B^{s}_{p,r}\hookrightarrow B^{s-d/p}_{\infty,r}\hookrightarrow B^{0}_{\infty,1}\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}\,,

whenever the triplet
(s,p,r)\in\mathbb{R}\times[1,+\infty]^{2}
satisfies

s>\frac{d}{p}\quad\quad\quad\text{or}\quad\quad\quad s=\frac{d}{p}\quad\text{and}\quad r=1\,.

This last chain of embeddings has been fundamental and of constant use in Chapter
4
.

In the sequel, we recall some definitions and properties of paradifferential calculus. Moreover, using those notions, we will focus on the study of transport equations in Besov spaces.

A.2
Paradifferential calculus

Let us introduce the Bony decomposition (see
[
8
]
). Formally, the product of two tempered distributions
u
and
v
can be decomposed into

u\,v=\mathcal{T}_{u}v+\mathcal{T}_{v}u+\mathcal{R}(u,v)\,,

where the
paraproduct

\mathcal{T}
and the
remainder

\mathcal{R}
are defined as follows:

\mathcal{T}_{u}v=\sum_{j}S_{j-1}u\,\Delta_{j}v\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{R}(u,v):=\sum_{j}\sum_{|k-j|\leq 1}\Delta_{j}u\,\Delta_{k}v\,.

The paraproduct and remainder operators have nice continuity properties. The following ones have been of constant throughout the manuscript.

Proposition A.7

For any
(s,p,r)\in\mathbb{R}\times[1,+\infty]^{2}
and
t>0
, the paraproduct operator
\mathcal{T}
maps continuously
L^{\infty}\times B^{s}_{p,r}
into
B^{s}_{p,r}
and
B^{-t}_{\infty,\infty}\times B^{s}_{p,r}
into
B^{s-t}_{p,r}
. Moreover, we have the following estimates

\|\mathcal{T}_{u}v\|_{B^{s}_{p,r}}\leq C\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla v\|_{B^{s-1}_{p,r}}\quad\text{and}\quad\|\mathcal{T}_{u}v\|_{B^{s-t}_{p,r}}\leq C\|u\|_{B^{-t}_{\infty,\infty}}\|\nabla v\|_{B^{s-1}_{p,r}}\,.

For any
(s_{1},p_{1},r_{1})
and
(s_{2},p_{2},r_{2})
in
\mathbb{R}\times[1,+\infty]^{2}
such that
s_{1}+s_{2}>0
,
1/p:=1/p_{1}+1/p_{2}\leq 1
and
1/r:=1/r_{1}+1/r_{2}\leq 1
, the remainder operator
\mathcal{R}
maps continuously
B^{s_{1}}_{p_{1},r_{1}}\times B^{s_{2}}_{p_{2},r_{2}}
into
B^{s_{1}+s_{2}}_{p,r}
. In the case when
s_{1}+s_{2}=0
and
1/r_{1}+1/r_{2}=1
, the operator
\mathcal{R}
is continuous from
B^{s_{1}}_{p_{1},r_{1}}\times B^{s_{2}}_{p_{2},r_{2}}
to
B^{0}_{p,\infty}
.

As a consequence of the Proposition
A.7
, the spaces
B^{s}_{p,r}
are Banach algebras, provided that condition

s>\frac{d}{p}\quad\quad\quad\text{or}\quad\quad\quad s=\frac{d}{p}\quad\text{and}\quad r=1

(A.2.1)

holds for
s>0
and
(p,q)\in[1,+\infty]^{2}
. Moreover, in that case, we have the so-called
tame estimates
.

Corollary A.8

Let
(s,p,r)\in\;]0,+\infty[\;\times[1,+\infty]^{2}
satisfy (
A.2.1
). Then, for every
f,g\in L^{\infty}\cap B^{s}_{p,r}
, one has

\|fg\|_{B^{s}_{p,r}}\leq C\left(\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}\|g\|_{B^{s}_{p,r}}+\|f\|_{B^{s}_{p,r}}\|g\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\,.

Remark A.9

The space
B^{0}_{\infty,1}
is not an algebra. If
f,g\in B^{0}_{\infty,1}
, applying Proposition
A.7
, one can bound the paraproducts
\mathcal{T}_{f}g
and
\mathcal{T}_{g}f
but not the remainder
\mathcal{R}(f,g)
.

To end this paragraph, we present a fine estimate for products in which one of the two functions is only bounded in
L^{\infty}
but its gradient belongs to the Besov space
B^{s-1}_{p,r}
.

Proposition A.10

Let
(s,p,r)\in\;]0,+\infty[\;\times\,[1,+\infty]^{2}
satisfy condition (
A.2.1
). Assume that
g\in L^{\infty}\cap B^{s}_{p,r}
and
f
is a bounded function such that
\nabla f\in B^{s-1}_{p,r}
. Then, the product
fg
belongs to
L^{\infty}\cap B^{s}_{p,r}
and one has the following estimate:

\|fg\|_{B^{s}_{p,r}}\leq C\left(\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}\|g\|_{B^{s}_{p,r}}+\|\nabla f\|_{B^{s-1}_{p,r}}\|g\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\,.

Proof:

Taking advantage of Bony decomposition, one can write

fg=\mathcal{T}_{f}g+\mathcal{T}_{g}f+\mathcal{R}(f,g)

and employing Proposition
A.7
, we get

\begin{split}\|\mathcal{T}_{f}g\|_{B^{s}_{p,r}}&\leq C\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}\|g\|_{B^{s}_{p,r}}\\
\|\mathcal{T}_{g}\,f\|_{B^{s}_{p,r}}&\leq C\|g\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla f\|_{B^{s-1}_{p,r}}\\
\|\mathcal{R}(f,g)\|_{B^{s}_{p,r}}&\leq C\|f\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,\infty}}\|g\|_{B^{s}_{p,r}}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}\|g\|_{B^{s}_{p,r}}\,.\end{split}

This completes the proof of the proposition.

A.3
Commutator estimates

In this paragraph, we recall the main commutator estimates widely employed throughout the chapter
4
.

Definition A.11

We say that the triplet
(s,p,r)\in\mathbb{R}\times[1,+\infty]^{2}
satisfies the Lipschitz condition if

s>1+d/p\quad\text{and}\quad r\in[1,+\infty]\quad\quad\quad\text{or}\quad\quad\quad s=1+d/p\quad\text{and}\quad r=1\,.

(A.3.1)

The proof of the following Lemma
A.12
can be found in
[
21
]
by Danchin.

Lemma A.12

Let
(s,p,r)\in\mathbb{R}\times[1,+\infty]^{2}
satisfy condition (
A.3.1
) and
\sigma
be in
]-1,\,s-1]
. Assume that
w\in B^{\sigma}_{p,r}
and
A
is a bounded function on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\nabla A\in B^{s-1}_{p,r}
. Then, there exists a constant
C=C(s,p,r,\sigma,d)
such that for all
i\in\{1,\dots,d\}
, we have:

\|\partial_{i}[A,\Delta_{j}]w\|_{L^{p}}\leq C\,c_{j}\,2^{-j\sigma}\|\nabla A\|_{B^{s-1}_{p,r}}\|w\|_{B^{\sigma}_{p,r}}\quad\text{for all }j\geq-1\,,

with
\|(c_{j})_{j\geq-1}\|_{\ell^{r}}=1
.

The next statement concerns a standard commutator estimate between the transport operator and the frequency localisation operator.

Lemma A.13

Assume that
v\in B^{s}_{p,r}
with
(s,p,r)
satisfying the Lipschitz condition (
A.3.1
). Denote by
[v\cdot\nabla,\Delta_{j}]f=(v\cdot\nabla)\Delta_{j}f-\Delta_{j}(v\cdot\nabla)f
the commutator between the transport operator
v\cdot\nabla
and the frequency localisation operator
\Delta_{j}
. Then, for every
f\in B^{s}_{p,r}
,

\left\|\left(2^{js}\|[v\cdot\nabla,\Delta_{j}]f\|_{L^{p}}\right)_{j}\right\|_{\ell^{r}}\leq C\left(\|\nabla v\|_{L^{\infty}}\|f\|_{B^{s}_{p,r}}+\|\nabla v\|_{B^{s-1}_{p,r}}\|\nabla f\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)

and also, for every
f\in B^{s-1}_{p,r}
,

\left\|\left(2^{j(s-1)}\|[v\cdot\nabla,\Delta_{j}]f\|_{L^{p}}\right)_{j}\right\|_{\ell^{r}}\leq C\left(\|\nabla v\|_{L^{\infty}}\|f\|_{B^{s-1}_{p,r}}+\|\nabla v\|_{B^{s-1}_{p,r}}\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\,,

for some constant
C=C(s,p,d)>0
.

Finally, the next result deals with commutators between paraproduct operators and Fourier multipliers.

Lemma A.14

Let
\kappa
be a smooth function on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which is homogeneous of degree
m
away from a neighborhood of
0
. Take
(s,p,r)\in\mathbb{R}\times[1,+\infty]^{2}
and
v
a vector field such that
\nabla v\in L^{\infty}
. Then, for every
f\in B^{s}_{p,r}
, one has

\left\|[T_{v},\kappa(D)]f\right\|_{B^{s-m+1}_{p,r}}\leq C(s,d)\,\|\nabla v\|_{L^{\infty}}\|f\|_{B^{s}_{p,r}}\,.

A.4
Transport equations

In this paragraph, we deal with the transport equations in non-homogeneous Besov spaces. We refer to Chapter 3 of
[
5
]
for additional details. We study, in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the initial value problem

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla f=g\\
f_{|t=0}=f_{0}\,.\end{cases}

(A.4.1)

We always assume the velocity field
v=v(t,x)
to be a Lipschitz divergence-free function.
In the case when the Lipschitz condition (
A.3.1
) is satisfied, we have the embedding
B^{s}_{p,r}\hookrightarrow W^{1,\infty}
.

We state now the main well-posedness result concerning problem (
A.4.1
) in Besov spaces. We point out also that the notation
C^{0}_{w}([0,T];X)
, with
X
a Banach space, refers to the space of functions which are continuous in time with values in
X
endowed with its weak topology.

Theorem A.15

Let
(s,p,r)\in\mathbb{R}\times[1,+\infty]^{2}
satisfy the Lipschitz condition (
A.3.1
). Given
T>0
, take
g\in L^{1}_{T}(B^{s}_{p,r})
. Assume that
v\in L^{1}_{T}(B^{s}_{p,r})
and that there exist two real numbers
q>1
and
\sigma>0
such that
v\in L^{q}_{T}(B^{-\sigma}_{\infty,\infty})
. Finally, let
f_{0}\in B^{s}_{p,r}
be the initial datum. Then, the transport equation (
A.4.1
) has a unique solution
f
in:

•

the space
C^{0}\left([0,T];B^{s}_{p,r}\right)
, if
r<+\infty
;

•

the space
\left(\displaystyle\bigcap_{s^{\prime}<s}C^{0}\left([0,T];B^{s^{\prime}}_{p,\infty}\right)\right)\cap C^{0}_{w}\left([0,T];B^{s}_{p,\infty}\right)
, if
r=+\infty
.

Moreover, the unique solution satisfies the following estimate:

\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(B^{s}_{p,r})}\leq\exp\left(C\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla v\|_{B^{s-1}_{p,r}}\,\,{\rm d}\tau\right)\left(\|f_{0}\|_{B^{s}_{p,r}}+\int_{0}^{T}\exp\left(-C\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla v\|_{B^{s-1}_{p,r}}\,\,{\rm d}\tau\right)\|g\|_{B^{s}_{p,r}}\,\,{\rm d}t\right),

for some constant
C=C(s,p,r,d)>0
.

To conclude this paragraph, we show a refinement of Theorem
A.15
, proved by Vishik in
[
70
]
and in a different way by Hmidi and Keraani (see
[
49
]
). It states that, if
{\rm div}\,v=0
and the Besov regularity index is
s=0
, the estimate in Theorem
A.15
can be replaced by an inequality which is
linear
with respect to
\|\nabla v\|_{L^{1}_{T}(L^{\infty})}
.

Theorem A.16

Given
T>0
, assume that
v
is a divergence-free vector field such that
\nabla v\in L^{1}_{T}(L^{\infty})
and let
g\in L^{1}_{T}(B^{0}_{\infty,r})
. Take
r\in[1,+\infty]
and
f_{0}\in L^{1}_{T}(B^{0}_{\infty,r})
. Then, there exists a constant
C=C(d)
such that, for any solution
f
to problem (
A.4.1
) in

C^{0}([0,T];B^{0}_{\infty,r})
(or with the usual modification of
C^{0}
into
C^{0}_{w}
if
r=+\infty
), we have

\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}(B^{0}_{\infty,r})}\leq C\left(\|f_{0}\|_{B^{0}_{\infty,r}}+\|g\|_{L^{1}_{T}(B^{0}_{\infty,r})}\right)\left(1+\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla v(\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}}\,\,{\rm d}\tau\right)\,.

Appendix B
Some well-known results

This appendix is thought for the reader’s convenience in order to quickly check the statement of some famous theorems. For that reason, we limit ourself to state such results, used throughout the whole thesis. Unless indicated otherwise, we refer to the introductory part of book
[
39
]
for details (see also Chapter 10) and to Chapter 1 of
[
5
]
.

B.1
Embedding theorems

We start by recalling the
Rellich-Kondrachov embedding theorem
.

Theorem B.1

Let
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded Lipschitz domain.

(i)

If
kp<d
and
p\geq 1
, then the space
W^{k,p}(Q)
is continuously embedded in
L^{q}(Q)
for any
1\leq q\leq p^{\ast}:=(dp)/(d-kp)
.

Moreover, the embedding is compact if
k>0
and
q<p^{\ast}
.

(ii)

If
kp=d
, the space
W^{k,p}(Q)
is compactly embedded in
L^{q}(Q)
for any
q\in[1,+\infty)
.

(iii)

If
kp>d
, then
W^{k,p}(Q)
is continuously embedded in
1
1

1

The symbol
\lfloor z\rfloor
denotes the integer part of
z
.

\mathcal{C}^{k-\lfloor d/p\rfloor-1,\,\nu}(\overline{Q})
, where either
\nu=\lfloor d/p\rfloor+1-d/p
if
(d/p)\not\in\mathbb{Z}
or
\nu
is an arbitrary positive number in
(0,1)
if
(d/p)\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Moreover, the embedding is compact if
0<\nu<\lfloor d/p\rfloor+1-d/p
.

As a direct consequence of the previous theorem we have the
embedding theorem for dual Sobolev spaces
.

Theorem B.2

Let
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded domain. Let
k>0
and
q<+\infty
satisfy

•

q>p^{\ast}/(p^{\ast}-1)
where
p^{\ast}:=(dp)/(d-kp)
, if
kp<d
;

•

q>1
, if
kp=d
;

•

q\geq 1
, if
kp>d
.

Then, the space
L^{q}(Q)
is compactly embedded into the space
W^{-k,p^{\prime}}(Q)
, with
1/p+1/p^{\prime}=1
.

B.2
Mollifiers

Here we recall the main properties of the mollifiers. Such properties are widely employed in Section
2.4
of Chapter
2
.

We start by fixing a smooth, radial and radially decreasing function
\chi\in{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, such that

0\leq\chi\leq 1,\quad\quad\chi(x)=0\;\text{ for }|x|\geq 1\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\chi(x)\,{\rm d}x=1.

Next, we define the
mollifying kernel

\big(\chi_{\varepsilon}\big)_{\varepsilon>0}
by the formula

\chi_{\varepsilon}(x)\,:=\,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{d}}\,\,\chi\!\left(\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\right)\qquad\qquad\text{for any}\;\varepsilon>0\;\text{ and }\;x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,.

Then, for any tempered distribution
\mathfrak{S}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any
\varepsilon>0
, we introduce

\mathfrak{S}_{\varepsilon}\,:=\,\chi_{\varepsilon}\,*\,\mathfrak{S}\,.

Theorem B.3

Let
X
a Banach space. If
\mathfrak{S}\in L^{1}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d};X)
, then we have
\mathfrak{S}_{\varepsilon}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};X)
. In addition, we have the following properties:

(i)

if
\mathfrak{S}\in L^{p}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d};X)
with
1\leq p<+\infty
, then
\mathfrak{S}_{\varepsilon}\in L^{p}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d};X)
and

\mathfrak{S}_{\varepsilon}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{S}\quad\text{in}\quad L^{p}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d};X)\quad\text{as}\quad\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}\,;

(ii)

if
\mathfrak{S}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};X)
with
1\leq p<+\infty
, then
\mathfrak{S}_{\varepsilon}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};X)
,

\|\mathfrak{S}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};X)}\leq\|\mathfrak{S}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};X)}

and

\mathfrak{S}_{\varepsilon}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{S}\quad\text{in}\quad L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};X)\quad\text{as}\quad\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}\,;

(iii)

if
\mathfrak{S}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};X)
, then
\mathfrak{S}_{\varepsilon}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};X)
and

\|\mathfrak{S}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};X)}\leq\|\mathfrak{S}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};X)}\,;

(iv)

if
\mathfrak{S}\in C^{k}(Q;X)
where
k
is a non-negative integer and
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a ball, then
(\partial^{\alpha}\mathfrak{S})_{\varepsilon}(x)=\partial^{\alpha}\mathfrak{S}_{\varepsilon}(x)
for all
x\in Q
,
\varepsilon\in(0,{\rm dist}[x,\partial Q])
and for any multi-index
\alpha
such that
|\alpha|\leq k
. Moreover,

\|\mathfrak{S}_{\varepsilon}\|_{C^{k}(\overline{B};X)}\leq\|\mathfrak{S}\|_{C^{k}(\overline{V};X)}

for any
\varepsilon\in(0,{\rm dist}[\partial B,\partial V])
, where
B
and
V
are balls in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\overline{B}\subset V\subset\overline{V}\subset Q
. Finally,

\mathfrak{S}_{\varepsilon}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{S}\quad\text{in}\quad C^{k}(\overline{B};X)\quad\text{as}\quad\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}\,.

B.3
Some assorted inequalities

We start this section by mentioning some classical inequalities:
Young’s inequality
(see Chapter 4 of
[
13
]
in this respect),
Hölder’s inequality
and
Minkowski’s inequality
.

Proposition B.4
(Young’s inequality)

If
a\geq 0
and
b\geq 0
are non-negative real numbers and if
p>1
and
q>1
are real numbers such that

\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1\,,

then
2
2

2

It is sometimes convenient to use the formulation
ab\leq\varepsilon a^{p}+\varepsilon^{-1/(p-1)}b^{q}
, with
\varepsilon>0
.

ab\leq\frac{a^{p}}{p}+\frac{b^{q}}{q}\,.

Proposition B.5
(Hölder’s inequality)

Let
(X,\mu)
be a measure space and
(p,q,r)
in
[1,+\infty]^{3}
be such that

\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{r}\,.

If the couple
(f,g)
belongs to
L^{p}(X,\mu)\times L^{q}(X,\mu)
, then the product
fg
belongs to
L^{r}(X,\mu)
and

\|fg\|_{L^{r}}\leq\|f\|_{L^{p}}\|g\|_{L^{q}}\,.

Proposition B.6
(Minkowski’s inequality)

Let
(X_{1},\mu_{1})
and
(X_{2},\mu_{2})
be two measure spaces and
f
a non-negative measurable function over
X_{1}\times X_{2}
. For all
1\leq p\leq q\leq+\infty
, we have

\left\|\|f(\cdot,x_{2})\|_{L^{p}(X_{1},\mu_{1})}\right\|_{L^{q}(X_{2},\mu_{2})}\leq\left\|\|f(x_{1},\cdot)\|_{L^{q}(X_{2},\mu_{2})}\right\|_{L^{p}(X_{1},\mu_{1})}\,.

The next two results are fundamental to give uniform bounds in Sobolev norms for the velocity fields and the temperatures. Such propositions are the so-called
generalized Poincaré inequality
and
generalized Korn-Poincaré inequality
respectively.

Proposition B.7

Let
1\leq p\leq+\infty
,
0<r<+\infty
,
V_{0}>0
and let
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded Lipschitz domain. Then, there exists a positive constant
c=c(p,r,V_{0})
such that

\|v\|_{W^{1,p}(Q)}\leq c\left[\|\nabla v\|_{L^{p}(Q;\,\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\left(\int_{V}|v|^{r}\,{\rm d}x\right)^{1/r}\right]\,,

for any measurable
V\subset Q
with
|V|\geq V_{0}
and any
v\in W^{1,p}(Q)
.

Proposition B.8

Let
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, with
d>2
, be a bounded Lipschitz domain, and let
1<p<+\infty
,
M_{0}>0
,
K>0
,
\alpha>1
. Then, there exists a positive constant
c=c(p,M_{0},K,\alpha)
such that the inequality

\|v\|_{W^{1,p}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq c\left(\|\nabla v+\,^{t}\nabla v-\frac{2}{d}\,{\rm div}\,v\,{\rm Id}\,\|_{L^{p}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\int_{Q}f|v|\,{\rm d}x\right)

holds for any
v\in W^{1,p}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d})
and any non-negative function
f
such that

0<M_{0}\leq\int_{Q}f\,{\rm d}x\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{Q}f^{\alpha}\,{\rm d}x\leq K\;\;\text{for a certain }\alpha>1\,.

We conclude this paragraph, by stating a classical result in the theory of Sobolev spaces that relates the
L^{p}
norms of the weak derivatives of a function: the so-called
Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality
(see
[
61
]
for details of the proof).

Theorem B.9

Let
f
be a function belonging to
L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and its derivatives of order
m
, denoted by
D^{m}f
, belong to
L^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
1\leq q,r\leq+\infty
. For the derivatives
D^{j}f
, where
0\leq j<m
, the following inequality holds

\|D^{j}f\|_{L^{p}}\leq C\|D^{m}f\|_{L^{r}}^{\alpha}\|f\|_{L^{q}}^{1-\alpha}\,,

(B.3.1)

where

\frac{1}{p}=\frac{j}{d}+\alpha\left(\frac{1}{r}-\frac{m}{d}\right)+(1-\alpha)\frac{1}{q}\,,

for all
\alpha
in the interval

\frac{j}{m}\leq\alpha\leq 1\,,

with the following exceptional cases:

(i)

if
j=0
,
rm<d
and
q=+\infty
, then we make the additional assumption that either
f
tends to zero at infinity or
f\in L^{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for some
b>0
;

(ii)

if
1<r<+\infty
and
m-j-d/r
is a non-negative integer, then (
B.3.1
) holds only for
\alpha
satisfying
j/m\leq\alpha<1
.

B.4
Elliptic estimates

This part of the appendix is devoted to a classical result pertaining
L^{2}
solutions of the following elliptic equation:

-{\rm div}\,(A\nabla\Pi)={\rm div}\,F\quad\text{in}\quad\mathbb{R}^{d}\,,

(B.4.1)

where
A=A(x)
is a given suitably smooth bounded function satisfying

A_{\ast}:=\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}A(x)>0\,.

(B.4.2)

Lemma B.10

For all vector fields
F
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, there exists a tempered distribution
\Pi
(unique up to constant functions) such that
\nabla\Pi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and equation (
B.4.1
) is satisfied. Moreover, we have

A_{\ast}\|\nabla\Pi\|_{L^{2}}\leq\|F\|_{L^{2}}\,.

(B.4.3)

We refer to
[
21
]
for details of the proof.

Let us now state higher order estimates for the pressure term in Besov spaces (we refer again to
[
21
]
for details).

Proposition B.11

Let
1<p<+\infty
and
1\leq r\leq+\infty
. Let
A
be a bounded function satisfying (
B.4.2
) such that
\nabla A\in B^{s-1}_{p,r}
for some
s>1+d/p
or
s\geq 1+d/p
if
r=1
.

(i)

If
\sigma\in\;]1,s]
and
\nabla\Pi\in B^{\sigma}_{p,r}
satisfies (
B.4.1
) for some function
F
such that
{\rm div}\,F\in B^{\sigma-1}_{p,r}
, then we have for some constant
C=C(s,\sigma,p,d)
that

A_{\ast}\|\nabla\Pi\|_{B^{\sigma}_{p,r}}\leq C\left(\|{\rm div}\,F\|_{B^{\sigma-1}_{p,r}}+A_{\ast}(1+A_{\ast}^{-1}\|\nabla A\|_{B^{s-1}_{p,r}})^{\sigma}\|\nabla\Pi\|_{L^{p}}\right).

(ii)

If
2\leq p<+\infty
and
F
is in
L^{2}
and satisfies
{\rm div}\,F\in B^{\sigma-1}_{p,r}
for some
\sigma\in\;]1+d/p-d/2,\,s]
, then (
B.4.1
) has a unique solution
\Pi
(up to constant functions) such that
\nabla\Pi\in L^{2}\cap B^{\sigma}_{p,r}
. Furthermore, inequality (
B.4.3
) is satisfied and there exist a positive exponent
\gamma=\gamma(\sigma,p,d)
and a positive constant
C=C(s,\sigma,p,d)
such that

A_{\ast}\|\nabla\Pi\|_{B^{\sigma}_{p,r}}\leq C\left(\|{\rm div}\,F\|_{B^{\sigma-1}_{p,r}}+(1+A_{\ast}^{-1}\|\nabla A\|_{B^{s-1}_{p,r}})^{\gamma}\|\nabla\Pi\|_{L^{2}}\right).

(iii)

If
\sigma>1
and
1<p<+\infty
, then the following inequality holds:

A_{\ast}\|\nabla\Pi\|_{B^{\sigma}_{p,r}}\leq C\left(\|{\rm div}\,F\|_{B^{\sigma-1}_{p,r}}+\|\nabla A\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\Pi\|_{B^{\sigma-1}_{p,r}}+\|\nabla\Pi\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla A\|_{B^{\sigma-1}_{p,r}}\right).

B.5
Compactness theorems

Next, a “milestone” in our analysis is the compactness of special quantities in order to perform the asymptotic process. In this sense, the
Ascoli-Arzelà
and
Aubin-Lions
theorems come in our “rescue”.

Theorem B.12
(Ascoli-Arzelà Theorem)

Let
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be compact and
X
be a compact topological metric space endowed with a metric
d_{X}
. Let
(v_{k})_{k}
be a sequence of functions in
\mathcal{C}^{0}(Q;X)
that is equi-continuous, i.e. for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\delta>0
such that

d_{X}\Big(v_{k}(y),\,v_{k}(z)\Big)\leq\varepsilon\,,

provided
|y-z|<\delta
independently of
k
.

Then,
(v_{k})_{k}
is precompact in
\mathcal{C}^{0}(Q;X)
, meaning that there exists a subsequence (not relabelled) and a function
v\in\mathcal{C}^{0}(Q;X)
such that

\sup_{y\in Q}d_{X}\Big(v_{k}(y),\,v(y)\Big)\longrightarrow 0\quad\text{as}\quad k\rightarrow+\infty\,.

The proof of the next statement can be found in Section 5.3 of Chapter 2 of
[
9
]
(see also Section 5 of Chapter 1 in
[
54
]
).

Theorem B.13
(Aubin-Lions Theorem)

Let
X_{0}\subset X_{1}\subset X_{2}
be three Banach spaces. We assume that the embedding of
X_{1}
in
X_{2}
is continuous and that the embedding of
X_{0}
in
X_{1}
is compact. Let
(p,r)
such that
1\leq p,r\leq+\infty
. For
T>0
, we define

E_{p,r}:=\left\{v\in L^{p}(0,T;X_{0})\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{dv}{dt}\in L^{r}(0,T;X_{2})\right\}\,.

Then,

(i)

if
p<+\infty
, the embedding of
E_{p,r}
in
L^{p}(0,T;X_{1})
is compact;

(ii)

if
p=+\infty
and
r>1
, the embedding of
E_{p,r}
in
\mathcal{C}^{0}(0,T;X_{1})
is compact.

Now, we present the celebrated
Div-Curl Lemma
which, roughly speaking, ensures that the product of two functions weakly converge, if each function weakly converges and in addition one has information on the
{\rm div}\,
of one and the
{\rm curl}\,
of the other.

Theorem B.14

Let
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an open set. Assume that

\begin{split}u_{n}\rightharpoonup u\quad&\text{weakly in }L^{p}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d})\\
v_{n}\rightharpoonup v\quad&\text{weakly in }L^{q}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d})\end{split}

where

\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{r}<1\,.

In addition, let
{\rm div}\,u_{n}
be precompact in
W^{-1,s}(Q)
and
{\rm curl}\,v_{n}
be precompact in
W^{-1,s}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
, for a certain
s>1
. Then,

u_{n}\cdot v_{n}\rightharpoonup u\cdot v\quad\text{weakly in }L^{r}(Q)\,.

B.6
Grönwall’s lemma

To conclude this appendix, we mention a
Grönwall estimate
which has been of constant use in Chapter
4
.

Lemma B.15

Let
a\in L^{1}(0,T)
,
a\geq 0
,
\beta\in L^{1}(0,T)
,
b_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and

b(\tau)=b_{0}+\int_{0}^{\tau}\beta(t)\,{\rm d}t

be given. Let
f\in L^{\infty}(0,T)
satisfy

f(\tau)\leq b(\tau)+\int_{0}^{\tau}a(t)\,f(t)\,{\rm d}t\quad\text{ for a.a. }\tau\in[0,T].

Then, one has

f(\tau)\leq b_{0}\exp\left(\int_{0}^{\tau}a(t)\,{\rm d}t\right)+\int_{0}^{\tau}\beta(t)\exp\left(\int_{t}^{\tau}a(s)\,{\rm d}s\right)\,{\rm d}t

for a.a.
\tau\in[0,T]
.
